### [1] [Lee-Yang zeros of modulated XY spin chains with Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction: zero-contour topology and quantum phase-diagram reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05469)
*Hong Jiang,Xiang-Ping Jiang,Yan-Chao Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies Lee-Yang zeros of inhomogeneous anisotropic XY spin chains with Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions in the complex transverse-field plane, revealing that LYZ topology evolution correlates with band deformation and can distinguish quantum phases beyond locating phase boundaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the fundamental connection between Lee-Yang zeros and quantum phase transitions in inhomogeneous XY chains with DM interactions, and to establish LYZ topology as a probe for band folding and phase diagram restructuring.

Method: Systematic study of uniform, period-2, period-3 modulated, and Fibonacci quasiperiodic chains of lengths 5 and 8, through analytical derivations connecting DM-induced band shifts, anisotropic pairing at particle-hole band crossings, and zero position modulation.

Result: As DM coupling increases, LYZ exhibit distinct topological evolutions: uniform chains show zeros collapsing toward the real axis; periodic chains show either bifurcation of closed zero contours or re-emergence of complex zeros; quasiperiodic chains exhibit repeated annihilation and revival of complex zeros. Isolated zero contact points correspond to discrete quantum critical fields, while continuous real-zero intervals identify gapless chiral phases.

Conclusion: Lee-Yang zeros provide a direct correspondence with band deformation, enabling not only phase boundary location but also distinction of characteristic phases and serving as an intuitive probe for band folding and phase diagram restructuring in complex-band systems.

Abstract: We investigate the Lee--Yang zeros (LYZ) of inhomogeneous anisotropic XY spin chains with Dzyaloshinskii--Moriya (DM) interactions in the complex transverse-field plane, focusing on their fundamental connection to quantum phase transitions. We systematically study uniform chains, period-2 and period-3 modulated chains, and Fibonacci quasiperiodic chains of lengths 5 and 8. As the DM coupling strength $D$ increases, the LYZ exhibit qualitatively distinct topological evolutions on the complex plane: the complex zeros of the uniform chain collapse toward the real axis; periodic chains feature either bifurcation of closed zero contours before all zeros become real or a single re-emergence of complex zeros; quasiperiodic chains exhibit repeated annihilation and revival of complex zeros. Analytical derivations demonstrate that this diverse behavior originates from DM-induced shifts of folded bands and the modulation of zero positions by anisotropic pairing at particle--hole band crossings. Our results establish a direct correspondence between LYZ topology and band deformation: isolated contact points of zeros with the real axis correspond to discrete quantum critical fields, while continuous real-zero intervals directly identify gapless chiral phases. Accordingly, beyond locating phase boundaries, LYZ can distinguish characteristic phases and serve as an intuitive probe for band folding and phase diagram restructuring.

</details>


### [2] [Quantum phase transitions and quantum-information characterization of a non-Hermitian XY chain with staggered Dzyaloshinskii--Moriya interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05470)
*Hong Jiang,Xiang-Ping Jiang,Yan-Chao Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper investigates a non-Hermitian XY chain with staggered Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions using quantum-information methods, mapping it to a standard DM-free XY chain via an alternating local-spin-rotation transformation that exhibits rotation-time-reversal symmetry. Single-site entanglement detects only the LL-PM transition (exceptional boundary), while quantum discord and quantum coherence identify both phase boundaries, with directional quantum coherence measurements distinguishing the staggered-DM chain from a zero-DM counterpart.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To construct the phase diagram of a non-Hermitian XY chain with staggered DM interactions and systematically assess the performance of quantum-information-based quantities in characterizing quantum phase transitions within this non-Hermitian system.

Method: The model is mapped onto a standard DM-free XY chain via an alternating local-spin-rotation transformation. Phase diagram construction combines correlation-function analysis with known phase results of the standard XY chain. Quantum-information quantities including single-site entanglement, quantum discord, and quantum coherence are computed and analyzed for their derivatives to detect phase boundaries.

Result: Single-site entanglement detects only the LL-PM transition (the exceptional boundary where RT symmetry is restored), while QD and QC, via their second-order derivatives, resolve both the exceptional boundary and the FM-LL transition inside the RT-broken region. Directional QC measurements capture the DM-induced relative rotation between sublattices, distinguishing the staggered-DM chain from a zero-DM chain with identical effective parameters.

Conclusion: Different quantum-information measures have varying sensitivities to phase transitions in non-Hermitian systems: entanglement is less capable than QD and QC, and directional QC serves as a unique tool to distinguish DM-induced structural features, making these measures valuable for probing non-Hermitian phase diagrams.

Abstract: We investigate the phase diagram of a non-Hermitian XY chain with staggered Dzyaloshinskii--Moriya (DM) interactions using quantum-information methods. Through an alternating local-spin-rotation transformation, the model is mapped onto a standard DM-free XY chain, and the resulting transformed Hamiltonian exhibits rotation--time-reversal ($\RT$) symmetry. Combining correlation-function analysis with known phase results of the standard XY chain, we construct the phase diagram for the present system. We further adopt quantum-information-based quantities to systematically assess their performance in characterizing quantum phase transitions within this non-Hermitian system. Our results show that single-site entanglement can only detect the Luttinger-liquid (LL)--paramagnetic (PM) phase transition, which corresponds to the exceptional boundary across which $\RT$ symmetry is restored. In contrast, quantum discord (QD) and quantum coherence (QC) identify both phase boundaries: in addition to locating the exceptional boundary, their second-order derivatives resolve the ferromagnetic (FM)--LL transition inside the $\RT$-broken region. Moreover, measurements of QC along different directions capture the DM-induced relative rotation between the two sublattices, thereby distinguishing the staggered-DM chain from a zero-DM chain with identical effective parameters.

</details>


### [3] [Atmospheric Turbulence-Aware Optimization of Generalized Kennedy Receivers for Coherent-State BPSK Quantum Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05471)
*Md Ashraf Hossain Ifty,Rahul Bhadani*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops an ergodic mutual-information framework for generalized Kennedy receivers in coherent-state BPSK communication over turbulent free-space optical channels, jointly optimizing receiver displacement and input prior probabilities under Gamma-Gamma fading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve performance of coherent-state BPSK communication over turbulent free-space optical channels by optimizing generalized Kennedy receivers.

Method: An ergodic mutual-information framework is developed to jointly optimize the receiver displacement and input prior probabilities under Gamma-Gamma fading.

Result: Not stated in the abstract; the framework enables joint optimization of displacement and priors under turbulent fading conditions.

Conclusion: The proposed ergodic mutual-information framework provides a means to jointly optimize generalized Kennedy receiver parameters for turbulent FSO BPSK communication.

Abstract: We investigate generalized Kennedy receivers for coherent-state BPSK communication over turbulent free-space optical channels. An ergodic mutual-information framework is developed to jointly optimize the receiver displacement and input prior probabilities under Gamma--Gamma fading.

</details>


### [4] [Boundary-Cancelled Score-Hamiltonian Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05904)
*Masayuki Ohzeki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper connects diffusion sampling to imaginary-time annealing via a supersymmetric Score Hamiltonian, showing that fixing the noising drift constrains the reverse score term. Score-learning error is recast as a Hamiltonian perturbation, leading to a schedule principle where vanishing terminal derivatives (up to order r) improve Hellinger error from T⁻² to T⁻²ʳ⁻² via Morita–Nishimori boundary cancellation, verified by reverse-SDE benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a principled theoretical framework linking score-based diffusion models to supersymmetric quantum mechanics and annealing dynamics, enabling improved schedule design through boundary cancellation.

Method: Formulates diffusion sampling as imaginary-time annealing within a forward/backward Euclidean Schrödinger pair, identifies the reverse score term as a one-sided imaginary-time counterdiabatic connection of a supersymmetric Score Hamiltonian, and derives a schedule principle based on vanishing terminal derivatives and Morita–Nishimori boundary cancellation.

Result: Demonstrates that vanishing the first r terminal derivatives suppresses residual Hellinger error from T⁻² to T⁻²ʳ⁻² (until score or sampling floors are reached). Reverse-SDE benchmarks including 2D learned-score tests confirm the predicted improvement.

Conclusion: The supersymmetric Score Hamiltonian view provides a rigorous error-control mechanism for diffusion schedules, with boundary cancellation yielding polynomial-order improvements in sampling accuracy.

Abstract: Diffusion sampling can be viewed as imaginary-time annealing of probability densities. From a forward/backward Euclidean Schrödinger pair, we show that fixing the noising drift forces the reverse score term, and that the same logarithmic force is the one-sided imaginary-time counterdiabatic connection of a supersymmetric Score Hamiltonian. The correspondence turns score-learning error into a Hamiltonian perturbation and yields a schedule principle: if the first $r$ terminal derivatives vanish, Morita--Nishimori boundary cancellation suppresses the residual Hellinger error from $T^{-2}$ to $T^{-2r-2}$ until score or sampling floors are reached. Reverse-SDE benchmarks, including two-dimensional learned-score tests, confirm the predicted improvement.

</details>


### [5] [Quantum information spreading via higher-order operator correlators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05472)
*M Süzen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces N-Operator Correlators (NOC), a generalization of the Out-of-Time-Ordered Correlator (OTOC) that extends beyond two-operator analysis to measure quantum information spreading more comprehensively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional OTOC approaches are restricted to selecting only two operators, which limits the scope of analysis for measuring quantum information spreading. The authors aim to formulate a more generalized correlator that can incorporate a larger set of measurement probes.

Method: The authors formulate N-Operator Correlators by leveraging the concept of N-point correlation functions from cosmology. They demonstrate the framework using two analytically tractable toy systems at finite temperature: a single qubit in a longitudinal field and an XYZ two-qubit system, computing thermal averages of operator time evolutions.

Result: The NOC formulation successfully reduces the limitations of restricted two-operator OTOC analysis and accommodates a broader set of measurement probes for quantifying quantum information spreading, as demonstrated on the two toy systems.

Conclusion: This work provides a foundational introduction to generalized operator correlators in quantum mechanics, offering a new perspective on measuring quantum information spreading through N-operator constructs.

Abstract: We provide a perspective into measuring the quantum information spreading by introducing N-operator correlators. Leveraging the idea from N-point correlation function from cosmology, we formulate a generalized Out-of-Time-Ordered Correlator (OTOC), called N-Operator Correlator (NOC). Such formulation can reduce the effect of restricted analysis associated with selecting only two operators for OTOC. NOC can take into account a larger set of measurement probes in measuring the degree of quantum information spreading. We demonstrate these concepts with two analytically tractable toy systems with computing thermal averages of operator time evolutions at a finite temperature: a single qubit in longitudinal field and XYZ two-qubit system. Our perspective serves as a basic introduction to generalized operator correlators in quantum mechanics.

</details>


### [6] [Unraveling-Dependent Metastability in Monitored Quantum Systems and Associative Memories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06056)
*Manali Malakar,Roberta Zambrini,Gian Luca Giorgi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper shows that metastability in open quantum systems, typically inferred from spectral separation in the Liouvillian, is incomplete at the level of individual quantum trajectories. Conditioned realizations of the same unconditional dynamics can bypass, transiently access, or preserve metastable memory depending on the monitored channel and measurement record—demonstrated in a driven-dissipative nonlinear oscillator implementing a quantum associative memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard diagnoses of metastability rely on spectral separation of the Liouvillian governing ensemble-averaged dynamics. The authors aim to show this coarse-grained view misses important phenomena at the trajectory level, where monitored channels and measurement records can fundamentally alter whether a metastable memory is preserved, transiently accessed, or bypassed entirely.

Method: The study compares spectral analysis with target fidelities and phase-space distributions in a driven-dissipative nonlinear oscillator acting as a quantum associative memory. It examines two types of conditioned dynamics: (1) non-Hermitian dynamics from post-selecting on the absence of detection events, and (2) stochastic jump trajectories incorporating post-measurement state updates.

Result: Post-selecting on no detected events supports metastable retrieval but with a distinct long-time fate—normalization selects the least-decaying non-Hermitian mode rather than the addressed memory branch. In contrast, stochastic jump trajectories can preserve retrieval over extended times when the measurement update remains compatible with the coherent memory structure. Trajectory-level metastability is therefore not determined by the averaged generator alone.

Conclusion: Metastability at the trajectory level requires compatibility between the monitored channel, the measurement record, and the metastable manifold—information not captured by the ensemble-averaged Liouvillian spectrum alone.

Abstract: Metastability in open quantum systems is usually inferred from spectral separation in the Liouvillian, which governs unconditional, ensemble-averaged dynamics. We show that this diagnosis is incomplete at the level of individual quantum trajectories: conditioned realizations of the same unconditional dynamics can bypass, transiently access, or operationally preserve a metastable memory, depending on the monitored channel and the observed record. We demonstrate these mechanisms in a driven-dissipative nonlinear oscillator realizing a quantum associative memory, by comparing spectra, target fidelities, and phase-space distributions. The non-Hermitian dynamics obtained by post-selecting on the absence of detected events supports metastable retrieval, but with a distinct long-time fate: normalization selects the least-decaying mode of the non-Hermitian spectrum rather than the addressed memory branch. In contrast, stochastic jump trajectories can preserve retrieval over extended times when the post-measurement update remains compatible with the coherent memory structure. Thus trajectory-level metastability is not determined by the averaged generator alone; it requires compatibility between the monitored channel, the measurement record, and the metastable manifold.

</details>


### [7] [Can a Local Quantum-Mechanical Description of Physical Reality Be Considered Complete?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05478)
*J. W. Moffat*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper argues that a complete quantum field theory should be constructed nonlocally from observable principles (Copenhagen philosophy), leading to a nonlocal Schrödinger equation in the one-particle nonrelativistic limit, with the canonical commutator and Heisenberg uncertainty emerging from translation covariance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors advocate constructing quantum field theory from operational, observable principles following the Copenhagen school (Heisenberg, Bohr), arguing it is most natural to build it on a nonlocal theory of observable principles rather than local assumptions.

Method: The authors derive the nonlocal Schrödinger equation from a nonlocal quantum field theory framework based on observable principles, and demonstrate that the canonical position-momentum commutator and Heisenberg uncertainty principle emerge from translation covariance.

Result: The derivation yields a nonlocal Schrödinger equation in the one-particle nonrelativistic limit, and shows that standard quantum mechanical features (the position-momentum commutator and Heisenberg uncertainty relation) arise as consequences of translation covariance within this nonlocal framework.

Conclusion: A complete quantum field theory is most naturally formulated nonlocally based on observable principles, and standard quantum mechanics emerges as its nonlocal nonrelativistic limit.

Abstract: In a complete quantum theory, one would construct it based on what we can observe in a system and operationally define in our universe. This philosophy goes back to some of the original ideals of quantum theory defined by W. Heisenberg, N. Bohr, and others as part of the Copenhagen school of thought. We argue in this letter that that if one wishes to construct a complete quantum field theory, that it is most natural to construct it based on a nonlocal theory of observable principles. If this interpretation is valid, then the one-particle nonrelativistic limit that recovers quantum mechanics would as well be nonlocal. In this interpretation we derive the nonlocal Schrödinger equation. We will as well demonstrate that the canonical position--momentum commutator and the Heisenberg uncertainty principle emerge from translation covariance.

</details>


### [8] [Effects of Trotter Error, Digitization Error, and Initial State Overlap on Tapered Quantum Phase Estimation for Minimum Eigenvalue Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06249)
*Elijah Pelofske,Stephan Eidenbenz*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper numerically implements full quantum circuits of tapered Quantum Phase Estimation (tQPE) using bandwidth-limited DPSS tapers with Trotterized Hamiltonian evolution, and systematically studies its performance across parameters like Trotter error, finite sampling, imperfect initial states, total evolution time, and digitization error on a 4-qubit Heisenberg magnet model with up to ℓ=10 phase qubits and 12th-order Trotterization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how tapered QPE performs under realistic implementation constraints, including Trotterization errors and circuit-level approximations, and to identify how algorithm parameters affect eigenvalue estimation accuracy.

Method: Full numerical quantum circuit simulations of optimal tapered QPE with implementable approximate bandwidth-limited DPSS tapers, using standard Trotterization for Hamiltonian time-evolution and classical exact simulations on representative quantum Hamiltonians.

Result: Three key findings: i) the steady-state optimal phase sampling rate depends on the initial state overlap with the ground state; ii) tQPE shows no strong evolution time dependence in phase sampling probability or eigenvalue error unless Trotter error is high; iii) approximated time-evolution unitaries can cause substantial ringing in tQPE, producing non-physical eigenvalue estimates.

Conclusion: Tapered QPE behaves similarly to standard QPE in terms of ringing artifacts from Trotterized evolution, and its performance characteristics depend critically on initial state overlap and Trotter error rather than evolution time alone.

Abstract: We numerically implement full quantum circuits of optimal tapered Quantum Phase Estimation (in the form of an implementable approximate bandwidth-limited DPSS taper) for the purpose of minimum eigenvalue computation, where the Hamiltonian time-evolution unitary is implemented using standard Trotterization, and the taper state is constructed using an optimized bandwidth-limited taper. We illustrate, on representative quantum Hamiltonians, with tractable exact classical numerical quantum circuit simulations, how tapered QPE performs with respect to optimal phase sampling rate, absolute error, Shannon entropy of the optimal phase distribution, when the tQPE algorithm parameters are changed. Those algorithm parameters are Trotter error, finite sampling, imperfect initial states, total evolution time, and digitization error (number of phase qubits). The reported numerical experiments include up to $\ell=10$ phase qubits of precision with $m=3$ additional phase-register error suppression qubits, on a $4$-qubit Heisenberg quantum magnet model Hamiltonian, using up to 12th-order Trotterization. We numerically show i) the steady-state optimal phase sampling rate is determined by the initial state overlap with the ground-state, ii) with respect to phase sampling probability, or overall eigenvalue sampling error rate, there is no strong evolution time dependence for tQPE unless there is high Trotter error, iii) the approximated time-evolution unitaries in tQPE, like in standard QPE, can result in substantial ringing, which leads to non-physical eigenvalue estimates.

</details>


### [9] [The I3322 quantum value is attained spatially but not in finite dimension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05555)
*Seth Douglas*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves that the quantum supremum S of the I_3322 Bell functional (in the Collins-Gisin normalization, with classical bound 0 and two-qubit maximum 1/4) is not attained by any finite-dimensional quantum strategy, settling a 2010 conjecture of Pal and Vertesi, while showing S is attained by a spatial strategy on ℓ²(Z)⊗ℓ²(Z). This establishes that C_q(3,3;2,2) is not closed—the smallest two-outcome bipartite scenario by input count where nonclosure is known—and that C_qs(3,3;2,2)∖C_q(3,3;2,2) is nonempty, answering a question of Dykema, Paulsen and Prakash. The paper also determines dimension complexity D(ε)=Θ(log(1/ε)) for approaching S, with core parts machine-checked in Lean 4.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To settle long-standing open questions in quantum Bell nonlocality: whether the I_3322 quantum supremum is attained by finite-dimensional strategies (conjectured not by Pal and Vertesi in 2010), whether the commuting-operator and tensor-product suprema are equal and attained in infinite dimensions, whether C_q is closed in this scenario, and what the minimum dimension scaling is to approximate the supremum.

Method: The proof combines certified interval enclosures of the supremum S, a certified equality between tensor-product and commuting-operator suprema, a concave critical Bellman storage argument with exact rational endpoint-exclusion certificates to show finite-dimensional nonattainment (finiteness forces two equality transports of a maximizer to coincide, capping value at 1/4<S), a reflection-gluing and convex-envelope theorem, and a spectral disintegration of a commuting maximizer over orbits of its response transports yielding an ℓ² Jacobi eigenvector for infinite-dimensional attainment. Dimension complexity upper bound is constructive with D(ε)≤23.9010650 log(1/ε), with lower bound via certificate chains. Core parts are machine-checked in Lean 4.

Result: (i) No finite-dimensional quantum strategy attains S for the I_3322 functional—proving the Pal-Vertesi conjecture. (ii) S is attained by an infinite-dimensional spatial strategy on ℓ²(Z)⊗ℓ²(Z). Consequently C_q(3,3;2,2) is not closed, C_qs(3,3;2,2)∖C_q(3,3;2,2) is nonempty, and the dimension complexity satisfies D(ε)=Θ(log(1/ε)) with explicit constant 23.9010650 in the upper bound.

Conclusion: The I_3322 scenario provides the smallest known bipartite two-outcome example (by input count) of nonclosure of the quantum set, with strict separation between commuting-operator and tensor-product suprema in terms of attainment. The sharp threshold between attainment and nonattainment, and the logarithmic dimension scaling, are established with machine-checked certificates.

Abstract: Let $S$ be the quantum supremum of the $I_{3322}$ Bell functional in the Collins-Gisin normalization (classical bound 0; two-qubit maximum exactly 1/4). From a certified window $S\in(0.2508753845015185,0.250875388108398]$ (independently and more tightly enclosed by Mghirbi's prior certificates) and a certified equality of the tensor-product and commuting-operator suprema, we prove: (i) no finite-dimensional quantum strategy attains $S$ -- any finite local dimensions, pure or mixed states, projective or POVM measurements -- proving the conjecture of Pal and Vertesi (2010); (ii) $S$ is attained by a spatial strategy on $\ell^2(\mathbb{Z})\otimes\ell^2(\mathbb{Z})$, the infinite-dimensional attainment those authors asserted, on an independent route. So $C_q(3,3;2,2)$ is not closed -- the smallest two-outcome bipartite scenario by input count where nonclosure is known -- and $C_{qs}(3,3;2,2)\setminus C_q(3,3;2,2)$ is nonempty, settling the attainment question raised by Dykema, Paulsen and Prakash. Nonattainment is proved via a concave critical Bellman storage, exact rational endpoint-exclusion certificates, reflection-gluing and a convex-envelope theorem: finiteness forces an exact maximizer's two equality transports to coincide, capping its value at $1/4<S$. Attainment is proved by disintegrating a commuting maximizer's spectral measure over the orbits of its two response transports, yielding an $\ell^2$ Jacobi eigenvector with inherited normalizability. We also determine the dimension complexity: with $S_d$ the optimum at local dimension $\le d$ and $D(ε)=\min\{d:S-S_d\leε\}$, $D(ε)=Θ(\log(1/ε))$ -- the upper half constructively, with $D(ε)\le 23.9010650\log(1/ε)$; the lower half in a certificate chain in the accompanying repository. Cores of both halves are machine-checked in Lean 4.

</details>


### [10] [Entanglement Growth as Transport Across Schmidt Scales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06643)
*Shi-Xin Zhang,Shuo Liu,Yu-Qin Chen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces two new dynamical coordinates—Schmidt-scale concentration and dominant Schmidt scale—that track where probability mass resides across Schmidt-rank space in logarithmic windows. Applied to quenches in a random-field spin chain, they reveal a clear hierarchy distinguishing entropy production, entanglement-spectrum reorganization, and dominant-Schmidt-scale transport, with a solvable head–tail model explaining the temporal ordering of these processes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional entanglement studies rely on a single entropy number that obscures how correlations are distributed across the exponentially large Schmidt spectrum and how they form over time. New coordinates are needed to resolve the fine-grained structure of entanglement dynamics beyond entropy alone.

Method: The authors define two Schmidt-scale coordinates (concentration and dominant scale) locating the probability maximum across logarithmic windows in ordered Schmidt-rank space. They apply these to disorder-averaged quenches of a random-field spin chain, complemented by a solvable head–tail model and controlled numerical experiments to identify the physical origins of the observed dynamics.

Result: At weak disorder, the dominant Schmidt scale rapidly transports to higher rank; at strong disorder, transport is suppressed despite sustained logarithmic entropy growth. Disorder-averaged dynamics show an ordered hierarchy: entropy production peaks first, followed by spectral roughness and exact nonlocal magic, with dominant-Schmidt-scale transport becoming typical only after substantial delay. The head–tail model and numerics trace these timings to the spectral path, local dynamics on active exchange bonds, and intra-subsystem many-body dressing.

Conclusion: Schmidt-scale coordinates provide powerful dynamical probes that distinguish entanglement production, spectrum reorganization, and dominant-scale transport, enabling deeper insight into fine-grained entanglement structure not accessible through entropy alone.

Abstract: Quantum entanglement growth is commonly summarized by a single entropy, obscuring where correlations reside in the exponentially large Schmidt spectrum and how they form. Here, we introduce Schmidt-scale concentration and dominant Schmidt scale, two coordinates that locate the probability maximum across logarithmic windows in ordered Schmidt-rank space. Applied to quenches of a random-field spin chain, these coordinates distinguish rapid transport of the dominant scale to higher Schmidt rank at weak disorder from strongly suppressed transport despite continued logarithmic entropy growth at strong disorder. The disorder-averaged dynamics exhibit an ordered hierarchy: entropy production peaks first, spectral roughness and exact nonlocal magic peak next, and dominant-Schmidt-scale transport becomes typical only after a substantial delay. Moreover, a solvable head--tail model and controlled numerical experiments reveal the physical origin of this hierarchy: the spectral path determines the order of events, local dynamics on active exchange bonds set their early timing, and intra-subsystem many-body dressing further delays dominant-Schmidt-scale transport. These results establish the Schmidt-scale coordinates as powerful dynamical probes for uncovering fine-grained entanglement structures distinguishing entanglement production, entanglement-spectrum reorganization, and dominant-Schmidt-scale transport beyond entropy alone.

</details>


### [11] [Matrix Product Belief Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05598)
*Gabriel Woolls,Shahin Jahanbani,Adarsh Pashikanti,Matthew T. Fishman,Joseph Tindall,Michael P. Zaletel*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces "matrix product belief propagation" (MP-BP), a controlled approximate method for contracting two-dimensional tensor networks and graphical models that achieves quadratic error reduction compared to existing methods—asymptotically doubling the number of accurate digits at similar computational cost. MP-BP generalizes belief propagation by assuming messages live on surfaces with matrix-product factorization, unifies with Baxter's corner-transfer-matrix method for infinite systems, and coincides with boundary MPS/CTMRG algorithms under reflection symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approximate methods for 2D tensor network contraction and graphical model inference suffer from limited accuracy. There is a need for a controlled, systematically improvable approach that reduces errors more rapidly than current algorithms.

Method: MP-BP generalizes belief propagation by treating messages as living on surfaces with matrix-product-state factorization. For infinite systems it implements Baxter's corner-transfer-matrix method; with unit matrix-product rank it reduces to standard BP; under reflection symmetry it coincides with boundary MPS and CTMRG algorithms. The approach achieves quadratic error reduction relative to existing methods.

Result: MP-BP yields asymptotically double the number of accurate digits at comparable computational cost to existing methods. The quadratic improvement extends to expectation-value computation and is numerically demonstrated across various classical and quantum 2D tensor network problems.

Conclusion: MP-BP provides a unified, controlled, and systematically improvable framework for 2D tensor network contraction that offers quadratic improvements in accuracy over prior approaches, connecting belief propagation, corner-transfer methods, and matrix-product-state boundary algorithms within a single formalism.

Abstract: We introduce "matrix product belief propagation" (MP-BP) as a controlled method for the approximate contraction of two-dimensional tensor networks and graphical models, which generically leads to a quadratic reduction of errors relative to existing methods: at similar computational effort, the number of accurate digits is asymptotically doubled. For infinite systems, MP-BP can be understood as a practical implementation of Baxter's corner-transfer-matrix method; for reflection-symmetric networks, it further coincides with the boundary matrix-product-state and corner-transfer-matrix renormalization group algorithms as conventionally implemented. In the limit of unit matrix-product rank, MP-BP is equivalent to belief propagation, and more generally, can be understood as a generalization of BP in which the "messages" live on surfaces and are assumed to have a matrix-product factorization. We further extend the quadratic improvement to the computation of expectation values, and numerically demonstrate the qualitatively improved convergence for a variety of classical and quantum 2D tensor network problems.

</details>


### [12] [Efficient Representation of multicategorical local Hilbert spaces: nonlinear Restricted Boltzmann Machines to Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06672)
*Abhiroop Lahiri*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a nonlinear representation for neural network quantum states (NQS) of multi-categorical systems (spin-S with S>1/2, q-state Potts models) that directly uses the raw multi-valued spin variable as input to a nonlinear wavefunction ansatz, rather than the standard one-hot/unary encoding. The approach preserves local basis labeling freedom, reproduces one-hot models with fewer parameters, and improves trainability. It is benchmarked on spin-1, 2, and 3 Heisenberg chains and quantum Potts Hamiltonians using both RBMs with nonlinear connections and Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs), demonstrating critical behavior recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard NQS for multi-state systems rely on one-hot encoded MLPs, which grow in parameter count with the number of local states and produce optimization landscapes that can hinder convergence. The authors seek a more parameter-efficient, better-conditioned representation for spin-S (S>1/2) and q-state Potts systems.

Method: The key idea is to allow the logarithm of the wavefunction, log Ψ, to be a nonlinear function of the raw multi-valued spin variable (a natural categorical generalization) instead of one-hot encoding. This is implemented first through shallow Restricted Boltzmann Machines (RBMs) with several distinct nonlinear connections, and then through Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs), where each edge has a learnable univariate nonlinearity, providing a strictly more expressive realization of the principle.

Result: Numerical benchmarks show the nonlinear raw-variable approach reproduces the one-hot model with strictly fewer parameters and often improved trainability. This is demonstrated on spin-1, 2, and 3 Heisenberg chains. The framework also captures the critical behavior of the quantum Potts Hamiltonian, recovering its phase transition.

Conclusion: Nonlinear encoding of raw multi-valued spin variables offers a parameter-efficient and better-conditioned alternative to one-hot encoding for NQS of multi-categorical quantum systems. Both RBM variants with nonlinear connections and KAN-based architectures validate the principle, and the method successfully captures quantum Potts critical phenomena.

Abstract: Neural network quantum states (NQS) for representing spin-$\frac{1}{2}$ systems are typically built from Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLPs) with binary visible variables, in which the input spins are first combined through linear affine maps before more expressive nonlinear transformations are applied. The standard way for representing multi-categorical systems, namely spin-1 and spin-2, or the q-state quantum Potts systems with more than two local degrees of freedom, is a unary or one-hot encoded MLP architecture. Following a few similar studies, I observe that while the one-hot construction is the mathematically faithful encoding for systems with more than two local states such as spin-$S$ models with $S > 1/2$ or $q$-state Potts models, its parameter count grows with the number of local states, and the resulting optimization landscape can hinder convergence. I find numerically that allowing $\log Ψ$ to be a nonlinear function of the raw multi-valued spin variable is a natural categorical generalization: it preserves the labelling freedom of the local basis and reproduces the one-hot model with strictly fewer parameters, often with improved trainability. I first benchmark this idea on shallow Restricted Boltzmann Machines (RBMs), equipping them with several distinct nonlinear connections, for spin-1, 2, and 3 Heisenberg chains. I then turn to Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs), where each edge carries a learnable univariate nonlinearity, and show that they provide a strictly more expressive realization of the same principle. Finally, I demonstrate that this framework captures the critical behaviour of the quantum Potts Hamiltonian, recovering its phase transition.

</details>


### [13] [Computing 256-bit elliptic curve discrete logarithms in 26 days on a fault-tolerant trapped-ion quantum computer with 20,000 qubits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05625)
*Thomas Häner,Felix Tripier,Jacob Young,Michael Naehrig,Andrii Maksymov,Safwan Alam,Dmitri Maslov,Matthew Parrott,Yvette de Sereville,Jordan Sullivan,Mark Webster,Nicolas Delfosse,John Gamble,Martin Roetteler*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents optimizations to the Walking Cat Architecture (a trapped-ion quantum computer design) specifically for solving the 256-bit elliptic curve discrete logarithm problem on secp256k1 (the curve behind Bitcoin) using Shor's algorithm. The optimized logical quantum circuit requires ~1450 qubits and 40×10^6 Toffoli gates, and full physical resource estimates suggest the problem can be solved in ~25.7 days using 19,397 physical qubits with 63% success probability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Demonstrate the extensibility and application-specific optimizability of the Walking Cat Architecture by targeting a concrete, high-value cryptographic problem—breaking secp256k1 ECDLP via Shor's algorithm—thereby providing rigorous resource estimates for a real-world quantum computing threat.

Method: The authors (1) optimize the quantum circuits from Schrottenloher's work for ECDLP on secp256k1, arriving at a logical circuit using ~1450 qubits and 40×10^6 Toffoli gates with a rigorous lower-bound success probability of 1−2^−128; (2) use a compilation toolchain with manual logical-layout optimization and integrated routing to produce logical measurement-depth and physical-qubit estimates; (3) employ a fast CCZ magic-state factory with depth-one CCZ state injection (31× speedup for CCZ gates); (4) increase measurement parallelism via non-overlapping cat-based measurements; (5) use the logical CliNR protocol for faster Clifford operations; (6) introduce a more efficient loss-correction protocol; and (7) design layouts that recycle CliNR ancilla qubits and provision cat-state resources according to peak measurement parallelism.

Result: The fully optimized trapped-ion quantum computer based on the Walking Cat Architecture can solve the ECDLP on secp256k1 in approximately 25.7 days using 19,397 physical qubits with an estimated success probability of 63%.

Conclusion: The results serve as a proof-of-concept that the Walking Cat Architecture can be effectively specialized for specific cryptanalytic applications, yielding concrete physical resource estimates for a practically relevant quantum threat to blockchain cryptography.

Abstract: One of the strengths of our recently proposed Walking Cat Architecture for a trapped-ion quantum computer is that it is straightforward to extend and optimize for a specific application. As a proof-of-concept, here we present such optimizations for solving the $256$-bit elliptic curve discrete logarithm problem (ECDLP) on $\mathtt{secp256k1}$, which is the elliptic curve used by blockchain technologies such as Bitcoin, using Shor's algorithm. We optimize the circuits from Schrottenloher's recent work and arrive at a logical quantum circuit for solving the ECDLP using about $1450$ qubits and $40\cdot 10^6$ Toffoli gates, with a rigorous lower bound on the logical-level success probability that holds with confidence at least $1-2^{-128}$. Using our compilation toolchain with manual optimization of the logical layout and integrated routing, we produce estimates for the logical measurement depth and the required number of physical qubits by compiling all components to measurement schedules that obey the architectural constraints. A key ingredient is a fast CCZ magic-state factory and a depth-one CCZ state injection, reducing the execution time of CCZ gates by a factor of $31$. We increase the logical-measurement parallelism using non-overlapping cat-based measurements in parallel, and we leverage the recently proposed logical CliNR protocol to speed up Clifford operations. To reduce the qubit overhead, we introduce a more efficient loss correction protocol, design a layout that allows us to recycle the CliNR ancilla qubits, and provision reusable cat-state resources according to the circuit's peak measurement parallelism. All results and optimizations combined, we conclude that a trapped-ion quantum computer based on our architecture can solve the ECDLP on $\mathtt{secp256k1}$ in approximately 25.7 days using 19,397 physical qubits with an estimated success probability of $63\%$.

</details>


### [14] [Topology Obstructs Pure Foundation Neural Quantum States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07591)
*Timothy Heightman,Elena Orlova,Philip Mantrov,Aleksei Ustimenko*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves a topological no-go theorem: any continuous pure-state (vector-valued) neural quantum state model fails to faithfully represent ground states of gapped Hamiltonian families with non-trivial ground-state bundles, exhibiting zero fidelity and energy penalty of at least one spectral gap at some parameter point. Operator-valued (density-matrix) models canonically avoid these obstructions and are thus structurally necessary for foundation neural quantum states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models for spin-1/2 ground states, which are currently pure-state (state-vector) representations conditioned on Hamiltonian parameters, are widely used but may be fundamentally limited in their expressivity. The authors seek to determine whether such pure-state representations are topologically capable of representing arbitrary ground-state families, and whether alternative representations are necessary.

Method: The authors employ topological arguments: they analyze the ground-state bundle of gapped Hamiltonian families, showing that non-trivial bundles obstruct any continuous normalized state-vector parametrization. They extend this no-go result to degenerate ground-state manifolds, time dynamics, and periodic systems with mixed space-time topology, and illustrate the obstructions with one- and two-qubit examples. They also analyze the resulting spike in fidelity susceptibility and demonstrate that operator-valued (mixed-state/density-matrix) models bypass these topological constraints.

Result: The work proves that for any gapped Hamiltonian family with a non-trivial ground-state bundle, any continuous normalized pure-state model has zero fidelity with the true ground state at some parameter value, where the energy is at least one spectral gap Δ, with an O(Δ) energy gap in an open neighborhood. These obstructions appear for degenerate manifolds, time evolution, and periodic systems. Operator-valued neural quantum state representations canonically avoid these issues and preserve topological information.

Conclusion: Pure-state (state-vector) neural quantum state models are topologically obstructed for representing ground states of certain Hamiltonian families, causing spurious numerical signatures resembling phase transitions. Operator-valued representations are structurally necessary to build faithful foundation neural quantum state models that preserve topological information.

Abstract: Foundation models for ground states in spin-1/2 systems are a promising method for problems ranging from quantum chemistry to identifying new phase diagrams. Nearly all such models are currently pure-states that condition on the Hamiltonian's parameters, whose Monte Carlo samples give energy estimates according to the variational principle. In this contribution, we show that this representation is topologically obstructed. For any gapped Hamiltonian family whose ground-state bundle is non-trivial, every continuous normalized state-vector model has zero fidelity with the ground state at some parameter value in the Hamiltonian family. For that value, the energy is at least one spectral gap, $Δ$, with an $O(Δ)$ gap in an open-neighbourhood of that point. We show that this is a sufficient no-go also in the case of degenerate ground-state manifolds, time dynamics, and periodic systems with mixed space-time topology, demonstrating these obstructions on one- and two-qubit systems. We discuss how this causes a spike in the fidelity susceptibility, giving a numerical signature of a phase-transition where there is none. We then show that operator-valued models canonically avoid these obstructions and preserve topological information, implying a structural necessity in representation for foundation neural quantum states.

</details>


### [15] [Hawking Radiation in non-Hermitian Microscopic Analogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05632)
*Diego F. Munoz-Arboleda,Cristiane Morais Smith,Marcus Stålhammar*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A fermionic tight-binding chain coupled to Markovian reservoirs is proposed as a microscopic open quantum system that emulates black-hole physics, producing exceptional cones defining a Painlevé-Gullstrand geometry with black-hole and white-hole sectors, and exhibiting analogue Hawking radiation signatures through frequency-resolved scattering and nonlocal correlations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a microscopic open quantum system platform that realizes black-hole physics phenomena, connecting non-Hermitian systems with analogue Hawking radiation through tractable, experimentally accessible many-body probes.

Method: The authors construct a quadratic Lindbladian for a fermionic chain coupled to Markovian reservoirs, take an effective non-Hermitian limit yielding a gain/loss lattice with non-reciprocal next-nearest-neighbor hopping, analyze the rapidity spectrum (identifying tilted exceptional cones and Painlevé-Gullstrand geometry), compute steady-state densities/currents, examine the effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonian via biorthogonal flux, and introduce a fermionic Gaussian Nambu extension to formulate Hawking-pair witnesses via frequency-resolved scattering, nonlocal density-density correlations, and anomalous Hawking-partner amplitudes.

Result: The spectrum forms tilted exceptional cones separating into black-hole and white-hole sectors; steady-state observables show clear signatures at horizon positions; biorthogonal flux identifies outgoing exterior/interior channels; and the correlations display analogue Hawking radiation signatures while satisfying the fermionic covariance-positivity constraint.

Conclusion: The work establishes a connection between microscopic open-system dynamics, emergent non-Hermitian geometry, and fermionic many-body probes of analogue Hawking radiation, offering a concrete microscopic platform for studying black-hole phenomena in quantum many-body systems.

Abstract: Non-Hermitian systems have recently emerged as a convenient platform to realize black-hole physics. Here, we propose a microscopic open quantum system, namely a fermionic tight-binding chain coupled to Markovian reservoirs, which emulates black-hole properties. The resulting quadratic Lindbladian admits an effective non-Hermitian limit that produces a gain and loss lattice model with a non-reciprocal next-nearest-neighbor hopping. The rapidity spectrum of the full Lindbladian forms tilted exceptional cones that define an effective Painlevé-Gullstrand geometry and separates into black-hole and white-hole sectors, while steady-state particle densities and currents retain clear signatures at the horizon positions. We then analyze the effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonian that arise from the microscopic quantum system. The biorthogonal flux identifies the outgoing exterior and interior channels. Finally, we introduce a fermionic Gaussian Nambu extension and formulate Hawking-pair witnesses through frequency-resolved scattering, nonlocal density-density correlations, and anomalous Hawking-partner amplitudes. The resulting correlations display analogue Hawking radiation signatures, while the frequency-resolved Hawking-partner correlations satisfy the fermionic covariance-positivity constraint. These results establish a connection between microscopic open-system dynamics, emergent non-Hermitian geometry, and fermionic many-body probes of analogue Hawking radiation.

</details>


### [16] [Restricted typicality in non-equilibrium quantum many-body systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07832)
*Konrad Pawlik,Piotr Sierant,Jakub Zakrzewski*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows that for quantum many-body systems with timescale separation governed by conserved-charge transport, complex nonlinear properties of the time-evolved state (such as half-chain entanglement entropy and participation entropy) can be reconstructed from the system's slow modes alone by constraining the maximum-entropy Scrooge ensemble. This establishes "global restricted typicality," generalizing canonical typicality to hydrodynamic restricted submanifolds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exact simulation of quantum many-body systems requires exponentially scaling resources. While hydrodynamic and statistical mechanics frameworks efficiently predict linear expectation values of local observables, they provide no information about nonlinear properties of the quantum state, such as entanglement structure.

Method: The authors employ the maximum-entropy Scrooge ensemble constrained only by the system's slow (hydrodynamic) modes to reconstruct the full time-evolved state. This enables access to nonlinear quantum properties rather than just local expectation values, exploiting the timescale separation where dynamics are bottlenecked by conserved-charge transport.

Result: The approach accurately reconstructs complex nonlinear quantum properties of the global time-evolved state, including half-chain entanglement entropy and participation entropy in the computational basis.

Conclusion: A hydrodynamically bottlenecked system behaves as a typical pure state within a dynamically restricted submanifold of Hilbert space—a phenomenon termed "global restricted typicality"—generalizing the canonical typicality paradigm to non-equilibrium settings governed by slow hydrodynamic modes.

Abstract: Characterizing the time evolution of generic quantum many-body systems is a fundamental challenge, as representing the exact state requires exponentially scaling computational resources. While hydrodynamics and statistical mechanics successfully simplify this task by predicting the expectation values of local observables, these macroscopic frameworks provide no information about nonlinear characteristics of the quantum state. In this work, we demonstrate that for systems exhibiting a timescale separation, with dynamics governed by the transport of conserved charges, this lost information can be systematically recovered. By constraining the maximum-entropy Scrooge ensemble solely by the system's slow modes, we accurately reconstruct complex, nonlinear quantum properties of the global time-evolved state, including the half-chain entanglement entropy and the participation entropy in the computational basis. Our results generalize the paradigm of canonical typicality, revealing that a hydrodynamically bottlenecked system behaves as a typical pure state within a dynamically restricted submanifold of the Hilbert space - a phenomenon we term global restricted typicality.

</details>


### [17] [$N$-dimensional discrete Fourier transform via bosonic Hamiltonian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05644)
*Edgar Barriga,Camila Muñoz,Alejandro Muñoz,Santiago Rojas-Rojas,Pablo Solano,Carla Hermann-Avigliano,Aldo Delgado*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes bosonic Hamiltonians that realize the N-dimensional discrete Fourier transform (DFT) in a single stage of multimode photonic evolution with only phase shifters before and after, using a geometry suited to 3D waveguides. Analytical solutions are found for complete graphs up to N=6, numerical solutions up to N=31, with a scaling law of O(N log log N) — enabling a 2520-dimensional DFT with only 2625 interferometers versus ~3×10^6 in Reck/Clements architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Implementing the N-dimensional DFT in photonic integrated circuits currently requires cascades of Mach-Zehnder interferometers, which is resource-intensive and limited by evanescent coupling constraints imposed by waveguide separation. A more efficient, fabrication-tolerant architecture is needed.

Method: The system is modeled as a graph (edges = couplings, vertices = waveguides). The authors search for bosonic Hamiltonians (possibly with non-uniform propagation constants for non-complete graphs) whose single-stage multimode evolution implements the DFT. They enumerate solutions analytically for complete graphs up to N=6, numerically up to N=31, report all solutions for N≤8, partial for N=9, selected for N=10, and propose three conjectures to guide numerical search for N≥11. A closed-form sensitivity criterion selects layouts least sensitive to fabrication errors.

Result: All solutions for N≤8, partial for N=9, and selected for N=10 are reported. The scaling law for their building block implementation is O(N log log N), enabling a 2520-dimensional DFT with only 2625 interferometers, contrasted against ~3×10^6 required by Reck and Clements architectures. The missing non-affine parameters of the 6-dimensional DFT are also uncovered.

Conclusion: The proposed single-stage multimode interaction architecture achieves the N-dimensional DFT far more efficiently than conventional cascaded interferometer designs, with dramatically reduced component counts and fabrication-error resilience, making large-scale quantum DFT implementations in photonic circuits practical.

Abstract: The discrete Fourier transform (DFT) underpins many classical algorithms and is a fundamental unitary operator for quantum information processing. Implementing the $N$-dimensional DFT in photonic integrated circuits (PICs) is limited by the cascades of Mach-Zehnder interferometers that current architectures require. Here we propose bosonic Hamiltonians that realize the $N$-dimensional DFT through a single stage of multimode evolution, complemented only by phase shifters before and after the interaction region, in a geometry suited to 3D waveguides. Modeling the system as a graph, where edges correspond to couplings and the vertices are the waveguides, we obtain analytical solutions for complete graphs up to $N=6$ and numerical solutions up to $N=31$. For non-complete graphs, different propagation constants are required in the Hamiltonian. We report all solutions for $N\leq 8$, partial exploration for $N=9$, and selected cases for $N=10$, together with three conjectures that guide the numerical search for $N \geq 11$. These configurations circumvent the vanishing evanescent coupling strength imposed by the waveguide separation, and a closed-form sensitivity criterion selects those that are admissible as a waveguide layout and least sensitive to fabrication error. We also uncover the missing non-affine parameters of the $6$-dimensional DFT, and show that the scaling law for implementing the $N$-dimensional DFT with our building blocks is $\mathcal{O}(N\log\log{N})$. This allows assembling the $2520$-dimensional DFT with only $2625$ interferometers, in contrast to the $\approx 3\times 10^6$ of Reck and Clements architectures.

</details>


### [18] [Ordered-Angle Coding for Exact Multiuser Unanimity Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05645)
*Luis Adrián Lizama-Pérez*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces ordered-angle coding for binary-unanimity testing among n transformation-only users in a serial quantum architecture. A user applies a private sign rotation R(s_i α_j) per trial, and the paper characterizes the complete family of deterministic pulses: α_j = q_j π/(2n). Mixed Hamming weight imitates the unanimous signature with probability M_q(w) = ∏ cos²(πq_j w/n). A finite perfect word exists iff n is a power of two, where the dyadic word (1,2,4,...,2^{r-1}) achieves exact verification in O(log n) trials, replacing O(n² log(1/ε)) worst-case burden of repeated smallest-angle tests. Robustness bounds under angular noise and binary-readout errors are derived, and Bell entanglement is shown to be superfluous for the additive algebra but useful for keeping the user locally maximally mixed. The result is positioned as a coded multiuser relation primitive, not a composably secure conference-key protocol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To replace the expensive O(n² log(1/ε)) worst-case burden of repeated smallest-angle tests for binary-unanimity verification with an exponentially more efficient exact approach, by designing optimal pulse schedules in a serial quantum architecture inherited from Loop-Back communication.

Method: Introduces ordered-angle coding where user B_i fixes a private sign s_i and applies R(s_i α_j) in trial j. Characterizes the complete deterministic-unanimity pulse family requiring α_j = q_j π/(2n) with integer q_j. Derives the imitation probability for mixed Hamming weight words M_q(w) = ∏ cos²(π q_j w/n). Analyzes finite perfect-word existence (iff n is a power of two), constructs the dyadic word (1,2,4,...,2^{r-1}) achieving m_min = r = log₂n pulses, provides balanced statistical words for odd primes, and establishes robustness bounds incorporating angular errors δ_ij and binary-readout noise via the lifted rejection probability sin² Δ_j with Δ_j = Σ_i δ_ij.

Result: Shows that the ordered-angle coding attains exponentially efficient verification: O(log n) exact pulses for n = 2^r using the dyadic word, versus the previous O(n² log(1/ε)) burden. Establishes the fundamental sufficiency/necessity that finite perfect words exist exactly when n is a power of two. For odd primes, balanced statistical words achieve uniform mixed-weight imitation 2^{-(p-1)}. Derives explicit robustness bounds under combined angular and binary-readout error. Confirms Bell entanglement is not required for the additive algebra but aids in local maximal mixing of the traveler during honest trials.

Conclusion: The ordered-angle coding framework provides a complete characterization of deterministic-unanimity pulses in serial quantum multi-user architectures, yielding an exact O(log n) verification protocol for power-of-two user counts. The work is presented as a coded multiuser relation primitive with optional raw conference-key-candidate use, explicitly not claiming composable security for the conference-key setting.

Abstract: We introduce ordered-angle coding for binary-unanimity testing among $n$ transformation-only users in a serial quantum architecture inherited from Loop-Back communication. User $B_i$ fixes one private sign across a logical word and applies $R(s_iα_j)$ in trial $j$. If $w$ users choose the negative sign, serial composition gives $R[(n-2w)α_j]$. We show that every fixed same-axis pulse that is deterministic for both unanimous inputs under the binary Bell readout has $α_j=q_jπ/(2n)$ with integer $q_j$. For a nonadaptive word $\mathbf q=(q_1,\ldots,q_m)$, a mixed Hamming weight imitates the unanimous signature with probability \[ M_{\mathbf q}(w)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\cos^2\!\left(\frac{πq_jw}{n}\right). \] Within this complete deterministic-unanimity pulse family, a finite perfect word exists if and only if $n$ is a power of two. For $n=2^r$, the dyadic word $(1,2,4,\ldots,2^{r-1})$ is exact and pulse-minimal with $m_{\min}=r=\log_2n$ trials. It replaces the $O(n^2\log(1/\varepsilon))$ worst-case burden of repeated smallest-angle tests by exact $O(\log n)$ verification. Odd primes instead admit balanced statistical words with uniform mixed-weight imitation $2^{-(p-1)}$.
  For actual operations $R(s_iα_j+δ_{ij})$, an ideal rejecting position is lifted to $\sin^2Δ_j$, where $Δ_j=\sum_iδ_{ij}$. This yields explicit robustness bounds with angular and binary-readout errors. Bell entanglement is not required for the additive algebra, but it keeps the traveler locally maximally mixed in every honest trial. We therefore present the result as a coded multiuser relation primitive with optional raw conference-key-candidate use, not as a composably secure conference-key protocol.

</details>


### [19] [Minimum Cardinalities of Multipartite Unextendible Product Bases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05657)
*Chenhao Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper settles the long-standing minimum-cardinality problem for unextendible product bases (UPBs) in finite multipartite quantum systems. The authors prove a stabilization theorem: for any non-all-qubit system with p≥3 parts, when parity arguments prevent the natural lower bound f_LB from being attained, the true minimum is exactly f_LB+1. Combined with earlier work, this completely resolves the problem in all finite quantum systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UPBs are fundamental objects in quantum information theory for understanding entanglement and nonlocality. The minimum-size problem for UPBs — determining the fewest pairwise orthogonal product states that block any further product state from being added — has remained open for two decades in the obstructed multipartite cases since Alon and Lovász's seminal work resolved when the natural lower bound is attainable but left the parity-obstructed cases unresolved.

Method: The proof introduces a unified graph-theoretic framework to analyze UPBs. The key result is a stabilization theorem showing that whenever p≥3 and the dimensional parity condition obstructs attainment of the lower bound f_LB (specifically, when the number of even local dimensions is positive and even, and at least one local dimension exceeds two), the minimum cardinality is precisely f_LB+1.

Result: The paper proves that f_m(d_1,...,d_p) = f_LB(d_1,...,d_p) + 1 exactly in the parity-obstructed regime (p≥3, positive even number of even local dimensions, and at least one dimension > 2). Together with prior results covering all other cases, this completely resolves the minimum-cardinality problem for UPBs in all finite quantum systems.

Conclusion: The minimum-size problem for UPBs is now fully settled across all finite multipartite quantum systems. The result confirms that the obstruction caused by parity can shift the minimum by at most one beyond the natural lower bound, exhibiting a clean stabilization phenomenon.

Abstract: In quantum information theory, the state space of a multipartite quantum system is modeled by a tensor product. In the tensor-product space $\mathbb C^{d_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb C^{d_p}$, a nonzero vector is a \emph{product state} if it can be written as $\lvert \varphi_1\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes\lvert \varphi_p\rangle$ with $\lvert \varphi_j\rangle\in\mathbb C^{d_j}\setminus\{0\}$. An \emph{unextendible product basis} (UPB) is a finite family of pairwise orthogonal product states such that no nonzero product state is orthogonal to all of them. UPBs play a key role in investigating quantum entanglement and nonlocal phenomena. Finding a smallest UPB is a natural extremal problem: it asks how few pairwise orthogonal product states suffice to prevent any further product state from being added. The general minimum-size problem for UPBs has been studied for over two decades since the seminal work of Alon and Lovász. For local dimensions $d_1,\ldots,d_p\ge2$, let $f_m(d_1,\ldots,d_p)$ be the minimum cardinality of a UPB and let $f_{LB}(d_1,\ldots,d_p)=1+\sum_{j=1}^{p}(d_j-1)$ be the natural lower bound. Alon and Lovász determined exactly when $f_m$ attains the lower bound $f_{LB}$, but the obstructed multipartite cases remained open in general.
  We prove a stabilization theorem: for every non-all-qubit system with $p\ge3$, whenever parity prevents the natural lower bound $f_{LB}$ from being attained, the true minimum is exactly $f_{LB}+1$. Equivalently, if the number of even local dimensions is positive and even, and at least one local dimension is greater than two, then $f_m(d_1,\ldots,d_p)=f_{LB}(d_1,\ldots,d_p)+1$. The proof is built on a unified graph-theoretic framework. Our result, together with earlier work, settles the minimum-cardinality problem for UPBs in all finite quantum systems.

</details>


### [20] [Robustness of Dynamical Casimir Effect-Induced Quantum Synchronization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05659)
*Sebastián Francisquez,Fernando C. Lombardo,Paula I. Villar*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the robustness of quantum synchronization induced by the dynamical Casimir effect (DCE) in a circuit quantum electrodynamics (cQED) architecture with two superconducting qubits coupled to a shared parametrically driven cavity. Using a Lindblad master equation, the study evaluates decoherence channels' impacts and finds DCE-induced synchronization persists under realistic dissipation rates, with pure dephasing identified as the dominant limiting factor. It also warns that Pearson correlation may overestimate synchronization in strongly dissipative regimes due to classical correlations mimicking synchronized behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess the experimental feasibility of DCE-induced quantum synchronization in superconducting cQED platforms by systematically analyzing how realistic decoherence channels affect synchronization dynamics, and to establish parameter regimes for observing genuine quantum synchronization.

Method: Lindblad master equation approach modeling two superconducting qubits coupled to a shared, parametrically driven cavity, evaluating the influence of photon loss, relaxation, thermal excitation, and pure dephasing on synchronization outcomes, with the Pearson correlation coefficient used as a synchronization metric.

Result: DCE-induced synchronization persists under dissipation rates achievable in state-of-the-art superconducting platforms, confirming experimental feasibility. Pure dephasing is the dominant limiting factor on synchronization fidelity. In strongly dissipative regimes, the Pearson correlation coefficient can overestimate genuine quantum synchronization because environmental relaxation generates classical correlations mimicking synchronized behavior.

Conclusion: The findings establish the parameter regimes required for observing genuine DCE-induced quantum synchronization and provide practical guidelines for designing future cQED experiments on quantum synchronization.

Abstract: The robustness of quantum synchronization induced by the dynamical Casimir effect (DCE) is analyzed within a circuit quantum electrodynamics (cQED) architecture consisting of two superconducting qubits coupled to a shared, parametrically driven cavity. Using a Lindblad master equation approach, this study evaluates the impact of various decoherence channels (including photon loss, relaxation, thermal excitation, and pure dephasing) on the resulting synchronization dynamics. The results demonstrate that DCE-induced synchronization persists under dissipation rates achievable in state-of-the-art superconducting platforms, confirming its experimental feasibility. Among the studied mechanisms, pure dephasing is identified as the dominant factor limiting the fidelity of the synchronized state. Furthermore, it is shown that in strongly dissipative regimes, the Pearson correlation coefficient may overestimate the degree of quantum synchronization, as environmental relaxation can generate classical correlations that mimic synchronized behavior. These findings establish the parameter regimes necessary for observing genuine DCE-induced quantum synchronization and provide practical guidelines for future cQED experiments.

</details>


### [21] [Kerr Induced Control of Synchronization and Quantum State Recovery in a Driven van der Pol Oscillator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05672)
*Amir Hossein Houshmand Almani,Ali Mortezapour,Alireza Nourmandipour*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper investigates how Kerr nonlinearity modifies quantum synchronization in a squeezed quantum van der Pol oscillator. The Kerr interaction induces an amplitude-dependent frequency shift that drives a saddle-node bifurcation, transforming phase-space dynamics from bistable to monostable. In the quantum regime, this manifests as frequency pulling and spectral broadening, with the steady-state Wigner function showing continuous correspondence between quantum state and semiclassical attractor. Global phase diagrams in the squeezing–Kerr parameter space reveal a remarkably linear dependence of critical squeezing strength (required for phase locking) on Kerr nonlinearity. Notably, the synchronization boundary does not coincide with the super-/sub-Poissonian photon statistics crossover, indicating the two characterize distinct aspects of the quantum steady state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how Kerr nonlinearity, a common and important interaction in quantum systems (trapped ions, superconducting circuits, optomechanics), affects quantum synchronization in squeezed van der Pol oscillators, and to provide quantitative design principles for controlling quantum synchronization.

Method: The study theoretically analyzes a squeezed quantum van der Pol oscillator with Kerr nonlinearity, employing semiclassical phase-space analysis to identify saddle-node bifurcations, and quantum steady-state analysis (Wigner function, spectra, photon statistics) to characterize the quantum regime. Global synchronization phase diagrams are constructed in the two-parameter (squeezing–Kerr) space.

Result: Kerr nonlinearity drives a saddle-node bifurcation transitioning the classical attractor from bistable to monostable. In the quantum regime this yields systematic frequency pulling and spectral broadening. The critical squeezing strength for maintaining phase locking depends remarkably linearly on the Kerr parameter. The synchronization boundary is distinct from the crossover between super- and sub-Poissonian statistics.

Conclusion: Kerr nonlinearity provides a tunable knob for controlling quantum synchronization, with a simple linear design rule relating required squeezing to Kerr strength. Synchronization and photon-number statistics are independent quantum features, not correlated with each other. The findings offer design principles relevant to trapped-ion, superconducting-circuit, and optomechanical systems.

Abstract: We investigate how Kerr nonlinearity modifies quantum synchronization in a squeezed quantum van der Pol oscillator. We show that the Kerr interaction produces an amplitude-dependent frequency shift that drives a saddle-node bifurcation, transforming the classical phase-space structure from bistable to monostable dynamics. In the quantum regime, this transition manifests as systematic frequency pulling and spectral broadening, while the steady-state Wigner function reveals a continuous correspondence between the quantum state and the semiclassical attractor despite finite quantum fluctuations. By constructing global synchronization phase diagrams in the squeezing--Kerr parameter space, we uncover a remarkably linear dependence of the critical squeezing strength required to maintain phase locking on the Kerr nonlinearity. We further demonstrate that the synchronization boundary does not coincide with the crossover between super- and sub-Poissonian photon statistics, showing that synchronization and photon-number statistics characterize distinct aspects of the quantum steady state. These results provide quantitative design principles for controlling quantum synchronization through Kerr nonlinearity, with potential relevance to trapped-ion, superconducting-circuit, and optomechanical platforms.

</details>


### [22] [Quantum lower bounds for convex optimization and real matrix-vector query problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05679)
*Andrew M. Childs*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes near-optimal quantum query lower bounds of ~Ω(n) for convex minimization over convex subsets of R^n (including quadratic minimization equivalent to matrix inversion), as well as linear or near-linear quantum lower bounds for computing the trace, determinant sign, and determinant magnitude of real matrices in the matrix-vector query model. This is achieved via a novel technique called the determinantal witness method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to close the gap between known upper and lower bounds on quantum query complexity for convex optimization and related linear algebra problems. Understanding fundamental quantum limits on these classical optimization/linear algebra tasks is important for characterizing the power of quantum algorithms.

Method: The authors develop a novel quantum lower bound technique called the determinantal witness method. This approach identifies a witness function whose Fourier transform vanishes on low-rank matrices while correlating well with the target function being computed, thereby yielding strong query lower bounds.

Result: The paper proves: (1) quantum query complexity of convex minimization in n dimensions with evaluation and membership queries is ~Ω(n), which nearly matches the best known upper bound, even for quadratic minimization (equivalent to inverting an n×n real matrix using matrix-vector queries); (2) linear or near-linear quantum lower bounds for computing trace, determinant sign, and determinant magnitude of real matrices in the matrix-vector query model.

Conclusion: The results significantly advance our understanding of quantum limits for convex optimization and matrix computation problems, providing near-tight bounds and introducing the determinantal witness method as a powerful new tool for quantum lower bound arguments.

Abstract: We (the author and the AI systems that did the heavy lifting) show that the quantum query complexity of minimizing a convex function over a convex subset of $\mathbb{R}^n$ with evaluation and membership queries is $\tildeΩ(n)$, nearly matching the best known upper bound. In particular, we show this even for quadratic minimization, which is equivalent to inverting an $n \times n$ real matrix using matrix-vector queries. We also show linear or nearly linear lower bounds on the quantum query complexity of computing the trace, the sign of the determinant, and the magnitude of the determinant of a real matrix in the matrix-vector query model. We use a novel quantum lower bound technique, the determinantal witness method, based on identifying a witness whose Fourier transform vanishes on low-rank matrices and that correlates well with the function being computed.

</details>


### [23] [Characterizing Privacy Risks of Quantum Machine Learning with Emergent Quantum-Native Access](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05702)
*Liou Tang,James Joshi,Ashish Kundu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper examines membership inference attacks against Quantum Machine Learning (QML) models in quantum-native access regimes, where users/adversaries possess both quantum computing abilities and quantum information outputs. The authors demonstrate that increased quantum access and computing power yield provable theoretical privacy leakage and empirical adversarial gains, revealing that existing privacy research on QML underestimates leakage in quantum-native settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing privacy-preserving QML research assumes a QML-as-a-service model where providers give only classical bit outputs and users/adversaries have only classical capabilities. This assumption is challenged in quantum-native environments where adversaries have quantum computing abilities and access to quantum information outputs, creating a gap in understanding privacy leakage risks.

Method: The paper conducts membership inference attacks against QML models, systematically demonstrating that increasing quantum access and quantum computing abilities for adversaries leads to provable theoretical privacy leakage. The analysis also examines the gap between theoretical and empirical adversarial advantage caused by the probabilistic nature of QML.

Result: The results show that enhanced quantum access and computational abilities provide both provable theoretical privacy leakage and empirical adversarial gains. However, the probabilistic nature of QML creates a discrepancy between theoretical and empirical adversarial advantage, which must be accounted for.

Conclusion: Existing research on privacy leakage in QML underestimates privacy risks in emergent quantum-native access regimes. This work establishes a first step toward examining potential privacy leakages for QML in the quantum-native world.

Abstract: Quantum Machine Learning (QML) has shown rapid advances by utilizing quantum computing for machine learning tasks. Meanwhile, the privacy risks accompanying QML is also starting to be studied, which inherit privacy leakage channels from "classical" ML and also quantum-unique risks.
