Existing work on privacy-preserving QML largely focuses on a QML-as-a-service scenario, which generally assumes that the QML model owner provides only classical bit outputs to queries, while users (and adversaries) have only classical computing abilities. However, this view is increasingly challenged in a quantum-native world of quantum-capable users/adversaries, which may have access to both quantum computing abilities and access to quantum information output from service providers.
  In this paper, we aim to bridge this gap by examining membership inference attacks against QML models by demonstrating that increasing quantum access and quantum computing abilities provides provable theoretical privacy leakage and empirical adversarial gain. However, the probabilistic nature of QML introduces a gap between theoretical and empirical adversarial advantage. These results show that existing research on privacy leakage on QML models underestimate privacy leakage in an emergent quantum-native access regimes, which we hope to establish a first step into examining potential privacy leakages for QML in the quantum-native world.These results show that existing research on privacy leakage in QML models underestimates privacy leakage in emergent quantum-native access regimes, and we hope to establish a first step in examining potential privacy leakages for QML in the quantum-native world.

</details>


### [24] [Tight Lower Bounds for State Tomography with Limited Entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05718)
*Ufuk Keskin,Jason Luo,Mahbod Majid,Matthew Radzihovsky*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes tight copy complexity bounds for quantum state tomography with k-copy measurements (no quantum memory between blocks), proving a lower bound that matches the upper bound from arXiv:2510.07788. The optimal copy complexity for estimating a d-dimensional state to trace distance ε is Θ(max{d³/(√k ε²), d²/ε²}) for all k and sufficiently small ε, removing earlier restrictions that k be small relative to accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work (arXiv:2402.16353) proved a lower bound for k-copy state tomography but with the restriction that k must be small as a function of the accuracy parameter ε. This paper aims to remove that restriction entirely and establish a fully general lower bound valid for all k and all sufficiently small ε, thereby settling the copy complexity question for arbitrary k-copy measurements.

Method: The proof introduces a uniform Fisher information bound for one measurement block that depends only on the smallest eigenvalue of the quantum state. This avoids the perturbative expansion that caused the earlier restriction in arXiv:2402.16353. The adaptive protocol is reduced to this block bound using Fano's inequality for metric balls and a log-Sobolev comparison between mutual information and Fisher information, leveraging tools from arXiv:1607.00550 and arXiv:1902.08582.

Result: The lower bound matches the upper bound in arXiv:2510.07788 exactly (up to absolute constants), giving copy complexity Θ(max{d³/(√k ε²), d²/ε²}) for all k and sufficiently small ε. The bound applies to arbitrary measurements within each block, adaptive choices between blocks, and holds in a small neighborhood of any state whose smallest eigenvalue is of order 1/d, even when the center is known.

Conclusion: The copy complexity of quantum state tomography with k-copy measurements is now fully characterized up to constant factors for all values of k and all sufficiently small error ε. The key technical contribution is a uniform Fisher information bound for a single measurement block, which circumvents the perturbative limitations of prior approaches and enables a clean proof via information-theoretic reductions.

Abstract: We study state tomography when each measurement acts on at most $k$ fresh copies and no quantum memory is retained between blocks. We prove a lower bound matching the upper bound in [arXiv:2510.07788]. Thus the copy complexity of estimating an arbitrary $d$-dimensional state to trace distance $ε$ is, up to absolute constant factors, $\max\{d^3/(\sqrt{k}ε^2),d^2/ε^2\}$ for every $k$ and all sufficiently small $ε$. This removes the earlier restriction that $k$ be small as a function of the accuracy. The lower bound applies to arbitrary measurements within each block and adaptive choices between blocks. The lower bound already applies in a small neighborhood of any state whose smallest eigenvalue is of order $1/d$, even when the center is known.
  The main ingredient is a uniform Fisher information bound for one measurement block that depends only on the smallest eigenvalue of the state. The proof avoids the perturbative expansion responsible for the restriction in [arXiv:2402.16353]. Fano's inequality for metric balls and a log-Sobolev comparison between mutual and Fisher information then reduce the adaptive protocol to this block bound [arXiv:1607.00550, arXiv:1902.08582].

</details>


### [25] [SAQC: A SAT-Aware Compilation Framework for QAOA-Based Quantum Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05737)
*Till Schnittka,Julie Maria Raju,Abhoy Kole,Rolf Drechsler*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: SAQC is a SAT-aware compilation framework that preserves Boolean structure (via a SAT Intermediate Representation, SIR) during QAOA compilation for SAT problems, enabling SAT-specific optimizations before quantum lowering and achieving significant reductions in circuit depth, two-qubit gate count, and compilation time compared to conventional Hamiltonian-based workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SAT-to-QAOA workflows discard SAT-specific information (clause structure, logical operators, variable dependencies) when translating Boolean formulas into penalty Hamiltonians and applying generic quantum compilation, preventing compiler optimizations from exploiting the underlying Boolean formulation.

Method: The paper introduces SAQC, a compilation framework built around a SAT Intermediate Representation (SIR) that preserves Boolean structure. It supports CNF and XOR-extended CNF (eCNF) formulations, performs SAT-aware optimizations (clause rewriting, dependency analysis, clause scheduling) before quantum lowering, and provides a unified framework for both penalty Hamiltonian generation and direct clause-to-ansatz synthesis. Ansatz optimizations include relative-phase decomposition, ancilla-assisted synthesis, and dynamic uncomputation.

Result: Experimental results show significant reductions in circuit depth, two-qubit gate count, and compilation time compared with conventional Hamiltonian-based workflows, while remaining compatible with existing quantum compilation frameworks.

Conclusion: Preserving and exploiting SAT-specific structure during compilation can substantially improve QAOA compilation efficiency for SAT problems, suggesting that problem-aware intermediate representations are valuable for quantum compilation workflows.

Abstract: The Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) is a promising variational approach for solving Boolean satisfiability (SAT) problems. Existing SAT-to-QAOA workflows first translate Boolean formulas into penalty Hamiltonians before applying generic quantum compilation, thereby discarding SAT-specific information such as clause structure, logical operators, and variable dependencies. Consequently, subsequent compiler optimizations cannot exploit the underlying Boolean formulation. This paper presents SAQC, a SAT-aware compilation framework that introduces a SAT Intermediate Representation (SIR) to preserve Boolean structure during compilation. The proposed framework supports both CNF and XOR-extended CNF (eCNF) formulations and performs SAT-aware optimizations including clause rewriting, dependency analysis, and clause scheduling prior to quantum lowering. Building on the optimized SIR, SAQC provides a unified framework for both penalty Hamiltonian generation and direct clause-to-ansatz synthesis, together with ansatz optimizations based on relative-phase decomposition, ancilla-assisted synthesis, and dynamic uncomputation. Experimental results demonstrate significant reductions in circuit depth, two-qubit gate count, and compilation time compared with conventional Hamiltonian-based workflows while remaining compatible with existing quantum compilation frameworks.

</details>


### [26] [Quantum Query Algorithms for the Constructive Diagonal Ramsey Theorem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05812)
*Cheng Xin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper provides the first sublinear worst-case quantum algorithm for the constructive diagonal Ramsey problem, achieving O(√N log N log(log N/η)) edge queries at the Ramsey scale N=2^n by using quantum search over implicit candidate sets with capped unknown-solution search and a scale-aware concentration schedule. It also proves quantum and randomized lower bounds via collision finding and random-Painter analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The constructive diagonal Ramsey problem asks for an algorithm that, given adjacency-oracle access to an N-vertex graph, finds a clique or independent set of the size guaranteed by Ramsey's theorem. The classical explicit recursion requires O(N) queries, and no sublinear worst-case algorithm was previously known.

Method: The algorithm runs the constructive Ramsey recursion over implicit candidate sets. Each set is represented by a short conjunction of adjacency constraints and sampled using capped unknown-solution quantum search, with a scale-aware concentration schedule balancing estimation accuracy against sampling cost at deeper recursion levels. The paper also presents an estimation-free size-biased recursion extended to any fixed number of edge colours.

Result: The quantum algorithm uses O(2^K K log(K/η)) edge queries for any K≥2 and N≥4^{K−1} with failure probability η. At Ramsey scale N=2^n it finds a homogeneous set of order ⌊n/2⌋+1 using O(√N log N log(log N/η)) queries. Quantum lower bound of Ω(N^{1/12}) via collision-finding reduction, and randomized lower bound of Ω(N^{1−1/√2}) on G(N,1/2). On random graphs, greedy quantum search uses only Õ(N^{1/4}) queries, giving polynomial quantum speedup.

Conclusion: This is the first sublinear worst-case quantum algorithm for constructive Ramsey search, with matching/associated lower bounds for both quantum and randomized regimes, and demonstrates a provable polynomial quantum speedup on random graphs.

Abstract: The constructive diagonal Ramsey problem asks, given adjacency-oracle access to an $N$-vertex graph, for a clique or independent set of the order guaranteed by Ramsey's theorem. We give a bounded-error quantum algorithm that, for every $K\ge2$ and $N\ge4^{K-1}$, finds and verifies a homogeneous $K$-set using $O\!\left(2^K K\log\frac Kη\right)$ edge queries with failure probability at most $η$. At the Ramsey scale $N=2^n$, this yields a homogeneous set of order $\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1$ using $O(\sqrt N\log N\log(\log N/η))$ queries, improving on the $O(N)$ queries of the explicit classical recursion and giving, to our knowledge, the first sublinear worst-case algorithm for the Ramsey relation. We also derive an $Ω(N^{1/12})$ quantum lower bound by a reduction from collision finding.
  The algorithm runs the constructive recursion over implicit candidate sets. Each set is represented by a short conjunction of adjacency constraints and sampled using capped unknown-solution quantum search, and a scale-aware concentration schedule balances estimation accuracy against the increasing cost of sampling deeper sets. We complement the upper bound with an $Ω(N^{1-1/\sqrt2})$ randomized lower bound, transported from the random-Painter analysis of online Ramsey numbers, which holds on the uniform distribution $G(N,1/2)$. On that distribution a greedy quantum search uses only $\widetilde O(N^{1/4})$ queries, giving a provable polynomial quantum speedup for Ramsey search on random graphs. We also give an estimation-free size-biased recursion and extend it to every fixed number of edge colours.

</details>


### [27] [Quantum Query Complexity of Persistence Statistics in Graph Zigzags](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05813)
*Cheng Xin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the query complexity of estimating scalar summaries of zigzag bar lifetimes from snapshot-adjacency bits, providing quantum and classical estimators with matching lower bounds for both fixed power weights and uniform-window mean statistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine the fundamental query complexity (both quantum and classical) needed to estimate scalar summaries of zigzag persistence barcodes from snapshot-adjacency data, without computing the full barcode.

Method: The approach relies on an exact identity: sampling R uniform times and computing the expected generalized rank between their minimum and maximum equals the statistic F_φ, which for intersection graphs becomes the circuit rank expressible as averages of edge and component counts. This avoids barcode computation entirely, enabling quantum estimators with additive error.

Result: A quantum estimator with additive error εn using Õ(√(m(K+n))/ε) queries (when a bound K≥F_φ is supplied), versus Õ(m·min{n²,(K+n)/ε²}) classically, with matching lower bounds in two regimes: worst-case Θ̃(n√m/ε) quantum and Θ(n²m) classical for fixed power weights and uniform-window means; on sparse instances under split-leakage promises, Θ̃(√(mK)/ε) quantum and Θ̃(m·min{n²,K/ε²}) classical.

Conclusion: The nonlinear barcode functional reduces to an average of edge and component counts without requiring spectral gap, homology-state, or QRAM assumptions, yielding optimal quantum and classical estimators whose complexities are characterized by n, m, K, and ε.

Abstract: We study the query complexity of estimating scalar summaries of zigzag bar lifetimes from snapshot-adjacency bits.
  For graphs $G_1,\ldots,G_m$ on $n$ labeled vertices, let $\ell_b$ be the snapshot lifetime of a degree-one bar $b$ of the intersection zigzag. For a probability generating function $φ(x)=\mathbb{E}[x^R]$, the statistic $F_φ=\sum_bφ(\ell_b/m)$ includes normalized degree-$r$ total persistence and the mean generalized rank over a uniform time window. An exact identity underlies our algorithm: sample $R$ uniform times; the expected generalized rank between their minimum and maximum equals $F_φ$. For graphs that rank is the circuit rank of an intersection graph, so a nonlinear barcode functional becomes an average of edge and component counts, and no barcode is computed.
  Without spectral-gap, homology-state, or QRAM assumptions, this gives a quantum estimator with additive error $\varepsilon n$ and $\widetilde O(\sqrt{m(K+n)}/\varepsilon)$ queries when a bound $K\ge F_φ$ is supplied, against $\widetilde O(m\min\{n^2,(K+n)/\varepsilon^2\})$ classically, and an adaptive quantum variant with the same instance dependence. These estimators are optimal in two regimes. For every fixed power weight $x^r$, $r\ge2$, and for the uniform-window mean, the worst-case complexities are $\widetildeΘ(n\sqrt m/\varepsilon)$ quantum and $Θ(n^2m)$ classical. On sparse instances, under an explicit split-leakage promise met by power and binomial weights of logarithmic degree and the promise $F_φ\le K$, they are $\widetildeΘ(\sqrt{mK}/\varepsilon)$ and $\widetildeΘ(m\min\{n^2,K/\varepsilon^2\})$. The classical lower bounds hold against fully adaptive algorithms, and fewer than $m$ such statistics cannot determine the positive-lifetime histogram. All bounds concern snapshot access; with an explicit update stream, near-linear full-barcode algorithms are known.

</details>


### [28] [Towards Continuous Profiling and Optimization of Quantum-Classical Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05814)
*Ayush Bansal,Owen Cochell,Santiago Núñez-Corrales,Marcos Frenkel,Seetharami Seelam,Apoorve Mohan,Tianyin Xu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: LLQM is a profiling-driven meta-framework for multi-stage quantum-classical pipelines that decomposes pipelines into fine-grained tasks and continuously profiles CPU/GPU, memory, QPU, and queue dependencies alongside real-time hardware states, revealing cross-stage resource dependencies and enabling hardware-, fidelity-, and workload-aware optimizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum applications increasingly execute as multi-stage quantum-classical pipelines with diverse resource requirements and stochastic behavior under drifting hardware noises, yet existing workflow frameworks treat these stages as static, isolated components, failing to capture cross-stage resource dependencies and interactions between classical and quantum decisions.

Method: LLQM decomposes pipelines into fine-grained tasks and continuously profiles CPU/GPU, memory, QPU, and queue dependencies along with real-time hardware states, using a unified runtime abstraction to capture cross-stage resource dependencies and characterize their impact on fidelity and resource consumption. It is evaluated using QEM as a representative pipeline stage on IBM 156-qubit Heron r2 processors with circuits up to 100 qubits and 1e7 transpiled gates.

Result: Continuous profiling exposes runtime bottlenecks and enables hardware-, fidelity-, and workload-aware optimizations in quantum-classical pipelines. The evaluation on IBM Heron r2 processors demonstrates the effectiveness of LLQM on circuits up to 100 qubits with millions of transpiled gates.

Conclusion: LLQM demonstrates that a profiling-driven, unified runtime abstraction for quantum-classical pipelines can reveal runtime bottlenecks and enable optimizations that account for hardware state, fidelity, and workload characteristics, addressing limitations of static, isolated workflow frameworks.

Abstract: Quantum applications increasingly execute as multi-stage quantum-classical pipelines, interleaving QPU computation with classical stages like circuit generation, transpilation, layout mapping, quantum error mitigation (QEM), and post-processing. These stages have diverse resource requirements and exhibit stochastic behavior under drifting hardware noises, yet existing workflow frameworks treat them as static, isolated components. We present LLQM (Low-Level Quantum Machine), a profiling-driven meta-framework for quantum-classical pipelines. LLQM decomposes pipelines into fine-grained tasks and continuously profiles their CPU/GPU, memory, QPU, and queue dependencies alongside real-time hardware states. This unified runtime abstraction captures cross-stage resource dependencies and reveals how classical and quantum decisions interact, enabling characterization of their impact on fidelity and resource consumption. We evaluate LLQM using QEM as a representative pipeline stage, on IBM 156-qubit Heron r2 processors with circuits up to 100 qubits and 1e7 transpiled gates. Our results show that continuous profiling exposes runtime bottlenecks and enables hardware-, fidelity-, and workload-aware optimizations.

</details>


### [29] [Topologically Protected Edge States in One-Dimensional Quantum Walks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05823)
*Emily Maxey,Jacob Mansfield,Beth Thacker,Wade DeGottardi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a class of topological quantum walks with variable step lengths that can access higher winding numbers, support multiple edge states, and demonstrate the reduction of topological protection from Z- to Z2-valued invariants when time-reversal symmetry is broken. Edge states are analyzed via a transfer-matrix approach, with results in excellent agreement with numerics and Jackiw-Rebbi zero-mode solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological insulators host robust protected boundary states desirable for applications, motivating their realization in engineered systems. Discrete-time quantum walks can exhibit topologically nontrivial phases, and the authors seek to extend this framework by introducing variable step lengths to gain deeper theoretical insight into topological classification.

Method: The authors introduce a class of topological quantum walks with variable step lengths. They employ a transfer-matrix approach to analyze the spatial profiles and spin structures of topologically protected edge states.

Result: These walks access higher winding numbers and support multiple edge states. The analysis reveals the reduction of topological protection from a Z- to Z2-valued invariant when time-reversal symmetry is broken. The transfer-matrix predictions show excellent agreement with numerical results and with the Jackiw-Rebbi zero-mode solution of the Dirac equation where applicable.

Conclusion: The analysis provides a roadmap for designing quantum walks with control over the number, spatial profile, and spin structure of their topological edge states.

Abstract: Topological insulators host protected boundary states that are robust to disorder, making them attractive for applications and motivating their realization in engineered systems. Discrete-time quantum walks, which have been implemented in a variety of experimental platforms, can exhibit topologically nontrivial phases and their associated boundary states. Here, we introduce a class of topological quantum walks with variable step lengths that offer theoretical insights into the topological classification of such walks. For example, these walks can access higher winding numbers and support multiple edge states. They also provide context for the reduction of topological protection from a $\mathbb{Z}$- to $\mathbb{Z}_2$-valued invariant when time-reversal symmetry is broken. The topologically protected edge states are investigated using a transfer-matrix approach that describes their spatial profiles and spin structures. These predictions are in excellent agreement with numerical results and, where applicable, with the Jackiw--Rebbi zero-mode solution of the Dirac equation. Taken together, our analysis provides a roadmap for designing quantum walks with control over the number, spatial profile, and spin structure of their topological edge states.

</details>


### [30] [On the sample complexity of the generalized hidden shift problem over arbitrary finite groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05828)
*Juntai Zhou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper formalizes the generalized hidden shift problem over arbitrary finite groups and establishes both upper and lower bounds on sample complexity expressed via group-theoretic parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the study of hidden shift problems beyond specific group types and characterize the minimal number of samples/queries needed to solve such problems using group-theoretic structure.

Method: Formulation of the generalized hidden shift problem over arbitrary finite groups, with analytical derivation of sample complexity bounds expressed in terms of group-theoretic data.

Result: Upper and lower bounds for the sample complexity of the generalized hidden shift problem, stated in terms of group-theoretic quantities.

Conclusion: The generalized hidden shift problem over arbitrary finite groups has sample complexity that can be tightly characterized by group-theoretic data, providing insights into the inherent computational/query difficulty across different group structures.

Abstract: We formulate the generalized hidden shift problem over arbitrary finite groups and obtain upper and lower bounds for the sample complexity in terms of group theoretic data.

</details>


### [31] [High-Resolution Dynamical Eigenspectroscopy via Variational Trotter Compression on a Superconducting Qubit Processor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05838)
*Liyang Sui,Xingrui Liu,Yufan Li,Sainan Huai,Zhiwen Zong,Kunliang Bu,Xiaopei Yang,Wenyan Jin,Bowen Chen,Xutao Zhang,Jianlan Wu,Shengyu Zhang,Yi Yin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces and experimentally demonstrates a dynamical eigenspectroscopy protocol for noisy intermediate-scale quantum devices, using Variational Trotter Compression to extend coherent evolution and extract fine-grained eigenenergies of H2 and Fermi-Hubbard models on a 9-qubit superconducting processor with accuracy within 2×10^-3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-resolution eigenspectroscopy on NISQ devices is limited by the trade-off between circuit depth and coherence time. Existing approaches struggle to extract fine-grained energy structures due to finite coherence windows on current superconducting hardware.

Method: The protocol extracts eigengies from time-dependent survival amplitudes, employs Variational Trotter Compression (VTC) to extend high-fidelity unitary evolution beyond native coherence limits, reconstructs time-domain autocorrelation signals via quantum state tomography, and applies multi-frequency fitting and Fourier analysis to resolve eigenenergies.

Result: On a multi-connected 9-qubit superconducting processor, eigenenergies for H2 at different bond lengths and Fermi-Hubbard models across correlation regimes agree with exact diagonalization to within 2×10^-3, including near-degenerate levels in the strongly interacting regime.

Conclusion: The work establishes experimental dynamical spectroscopy as a robust and generalizable framework for quantum chemistry and strongly correlated lattice simulations on near-term quantum hardware, bridging weak- and strong-coupling regimes.

Abstract: The pursuit of high-resolution eigenspectroscopy on noisy intermediate-scale quantum devices is often hindered by the trade-off between circuit depth and coherence time. In this work, we introduce and experimentally demonstrate a dynamical eigenspectroscopy protocol that extracts fine-grained energy structures from time-dependent survival amplitudes. To overcome the finite coherence window of current superconducting processors, we employ Variational Trotter Compression (VTC) as a practical means to extend the duration of high-fidelity unitary evolution. Using a multi-connected 9-qubit superconducting processor, we reconstruct the time-domain autocorrelation signal via quantum state tomography for the H2 molecule at different bond lengths and for the Fermi-Hubbard model across different correlation regimes. Through multi-frequency fitting and Fourier analysis, the extracted eigenenergies agree with the exact-diagonalization values to within 2x10^-3, including the near-degenerate levels in the strongly interacting regime. Our results establish experimental dynamical spectroscopy as a robust and generalizable framework for simulating both quantum chemistry and strongly correlated lattice systems, bridging weak- and strong-coupling regimes on near-term quantum hardware.

</details>


### [32] [Do Quantum AIs Dream in Paths? Path-Integral Slow Thinking through Grover Interference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05842)
*Xiansheng Cai,Xiu-Hao Deng,Kun Chen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a quantum AI alternative to slow thinking in LLMs, formulating reasoning as a discrete path integral over trajectories that coexist in superposition and recombine before measurement. A verifiable reward partitions ensembles into accepted/rejected components that interfere under Grover amplitude amplification, with an analytically determined interior (sub-unit) success target that prevents policy collapse. On a 2x3 sliding puzzle benchmark, Grover-based training outperforms classical controls on training accuracy and preserves far more generalization across held-out questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL with verifiable rewards enables LLMs to "think slowly" but induces policy collapse—probability concentrates onto few successful trajectories and exploratory diversity erodes. The authors ask whether quantum AI can achieve slow thinking without this collapse, motivating a framework where reasoning trajectories can be processed coherently rather than sampled one at a time.

Method: Slow thinking is formulated as coherent quantum dynamics over reasoning trajectories via a discrete path integral, where action sequences coexist in superposition. An exact verifier partitions the ensemble into collective accepted/rejected components that interfere under Grover amplitude amplification. Training maximizes finite Grover evolution at an analytically determined pre-amplification success probability below one, making inference itself define an interior training target and removing monotonic pressure toward unit success. Simulations use exact statevector methods on a 2x3 sliding puzzle.

Result: On a 32-question 2x3 sliding puzzle training set, Grover training reaches 0.95 accuracy in one round vs 0.73 for the strongest classical control. Quantum training preserves far more held-out accuracy than classical training—at four rounds with matched circuit applications, quantum models reach 3.2x and 3.9x the strongest classical controls. The number of training questions supported by fixed-size policies grows faster with amplification budget than with matched classical repetition.

Conclusion: The results establish a Grover-based realization of path-integral slow thinking where the interior target preserves exploratory path diversity, and ensemble-level interference converts this diversity into verified performance—demonstrating a distinct quantum advantage in avoiding the policy collapse suffered by classical verifiable-reward training.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards enables large language models to think slowly, but the same training can induce policy collapse: probability concentrates onto a few successful trajectories and exploratory diversity erodes. We ask whether quantum AI can realize slow thinking differently. We formulate slow thinking as coherent dynamics over reasoning trajectories, a discrete path integral in which action sequences coexist in superposition and recombine before measurement. In our trainable realization, an exact verifier partitions the ensemble into collective accepted and rejected components that interfere under Grover amplitude amplification. A finite Grover evolution is maximized when the pre-amplification success probability lies at an analytically determined value below one, so inference itself defines an interior training target and removes the monotonic pressure toward unit success. In exact statevector simulations of a 2x3 sliding puzzle, Grover training reaches accuracy 0.95 on a 32-question training set at one round, against 0.73 for the strongest classical control. On held-out questions specialization has a cost: an untrained uniform policy read out through the same amplification remains the strongest reference on this solution-dense benchmark, and quantum training preserves far more held-out accuracy than classical training - at four rounds with matched circuit applications the two quantum models reach 3.2 and 3.9 times the strongest classical controls. The number of training questions supported by fixed-size policies trained at each amplification budget also grows faster with the budget than with matched classical repetition. These results establish a Grover-based realization of path-integral slow thinking: the interior target preserves exploratory path diversity, and ensemble-level interference converts it into verified performance.

</details>


### [33] [Quantum Message Passing Convergence and Vanishing Block-Error Probability for Random LDPC Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05857)
*Avijit Mandal,Christophe Piveteau,Joseph M. Renes,Henry D. Pfister*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper constructs a two-stage BPQM (belief propagation with quantum messages) decoder for random q-ary LDPC codes over symmetric q-ary pure-state channels and proves that its ensemble-average block-error probability vanishes as blocklength tends to infinity, extending BPQM analysis from tree factor graphs to LDPC Tanner graphs with cycles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BPQM achieves optimal decoding on tree factor graphs for pure-state classical-quantum channels, but this tree-based analysis does not guarantee vanishing block-error probability for LDPC codes with cycles (i.e., real LDPC Tanner graphs). The paper seeks to close this gap by proving BPQM works for LDPC codes over cyclic graphs.

Method: The authors construct a two-stage BPQM decoder. In the first stage, depth-ℓ BPQM is applied only to coordinates with tree neighbourhoods (ensuring the BPQM computations remain on a tree), while remaining coordinates are treated as erasures. Choosing ℓ = Θ(log log N), a noncommutative union bound controls BPQM decoding errors, and the minimum-distance property of the code guarantees erasure recovery. The analysis is performed for regular ensembles with d_v ≥ 3 (yielding double-exponential decay of average symbol-error probability via fidelity bounds) and extended to finite-support irregular ensembles.

Result: The ensemble-average block-error probability of the two-stage BPQM decoder vanishes as blocklength N → ∞ for random q-ary LDPC codes over symmetric q-ary pure-state channels (q prime). For regular ensembles with d_v ≥ 3, fidelity bounds give double-exponential decay of the average symbol-error probability throughout the BPQM success region.

Conclusion: The results justify the use of BPQM for coherent decoding in quantum algorithms based on Regev's reduction and in decoded quantum interferometry (DQI), whenever the code is from the analyzed random LDPC ensembles and the induced memoryless symmetric pure-state channel lies in the BPQM success region.

Abstract: Belief propagation with quantum messages (BPQM) is a quantum algorithm that decodes classical codes transmitted over classical--quantum channels. It realizes optimal decoding on tree factor graphs over pure-state classical-quantum channels. However, this tree-based analysis does not ensure vanishing block-error probability for LDPC Tanner graphs with cycles. In this work, we construct a two-stage BPQM decoder for random $q$-ary LDPC codes over symmetric $q$-ary pure-state channels, where $q$ is prime, and prove that its ensemble-average block-error probability vanishes as the blocklength $N$ tends to infinity. For regular ensembles with $d_v\geq3$, fidelity bounds yield double-exponential decay of the average symbol-error probability throughout the BPQM success region. We apply depth-$\ell$ BPQM to coordinates with tree neighbourhoods and treat the remaining coordinates as erasures. With a suitable $\ell=Θ(\log\log N)$, a noncommutative union bound controls the BPQM decoding errors, while the minimum-distance property guarantees erasure recovery. We also extend the analysis to finite-support irregular ensembles. These results are relevant to quantum algorithms based on Regev's reduction, where coherent decoding uncomputes a codeword register. Decoded quantum interferometry (DQI) uses a closely related Fourier-based framework that reduces sparse max-LINSAT optimization problems to LDPC decoding problems on pure-state channels. Our results justify the use of BPQM in the decoding step of DQI and of coding-theoretic algorithms based on Regev's reduction whenever the code is drawn from one of the random LDPC ensembles analyzed here and the induced memoryless symmetric pure-state channel lies in the BPQM success region.

</details>


### [34] [Demailly's Conjecture in Quantum Language and Its Solution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05866)
*Trung Hoa Dinh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes an exact dictionary between fat-point interpolation in complex projective space and a dark-state problem for fixed-particle-number bosons, then uses Frobenius amplification to prove Demailly's conjectured inequality on Waldschmidt constants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge algebraic geometry (fat-point interpolation and symbolic powers) with quantum physics (dark states of fixed-particle-number bosons), and to resolve an open conjecture (Demailly's inequality) by exploiting the structure afforded by this dictionary.

Method: The authors construct an exact translation: under the Fischer–Bargmann realization, homogeneous degree maps to particle number, the Veronese lift to a bosonic symmetric product state, and order-m vanishing to orthogonality against coherent jet excitations below order m. Symbolic power initial degrees correspond to particle numbers admitting dark states of positive constraint Hamiltonians, and Waldschmidt constants to asymptotic particle cost per suppression order. They then prove a finite-scale no-dark-state amplification theorem via Frobenius in positive characteristic, where a full-rank certificate in one good fiber is lifted by a nonzero maximal minor back to characteristic zero, yielding the asymptotic Demailly inequality.

Result: A rigorous, exact dictionary between fat-point interpolation and bosonic dark-state problems, a finite-scale no-dark-state amplification theorem, and a proof of Demailly's conjectured inequality for Waldschmidt constants as an asymptotic consequence.

Conclusion: The paper provides a self-contained, modular account (projective, quantum, and arithmetic parts independently readable) that both reformulates a classical interpolation problem in quantum terms and resolves an open conjecture, demonstrating the fruitfulness of this cross-disciplinary dictionary.

Abstract: We give an exact translation between fat-point interpolation in complex projective space and a dark-state problem for fixed-particle-number bosons. Under the unitary Fischer--Bargmann realization, homogeneous degree becomes particle number, the Veronese lift becomes a bosonic symmetric product state, and order-$m$ vanishing becomes orthogonality to all coherent jet excitations of orders below $m$. The initial degree of a symbolic power is therefore the first particle number admitting a dark state of a positive constraint Hamiltonian, and the Waldschmidt constant is the asymptotic particle cost per suppression order. After developing both sides of this dictionary from first principles, we prove a finite-scale no-dark-state amplification theorem. Frobenius amplifies the associated full-rank certificate in one good positive-characteristic fiber; a nonzero maximal minor returns it to characteristic zero. Its asymptotic consequence is Demailly's conjectured inequality. The paper is written so that the projective statement, the quantum reformulation, and the arithmetic part of the solution can be read independently and then identified exactly.

</details>


### [35] [Reducing quantum and classical resources for quantum-centric supercomputing workloads on near-term hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05957)
*Maxence Grandadam,Vladyslav Bohun,Dikshant Dulal,Maciej Koch-Janusz*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes sample-based Krylov quantum diagonalization (SKQD) under depolarizing noise, deriving shot-count resource estimates that reveal an exponential depth penalty. Experiments on a 20-site (40-qubit) Anderson model show that device noise breaks noiseless convergence predictions, but approximate circuit compilation compresses Krylov time-evolution circuits, recovering energy convergence while reducing both quantum shot budget and classical subspace dimension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SKQD is a paradigmatic quantum-centric supercomputing workflow combining Krylov quantum diagonalization with classical sampling-based post-processing. While it provides convergence guarantees under the assumption that important computational-basis configurations can be sampled with sufficient probability, this assumption has not been rigorously analyzed under realistic noise conditions. The authors aim to understand whether and how device noise undermines these guarantees on actual quantum hardware.

Method: The authors derive analytical shot-count resource estimates for SKQD under depolarizing noise, exposing an exponential depth penalty in the sampling assumption. They perform experiments on current noisy quantum hardware using a one-dimensional single-impurity Anderson model on a 20-site (40-qubit) instance, and apply approximate circuit compilation techniques to compress Krylov time-evolution circuits before execution.

Result: Device noise breaks the practical convergence predicted by noiseless SKQD analysis. Compressed circuits accumulate less hardware noise, recover the expected energy convergence, and reduce both the quantum shot budget and the classical subspace dimension. The benefits persist even when classical configuration recovery is applied.

Conclusion: Noise poses a fundamental exponential-depth challenge to SKQD sampling assumptions; however, circuit compression provides a practical mitigation strategy that restores convergence and improves resource efficiency on near-term hardware, making SKQD viable as a quantum-centric supercomputing approach.

Abstract: Sample-based Krylov quantum diagonalization (SKQD) is a paradigmatic example of a quantum-centric supercomputing workflow that combines the convergence structure of Krylov quantum diagonalization with classical sampling-based post-processing. It provides convergence guarantees assuming that the important computational-basis configurations can be sampled from a set of Krylov states with sufficient probability. We analyze this assumption under depolarizing noise, deriving shot-count resource estimates that expose an exponential depth penalty, and perform experiments on current noisy hardware with the one-dimensional single-impurity Anderson model on a 20-site (40-qubit) instance. Device noise breaks the practical convergence predicted by the noiseless SKQD analysis, but approximate compilation techniques can compress the Krylov time-evolution circuits before execution. The compressed circuits accumulate less hardware noise, recover the expected energy convergence, and reduce both the quantum shot budget and the classical subspace dimension, remaining beneficial even when classical configuration recovery is applied.

</details>

Abstract: The quest for autonomous alignment and maintenance of complex free-space optical systems is increasingly urgent for large-scale neutral-atom quantum processors. </details>


### [37] [The $I_{3322}$ Bell inequality requires infinite dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06038)
*Andrea Coladangelo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves a long-standing conjecture by Pál and Vértesi that the I_3322 Bell inequality, despite being one of the simplest bipartite Bell inequalities, has no finite-dimensional quantum strategy achieving its maximal violation, though an infinite-dimensional strategy exists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The I_3322 inequality is the simplest Bell scenario in which a quantum strategy could potentially require infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces to achieve maximal violation—a phenomenon distinct from what can occur in simpler Bell scenarios. While a similar property was proven for a more complex Bell correlation by Coladangelo and Stark, proving it for I_3322 has remained an open challenge since Pál and Vértesi conjectured it in 2010.

Method: The proof leverages an approximate proof framework initially developed through iterative interaction with GPT 5.5 Pro, which the author subsequently revised substantially for completeness, correctness, and clarity.

Result: The paper provides a rigorous proof confirming that I_3322 cannot be maximally violated by any finite-dimensional quantum strategy, while an infinite-dimensional strategy can attain the maximal violation, thus resolving the conjecture.

Conclusion: The conjecture regarding I_3322 is proven true, establishing the simplest known Bell inequality exhibiting the phenomenon where maximal quantum violation requires infinite-dimensional resources.

Abstract: The $I_{3322}$ inequality is one of the simplest bipartite Bell inequalities, with three possible questions and two possible answers per party. Yet, despite its simplicity, it has long been conjectured by Pál and Vértesi (Physical Review A, 2010) to possess the following quirk: no finite-dimensional quantum strategy can attain its maximal violation, but an infinite-dimensional strategy can. A Bell correlation, with five questions and three answers per party, that provably possesses the same property has since been discovered by Coladangelo and Stark (Nature Communications, 2020). However, proving that the same property holds for $I_{3322}$, which lives in the simplest Bell scenario in which such a phenomenon could occur, has remained elusive. Here, we provide a proof of this conjecture.
  An approximate proof, containing all the main ideas, was produced by GPT 5.5 Pro after various rounds of interaction. The presentation of this proof was revised substantially by the author for completeness, correctness, and clarity.

</details>


### [38] [Propagating sparsely supported states with time-dependent neural quantum states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06054)
*Lexin Ding,Markus Reiher*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces interpolation sampling, a novel importance-sampling strategy for time-dependent neural quantum states (NQS) that samples from a distribution interpolating between the wave function and its time derivative, enabling accurate propagation of sparsely supported initial states where standard wave function sampling fails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing time-dependent NQS methods cannot propagate sparsely supported initial states effectively due to the inherent mismatch between the supports of such states and their time derivatives. This limits the applicability of NQS methods to a wider range of physically relevant scenarios.

Method: The authors propose interpolation sampling, a new form of importance sampling where samples come from a distribution interpolating between the wave function and its time derivative. This is combined with a global-in-time variational principle. They also introduce two additional enhancements: (i) a configuration-time joint sampling scheme treating both spin configurations and time as random variables, and (ii) a hybrid sample proposal strategy incorporating Krylov subspace knowledge.

Result: Using the two-dimensional transverse-field Ising model as a test case, the authors demonstrate that interpolation sampling enables accurate propagation of extremely peaked states that standard wave function sampling could not handle.

Conclusion: This work extends time-dependent NQS methods to broader physical scenarios and challenges the prevailing default reliance on the Born distribution and its close variants as the standard sampling basis.

Abstract: Neural quantum states (NQSs) have emerged as a powerful ansatz for quantum dynamics. Their high entanglement capacity promises to overcome the entanglement barrier. However, existing time-dependent NQS methods are ill-equipped to propagate sparsely supported initial states due to the inherent mismatch between the supports of such states and their time derivatives. To address this issue, we introduce the interpolation sampling method as a novel form of importance sampling, where samples are drawn from a distribution that interpolates between the wave function and its time derivative. Combined with a global-in-time variational principle, we demonstrate with the example of the two-dimensional transverse-field Ising model that interpolation sampling allows for accurate propagation of extremely peaked states that standard wave function sampling could not tackle. We further improve sampling efficiency by introducing (i) a configuration-time joint sampling scheme where spin configurations and time are both treated as random variables, and (ii) a hybrid strategy for sample proposal that incorporates the knowledge of a Krylov subspace. Our work extends the scope of time-dependent NQS methods to a wider range of physically relevant scenarios, while challenging the prevailing reliance on the Born distribution and its close variants as the default basis for sampling.

</details>


### [39] [Battery Locality Is Necessary in Noncommuting Quantum Charging Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06104)
*Sheryl Mathew*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes that battery locality is an operationally necessary resource for quantum batteries whose internal interactions are noncommuting. By comparing commuting and noncommuting high-locality batteries with identical interaction supports and energy scales driven by the same charger, the authors show that noncommuting batteries can parametrically exceed the battery-locality-independent charging power bound, whereas commuting ones saturate it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on direct charging protocols interpreted power bounds as charging-side (Hamiltonian locality) constraints. Whether the locality of the battery itself is operationally necessary for achieving these power limits remained unresolved, and the authors aim to settle this question.

Method: The authors construct commuting and noncommuting high-locality spin battery models with identical interaction supports and local energy scales, driven by the same charging Hamiltonian. They compare the charging power against the battery-locality-independent bound, and analyze the dynamical attainment of enhanced commutator norms from a joint state supported entirely in the battery ground-energy sector (which may be correlated with auxiliary fermionic degrees of freedom).

Result: Noncommuting batteries exceed the battery-locality-independent charging power bound by a parametrically growing factor, while commuting batteries saturate it. The enhanced commutator norm is dynamically achieved from a suitable initial state in the battery ground-energy sector.

Conclusion: Battery locality itself is an operational resource for charging power, enabled specifically by noncommuting battery interactions. This invalidates the general applicability of the battery-locality-independent power bound to noncommuting systems.

Abstract: Bounds on the charging power of quantum batteries in direct charging protocols are often interpreted as charging-side constraints. Although the locality of the charging Hamiltonian is known to restrict attainable power in certain protocols, whether battery locality is itself operationally necessary has remained unresolved. Here we show that it is necessary for spin batteries whose interactions do not pairwise commute. We construct commuting and noncommuting high-locality batteries with identical interaction supports and local energy scale, driven by the same charging. The commuting battery saturates the battery-locality-independent bound, whereas the noncommuting battery exceeds it by a parametrically growing factor, demonstrating that this bound cannot apply generally. The enhanced norm of the commutator is dynamically attained from a suitable joint state supported entirely in the battery ground-energy sector, although this state may be correlated with the auxiliary fermionic degrees of freedom. Our results establish battery locality as an operational resource enabled by noncommuting battery interactions.

</details>


### [40] [Measurement-Based Feedback of Open Quantum Systems: A Control-Theoretic Review and Tutorial](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06111)
*Weichao Liang,Jing Zhang,Gaoyue Guo,Daoyi Dong*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A control-theoretic review of measurement-based feedback for continuously monitored open quantum systems, centered on a recurrence–contraction framework where Hamiltonian feedback removes non-target invariant obstructions and measurement-induced dynamics enable local exponential contraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge measurement-based quantum feedback and nonlinear stochastic control by organizing developments around invariance, estimation, recurrence, and contraction, and to address implementation challenges and open problems in scalable estimation, sampling, delay, adaptation, and hybrid/non-Markovian dynamics.

Method: Develops a control-theoretic framework for finite-dimensional systems governed by diffusive stochastic master equations, introducing state-space, invariant-subspace, and quantum non-demolition structures; interprets quantum filtering as nonlinear observer dynamics; and reviews open-loop asymptotics, filter stability, state-feedback stabilization, robustness, and reduced-order observer-based control within a recurrence–contraction paradigm.

Result: Provides a unifying tutorial framework showing that Hamiltonian feedback removes non-target invariant obstructions while measurement-induced dynamics yield local exponential contraction, with the measurement–estimation–feedback architecture applicable across broad quantum platforms.

Conclusion: The review synthesizes measurement-based quantum feedback with nonlinear stochastic control theory, offering a structured perspective on stability, estimation, and robustness while outlining open problems including scalable estimation, time delays, adaptation, practical stability, optimal control, and learning-based design.

Abstract: This review develops a control-theoretic perspective on measurement-based feedback for continuously monitored open quantum systems, with the main analysis focused on finite-dimensional systems governed by diffusive stochastic master equations. We introduce the relevant state-space, invariant-subspace, and quantum non-demolition structures, interpret quantum filtering as nonlinear observer dynamics, and review open-loop asymptotics, filter stability, state-feedback stabilization, robustness, and reduced-order observer-based control. Particular emphasis is placed on a recurrence--contraction framework, in which Hamiltonian feedback removes non-target invariant obstructions while measurement-induced dynamics provide local exponential contraction. Although the detailed analysis is developed for finite-dimensional diffusive models, the underlying measurement--estimation--feedback architecture is relevant across a broad range of quantum platforms. We further discuss implementation challenges and open problems involving scalable estimation, sampling and delay, adaptation, hybrid and non-Markovian dynamics, practical stability, optimal control, and learning-based design. By organizing these developments around invariance, estimation, recurrence, and contraction, the review provides a tutorial bridge between measurement-based quantum feedback and nonlinear stochastic control.

</details>


### [41] [The Future-Record Model of Everettian Probability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06130)
*Jonathan Baxter*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The author extends a previously developed model of objective Everettian probability defined over observer continuations to encompass all decoherent physical record states, including those that do not support observer continuations, making the observer-indexed model a restricted case of an exhaustive physical-record model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The prior model defined objective Everettian probability only over records borne by an observer's later continuations. This is restrictive since not all physical records necessarily support an observer continuation. The author aims to generalize the framework to cover all decoherent physical record states.

Method: Extends the previously established observer-indexed Everettian probability model to a more general exhaustive physical-record model. The observer-indexed case is shown to be a restriction of this broader framework.

Result: The model is successfully extended to all decoherent physical record states, including those lacking observer continuations, with the observer-indexed model subsumed as a special case.

Conclusion: Objective Everettian probability can be defined exhaustively over physical records rather than only over observer continuations, generalizing and strengthening the prior framework.

Abstract: I recently showed how objective Everettian probability can be defined over the records borne by an observer's later continuations, without unique actualization or additional premeasurement subjects \citep{Baxter2026}. Here I extend that model to all decoherent physical record states, including records that support no such continuation. The observer-indexed model is then a restriction of an exhaustive physical-record model.

</details>


### [42] [Quantifying Nonstabilizerness of Quantum Codes by Removing the Inert Background](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06177)
*Yuan Liu,Ke-Mi Xu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a structural observation that decomposes quantum states into a magic-free Clifford sector (removable via Clifford transformation) and an order-three object whose nonstabilizerness (magic) is exactly computable as a fourth moment. This leads to closed-form formulas for magic in several code families: all Dicke states (reducing exponential sums to short binomial expressions), cubic-phase codes (with a bound saturated only by the D4 code), and group multiplication states (with a cyclic/zero criterion).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantifying nonstabilizerness (magic) is essential for understanding quantum computational advantage, fault-tolerant codes, and topological order, but the standard measure requires summing over exponentially many Pauli operators. For quantum codes in particular, no quantitative theory of magic has existed. The authors aim to make magic computation tractable for broad classes of quantum codes.

Method: The key structural insight is that the Clifford sector of a state carries no magic and can be eliminated via a Clifford transformation, leaving an order-three (trine-like) object whose magic reduces to an exactly evaluable fourth moment. Removing this inert background enables closed-form evaluation of nonstabilizerness for structured code families, converting exponential Pauli sums into finite classical counting problems.

Result: The authors obtain: (1) a closed-form magic formula for all Dicke states—e.g., a 100-qubit case drops from 4^100 terms to a short binomial sum; (2) a magic bound for cubic-phase codes (including twisted quantum doubles and non-Abelian topological order) that is saturated only by the D4 code; and (3) a cyclic/zero criterion for magic of group multiplication states.

Conclusion: The framework reduces nonstabilizerness computation for a broad class of quantum codes to finite classical counting problems and identifies which codes among them are maximally magical, providing the first quantitative theory of magic for quantum codes.

Abstract: Nonstabilizerness (magic) is the quantum resource that, together with stabilizer operations, makes universal quantum computation possible. It appears in fault-tolerant codes and topological order. However, quantifying nonstabilizerness is difficult. The standard measure sums over exponentially many Pauli operators, and for quantum codes no quantitative theory has been available. We resolve this by a structural observation: the Clifford sector carries no magic and can be removed by a Clifford transformation, leaving an order-three object whose magic is an exactly evaluable fourth moment. Removing this inert background yields closed forms for several code families: a formula for all Dicke states, reducing a 100-qubit case from $4^{100}$ terms to a short binomial sum; a bound for cubic-phase codes (twisted quantum doubles and non-Abelian topological order), saturated only by the $D_4$ code; and a cyclic/zero criterion for group multiplication states. Together these results reduce the computation of nonstabilizerness for a broad class of codes to finite classical counting problems, and identify the maximally magical codes among them.

</details>


### [43] [Quantum lattice Boltzmann method via density-matrix encoding for fluid simulation with wall boundary conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06179)
*Hao Su,Boyuan Wang,Yue Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a quantum lattice Boltzmann method (QLBM) that incorporates realistic boundary conditions—solid walls with arbitrary geometry, inlet, and outlet velocity conditions—using density-matrix encoding, Kraus operators for non-unitary operations, a quantum half-way bounce-back scheme, and controlled SWAP operations for inlet/outlet. The method is validated on 2D flows past a backward-facing step, cylinder, and complex obstacle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While QLBM shows promise for efficient fluid simulations, treatment of realistic boundary conditions—especially solid wall boundaries with arbitrary geometry—remains a critical unsolved challenge.

Method: The approach builds on existing QLBM with ensemble transformations, adopting density-matrix encoding for greater circuit flexibility and enabling non-unitary operations via Kraus operators. Boundary enforcement uses a quantum implementation of the half-way bounce-back scheme. A component exchange step before streaming enforces no-slip conditions without modifying transport operations. Inlet/outlet conditions are imposed through controlled SWAP operations combined with ancilla registers prepared in prescribed velocity states.

Result: The QLBM is quantitatively validated through simulations of 2D flows past a backward-facing step, a cylinder, and a complex-geometry obstacle, demonstrating both accuracy and extensibility of the boundary algorithm.

Conclusion: The proposed boundary-aware QLBM achieves accurate and extensible handling of arbitrary solid wall boundaries alongside inlet/outlet conditions, addressing a key open challenge in quantum fluid simulations.

Abstract: The quantum lattice Boltzmann method (QLBM) holds promise for efficient fluid simulations, yet the treatment of realistic boundary conditions, particularly solid wall boundary with arbitrary geometry, remains a critical open challenge. We propose a QLBM algorithm that incorporates wall boundaries as well as inlet/outlet velocity conditions. Building upon the existing QLBM with ensemble transformations, we adopt a density-matrix encoding that offers greater flexibility in circuit design and enables the implementation of non-unitary operations through Kraus operators. For boundary enforcement, we design a quantum implementation of the half-way bounce-back scheme and introduce a component exchange step prior to the streaming step, which naturally enforces the no-slip condition without modifying the transport operation. Inlet and outlet conditions are imposed via controlled SWAP operations combined with ancilla registers prepared in the prescribed velocity states. Our QLBM is quantitatively validated through simulations of two-dimensional flows past a backward-facing step, a cylinder, and an obstacle with complex geometry, demonstrating both accuracy and extensibility of our boundary algorithm.

</details>


### [44] [Wigner Time: a data-oriented approach to experimental timeline creation for quantum science and technology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06230)
*T. W. Clark,B. Sárközi,A. Dombi,Á. Kurkó,D. Nagy,A. Simon,D. Varga,P. Domokos,A. Vukics*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Wigner Time, a Python package that represents experimental procedures in atomic, molecular, and optical physics as data (a table of timed updates) rather than as tightly coupled programs over a global clock. It separates experiment description from hardware, improving temporal resolution five-fold in two cold-atom setups over two years.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional real-time control systems in AMO physics couple experimental stages to absolute times, making procedures tightly coupled, hard to reuse, inspect, or port to other hardware. The paper aims to address this rigidity by decoupling experiment description from timing/hardware specifics.

Method: Wigner Time represents the experimental procedure as data—a table of timed updates assembled by composing functions, referencing named points in the experiment rather than absolute instants. Hardware-specific details are deferred to a final conversion step, keeping the description back-end agnostic.

Result: Wigner Time successfully ran two cold-atom experimental setups for over two years. Replacing a hand-written real-time program improved temporal resolution five-fold.

Conclusion: The data-centric, hardware-agnostic design of Wigner Time is broadly applicable to any domain requiring precise multi-device timing, offering improved flexibility, readability, and reusability over traditional global-clock-based real-time control.

Abstract: Precisely timed, multi-device control in atomic, molecular and optical physics is provided by specialized real-time systems, which impose their own terms on the description of the experiment: a program over a global clock, in which every stage boundary is an absolute time computed from everything preceding it. Such descriptions are tightly coupled -- changing one stage affects all later ones -- and are correspondingly hard to reuse, inspect, or move elsewhere. We introduce Wigner Time, a Python package in which the experimental procedure is instead represented as data: a table of timed updates, assembled by composing functions and referred to named points in the experiment rather than to absolute instants. Hardware enters only at a final conversion step, leaving the description readable and back-end agnostic. Wigner Time has run two cold-atom setups for more than two years, where replacing a hand-written real-time program improved the achievable temporal resolution five-fold; the design applies to any domain requiring precise multi-device timing.

</details>


### [45] [Algorithmic Randomness and Physical Typicality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06268)
*Jeffrey A. Barrett,Eddy Keming Chen,Josiah Lopez-Wild*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces Algorithmic Bohmian mechanics (aBM), which uses Martin-Löf randomness from algorithmic information theory to give precise content to the notion of typicality in physics, specifically reformulating the distribution postulate in Bohmian mechanics as a statistical constraining law.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The concept of typicality in physics is often vaguely defined relative to a probability measure, making it unclear what laws that appeal to typicality actually assert. The authors aim to provide a rigorous characterization of physical typicality using tools from algorithmic randomness theory.

Method: The paper characterizes physical states as typical relative to a computable measure when they are Martin-Löf random. This definition is applied to formulate the distribution postulate in Bohmian mechanics as a statistical constraining law, and a toy model is used to demonstrate that this constraint yields the standard Born statistics for computable experimental protocols.

Result: The proposed framework—Algorithmic Bohmian mechanics (aBM)—shows that Martin-Löf randomness can serve as a precise notion of typicality, and that this guarantees the standard Born statistics for computable experimental protocols in the toy model considered.

Conclusion: Algorithmic randomness provides a rigorous way to give precise content to statistical laws in physics, as illustrated by the aBM formulation of the distribution postulate in Bohmian mechanics.

Abstract: Appeals to typicality are common in physics, but it is often unclear what it means for a physical state to be typical relative to a probability measure, and correspondingly unclear what a law that appeals to typicality asserts. Here we consider how one might characterize physical typicality using ideas from the theory of algorithmic randomness. As a concrete example, we show how taking a physical state to be typical relative to a computable measure when it is Martin-Löf random allows one to formulate the distribution postulate in Bohmian mechanics as a statistical constraining law of the theory. Using a toy model, we show how this constraint guarantees the standard Born statistics for computable experimental protocols. Algorithmic Bohmian mechanics (aBM) thus illustrates how algorithmic randomness may be used to provide precise content to a statistical law.

</details>


### [46] [Efficient Quantum Circuits for Electronic Hamiltonian Simulation without Pauli Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06285)
*Tamiya Onodera,Takeshi Sato*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a Ladder-string-pair (Lasp) diagonalization approach for simulating the electronic Hamiltonian in second quantization, avoiding Pauli expansion entirely. It constructs time-evolution circuits directly from fermionic Lasp operators, reducing CX-gate counts from O(n²) to O(n) for n spin orbitals through systematic optimization across triads of operators, while avoiding Trotter error at intermediate stages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional electronic Hamiltonian simulation maps fermionic operators to qubit operators and expands products into Pauli strings, which obscures higher-level fermionic structure and hides opportunities for circuit optimization.

Method: The approach builds on ladder-string-pair (Lasp) diagonalization, originally developed for Hamiltonian simulation of PDEs. Time-evolution circuits are constructed directly for the second-quantized electronic Hamiltonian without Pauli expansion. Optimization is applied at increasing scope: (1) single two-body fermionic Lasp operator (equivalent to 16 Pauli strings), (2) triads of Lasp operators sharing the same four spin-orbital indices enabling CX cancellation (36→12 CX gates), and (3) sequences of O(n) triads enabling cascade CX reductions across triad boundaries (O(n²)→O(n) gates). The method also handles controlled time evolution and real-valued Hamiltonians.

Result: For a single two-body fermionic Lasp operator, a time-evolution circuit is constructed without Trotter error at that stage. For a triad of three Lasp operators, CX-gate count reduces from 36 to 12 with no Trotter error. For n spin orbitals, cascading across a sequence of O(n) triads reduces total CX-gate count from O(n²) to O(n). Additional optimizations are achieved for real-valued Hamiltonians and controlled time evolution.

Conclusion: Preserving high-level fermionic structure through Lasp diagonalization rather than Pauli expansion enables systematic and substantial circuit optimizations, providing a route toward more efficient electronic Hamiltonian simulation.

Abstract: Electronic Hamiltonian simulation is commonly formulated by mapping fermionic operators to qubit operators and subsequently expanding the resulting ladder-operator products into Pauli strings. While general, this procedure obscures the higher-level fermionic structure and can hide opportunities for circuit optimization. Building on ladder-string-pair (Lasp) diagonalization originally developed for Hamiltonian simulation of partial differential equations, we construct time-evolution circuits for the second-quantized electronic Hamiltonian without performing a Pauli expansion. For the most general case of complex-valued coefficients, we present a time-evolution circuit for a two-body fermionic Lasp operator that corresponds to 16 Pauli strings in the Pauli-expansion approach but does not suffer from Trotter error at this stage. Expanding the optimization scope from a single operator to a triad of three fermionic Lasp operators sharing the same four spin-orbital indices enables systematic cancellation of CX gates, reducing the CX-gate count from 36 to 12 in the example considered, still without introducing Trotter error at this stage. For $n$ spin orbitals, further expanding the optimization scope to a sequence of $O(n)$ suitably ordered triads enables a cascade of CX-gate reductions across triad boundaries, reducing the CX-gate count from $O(n^2)$ to $O(n)$. The Lasp-based approach also naturally accommodates controlled time evolution and yields further optimizations for real-valued Hamiltonians. These results demonstrate that the Lasp-based approach enables more efficient time-evolution circuits by preserving high-level circuit structures and thereby expanding the scope of optimization, providing a systematic route toward more efficient electronic Hamiltonian simulation.

</details>


### [47] [Quantum Sensing of Non-Repeatable Events Enhanced by In-Sensor Quantum Reservoir Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06292)
*Daiki Sasaki,Hideaki Kawaguchi,Yuichiro Matsuzaki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The authors propose using nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamond as an in-sensor quantum reservoir computer (NV-QRC) to sense non-repeatable events. Unlike dynamical decoupling (DD) magnetometry which requires repeated measurements under different pulse settings, NV-QRC uses field-driven many-body dynamics and simultaneous fluorescence readout from multiple spatial regions to classify single events. Benchmarking against fixed-DD magnetometry shows NV-QRC retains class-dependent information where the fixed protocol fails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard quantum sensing protocols like DD magnetometry rely on reproducible target fields, requiring repeated measurements under varying sensing conditions to estimate unknown field parameters. This fails for non-repeatable events where the same waveform cannot be reproduced. There is a need for sensing protocols that can extract information from a single event occurrence.

Method: NV-based in-sensor quantum reservoir computing (NV-QRC). Field-driven many-body dynamics among NV ensembles provide a high-dimensional feature space, and simultaneous fluorescence readout from multiple spatial regions supplies multiple features from a single event. For binary phase classification, the scheme uses a single fixed pulse sequence, in contrast to DD magnetometry which sweeps pulse intervals across repeated measurements.

Result: In binary phase classification benchmarks, NV-QRC demonstrates the ability to retain class-dependent information in parameter regimes where fixed-DD magnetometry fails to capture it, indicating superior performance for sensing non-repeatable events.

Conclusion: The results identify non-repeatable-event sensing as a promising application of quantum reservoir computing, extending quantum sensing capabilities beyond protocols that require reproducible target waveforms.

Abstract: High-density nitrogen-vacancy (NV) ensembles in diamond enable sensitive magnetometry. Many quantum-sensing protocols rely on reproducible target fields, allowing repeated measurements under different sensing conditions. In dynamical decoupling (DD) magnetometry, sweeping the interval between $π$ pulses across repeated measurements enables estimation of the frequency and amplitude of an unknown alternating field. For non-repeatable events, however, the same field waveform cannot be reproduced for measurements under different settings. Here we propose NV-based in-sensor quantum reservoir computing (NV-QRC) for sensing such events. Field-driven many-body dynamics and simultaneous fluorescence readout from multiple spatial regions provide a classical classifier with multiple features from a single event. For binary phase classification, we benchmark NV-QRC against DD magnetometry using a single pulse sequence fixed in advance. We show that NV-QRC can retain class-dependent information in regimes where the fixed-DD protocol fails to capture it. These results identify non-repeatable-event sensing as a promising application of quantum reservoir computing.

</details>


### [48] [Hierarchical Fourier Approximation for Variational Quantum Distribution Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06307)
*Taha Hoseinpour Asli,Sajjad Hashemian,Ebrahim Ardeshir-Larijani*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a theoretical framework for variational quantum distribution learning via Walsh–Fourier spectral truncation on the Boolean cube. It proves end-to-end expected learning guarantees decomposing error into approximation (omitted Fourier mass), estimation (statistical bounds from unbiased estimators), and optimization components, while noting limitations including a 2^{n-1} factor in total-variation bounds and no guarantee of global trainability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide rigorous theoretical foundations for quantum circuit Born machines used in distribution learning, specifically by establishing an approximation–estimation–optimization framework based on Fourier-spectral hierarchies that can be analyzed end-to-end.

Method: The method introduces a hierarchy of Walsh–Fourier approximations on the Boolean cube. At each level, selected target Fourier coefficients define a spectral truncation, which is projected onto the probability simplex and used as a target for a quantum circuit Born machine. Warm-start initialization maps parameters learned at one level to the next. The analysis proves an expected learning guarantee with an approximation term from omitted Fourier mass and a statistical bound from a normalized unbiased estimator. Discrepancy conditions are specialized to total variation distance, connecting distributional error to quantum-state fidelity.

Result: The paper proves an end-to-end expected learning guarantee decomposed into approximation and statistical estimation terms. For total variation distance, an explicit factor of 2^{n-1} arises under the normalized ell_2 convention, making the bound informative only for sufficiently concentrated Fourier tails. The framework does not establish global trainability or address barren plateaus but identifies conditions enabling low-to-high spectral training analysis.

Conclusion: The framework provides a principled spectral-hierarchy approach to variational quantum distribution learning with rigorous error bounds, while honestly acknowledging its limitations: the total-variation specialization suffers from exponential scaling and the framework does not solve optimization hardness problems such as barren plateaus. It delineates exactly when the approximation–estimation–optimization analysis hold.

Abstract: We study variational quantum distribution learning through a hierarchy of Walsh--Fourier approximations on the Boolean cube. At each level, a selected set of target Fourier coefficients defines a spectral truncation, which is projected onto the probability simplex and used as the target of a quantum circuit Born machine. Parameters learned at one level initialize the next through a warm-start map. We prove an end-to-end expected learning guarantee where the approximation term is determined by the omitted Fourier mass, while a normalized unbiased estimator yields an explicit statistical bound for empirical truncations. We then instantiate the abstract discrepancy conditions for total variation distance and relate the resulting distributional error to quantum-state fidelity. The total-variation specialization incurs the explicit factor $2^{n-1}$ under our normalized $\ell_2$ convention and is therefore informative only for sufficiently concentrated Fourier tails. The framework does not establish global trainability or eliminate barren plateaus; rather, it identifies the conditions under which low-to-high spectral training admits a approximation--estimation--optimization analysis.

</details>
