### [49] [Quantum Homotopy Perturbation Method to Solve Nonlinear Partial Differential Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06357)
*Jungin E. Kim,Eunsik Choi,Yan Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes the quantum homotopy perturbation method (QHPM), a variational quantum approach for solving nonlinear PDEs that maintains Hilbert space dimension after linearization via homotopy perturbation and reduces qubit count through functional encoding. It also introduces criteria for selecting truncation order and circuit depth, demonstrated on vorticity transport and reduced MHD equations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlinear PDEs are important across science and engineering, and quantum computing offers a new paradigm for solving them. However, existing approaches face scalability challenges—both in terms of Hilbert space dimension after linearization and in terms of qubit count and circuit depth in quantum simulations.

Method: QHPM combines homotopy perturbation to linearize nonlinear PDEs (while keeping the Hilbert space dimension unchanged) with a variational quantum simulation framework. Functional encoding reduces the required number of qubits, and parametrized circuit depth is minimized. The paper also introduces novel criteria for selecting the homotopy series truncation order and circuit depth to ensure cost-effective execution.

Result: The method is demonstrated on several benchmark examples including the vorticity transport equation and the reduced magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) equations, validating the scalability improvements and cost-effectiveness of the proposed criteria.

Conclusion: QHPM provides a scalable quantum computational framework for nonlinear PDEs by jointly addressing Hilbert space growth, qubit overhead, and circuit depth, with practical selection criteria for accuracy-cost trade-offs in variational quantum simulation.

Abstract: Solving nonlinear partial differential equations (PDEs) is important in various scientific and engineering applications. Recently, quantum computing was introduced as an alternative computational paradigm for solving nonlinear PDEs. In this paper, a new method called the quantum homotopy perturbation method (QHPM) is proposed to improve the scalability of solving nonlinear PDEs through two aspects. First, the dimension of the Hilbert space remains the same after the nonlinear PDE is linearized through the homotopy perturbation. Second, the solutions are obtained with a variational quantum simulation framework, where the number of qubits is decreased with functional encoding and the depth of parametrized circuits is reduced. The additional contribution of this paper is the introduction of new criteria for selecting the homotopy series truncation order and circuit depth for cost-effective QHPM. The proposed approach is demonstrated with several examples, including the vorticity transport equation and the reduced magnetohydrodynamics equations.

</details>


### [50] [Trapped Ion Quantum Networking and Telecommunications Coexisting on One Fiber](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06387)
*Denton Wu,Mingzhe Han,Zehao Wang,Ana Luiza Ferrari,Mika Zalewski,Yuanheng Xie,Tingjun Chen,Norbert Linke*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents the first experimental demonstration of a quantum memory-based network operating over an active deployed fiber carrying classical telecom traffic (Ethernet and 5G), overcoming the previous limitation of requiring dark fibers. Ion-photon entanglement using 1092 nm photons from a Strontium-88 ion was successfully distributed over a 2.8 km fiber loop while all classical control signals, including polarization-stabilizing fiber sensing, co-propagated on the same fiber.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous quantum memory-based networking research exclusively relied on dark fibers to avoid detector background from telecom traffic. This severely limits practical deployment since most deployed fibers carry active classical traffic. The authors aim to demonstrate that quantum memory links can coexist with classical telecom signals on the same fiber, thereby making the entire existing classical fiber infrastructure available for quantum networks.

Method: Ion-photon entanglement was generated using 1092 nm photons emitted by a Strontium-88 ion and distributed over a deployed 2.8 km fiber loop that simultaneously carried Ethernet and 5G classical traffic. All classical control signals—including fiber sensing for polarization stabilization required to coordinate the quantum transmitter and receiver—were made to co-propagate on the same active fiber.

Result: The experimental demonstration successfully achieved memory-photon entanglement distribution over an active, deployed fiber carrying concurrent Ethernet and 5G traffic, with all classical control signals sharing the same fiber. This is the first such coexistence demonstration.

Conclusion: The results show that memory-based quantum networks can be practically realized on active classical network infrastructure, eliminating the need for dedicated dark fibers and vastly expanding the addressable fiber resources for future quantum network deployment.

Abstract: Research into long-distance quantum memory-based networking to date has exclusively used dark fibers. This avoids the detector background from telecommunications (telecom) traffic, but as a result excludes many fibers deployed in the field. If memory-photon entanglement and telecom signals coexist on one fiber, the entire classical fiber infrastructure becomes available for quantum links. We present the first experimental demonstration of such coexistence. Ion-photon entanglement using 1092 nm photons emitted by a Strontium-88 ion is distributed over a deployed 2.8 km fiber loop which carries Ethernet and 5G traffic. All classical control signals required to coordinate the quantum transmitter and receiver systems co-propagate on the same fiber. These include fiber sensing for polarization stabilization. Our results demonstrate that memory-based quantum networks can be realized on active classical network infrastructure.

</details>


### [51] [Telecom-Integrated Photonic Memory Operating Near the Mechanical Ground State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06399)
*Jindao Tang,Bo Jing,Liping Zeng,Peiqin Chen,Hengrui Liang,Yifei Zhang,Xinyao Xu,Qizhi Cai,Xueying Zhang,Qiang Zhou,You Wang,Haizhi Song,Guangcan Guo,Guangwei Deng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates an on-chip, absorptive optomechanical quantum memory for telecom-band photons, operating near the mechanical ground state in a dilution refrigerator at 20 mK. The device achieves a remarkably low phonon occupancy of 0.32 during storage while enabling on-demand retrieval of few-photon-level telecom photons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scalable quantum networks need quantum memories that are chip-integrated, telecom-band compatible, and support flexible retrieval. Nanofabricated mechanical resonators offer independent tunability of optical and mechanical modes, long-lived phonon states, and design flexibility beyond atomic systems, making them strong candidates for practical integrated quantum memory.

Method: The device is an on-chip absorptive optomechanical memory based on optomechanically induced transparency (OMIT). It operates near the mechanical ground state by placing the device in a dilution refrigerator at 20 mK and tailoring the control field to suppress optical heating.

Result: The device successfully stores telecom-band photons with compatibility to external photon sources at the few-photon level, enables on-demand retrieval, and achieves a low phonon occupancy of 0.32 during the storage process.

Conclusion: The results lay the groundwork for scalable, phonon-based quantum memory devices and open new avenues for integrating mechanical systems into practical quantum network architectures.

Abstract: Scalable quantum networks require quantum memories that are chip-integrated, telecom-band compatible, and capable of flexible retrieval. Nanofabricated mechanical resonators meet these criteria. They offer independent tunability of optical and mechanical modes, long-lived phonon states, and design flexibility beyond atomic systems, making them strong candidates for practical integrated quantum memory. Here, we demonstrate an on-chip, absorptive optomechanical memory for telecom-band photons, based on optomechanically induced transparency (OMIT) and operating near the mechanical ground state. The device stores telecom-band photons, demonstrating compatibility with external photon sources at the few-photon level, while enabling on-demand retrieval. By placing the device in a dilution refrigerator at 20 mK and tailoring the control field to suppress optical heating, we achieve a remarkably low phonon occupancy of just 0.32 during the storage process. Our results lay the groundwork for scalable, phonon-based quantum memory devices and open new avenues for integrating mechanical systems into practical quantum network architectures.

</details>


### [52] [After Theft: From Revocation to Neutralization in the Custody of Quantum Clones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06415)
*Gabriele Gianini,Jianyi Lin,Corrado Mio,Stelvio Cimato,Ernesto Damiani*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes using encrypted quantum cloning as a security primitive for post-compromise response, specifically introducing "neutralization"—a mechanism where a legitimate custodian unseals a trusted clone to exhaust the sole unsealing opportunity, permanently rendering a stolen encrypted clone useless. The work formalizes this capability, distinguishes it from mere revocation, situates it within a post-theft response space, introduces a temporal threat model (adversary has the clone but not the key), and compares it against classical analogues, concluding that encrypted quantum cloning is a foundational primitive for post-compromise quantum custody in distributed preservation contexts, including Cyber-Humanities preservation architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to go beyond classical revocation in responding to the theft of encrypted quantum data. In scenarios where an attacker steals an encrypted quantum clone but lacks the decryption key, standard revocation is inadequate; the paper motivates a stronger response mechanism ("neutralization") that exploits the inherent consumption of the single decryption opportunity in encrypted quantum cloning to permanently disable the stolen clone.

Method: The paper formalizes the distinction between revocation and neutralization, situates neutralization within a broader post-theft response space, introduces a temporal threat model where the adversary possesses an encrypted clone but not the decryption key, and compares the quantum mechanism with its closest classical analogue to show that the classical case can only achieve the same policy outcome through external procedural composition.

Result: The analysis demonstrates that encrypted quantum cloning enables a fundamentally stronger post-theft response than revocation, where unsealing a legitimate trusted clone exhausts the single decryption opportunity and permanently neutralizes the stolen clone. The comparison shows classical mechanisms cannot naturally reproduce this behavior and require external procedural composition to approximate the same policy outcome.

Conclusion: Encrypted quantum cloning serves as an effective primitive for post-compromise quantum custody, particularly relevant to distributed preservation architectures in Cyber-Humanities contexts, by providing an intrinsic, non-composable, cryptographic mechanism for neutralizing stolen quantum assets.

Abstract: Encrypted quantum cloning enables the creation of multiple encrypted clones of an unknown quantum state while allowing only one effective decryption, with the decryption resource being intrinsically consumed in the process. In this paper, we argue that this property supports a distinctive custodial security primitive for post-compromise response. We consider a threat model in which an adversary steals an encrypted quantum clone but does not yet possess the corresponding decryption key. In such a scenario, a legitimate custodian may unseal a different trusted clone, thereby exhausting the sole available unsealing opportunity and rendering the stolen clone permanently useless. We argue that this mechanism is not adequately described as mere revocation. Rather, it realizes a stronger form of post-theft response, which we call neutralization. We formalize this distinction, locate it within a broader post-theft response space, and introduce a temporal threat model. We compare the mechanism with its closest classical analogue, showing that the classical case can reproduce the policy outcome only through external procedural composition. We finally interpret encrypted quantum cloning as a primitive for post-compromise quantum custody in distributed preservation settings, with prospective relevance for Cyber-Humanities-oriented preservation architectures.

</details>


### [53] [Generation of entanglement statistics with a large-scale integrated photonic-electronic circuit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06471)
*Volkan Gurses,Ali Hajimiri*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates optoelectronic quantum information processing using a large-scale integrated photonic-electronic circuit, a 32-channel coherent receiver array where radio-frequency electronics mix analog photocurrents after homodyne detection instead of performing optical mode mixing. The researchers reconstruct the two-mode covariance and show the partial transpose has a smallest symplectic eigenvalue of 0.99270, three standard deviations below the separability bound, confirming entanglement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous-variable quantum processors scale with the number of modes they can transform, but each optical transformation adds loss, limiting scalability. The authors aim to overcome this limitation by moving mode mixing from the optical domain into radio-frequency electronics after homodyne detection, leveraging integrated electronics for scalability.

Method: The approach uses squeezed vacuum directed onto a large-scale integrated photonic-electronic circuit comprised of a 32-channel coherent receiver array. The RF network within the chip mixes the analog photocurrents after homodyne detection, performing mode transformations electronically. A local-oscillator ramp is used to reconstruct the two-mode covariance at the aperture.

Result: The reconstructed two-mode covariance is physical, and its partial transpose has a smallest symplectic eigenvalue of 0.99270, which is three standard deviations below the separability bound, demonstrating non-separable (entangled) states processed through the optoelectronic platform.

Conclusion: Mode transformation becomes a circuit function whose scale follows integrated electronics rather than optical components. With electro-optic transducers replacing photodiodes, the same architecture could enable transferring optical modes into superconducting circuits and back, paving the way toward a photonic-electronic quantum computer spanning optical and microwave frequency bands.

Abstract: Continuous-variable quantum processors scale with the modes they can transform, and each transformation adds loss. We demonstrate optoelectronic quantum information processing, moving the mixing into radio-frequency electronics after homodyne detection. Squeezed vacuum falls on a large-scale integrated photonic-electronic circuit, a 32-channel coherent receiver array whose radio-frequency network mixes the analog photocurrents. A local-oscillator ramp reconstructs the two-mode covariance at the aperture, which is physical, and its partial transpose has smallest symplectic eigenvalue 0.99270, three standard deviations below the separability bound. Mode transformation becomes a circuit function whose scale follows integrated electronics. With electro-optic transducers in place of the photodiodes, the same receiver would transfer optical modes into superconducting circuits and back, toward a photonic-electronic quantum computer spanning the optical and microwave bands.

</details>


### [54] [Quantum Optics of harmonic generation in the strongly driven Jaynes-Cummings-type system](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06480)
*M Suman,Sili Yi,Maria Chekhova,Misha Ivanov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper adapts the Jaynes-Cummings model to bridge cavity quantum electrodynamics with strong-field and attosecond physics, demonstrating that multi-photon resonances driven by a strong low-frequency classical field generate highly non-classical, quantum-correlated harmonics of the driving field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To connect two traditionally separate fields—cavity QED and strong-field/attosecond physics—by extending the Jaynes-Cummings model to study nonlinear optical phenomena in a fully quantum framework without approximations.

Method: The authors employ the Jaynes-Cummings model with a few discrete quantum modes of light, driven by a strong low-frequency classical field, and analyze the resulting multi-photon resonances and their harmonic output without introducing additional approximations beyond the standard Jaynes-Cummings assumptions.

Result: Multi-photon resonances in the driven Jaynes-Cummings system are shown to generate highly non-classical, quantum-correlated harmonics of the classical driver.

Conclusion: The Jaynes-Cummings model provides a viable framework for uniting cavity quantum electrodynamics with strong-field and attosecond physics, revealing quantum-correlated harmonic generation as a bridge phenomenon. The work is dedicated to Joseph Henry Eberly for his contributions to both fields.

Abstract: We adapt the Jaynes-Cummings model to study the interface of cavity quantum electrodynamics with strong field and attosecond physics. We show how multi-photon resonances in the Jaynes- Cummings system driven by a strong low-frequency classical light field lead to the generation of highly non-classical, quantum-correlated harmonics of the classical driver. Our treatment assumes no approximations, apart from the typical Jaynes-Cummings model assumption of only a few discrete quantum modes of light. The paper is dedicated to Joseph Henry Eberly, whose remarkable research has left indelible mark on both strong field physics and quantum optics.

</details>


### [55] [Low-Depth Random Unitaries without Ancillae](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06528)
*Zhenyu Du,Siyuan Cheng,Xiongfeng Ma*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves that random unitary k-designs can be generated in optimal depth without any ancillary qubits, achieving circuit depths of Õ(k)(log n)^{1/δ} on δ-dimensional architectures and Õ(k) log log n with all-to-all connectivity for approximate designs. A general "exactification lemma" lifts these constructions to optimal-depth exact k-designs, exponentially reducing resources compared to state-of-the-art exact constructions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Random unitaries are essential tools across quantum information and many-body physics, but generating them efficiently requires balancing both spatial (ancilla qubits) and temporal (circuit depth) resources. Existing low-depth generation methods depend heavily on an extensive number of ancillary qubits, creating severe spatial overhead. This paper aims to eliminate this overhead by generating random unitaries in optimal depth without any ancillae.

Method: The authors prove that random unitaries can be generated in optimal depth with no ancillary qubits. For multiplicative-error approximate k-designs on n qubits, they construct circuits with depth Õ(k)(log n)^{1/δ} on δ-dimensional architectures and Õ(k) log log n with all-to-all connectivity. They also introduce a general "exactification lemma" that lifts approximate constructions to optimal-depth exact k-designs.

Result: The constructions achieve optimal depth for random unitary generation without ancillae. For approximate k-designs, depths are Õ(k)(log n)^{1/δ} (δ-dimensional) and Õ(k) log log n (all-to-all). For exact k-designs, the exactification lemma yields an exponential resource reduction over the best existing exact constructions.

Conclusion: The results simultaneously minimize both space and time (depth) costs for generating random unitaries, providing a space-time optimal solution that benefits a wide range of quantum protocols including quantum learning, metrology, and device benchmarking.

Abstract: Random unitaries are fundamental to quantum information and many-body physics, with widespread applications ranging from quantum learning and metrology to device benchmarking. A central pursuit is to minimize the space and circuit depth required to generate them. However, existing methods for generating low-depth random unitaries rely heavily on an extensive number of ancillary qubits, imposing severe spatial overhead. In this work, we prove that random unitaries can be generated in optimal depth without ancillae. For multiplicative-error approximate $k$-designs on $n$ qubits, our circuits achieve a depth of $\widetilde{O} (k) (\log n)^{1/δ}$ on $δ$-dimensional architectures and $\widetilde{O}(k) \log \log n$ with all-to-all connectivity. Furthermore, by introducing a general exactification lemma, we lift our construction to optimal-depth exact $k$-designs, yielding an exponential resource reduction over state-of-the-art exact constructions. Our results minimize the space-time costs for a wide range of quantum protocols.

</details>


### [56] [Quantum bivariate bicycle codes with weight-8 checks surpassing the BB benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06572)
*Liangdong Lu,Ruipan Yang,Guanmin Guo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops the algebraic structure theory of Bivariate Bicycle (BB)-type quantum LDPC codes with weight-8 checks (weight-4 generator polynomials), proving an exact dimension formula, symmetry group properties, X/Z distance equality, and rigorous distance upper bounds via subgroup-coset kernel vectors. Using an exactly validated search pipeline, the authors construct and certify new codes that surpass the weight-6 BB benchmarks at several block lengths (e.g., [[144,6,d≥15]] exceeds the benchmark distance 12, [[72,14,8]] more than doubles the kd²/n ratio of the same-length BB code), though circuit-level memory experiments quantify a pseudo-threshold penalty (~0.1% vs ~0.4%) from heavier checks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bivariate bicycle (BB) codes achieve weight-6 checks with outstanding parameters (e.g., [[144,12,12]]), but exploring weight-8 check variants could unlock better coding parameters. A systematic algebraic framework is needed to understand, search, and certify such BB-type codes with weight-4 generator polynomials.

Method: The authors develop an algebraic structure theory for BB-type codes based on weight-4 generator polynomials over quotient polynomial rings, prove key structural theorems (dimension formula k=2·dim R/(A,B), a 4𝓁m-element symmetry group on generator pairs, X/Z distance equality, and subgroup-coset kernel vectors for rigorous distance bounds), and combine this with an exactly validated search pipeline including a cross-validated bit-mask distance verifier.

Result: The census at n=144 yields 53 codes with strong parameters including [[144,6,d≥15]] (exceeding BB's benchmark distance 12), [[144,10,12]], and [[144,16,10]] at kd²/n=11.11 (7.4% below benchmark but encoding more qubits). At n=72, [[72,14,8]] achieves kd²/n=12.44, more than double the same-length BB code. Circuit-level memory experiments show ≈0.1% pseudo-threshold for weight-8 codes vs ≈0.4% for the BB reference under identical modeling, quantifying the threshold cost of heavier checks.

Conclusion: BB-type codes with weight-8 checks can surpass weight-6 BB benchmarks in distance and kd²/n ratios, supported by a complete algebraic structure theory and certified exhaustive distance computations. However, heavier checks incur a measurable pseudo-threshold penalty in circuit-level simulations (~4× worse), presenting a trade-off between asymptotic coding parameters and noise robustness.

Abstract: Bivariate bicycle (BB) codes of Bravyi \emph{et al.}~\cite{Bravyi2024} are quantum low-density parity-check codes with weight-$6$ checks, exemplified by $[[144,12,12]]$ with $kd^2/n=12$. We develop the algebraic structure theory of BB-type codes with weight-$8$ checks (weight-$4$ generator polynomials) and use it, together with an exactly validated search pipeline, to construct and certify new codes. We prove an exact dimension formula $k=2\dim R/(A,B)$ (forcing even $k$), a $4\ell m$-element symmetry group on generator pairs, an $X/Z$ distance equality $d_X=d_Z$, and a family of subgroup-coset kernel vectors giving rigorous distance upper bounds and a design rule for high-distance constructions; all distances are computed exhaustively by a cross-validated bit-mask verifier. At $n=144$ the pipeline returns a census of $53$ codes whose strongest members surpass the BB benchmark: $[[144,6,d\ge 15]]$ exceeds the benchmark distance $12$ (certified $d\ge 15$), $[[144,10,12]]$ reaches it with weight-$8$ checks, and $[[144,16,10]]$ encodes a third more logical qubits at $kd^2/n=11.11$ ($7.4\%$ below benchmark) while decoding no worse. At $n=72$, $[[72,14,8]]$ attains $kd^2/n=12.44$---more than twice the same-length BB code---and decodes better; a circuit-level memory experiment places our weight-$8$ codes at $\approx 0.1\%$ pseudo-threshold versus $\approx 0.4\%$ for the BB reference under an identical model, quantifying the threshold cost of the heavier checks. All structural statements are verified numerically on the whole census.

</details>


### [57] [Solution-phase fluorination of nanodiamond: near-surface NV$^-$ activation and spin relaxation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06605)
*Szabolcs Czene,Olga Krafcsik,Nikoletta Jegenyés,Dávid Beke,László Péter,Vladimir Verkhovlyuk,Adam Gali*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents two solution-phase fluorination routes (XeF₂-based decarboxylation and Balz-Schiemann reaction) for stabilizing the negatively charged nitrogen-vacancy (NV⁻) centre in fluorescent nanodiamonds. Both routes achieve high NV⁻ fractions (~90% average, approaching 100% in fluorine-rich regions) while retaining long spin-lattice relaxation times, though these are shorter than the as-received material, revealing a trade-off between charge-state stability and spin lifetime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The negatively charged nitrogen-vacancy (NV⁻) centre in nanodiamonds is a leading platform for quantum technology, serving as a room-temperature qubit. Its stability is governed by surface functional groups, so finding surface termination routes that enhance NV⁻ stability while maintaining long spin coherence times is critical for practical quantum applications.

Method: Two solution-phase fluorination routes were developed and compared: (1) direct C-F bond formation via radical decarboxylation using xenon difluoride, and (2) the Balz-Schiemann reaction replacing surface amino groups with fluorine. The materials were characterized using infrared spectroscopy, X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy, energy-dispersive X-ray spectroscopy, Raman and photoluminescence spectroscopy, alongside frequency-domain relaxometry for spin measurements.

Result: Both fluorination routes yielded high NV⁻ fractions—up to ~90% on average and approaching 100% in fluorine-rich regions—among the highest reported for surface-terminated nanodiamonds of this size. Fluorinated particles retained long spin-lattice relaxation times (733±56 μs for the XeF₂ route and 712±20 μs for the Balz-Schiemann route), which are several times longer than commercial HPHT nanodiamonds but shorter than the as-received material (1173±123 μs). This indicates that charge-state stability and spin lifetime do not improve simultaneously, as fluorination activates near-surface NV⁻ centres exposed to surface noise.

Conclusion: Fluorination via either route effectively stabilizes the NV⁻ charge state to among the highest reported levels, but this comes at the cost of reduced spin coherence compared to the as-received material. The trade-off stems from fluorination activating near-surface NV⁻ centres—those most impacted by surface noise yet most important for relaxometric sensing—suggesting that surface termination strategies must balance charge-state stability against spin-lattice relaxation performance.

Abstract: Nanodiamonds hosting luminescent point defects, known as fluorescent nanodiamonds (FND), are a leading platform for quantum technology. The nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centre is the most intensively studied of these; its negatively charged state (NV$^-$) can serve as a qubit at room temperature, and its stability is governed by surface functional groups. We present two solution-phase fluorination routes for stabilising NV$^-$: direct C-F bond formation by decarboxylation with xenon difluoride via a radical mechanism, and the Balz-Schiemann reaction, which replaces surface amino groups with fluorine. The two routes were compared by infrared, X-ray photoelectron, energy-dispersive X-ray, Raman and photoluminescence spectroscopy. Both gave a high NV$^-$ fraction, up to $\sim$90% on average and approaching 100% in fluorine-rich regions, which to our knowledge is among the highest reported for surface-terminated nanodiamonds of this size and, in particular, for fluorine termination. Frequency-domain relaxometry shows that the fluorinated particles retain a long spin-lattice relaxation time, $733\pm56$ and $712\pm20$ $μ$s for the XeF$_2$ and Balz-Schiemann routes, several times the values reported for commercial HPHT nanodiamonds, although shorter than the $1173\pm123$ $μ$s of the as-received material. Charge-state stability and spin lifetime therefore do not improve together: fluorination activates near-surface NV$^-$ centres, which are the most exposed to surface noise but also the ones that dominate relaxometric sensing.

</details>


### [58] [Spectral bounds for the partial transpose](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06617)
*Wei-Jie Jiang,Jing-Tao Qiu,Xiao-Dong Yu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Ky Fan k-negativity, a hierarchical generalization of standard negativity that captures the finer structure of the negative spectrum of the partial transpose. The authors develop a unified analytical framework that reduces the problem of finding absolute spectral bounds to a spectral graph optimization, solvable exactly for every k via a single cubic equation. They fully solve the k=1 case under fixed purity, uncover a simple underlying graph structure, and demonstrate applications in certifying genuine multilevel entanglement and converting the p3-PPT condition into a quantitative lower bound on negativity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard negativity only captures the total weight of negative eigenvalues of the partial transpose, discarding the finer structure of the negative spectrum. This gap limits our understanding of entanglement beyond the coarse aggregate measure.

Method: The authors introduce Ky Fan k-negativity as a hierarchical family of entanglement measures. They reduce the maximization of spectral bounds on partial transpose to a spectral graph optimization problem, whose solution gives exact bounds for each k through a single cubic equation. They investigate these bounds analytically and numerically under fixed purity, solving the k=1 case completely.

Result: Exact spectral bounds are obtained for every k via a cubic equation. The k=1 case is fully solved, revealing a simple underlying graph structure. The Ky Fan k-negativity robustly certifies genuine multilevel entanglement and converts the p3-PPT condition into a quantitative lower bound on negativity.

Conclusion: The Ky Fan k-negativity provides a richer, hierarchical characterization of entanglement that goes beyond standard negativity, with computable spectral bounds and concrete applications in entanglement certification and bounding negativity from the p3-PPT condition.

Abstract: Among the various entanglement measures, the negativity stands out not only for its clear physical meaning but also for being directly computable from the spectrum of the partial transpose. However, the negativity captures only the total weight of the negative eigenvalues, whereas the finer structure of the negative spectrum remains largely unexplored. In this work, we fill this gap by introducing a hierarchical generalization of the negativity, the Ky Fan $k$-negativity, and developing a unified analytical framework for deriving spectral bounds on the partial transpose. To obtain the absolute bounds, we reduce the maximization problem to a spectral graph optimization, whose solution yields the exact bound for every $k$ through a single cubic equation. We further investigate these spectral bounds both analytically and numerically under a fixed-purity constraint. In particular, we solve the $k=1$ case completely and uncover a simple underlying graph structure. As two direct applications, we show that the Ky Fan $k$-negativity robustly certifies genuine multilevel entanglement and that it converts the $p_3$-PPT condition into a quantitative lower bound on the negativity.

</details>


### [59] [Anomaly fluctuation theorem for quantum coherence dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06632)
*Kun Zhang,Hai-Long Shi,Xiao-Hui Wang,Wen-Li Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes an exact integral fluctuation theorem (FT) for quantum coherence dynamics using Kirkwood-Dirac (KD) quasiprobability trajectories, valid for arbitrary initial states and dynamics. Unlike standard FTs, it deviates from the standard unit-valued form, with a complex "anomaly" term quantifying the weighted overlap between residual final-state coherence and coherence generated from a dephased input. The real part bounds coherence change while the imaginary part constrains the second-order moment of stochastic coherence change weighted by imaginary KD quasiprobabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum coherence is a key resource in quantum information, yet general frameworks governing its dynamics are lacking since coherence lacks a general monotonicity law. The authors aim to establish general statistical constraints on coherence dynamics by leveraging fluctuation theorems.

Method: The authors formulate an integral fluctuation theorem for coherence dynamics expressed in terms of Kirkwood-Dirac (KD) quasiprobability trajectories. The theorem is valid for arbitrary initial states and dynamics, and incorporates a complex deviation term ("anomaly") that captures the weighted overlap between residual final-state coherence and coherence generated from the dephased input. Analysis of the real and imaginary parts of the anomaly provides respective bounds on coherence change and second-order moments of stochastic coherence change.

Result: An exact integral FT for coherence dynamics is established that deviates from the standard unit-valued form. The real part of the anomaly bounds coherence change, while its imaginary part constrains the second-order moment of the stochastic coherence change weighted by imaginary KD quasiprobabilities.

Conclusion: The results establish general statistical constraints on coherence dynamics and demonstrate the utility and broad applicability of fluctuation theorems for studying quantum-resource dynamics.

Abstract: Quantum coherence is a central resource in quantum information science, yet general frameworks and constraints governing its dynamics remain limited. Although quantum coherence lacks a general monotonicity law, we establish an exact integral fluctuation theorem (FT) for coherence dynamics, formulated in terms of Kirkwood-Dirac (KD) quasiprobability trajectories and valid for arbitrary initial states and dynamics. This integral FT deviates from the standard unit-valued form, and the complex deviation, termed the anomaly, quantifies a weighted overlap between the residual final-state coherence and the coherence generated from the dephased input. The real part of the anomaly yields bounds on coherence change, while its imaginary part constrains the second-order moment of the stochastic coherence change weighted by the imaginary parts of the KD quasiprobabilities. Our results establish general statistical constraints on coherence dynamics, demonstrating the utility and broad applicability of FTs for studying quantum-resource dynamics.

</details>


### [60] [Contact order governs the onset of entanglement cascades](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06675)
*Sugumi Kanno,Jiro Soda*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces the concept of "entangling rates" versus "contact order" for describing when genuine multipartite entanglement can arise in finite-dimensional analytic pure-state dynamics. The contact order m★ is the first Taylor order where the trajectory cannot be reproduced by any product curve, equivalently the first nonvanishing order of the Fubini–Study distance from the product manifold. The authors derive a time-ordered recursion to compute m★ from Taylor coefficients of H(t) without curvature-induced kinematic terms, providing a geometric description of an entanglement cascade.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Entangling rates describe how quickly entanglement forms when an entangling channel is available, but offer no information when that channel is forbidden. The paper seeks a complementary concept that characterizes when and how genuine multipartite entanglement can emerge at all, beyond mere rates.

Method: For finite-dimensional analytic pure-state dynamics, the authors define m★ as the first Taylor order of contact between the physical trajectory and the S|E product manifold. They derive a time-ordered recursion that removes curvature-induced kinematic terms, enabling computation of m★ from the Taylor coefficients of the Hamiltonian H(t).

Result: The framework yields a geometric description of an entanglement cascade: new subsystems can join multipartite entanglement only after one or more interaction steps. A symmetry-protected three-qubit model realizes m★=2, and a three-mode bosonic example demonstrates that genuine tripartite entanglement can arise even when the two newly formed reduced pairs remain separable.

Conclusion: The contact order m★ provides a rigorous and computable criterion for the onset of genuine (n+1)-partite entanglement, complementing entangling rates by describing the geometric conditions under which entanglement generation becomes possible at all.

Abstract: Entangling rates describe a direct entangling channel, but give no information when that channel is forbidden. For finite-dimensional analytic pure-state dynamics, we show that the onset of genuine $(n+1)$-partite entanglement is determined by the contact order $m_\star$ between the physical trajectory and the $S|E$ product manifold. It is the first Taylor order that cannot be reproduced by any product curve, or equivalently the first nonvanishing order of the Fubini--Study distance from the product manifold. We derive a time-ordered recursion that removes curvature-induced kinematic terms and computes $m_\star$ from the Taylor coefficients of $H(t)$. This provides a geometric description of an entanglement cascade, in which new subsystems can join multipartite entanglement only after one or more interaction steps. A symmetry-protected three-qubit model realizes $m_\star=2$, and a three-mode bosonic example shows that genuine tripartite entanglement can arise even when the two newly formed reduced pairs remain separable.

</details>


### [61] [Error Exponents of Probabilistic Quantum Resource Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06688)
*Xian Shi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes a unified framework for analyzing conditional error exponents in probabilistic quantum resource distillation under approximately resource-nongenerating instruments, deriving general bounds and analytical characterizations for entanglement, coherence, and magic distillation, and identifying regimes where postselection yields strict improvements in error decay rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a unified theoretical framework for analyzing probabilistic resource distillation with conditional error exponents, moving beyond case-by-case analyses and connecting probabilistic distillation to postselected composite quantum hypothesis testing to obtain quantitative characterizations.

Method: The authors (1) derive general one-shot bounds on conditional distillation error exponents for generic quantum resource theories satisfying structural conditions, (2) relate probabilistic distillation to postselected composite quantum hypothesis testing, (3) obtain analytical characterizations for entanglement, coherence, and magic distillation at finite blocklength, and (4) derive asymptotic expressions in the zero-rate regime along with closed-form formulas for representative state families.

Result: The framework yields explicit closed-form conditional error exponents for several representative families of states in entanglement and magic resource theories, and comparison with deterministic distillation exponents reveals regimes where postselection strictly improves the exponential decay rate of the conditional error.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate an operational advantage of postselection in quantum resource distillation and establish postselected composite hypothesis testing as a general mathematical tool for characterizing probabilistic resource-processing tasks.

Abstract: In this manuscript, we establish a unified framework for analyzing the conditional error exponents of probabilistic resource distillation under approximately resource-nongenerating instruments. For generic quantum resource theories satisfying suitable structural conditions, we derive general one-shot bounds on the conditional distillation error exponents. By relating probabilistic distillation to postselected composite quantum hypothesis testing, we further obtain analytical characterizations of the conditional error exponents for entanglement, coherence, and magic distillation at finite blocklength, and derive their asymptotic expressions in the zero-rate regime. For several representative families of states in entanglement and magic resource theories, these characterizations reduce to explicit closed-form formulas. Comparing them with the corresponding deterministic distillation exponents, we identify regimes in which postselection yields a strict improvement in the exponential decay rate of the conditional error. Our results reveal an operational advantage of postselection in quantum resource distillation and establish postselected composite hypothesis testing as a general tool for characterizing probabilistic resource-processing tasks.

</details>


### [62] [Thermodynamic Performance of a Measurement-Driven Quantum Engine with a Two-Parameter $(p,q)$-Deformed Harmonic Oscillator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06695)
*Tunde Joseph Osunmusanmi,Berihu Teklu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes a measurement-driven, single-bath quantum engine whose working medium is a (p,q)-deformed harmonic oscillator. The nonlinear spectrum alters energy gaps sampled by nonselective Gaussian measurements and the work exchanged in adiabatic strokes. Using a consistent first-law framework, the authors derive measurement heat, adiabatic work, thermalization heat, and a reduced efficiency, and show via numerical scans that deformation can enhance extracted work relative to the undeformed oscillator within the validated operational regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how quantum measurement backaction can be harnessed as a fuel for quantum engines and to explore whether spectral engineering via (p,q)-deformation of a harmonic oscillator can enhance engine performance beyond the standard (undeformed) case.

Method: The engine operates in four steps: adiabatic change of parameters (ω,p,q), nonselective Gaussian measurement of the position quadrature, reverse adiabatic stroke, and thermalization. The working medium is modeled as a (p,q)-deformed oscillator. Thermodynamic quantities (measurement heat, adiabatic work, thermalization heat, efficiency) are derived using a consistent first-law convention. The measurement stroke is described via a transition matrix, and passivity/unitality constraints ensure nonnegative measurement heat. Numerical scans enforce finite-basis, spectral-ordering, and engine-operation checks.

Result: Numerical results indicate that deformation can enhance the measurement-induced energy input and the work extracted relative to the undeformed oscillator, subject to finite-basis, spectral-ordering, and engine-operability constraints. Valid engine regimes are identified by Q_M>0, W_cyc<0, Q_T<0, and 0<η<1.

Conclusion: The (p,q)-deformation of the harmonic oscillator offers a viable route to boost the performance of measurement-driven quantum engines in the single-bath, measurement-fuelled setting. The reported efficiency is reduced in the sense that measurement-apparatus costs are excluded.

Abstract: We address a measurement-driven, single-bath quantum engine that generates usable work using quantum measurement backaction. The working medium is a two-parameter $(p,q)$-deformed harmonic oscillator, whose nonlinear spectrum modifies the energy gaps sampled by a nonselective Gaussian measurement and the work exchanged during quasistatic adiabatic strokes. The cycle starts from a Gibbs state and consists of an adiabatic change of $(ω,p,q)$, measurement of the final effective position quadrature, a reverse adiabatic stroke, and thermalization with the original bath. Using a consistent first-law convention, we derive the measurement heat, adiabatic work contributions, thermalization heat, and reduced working-medium efficiency. We also express the measurement stroke via a transition matrix and identify passivity/unitality conditions that ensure nonnegative measurement heat. Numerical scans show that, within the finite-basis, spectral-ordering, and engine-operation checks used in this work, deformation can enhance measurement-induced energy input and extracted work relative to the undeformed oscillator. The physical engine regime is selected by $\QM>0$, $\Wcyc<0$, $\QT<0$, and $0<η<1$. The reported efficiency is reduced and excludes measurement-apparatus costs.

</details>


### [63] [Scale-Free Localization Morphing in Bilocally Coupled Hatano-Nelson Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06799)
*Chong Wang,Linhu Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper investigates an anomalous critical non-Hermitian skin effect (CNHSE) in two Hatano–Nelson chains locally coupled at two bulk sites separated by distance d. Unlike conventional CNHSE in weakly coupled subsystems, this critical behavior persists under strong inter-chain coupling. Strong local hybridization creates high-energy impurity modes and fragments the low-energy Hilbert space into geometry-selected sectors, one supporting scale-free skin states. The outer sector hosts 2(L−d) selected states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore a new route to critical non-Hermitian skin effects beyond the conventional weak-coupling paradigm, demonstrating that strong local coupling can also induce CNHSE via Hilbert-space fragmentation.

Method: Analysis of two Hatano–Nelson chains coupled locally at two bulk sites separated by an inclusive distance d, examining how strong inter-chain hybridization generates impurity modes and fragments the low-energy Hilbert space into geometry-selected sectors.

Result: Critical non-Hermitian skin behavior persists even in the strong inter-chain-coupling regime. The fragmented Hilbert space contains one sector supporting scale-free skin states, with the count of selected outer-sector states given by 2(L−d) for the two-chain system.

Conclusion: Local-coupling-induced fragmentation is established as a mechanism for critical non-Hermitian behavior that extends beyond the weak-coupling paradigm, opening new avenues for engineering CNHSE in strongly coupled systems.

Abstract: We investigate an anomalous critical non-Hermitian skin effect (CNHSE) in two Hatano--Nelson chains coupled locally at two bulk sites separated by an inclusive distance $d$. Whereas conventional CNHSE is commonly associated with weakly coupled subsystems, the critical behavior in our system persists in the strong inter-chain-coupling regime. Strong local hybridization generates high-energy impurity modes and effectively fragments the low-energy Hilbert space into geometry-selected sectors, one of which supports scale-free skin states. Independently, the count of states selected in the outer sector follows $2(L-d)$ for the two-chain system. These results establish local-coupling-induced fragmentation as a route to critical non-Hermitian behavior beyond the weak-coupling paradigm.

</details>


### [64] [Hilbert space geometry change by a nonadiabatic electromagnetic field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06800)
*Ivan Georgiev Koprinkov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper discovers a change in the geometry of the Hilbert space for a quantum system interacting with a nonadiabatic electromagnetic field. Quantum states evolve from orthogonal (in closed systems and adiabatic fields) to non-orthogonal in nonadiabatic fields, enabling a time-dependent Fubini-Study metric that resembles the dynamical spacetime metric in general relativity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify the physical origin of the geometric change in Hilbert space when a quantum system couples to a nonadiabatic electromagnetic field, and to understand how field dynamics affect the orthogonality structure of quantum states.

Method: The analysis is based on examining matrix elements of quantum states across three physical regimes: (1) a closed quantum system in zero external field, (2) a system in a slowly changing (adiabatic) field, and (3) a system in a rapidly changing (nonadiabatic) field. The orthogonality to non-orthogonality transition is traced, and a time-dependent Fubini-Study metric is derived from the state matrix elements in the nonadiabatic case.

Result: Quantum states remain orthogonal in zero-field (closed) and adiabatic field cases, but become non-orthogonal in a nonadiabatic field, with the non-orthogonality continuously following field parameters. A time-dependent Fubini-Study metric can be constructed in the nonadiabatic case, establishing a formal analogy between the dynamical Hilbert-space metric and the dynamical spacetime metric of general relativity.

Conclusion: Nonadiabatic field interactions fundamentally alter the geometry of quantum state space by inducing non-orthogonality between states, giving rise to a dynamical Fubini-Study metric. This parallels the dynamical metric of curved spacetime in general relativity, suggesting a deep structural analogy between quantum Hilbert-space geometry and spacetime geometry.

Abstract: A change in the geometry of the Hilbert space of a quantum system interacting with a nonadiabatic electromagnetic field is found. To identify its physical origin, the evolution of orthogonality to non-orthogonality of the states of the quantum system has been investigated based on the matrix elements of the quantum states in three main physical cases: a quantum system in zero external field (closed quantum system), a quantum system in slowly changing (adiabatic) field, and a quantum system in rapidly changing (nonadiabatic) field. The quantum states are orthogonal in the first two cases and change to non-orthogonal in a nonadiabatic field, following continuously the parameters of the field. A time-dependent Fubini-Study metric of the Hilbert space can be determined based on the matrix elements of the quantum states in the nonadiabatic case. Such a dynamical metric of the Hilbert space in quantum mechanics resembles the dynamical metric of spacetime in general relativity.

</details>


### [65] [Spectral Twisting in a Common Bosonic Reservoir: Fragility of Two-Qubit Dark-State Protection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06802)
*Fabio Borrelli,Giovanni Miano,Carlo Forestiere*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies "spectral twisting" in two-qubit systems coupled to a common bosonic reservoir, where the locally dark coupling direction rotates with frequency due to the spectral-density matrix's varying diagonal ratio. Using nonperturbative hierarchical-equations-of-motion (HEOM) calculations, they show that twisting causes leakage from locally dark states (e.g., the singlet at the spectral crossing), shifts the optimal protected state, accelerates Werner-state concurrence decay, and yields a quadratic weak-twisting scaling P_leak ∝ [ω×τ(ω×)]².


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how frequency-dependent rotations of the bright/dark coupling directions in a maximally correlated bosonic reservoir—termed spectral twisting—degrade dark-state protection in two-qubit systems, thereby informing correlated-noise spectroscopy and decoherence-free encoding design.

Method: The authors define the spectral-density matrix J(ω) for two qubits sharing a bosonic reservoir, introduce spectral twisting via the Fubini–Study speed τ(ω) of the bright spectral projector, quantify protection loss through the survival leakage P_leak(t), and use nonperturbative hierarchical-equations-of-motion (HEOM) calculations for mismatched Drude–Lorentz spectra. They compare rotating-wave dynamics, untwisted asymmetric reservoirs, and qubit detuning to isolate twisting effects from asymmetry, thermal absorption, counter-rotating terms, and Hamiltonian symmetry breaking.

Result: For maximally correlated reservoirs with Drude–Lorentz spectra mismatched between the two qubits, twisting induces leakage from the singlet state that is locally dark at the spectral crossing ω×. Twisting shifts the optimally protected state, accelerates Werner-state concurrence decay, and at fixed observation time produces the quadratic weak-twisting scaling P_leak(t) ∝ [ω×τ(ω×)]².

Conclusion: Spectral twisting is a distinct mechanism—separate from coupling asymmetry, thermal absorption, counter-rotating processes, and Hamiltonian symmetry breaking—that limits dark-state protection. These findings provide guidance for dark-state engineering in structured reservoirs, with implications for correlated-noise spectroscopy and decoherence-free encodings.

Abstract: The interaction of two qubits with a common bosonic reservoir is characterized by the spectral-density matrix $\mathbf{J}(ω)$, whose diagonal entries $J_{11}(ω)$ and $J_{22}(ω)$ describe the local spectra, while the off-diagonal entries encode cross-correlations. For a maximally correlated reservoir, $\mathbf{J}(ω)$ has rank one and therefore a \textit{locally} dark coupling direction at each frequency. If $J_{22}(ω)/J_{11}(ω)$ varies with frequency, however, the bright and dark directions rotate and $\ker\mathbf{J}(ω)$ is frequency dependent. We call this \textit{spectral twisting} and quantify it through the Fubini--Study speed $τ(ω)$ of the bright spectral projector. We investigate how spectral twisting affects coupled two-qubit dynamics and dark-state protection. We quantify protection loss by the survival \textit{leakage} $P_{\mathrm{leak}}(t)$, which can become finite for states that are dark only locally in frequency. By comparing rotating-wave dynamics and untwisted asymmetric reservoirs, together with analyzing qubit detuning, we distinguish twisting from coupling asymmetry, thermal absorption, counter-rotating processes, and Hamiltonian symmetry breaking. For mismatched Drude--Lorentz spectra, our nonperturbative hierarchical-equations-of-motion calculations show that twisting induces leakage from the singlet, which is locally dark at the spectral crossing $ω_\times$ defined by $J_{11}(ω_\times)=J_{22}(ω_\times)$. Twisting also shifts the optimally protected state and accelerates the decay of Werner-state concurrence. At fixed observation time, we find the quadratic weak-twisting scaling $P_{\mathrm{leak}}(t)\propto[ω_\timesτ(ω_\times)]^2$. These results may guide dark-state engineering in structured reservoirs, with implications for correlated-noise spectroscopy and decoherence-free encodings.

</details>


### [66] [Quantum-Enhanced Learning Framework for Intelligent and AI-Native 6G Wireless Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06814)
*Muhammad Bilal Akram Dastagir,Rakesh Saini,Omer Tariq,Saif Al-Kuwari,Shahid Mumtaz,Anwer Al-Dulaimi,Ahmed Farouk*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Quantumer, a hybrid TinyML-quantum framework that combines multi-scale dilated convolutions with scaled dot-product attention in a lightweight transformer for edge intrusion detection. Quantum pre-training (Quantumer-Q) uses a four-qubit variational circuit as a nonlinear embedding teacher, followed by classical fine-tuning (Quantumer-C) that removes quantum execution at runtime. The framework achieves strong detection performance on three IoT datasets with only 105.86K parameters and ~16.8ms latency on an INT8 Raspberry Pi 4 deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The convergence of 6G and TinyML demands on-device intelligence under ultra-low latency, strict energy budgets, and tight compute constraints. Lightweight deep models capture local patterns but miss global dependencies, while attention mechanisms incur prohibitive energy and computational costs—creating a need for a novel architecture that balances representational power with resource efficiency.

Method: Quantumer integrates multi-scale dilated convolutions and scaled dot-product attention within a lightweight transformer, trained via a two-stage pipeline: Quantum Pre-Training (Quantumer-Q) followed by Classical Fine-Tuning (Quantumer-C). A QuantiblentLayer—a four-qubit variational circuit with trainable rotations and cyclic entangling operations—serves as a nonlinear embedding teacher during offline pre-training only, and is removed before deployment, leaving fully classical inference.

Result: On Edge-IIoTset, TON IoT, and WUSTL-IIoT-2021 datasets, Quantumer-Q achieves competitive detection performance with 105.86K parameters, 0.4038 MB memory, 0.5525 MB model size, and 5.5646 MFLOPs. INT8 Raspberry Pi 4 deployment achieves 16.8413 ms latency with a 0.6493 MB footprint.

Conclusion: Training-time quantum-assisted representation learning can enhance compact, energy-efficient edge-deployable intrusion detection systems without requiring runtime quantum execution, demonstrating a practical pathway for integrating quantum-inspired features into classical TinyML inference.

Abstract: The recent convergence of 6G wireless systems and Tiny Machine Learning (TinyML) has driven the need for on-device intelligence in edge networks, where ultra-low latency, stringent energy budgets, and tight compute constraints demand novel architectures. Lightweight deep models efficiently extract local patterns but fail to capture global dependencies, while attention mechanisms do so at the expense of energy and computational cost. To bridge this gap, we introduce Quantumer, a hybrid TinyML--quantum framework that integrates multi-scale dilated convolutions and scaled dot-product attention within a lightweight transformer architecture, employing a two-stage transfer learning pipeline from Quantum Pre-Training (Quantumer-Q) to Classical Fine-Tuning (Quantumer-C). We also present QuantiblentLayer, a four-qubit variational circuit that maps compact traffic representations into measurement-based Hilbert-space features using trainable rotations and cyclic entangling operations. The circuit is used only during offline pre-training as a nonlinear embedding teacher and is removed before Quantumer-C deployment, leaving a fully classical inference model without runtime quantum execution. By transferring these quantum-assisted embeddings into an energy-efficient, lightweight transformer, Quantumer achieves strong detection performance with minimal compute and memory overhead on resource-constrained edge devices. The intrusion detection system (IDS) is used as a case study and evaluated on the Edge-IIoTset, TON IoT, and WUSTL-IIoT-2021 datasets. Quantumer-Q achieves competitive compact-model performance with 105.86K parameters, 0.4038 MB memory usage, 0.5525 MB model size, and 5.5646 MFLOPs; the INT8 Raspberry Pi 4 deployment obtains 16.8413 ms latency with a 0.6493 MB footprint. These results support training-time quantum-assisted representation learning for compact edge-deployable IDS.

</details>


### [67] [Trace-class spectra of irreducible Gaussian quantum Markov semigroups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06957)
*Franco Fagnola,Zheng Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper determines the spectrum of the predual generator of irreducible Gaussian quantum Markov semigroups on trace-class operators over finite-mode bosonic Fock spaces, covering stable, strictly unstable, and periodic critical drift regimes. Results include full spectral characterization, distinguishing point, approximate point, and residual spectra across regimes; the nonperiodic critical drift case remains open.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To fully characterize the spectral properties of predual generators of Gaussian quantum Markov semigroups, which is fundamental for understanding relaxation, convergence rates, and the structure of quantum dynamical semigroups in open quantum systems.

Method: The proofs employ characteristic functions, Gaussian diffusion semigroups, and bounded eigenoperators of the dual semigroup. Spectral analysis is conducted across three drift regimes: stable, strictly unstable, and periodic critical.

Result: For stable drift, irreducibility is equivalent to existence of a unique faithful normal invariant state; spectrum and approximate point spectrum equal the closed left half-plane, point spectrum equals the open left half-plane plus zero (polynomial eigenvalues from drift do not exhaust the point spectrum). For strictly unstable drift, spectrum is the closed left half-plane with empty point spectrum, and the open left half-plane plus zero belongs to the residual spectrum. For periodic critical drift, an explicit spectral formula including the displacement parameter yields horizontal half-lines or parabolic regions.

Conclusion: The spectrum of the predual generator exhibits qualitatively different structures depending on the drift regime, revealing that drift-matrix polynomial eigenvalues capture only part of the trace-class point spectrum in the stable case. The nonperiodic critical drift case remains an open problem.

Abstract: We determine the spectrum of the predual generator of an irreducible Gaussian quantum Markov semigroup on the trace-class operators over a finite-mode bosonic Fock space in the stable, strictly unstable, and periodic critical drift regimes. For stable drift, irreducibility is equivalent to the existence of a unique faithful normal invariant state. The spectrum and approximate point spectrum are the closed left half-plane, whereas the point spectrum is the open left half-plane together with zero. Thus the polynomial eigenvalues generated by the drift matrix do not exhaust the trace-class point spectrum. If the drift has an eigenvalue with strictly positive real part, the spectrum is again the closed left half-plane, but the point spectrum is empty and the open left half-plane together with zero belongs to the residual spectrum. For periodic critical drift, we obtain an explicit spectral formula that includes the displacement parameter and yields horizontal half-lines or parabolic regions. The proofs use characteristic functions, Gaussian diffusion semigroups, and bounded eigenoperators of the dual semigroup. The nonperiodic critical drift case remains open.

</details>


### [68] [Coherence-Mediated Boundary Control of the Liouvillian Gap in Nonreciprocal Open Quantum Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06981)
*K. L. Li,M. H. Yu,X. Z. Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies a single-particle Lindblad lattice combining nonreciprocal incoherent hopping with a tunable coherent boundary link, showing that the boundary link modifies the Liouvillian gap via population-coherence-population feedback without directly altering the diagonal population generator. Exact periodic solutions and finite-size simulations reveal geometry-dependent gap enhancement/suppression and cases where observed relaxation differs from the inverse Liouvillian gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how coherent boundary conditions affect the Liouvillian gap and relaxation dynamics in Lindblad lattices with nonreciprocal incoherent hopping, and to identify when coherence-mediated feedback—rather than direct population dynamics—controls relaxation timescales.

Method: The authors derive an exact fixed-momentum reduction for periodic systems with a scalar secular equation for the population-connected branch, yielding hydrodynamic drift and coherence-corrected diffusion. For finite open lattices, they employ exact diagonalization and sparse near-zero eigensolvers to compute Liouvillian gaps in one and two dimensions.

Result: The coherent boundary link can enhance or suppress the Liouvillian gap in 1D and produce geometry-dependent responses in 2D. The observed relaxation scale can differ from 1/Δ_L when the gap eigenmode has weak visibility in the chosen perturbation and observable. Exact analytical benchmarks from the periodic construction are confirmed by finite-size simulations.

Conclusion: Coherence-mediated population-population feedback is a general control mechanism for Liouvillian relaxation, and measuring relaxation rates requires care since the spectral gap alone may not directly correspond to observable decay when gap modes are weakly visible.

Abstract: We study a single-particle Lindblad lattice in which nonreciprocal incoherent hopping is combined with a tunable coherent boundary link. The link changes the Hamiltonian boundary condition while the incoherent jump rates are kept fixed, so it does not directly alter the diagonal population generator. Its effect on the Liouvillian gap comes from population-coherence-population feedback: the Hamiltonian couples populations to damped coherences, and eliminating those coherences produces a frequency-dependent self-energy for the slow population branch. For the periodic reference system, where translation symmetry is imposed on both the Hamiltonian and the dissipator, we derive an exact fixed-momentum reduction, a scalar secular equation for the population-connected branch, and the associated hydrodynamic drift and coherence-corrected diffusion. For open finite lattices, exact diagonalization and sparse near-zero eigensolvers show that the coherent boundary link can enhance or suppress the gap in one dimension and produce geometry-dependent responses in two dimensions. We also show that an observed relaxation scale can differ from $1/Δ_{\mathcal L}$ when the gap family has weak visibility in the chosen perturbation and observable. The periodic construction gives exact analytical benchmarks, while finite-size simulations in one and two dimensions demonstrate how the same coherence-mediated mechanism controls open-boundary gaps and observable relaxation.

</details>


### [69] [A Single Fixed Shallow Circuit for Classical Shadows of Arbitrary n-Qubit States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07032)
*Yu Wang,Xiuwu Zhu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs a single fixed shallow quantum analyzer circuit that replaces the randomized measurement settings used in classical shadow tomography. Using a fiducial n-qubit register and parallel Bell readout, the analyzer implements a rank-one minimal informationally complete measurement with a single fixed setting instead of the 3^n local-Pauli settings, while maintaining the minimum d^2 outcomes. The authors derive analytic inverses for the Pauli-diagonal frame, analyze variance behavior across different sectors, and characterize resource tradeoffs including circuit depth and auxiliary qubit overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical shadow tomography traditionally relies on randomized measurement settings selected shot-by-shot. While individual settings may be individually shallow, the switching between them imposes control, calibration, and reconfiguration costs that standard cost metrics fail to capture. The authors aim to eliminate this switching overhead by compiling all measurement-setting randomness into a single fixed reusable analyzer circuit.

Method: The authors construct an analyzer circuit combining a freshly prepared n-qubit fiducial register A with parallel Bell readout against the unknown system S. The circuit implements a rank-one minimal informationally complete (MIC) measurement that replaces the 3^n local-Pauli settings. The Pauli-diagonal frame admits an analytic inverse. Analysis covers Haar-averaged conditional variance (dimension-independent coefficient for fixed observables), state-uniform variance (dimension-dependent coefficient), and worst-state Pauli variances for n≥3 (bounded in axial sector, Θ(d) in mixed sector). Resource analysis includes fiducial preparation using n-1 two-qubit gates with logarithmic depth, measurement-assisted preparation offering O(1) adaptive depth with n+1 extra qubits (heralded) or O(n log n) auxiliaries (deterministic).

Result: A single fixed shallow quantum circuit can replace the measurement-setting randomness of classical shadows. The analyzer achieves rank-one minimal informationally complete measurement with one fixed setting, maintains minimum d^2 outcomes, and provides analytic inversion. The variance analysis reveals distinct scaling behavior: dimension-independent coefficients for fixed observables under Haar averaging versus dimension-dependent for state-uniform averaging, with worst-state Pauli variances scaling as Θ(d) in the mixed sector for n≥3. Practical resource costs are modest: logarithmic-depth fiducial preparation with n-1 gates, and O(1) adaptive depth via measurement-assisted heralded preparation with only n+1 extra qubits.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that measurement-setting randomness and associated control complexity, normally distributed across many shots, can be compiled into a fixed reusable analyzer. This shifts overhead from per-shot switching cost to a one-time circuit construction, with favorable depth and qubit tradeoffs. The construction offers a practical pathway for classical shadow tomography that avoids the conventional randomized-settings infrastructure.

Abstract: Classical shadows extract quantum properties from reusable classical records, typically obtained through randomized measurement settings. Although individual settings may be shallow, switching among them introduces control, calibration, and reconfiguration costs beyond conventional metrics. Here we construct, for every $n$, a single fixed shallow quantum analyzer whose Born outcomes replace externally sampled settings as labels for reusable shadow snapshots. The analyzer combines a freshly prepared $n$-qubit fiducial register $A$ with parallel Bell readout of $A$ and the unknown system $S$, producing one $2n$-bit record per copy. The same circuit realizes a rank-one minimal informationally complete measurement: one fixed setting replaces the $3^n$ local-Pauli settings conventionally used for complete reconstruction while retaining the minimum $d^2$ outcomes with $d=2^n$. Its Pauli-diagonal frame admits an analytic inverse. For fixed Hermitian observables, the Haar-averaged conditional variance has a dimension-independent coefficient, whereas the state-uniform coefficient is dimension dependent. For $n\ge3$, worst-state Pauli variances remain bounded in the axial sector and scale as $Θ(d)$ in the mixed sector. Fiducial preparation before system contact uses $n-1$ arbitrary two-qubit gates, no work qubits beyond $A$, and logarithmic depth under all-to-all connectivity. The unknown system undergoes one parallel system-ancilla entangling layer followed by local Hadamards and readout. Measurement-assisted preparation achieves $O(1)$ adaptive quantum depth using $n+1$ extra qubits in the heralded route, or $O(n\log n)$ auxiliaries for deterministic completion. These tradeoffs show that part of the measurement-setting randomness and control complexity normally supplied shot by shot can instead be compiled into a fixed reusable analyzer.

</details>


### [70] [Capability-Gated Conformance Testing of Quantum Error-Correction Decoder Libraries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07035)
*Jiachen Shen,Hui Zhong*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes an oracle-free conformance testing framework for quantum error correction decoders, validating that returned corrections explain syndromes in the caller's index space and checking minimum-weight feasibility under different bookkeeping presentations. Applied to five public libraries and nine configurations, it finds that 0% of bounded-distance correctness declarations are directly verifiable (though hypotheses hold 62.1% of the time), documentation covers only 4 of 54 capability questions, and presentation changes cause meaningful weight shifts (up to 26% heavier corrections) while traditional error-rate metrics miss parity-preserving corruptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum error correction decoders are judged almost exclusively by logical error rate, but this dominant metric misses important failure modes such as corruptions preserving logical parity and presentation-sensitive behavior. Since decoders serve as libraries upon which other results depend, the authors seek additional, oracle-free conformance checks that verify correctness properties callers actually rely on, without requiring access to ground-truth error simulations.

Method: The paper introduces a conformance contract requiring no oracle. First, it checks that a decoder's returned correction explains the observed syndrome within the caller's index space. Second, it presents a decoder one instance under two presentations that differ only in bookkeeping (numbering/labeling); when two feasible corrections of different weights emerge, the heavier one is provably not minimum-weight, exposing violations of the decoder's published guarantee. Verdicts are gated on what each library itself declares, and bundled certificates are re-derivable by a standalone verifier from first principles.

Result: Across nine configurations from five public libraries: documentation answered only 4 of 54 capability questions; bounded-distance correctness, the property callers most depend on, yielded a direct declaration rate of 0.0% despite hypotheses holding in 62.1% of cases; presentation sensitivity is real but shallow—one solver produced a 26% heavier correction under a different numbering, reaching the logical class at most once in 20,000 shots. Traditional evaluation missed corruptions preserving logical parity, while the proposed weight-summation check caught every injected corruption. All 639 certificates shipped as verifier-re-derivable bundles.

Conclusion: Established error-rate-based evaluation is insufficient for validating quantum error correction decoders as dependable libraries. Oracle-free conformance checks—syndrome explanation in caller index space and minimum-weight verification across bookkeeping presentations—reveal gaps that standard metrics hide, including a universal absence of declared bounded-distance guarantees and measurable presentation sensitivity. The authors advocate for richer conformance testing alongside (or in place of) reliance on logical error rate alone, with independently verifiable certificate bundles.

Abstract: A quantum error correction decoder is a library other people's results depend on, judged in one dominant way. Sample errors, decode, and count wrong logical observables. We ask what else can be checked there. Our conformance contract needs no oracle. One check asks that a returned correction explain the syndrome in the caller's index space. The other hands a decoder one instance under two presentations differing only in bookkeeping, where two feasible corrections of different weight prove the heavier is not minimum-weight. Verdicts are gated on what each library declares, so a firing contradicts a published guarantee. Nine configurations from five public libraries give three results. Documentation answers 4 of 54 capability questions. Bounded-distance correctness, the property callers most depend on, has a direct declaration yield of 0.0%, though its hypotheses hold in 62.1% of cases. Presentation sensitivity is real but shallow. One solver moved to a 26% heavier correction under a different numbering, which reaches the logical class at most once in twenty thousand shots. Established evaluation misses corruptions that preserve logical parity, while one summation over the caller's weights catches every one we injected. All 639 certificates ship as bundles a standalone verifier re-derives from first principles.

</details>


### [71] [2-Fold Forrelation is in QAC$^0$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07060)
*Francisca Vasconcelos*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper shows that 2-fold Forrelation with an inverse-polylogarithmic promise gap can be solved in bounded error by polynomial-size QAC⁰ circuits, where the input is provided explicitly (not via an oracle), matching the input format for which Forrelation is hard against AC⁰. At constant gap, this yields a natural promise-problem separation between QAC⁰ and AC⁰.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a natural promise-problem separation between quantum constant-depth circuits (QAC⁰) and classical constant-depth circuits (AC⁰), by demonstrating that Forrelation—known to be hard for AC⁰—can be solved efficiently by QAC⁰ when the input is given explicitly rather than through an oracle.

Method: The authors construct polynomial-size QAC⁰ circuits that solve 2-fold Forrelation with bounded error, relaxing the promise gap to inverse-polylogarithmic (rather than requiring a larger gap). The circuits consume the input explicitly in the standard bit-string form used in AC⁰ hardness results, rather than relying on oracle access.

Result: Polynomial-size QAC⁰ circuits can solve 2-fold Forrelation under an inverse-polylogarithmic promise gap. When the gap is held constant, this gives a natural promise-problem separation between QAC⁰ and AC⁰.

Conclusion: Quantum constant-depth circuits provably outperform classical constant-depth circuits on a natural promise problem (Forrelation) when the input is provided explicitly, strengthening the evidence for a QAC⁰ vs. AC⁰ separation.

Abstract: We show that 2-fold Forrelation with inverse-polylogarithmic promise gap can be solved, with bounded error, by polynomial-size QAC$^0$ circuits.
