Unlike the standard oracle-based Forrelation algorithm, our circuits receive the input explicitly, in the same form as the AC$^0$ circuits against which Forrelation is known to be hard. At constant gap, this yields a natural promise-problem separation between QAC$^0$ and AC$^0$.

</details>


### [72] [Single-tone drive-enhanced CROT gate for bosonic quantum error correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07076)
*Shushen Qin,Long D. H. My,Adrian Copetudo,Amon M. Kasper,Yvonne Y. Gao,Hui Khoon Ng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a direct implementation of the two-mode controlled-rotation (CROT) gate in a circuit-QED architecture by driving a transmon simultaneously coupled to two microwave cavities, enabling code-agnostic, error-propagation-friendly bosonic error correction across rotation-symmetric bosonic codes without composite primitive sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CROT gates are essential for error correction of rotation-symmetric bosonic (RSB) codes such as cat codes, but existing compositional approaches built from imperfect primitive bosonic operations lose the gate's desirable code-agnostic generality and favorable error-propagation properties. Direct implementations to date rely on code-specific features, motivating a general, code-agnostic direct implementation.

Method: The authors engineer the CROT gate in circuit QED by driving a transmon that is simultaneously coupled to two microwave cavities. A single drive frequency controllably enhances effective nonlinearities between the cavities, producing the required two-mode interaction. They derive simple analytical formulas to identify working regimes, refine them with exact numerical simulation, and validate the approach in an error-correction demonstration involving multiple RSB codes within the same circuit.

Result: A single-drive-frequency scheme achieves a sufficient on-off ratio for implementing the CROT gate while minimizing mode distortions. Analytical formulas identify viable operating regimes, confirmed and refined by exact numerics, and the gate succeeds in an error-correction example spanning different RSB codes on the same circuit.

Conclusion: The proposed direct CROT gate preserves code-agnostic generality and beneficial error-propagation characteristics at the hardware level, adding a general two-mode gate to the toolkit for bosonic quantum information processing and error correction.

Abstract: Bosonic error correction provides a hardware-efficient route to protected qubits for accurate quantum information processing. A critical component for error correction of rotation-symmetric bosonic (RSB) codes, like the well-known cat codes, is the two-mode controlled-rotation (CROT) operation. The CROT gate is useful because of its code-agnostic generality and its error-propagation properties. While the CROT gate can in principle be composed from existing bosonic physical primitives, the desirable properties are typically lost when composed as a sequence of imperfect primitive operations. Past work has focused on direct implementations that rely on features of specific codes. Here, we present a direct route to CROT within the circuit-QED architecture that retains its code-agnostic and error-propagation characteristics. By driving a transmon simultaneously coupled to two microwave cavities, we controllably enhance the effective nonlinearities between the microwave cavities and thus engineer the necessary two-mode interaction underlying the CROT gate. Using only a single drive frequency, we can achieve an on-off ratio sufficient for gate implementation, while minimizing mode distortions. We provide simple analytical formulas that permit the identification of potential working regimes, further refined by exact numerics, and illustrate the efficacy of our CROT approach in an error-correction example involving different RSB codes within the same circuit. Our CROT gate adds to the arsenal of direct two-mode gates available for general bosonic information processing.

</details>


### [73] [Ancilla-assisted nondestructive discrimination of distributed GHZ-class states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07084)
*Abdul Q. Batin,Suman Chand,Rajiuddin Sk,Prasanta K. Panigrahi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a scalable ancilla-assisted protocol for strict nondestructive discrimination of n-qubit GHZ-class states distributed among distant parties, using multipartite GHZ ancillae and local unitary operations to map computational-pattern and relative-phase information onto ancillary registers while preserving the system state. The protocol is derived for three- and four-qubit cases, extended to arbitrary n, analyzed under depolarizing and amplitude-damping noise (yielding analytical success probabilities and critical noise thresholds), and experimentally validated via IBM Qiskit. The work also suggests a connection to quantum decoherence sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nondestructive quantum state discrimination is a fundamental primitive in distributed quantum information processing, where shared multipartite entangled resources—such as GHZ-class states—must be identified without being consumed. No scalable, strict nondestructive discrimination protocol for such states had been established, motivating the development of a systematic ancilla-assisted approach.

Method: The protocol employs multipartite GHZ ancillary states and local unitary interactions to coherently map the computational-pattern and relative-phase information of the system's GHZ state onto two ancillary registers while leaving the system state invariant. Explicit discrimination rules are derived for three- and four-qubit GHZ-class states, with a systematic generalization to arbitrary n. The method is further analyzed under depolarizing and amplitude-damping noise in the ancillary system, yielding analytical expressions for success probability and critical noise thresholds, and is implemented on IBM Qiskit for experimental validation.

Result: The authors derive explicit discrimination rules for three- and four-qubit GHZ-class states, extend the protocol to arbitrary n, obtain analytical expressions for success probability and critical noise thresholds under depolarizing and amplitude-damping noise, and demonstrate experimental feasibility via IBM Qiskit with matching ancillary measurement signatures.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a scalable approach to strict nondestructive discrimination of multipartite GHZ-class states and suggests a broader interpretation of ancillary GHZ resources as decoherence probes, potentially opening new connections to quantum decoherence sensing in distributed quantum systems.

Abstract: Nondestructive quantum state discrimination is a fundamental primitive in distributed quantum information processing, where shared multipartite entangled resources need to be identified without being consumed. In this work, we present a scalable ancilla-assisted protocol for the strict nondestructive discrimination of $n$-qubit GHZ-class states distributed among distant parties. By employing multipartite GHZ ancillary states and local unitary interactions, we show that the computational-pattern and relative-phase information of the system GHZ state can be coherently mapped onto two ancillary registers while leaving the system state unchanged. We explicitly derive the discrimination rules for three- and four-qubit GHZ-class states and develop a systematic extension to arbitrary $n$. We further investigate the discrimination protocol in the presence of depolarizing and amplitude-damping noise in the ancillary system and obtain analytical expressions for the success probability and the corresponding critical noise thresholds. The protocol is also implemented using the IBM Qiskit platform to demonstrate its experimental feasibility and reproduce the expected ancillary measurement signatures. The proposed framework provides a scalable approach to the strict nondestructive discrimination of multipartite GHZ-class states and suggests a broader interpretation of the ancillary GHZ resources as probes of decoherence, opening a possible connection to quantum decoherence sensing in distributed quantum systems.

</details>


### [74] [QCxSimulation: Scatter-Aware X-Ray Projection Radiography via Discrete-Time Quantum Walks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07089)
*Anja Heim,Theobald Fuchs,Thomas Lang,Dimitri Prjamkov,Kilian Dremel,Stefan Kasperl,Christoph Heinzl*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a discrete-time quantum walk algorithm for simulating X-ray photon transport through heterogeneous volumes, encoding all admissible photon scattering paths into a single quantum state to enable simultaneous propagation of scattering histories via superposition. The approach models photoelectric absorption, Compton and Rayleigh scattering, and enables flexible readout of imaging modalities (primary or specified scattering orders). Quantitative comparisons with classical reference simulations show accurate reproduction of radiographic projections, suggesting potential quantum advantage over classical Monte Carlo methods as quantum hardware scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical photon transport simulations incorporating realistic X-ray scattering physics are computationally expensive due to the large number of distinct scattering paths that must be sampled, which limits practical exploration of parameter spaces such as beam energy. Quantum computing offers a potential speedup by leveraging superposition to handle high-dimensional problems more efficiently.

Method: The authors develop a discrete-time quantum walk algorithm that simulates X-ray photon transport through heterogeneous volumes. The quantum walk encodes all admissible photon paths into a single quantum state, propagating all scattering histories simultaneously via the superposition principle. The physical model includes photoelectric absorption, and higher-order Compton and Rayleigh scattering. The quantum state representation supports flexible readout of different imaging modalities, such as primary (unscattered) images or images containing only specified scattering orders.

Result: Quantitative comparison with classically computed reference simulations demonstrates that the proposed quantum walk accurately reproduces radiographic projections within the limitations of the underlying physical model. The algorithm successfully produces accurate radiographic images including primary and scatter-specific modalities.

Conclusion: Quantum circuits for X-ray transport can produce accurate radiographic images. As quantum hardware scales up, these algorithms could outperform classical Monte Carlo-based approaches in large-scale, scatter-aware virtual imaging studies.

Abstract: X-ray projection radiography is a non-invasive imaging technique used in medical diagnostics and industrial inspection. The simulation of X-ray projections is commonly used to optimise acquisition protocols and improve image quality before performing costly scans. Classical photon transport simulations that include realistic X-ray scattering physics are computationally expensive because they require the sampling of a large number of distinct scattering paths. This limits the practical exploration of parameter spaces such as beam energy. Quantum computing offers the potential to solve high-dimensional problems faster by making use of quantum properties such as superposition. This work introduces a discrete-time quantum walk algorithm that simulates the transport of X-ray photons through heterogeneous volumes. It approximates the physics of X-ray projection radiography, including processes such as photoelectric absorption and higher-order scattering, including Compton and Rayleigh scattering. The quantum walk encodes all admissible photon paths into a single quantum state, enabling all scattering histories to be propagated simultaneously via the superposition principle. This quantum state representation enables flexible readout of various imaging modalities, including the primary, i.e., unscattered, image, or images exclusively containing Rayleigh and Compton scattering of specified orders. A quantitative comparison with classically computed reference simulations shows that the proposed quantum walk accurately reproduces radiographic projections, given the limitations of the underlying physical model. These results indicate that quantum circuits for X-ray transport can produce accurate radiographic images and imply that, as quantum hardware scales up, these algorithms could outperform classical Monte Carlo-based approaches in large-scale, scatter-aware virtual imaging studies.

</details>


### [75] [Complexity Amplification from Compression in Quantum Random Access Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07090)
*Stuart Hadfield*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes worst-case computational complexity results for Quantum Random Access Optimization (QRAO), showing that compressed quantum encodings mapping classical binary variables to Pauli observables (X, Y, Z) on fewer qubits can amplify hardness. The authors prove QRAO energy decision problems are NP-complete, StoqMA-complete, or QMA-complete depending on the number of aligned Pauli axes used, with inverse-polynomial promise gaps preserved via reductions that avoid gadgets or ancillas. These hardness results hold even for the practical Qiskit Optimization 0.7.0 compiler implementation, confirming they are not artifacts of contrived packing rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum encodings that compress classical optimization problems into fewer qubits via Pauli correlation encodings are promising for tackling large-scale problems on current hardware, but their worst-case computational complexity was not well understood. The authors aim to characterize the hardness of energy decision problems arising from QRAO/PCE frameworks, identifying fundamental complexity barriers distinct from classical semidefinite programming relaxations.

Method: The authors formalize QRAO energy promise problems based on the Pauli correlation encoding (PCE) framework, which assigns up to three binary variables to X, Y, Z observables per qubit. They construct polynomial-time reductions from canonical complete problems for NP, StoqMA, and QMA, showing that weighted MaxCut instances compress (up to known shift and rescaling) to arbitrary nonnegative-weight pairwise Pauli couplings for any prescribed packing. They analyze complexity based on the number of aligned Pauli axes (one, two, or three), including bipartite restrictions, and verify their results against the actual Qiskit Optimization 0.7.0 compiler implementation.

Result: They prove: (1) QRAO with one aligned Pauli axis yields an NP-complete energy decision problem; (2) QRAO with two or three positive aligned Pauli axes yields QMA-complete problems in general, with bipartite restrictions lying in StoqMA; (3) reductions preserve inverse-polynomial promise gaps without requiring gadgets or ancillas; (4) these hardness results persist in the practical Qiskit compiler implementation, demonstrating practical relevance rather than artificial artifacts.

Conclusion: Quantum compression via Pauli correlation encodings introduces worst-case complexity barriers (NP-complete, StoqMA-complete, and QMA-complete energy problems) that depend on the packing choices and number of aligned axes. While this underscores significant hardness for worst-case instances, the authors deliberately avoid broad claims about typical cases or the trainability/performance of algorithm pipelines that use QRAO.

Abstract: Compressed quantum encodings aim to overcome hardware limitations towards tackling challenging problems at scale, with many classical variables mapped onto noncommuting observables of fewer qubits. Classically, relaxations such as the semidefinite program formulation of MaxCut trade solution quality for computational efficiency. By contrast, quantum relaxations based on compression can amplify the worst-case complexity of the problem being solved. We study quantum random access optimization (QRAO), a special case of the Pauli correlation encoding (PCE) framework that assigns up to three binary variables to the Pauli $X$, $Y$, and $Z$ observables of each qubit, with the packing choices determining the compressed Hamiltonian to be optimized. We identify explicit QRAO optimal energy promise problems complete for NP, StoqMA, and QMA, with inverse-polynomial promise gaps for the latter two. Our problem reductions preserve inverse-polynomial promise gaps without requiring gadgets or ancillas. For any prescribed packing, we show that weighted MaxCut instances compress, up to a known shift and rescaling, to arbitrary nonnegative-weight pairwise Pauli couplings allowed by the packing. For QRAO, using one aligned axis gives an NP-complete energy problem. Using two or three positive aligned Pauli axes generally gives QMA-complete problems, while their bipartite restrictions lie in StoqMA.
  We show that this computational hardness survives compilation and is practically relevant. Notably, this result applies directly to the current QRAO compiler implementation in Qiskit Optimization 0.7.0, confirming our hardness results are not artifacts of artificial or contrived packing rules. Altogether our results identify worst-case complexity barriers arising from quantum compression, while making no broad claims about typical cases or the performance and trainability of algorithm pipelines that use it.

</details>


### [76] [From Quantum Cryptography to Intuitionism and beyond: relativity, many-worlds and non-locality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07127)
*Nicolas Gisin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper is a tribute to Gilles Brassard on his 70th birthday, presenting two contributions: a new idea about creating information at a limited rate, and a development of the concept that "indeterminacy is relative," which sheds light on how relativity and non-locality can be compatible.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The author celebrates interactions with Gilles Brassard and offers new conceptual insights into fundamental physics questions, particularly the tension between relativity and quantum non-locality.

Method: The paper combines personal recollections with conceptual/theoretical development of two ideas: (1) bounded-rate creation of new information, and (2) the relativity of indeterminacy to reconcile relativistic principles with quantum non-locality.

Result: Presents a novel framework suggesting that indeterminacy (uncertainty about which outcomes are determined) is observer-dependent, which helps resolve the apparent conflict between relativity and non-locality.

Conclusion: The author extends prior work suggesting that relativity and quantum non-locality need not be contradictory when indeterminacy itself is understood as relative, alongside offering a new perspective on information creation rates.

Abstract: After a recollection of some of my encounters with Gilles Brassard, on the occasion of his 70th birthday I present, first, a new idea on creation of new information at a limited rate. Next, I develop the idea that "indeterminacy is relative" [Entropy {23} 1326 (2021)] and how this illuminates the compatibility between relativity and non-locality.

</details>


### [77] [Electron beam driven collective self hybridized exciton polaritons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07164)
*Parsa Darman,Maximilian Black,Prabhdeep Singh,Masoud Taleb,Victor DeManuel-Gonzalez,Sara Darbari,Fatemeh Chahshouri,Nahid Talebi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates self-hybridized exciton polaritons in Ruddlesden–Popper perovskite flakes using both photoluminescence and cathodoluminescence spectroscopy. The authors discover that electron-beam excitation yields stronger polaritonic level repulsion than optical excitation, attributed to collective coherent excitation of multiple excitons (up to N≈23) coupled to the same photonic mode with g_N=g_1√N scaling. Reducing electron kinetic energy reveals a crossover to a partially incoherent regime where higher-order polariton branches vanish, showing that electron beams can actively control collective light–matter coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While self-hybridized exciton polaritons in layered semiconductors offer a versatile platform for nanoscale light–matter control, the nature of exciton–photon interactions and the collective/synchronous strong coupling of multiple excitons to the same photonic mode remain largely unexplored—particularly under different excitation schemes.

Method: Complementary momentum-resolved photoluminescence and cathodoluminescence spectroscopy are used to compare self-hybridized exciton polaritons in Ruddlesden–Popper perovskite flakes under optical versus electron-beam excitation, with varying electron kinetic energies to probe excitation regimes.

Result: Cathodoluminescence shows significantly larger polaritonic level repulsion than photoluminescence, indicating enhanced effective coupling under electron-beam excitation via collective coherent excitation of up to N≈23 excitonic oscillators following g_N=g_1√N scaling. Reducing electron kinetic energy transitions the system into a partially incoherent regime where higher-order Fabry–Pérot polariton resonances disappear while lowest polariton branches and coherent transition radiation persist.

Conclusion: Electron beams can actively modify collective light–matter coupling in layered semiconductors, and cathodoluminescence provides access to excitation regimes (e.g., collective strong coupling of multiple excitons) that cannot be reached by all-optical excitation schemes.

Abstract: Self-hybridized exciton polaritons in layered semiconductors emerge from the hybridization of excitonic resonances with confined photonic modes in films and provide a versatile platform for controlling light--matter interactions at the nanoscale. Yet, the nature of exciton--photon interactions and the collective and synchronous strong coupling of multiple excitons to the same photonic mode remain largely unexplored. Here, we use complementary momentum-resolved photoluminescence and cathodoluminescence spectroscopy to compare self-hybridized exciton polaritons in Ruddlesden--Popper perovskite flakes under optical and electron-beam excitation. We find that cathodoluminescence exhibits remarkably larger polaritonic level repulsion than photoluminescence, revealing an enhanced effective coupling strength under electron-beam excitation. We attribute this enhancement to the collective coherent excitation of multiple excitons by fast electrons, which couple to the same photonic mode and increase the interaction strength according to a $g_N=g_1\sqrt{N}$ scaling, with an effective number of up to $N\approx23$ coherently coupled excitonic oscillators. By reducing the electron kinetic energy, we further uncover a crossover to a partially incoherent excitation regime in which higher-order Fabry--Pérot exciton-polariton resonances disappear, leaving only the lowest polariton branches while coherent transition radiation persists. This transition arises because low-energy electrons excite excitons over extended spatial volumes, washing out the phase coherence required to sustain collective coupling and form polaritonic resonances. Our work shows that electron beams can actively modify collective light--matter coupling in layered semiconductors and establishes cathodoluminescence as a route to access excitation regimes beyond all-optical excitation schemes.

</details>


### [78] [Remote Flux Refocuses Nonadiabatic Excursions in Compact-State Quantum Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07198)
*X. S. Meng,Y. S. Liu,X. Z. Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Compact states in propagating bands can carry quantum information but suffer from finite-time transfer populating unwanted modes. A remote flux can refocus the leaked amplitude without altering the transfer states or their coupling, enabling near-complete logical transfer in resonator rings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite-time quantum state transfer using compact states can populate modes outside their subspace, degrading fidelity. The paper seeks a mechanism to correct this leakage without modifying the transfer states or the intrinsic coupling that moves amplitude out of the compact-state subspace.

Method: The authors introduce a remote flux applied to a bond whose endpoints are nodes of every compact transfer state in a resonator ring, making the separation between the desired transfer subspace and the flux-induced refocusing exact. They analyze interference between matrix amplitudes from different winding paths that suppresses endpoint error, and compare against numerical optimization under matched control bounds.

Result: Zero flux and refocusing flux both produce substantial excursions during identical pulses, but only the refocusing flux yields near-complete logical transfer. The refocusing flux also achieves higher worst-input fidelity under matched control bounds than the optimized zero-flux case.

Conclusion: Applying a remote flux provides an accurate route to quantum information transfer through coherent return of leaked amplitude, without changing the compact transfer states or their out-of-subspace motion-induced coupling.

Abstract: Compact states embedded in a propagating band can carry quantum information, but finite-time transfer can populate modes outside their subspace. We show that a remote flux can refocus this amplitude without changing the transfer states or the motion-induced coupling out of their subspace. In a resonator ring, this separation is exact because both endpoints of the flux-bearing bond are nodes of every compact transfer state. For the same pulse, zero and refocusing flux both produce substantial excursions, but only the latter yields near-complete logical transfer. Interference between matrix amplitudes associated with different windings suppresses the endpoint error. Under matched control bounds, numerical optimization yields higher worst-input fidelity at the refocusing flux, providing a route to accurate transfer through coherent return.

</details>


### [79] [From NISQ to Fault-Tolerance: Applications and Algorithmic Benchmarks for Spin Qubits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07210)
*Frederik Lohof,Florian Ginzel,Wolfgang Lechner*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper provides a comprehensive roadmap for implementing quantum algorithms on exchange-only (EO) spin qubit platforms, covering applications from NISQ to fault-tolerant eras. It demonstrates that the Parity Twine compilation method aligns well with EO hardware for tasks like quantum Fourier transforms and QAOA, introduces an error detection technique native to Parity Twine that EO qubits can exploit, and provides detailed resource estimates for digital quantum simulation (fermionic fast Fourier transform and Fermi-Hubbard models), including in a partially fault-tolerant context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With recent advances in spin-based quantum processing units using exchange-only qubits, there is a need for an algorithm-driven perspective to guide hardware scaling decisions, identify bottlenecks, and chart a practical path from near-term (NISQ) to long-term (fault-tolerant) quantum computing on this platform.

Method: The authors analyze a range of quantum applications mapped to the EO platform across different hardware maturity stages. They evaluate the Parity Twine compilation method for tasks such as the quantum Fourier transform and QAOA, describe an error detection technique native to Parity Twine that is uniquely advantageous for EO qubits, and perform detailed resource estimation for digital quantum simulation, including the fermionic fast Fourier transform and Fermi-Hubbard model under quantum error correction with partial fault tolerance.

Result: The Parity Twine compilation method is shown to complement EO hardware capabilities effectively for key algorithms. An error detection scheme native to Parity Twine provides unique performance advantages for EO qubits. Detailed resource estimates are provided for digital quantum simulation benchmarks, identifying scaling bottlenecks at each level of hardware maturity.

Conclusion: The work offers a quantitative, algorithm-driven perspective on EO-based quantum computing, providing guidance and resource requirements that will inform future hardware design choices for scaling from NISQ to fault-tolerant operation.

Abstract: Motivated by recent breakthroughs in the development of spin-based quantum processing units based on exchange-only (EO) spin qubits, we provide a roadmap for the implementation of quantum algorithms on the EO platform, ranging from the NISQ to the fault-tolerant era. To provide an algorithm-driven perspective on the scaling of quantum chips, we consider a range of applications targeting different stages of hardware maturity and formulate requirements for a successful realization. We show that the compilation method Parity Twine perfectly complements the hardware's capabilities to perform tasks such as the quantum Fourier transform or QAOA. Furthermore, we describe an error detection technique native to Parity Twine, which EO qubits can leverage in a unique and advantageous way to improve algorithm performance. Finally, since both near-term algorithmic benchmarks and a long-term perspective can be found in digital quantum simulation, we specifically discuss the fermionic fast Fourier transform and the simulation of Fermi-Hubbard models. The latter is explicitly discussed in the context of quantum error correction and a partially fault-tolerant realization. By providing detailed resource estimates and identifying scaling bottlenecks on each level, our work offers a quantitative perspective on EO-based quantum computing and will inform future hardware design choices.

</details>


### [80] [Power and Limits of Collective Local Measurements in Multicopy State Discrimination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07233)
*Mao-Sheng Li,Yan-Ling Wang,Zhu-Jun Zheng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper resolves an open question posed by Bennett et al. (1999) about whether perfect local discrimination of orthogonal quantum states can require more than two copies, in the stronger setting where each laboratory can collectively process its local copies. For every odd-prime local dimension, maximal-stabilizer eigenbases have diverging copy complexity under individual-copy separable measurements but can be perfectly decoded with at most three copies via one-round collective LOCC. However, collective processing does not remove multicopy hardness in general: for every local dimension d≥2, there exist complete bases within an explicit phase family whose copy complexity remains unbounded even under collective separable measurements, with lower bound scaling as the square root of the number of subsystems. This establishes sample number, spatial measurement power, and coherent access across repeated local inputs as distinct resources in distributed quantum measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work addresses a two-decade-old open question from Bennett et al. (1999) concerning the minimum number of copies required for perfect local discrimination of orthogonal quantum states. While prior work resolved this question for adaptive protocols processing copies separately, the setting where each laboratory collectively processes local copies remained open. This stronger setting is conceptually important because collective processing could in principle reduce copy complexity, and understanding whether it does reveals fundamental distinctions between quantum information resources.

Method: The authors analyze copy complexity of complete maximal-stabilizer eigenbases and phase-family bases in odd-prime local dimensions. They compare two operational settings: individual-copy separable measurements versus collective LOCC (one-round) and collective separable measurements. They establish divergence of copy complexity under individual-copy processing, construct explicit one-round collective LOCC decoding protocols using at most three copies for maximal-stabilizer eigenbases, and prove lower bounds (scaling as square root of number of subsystems) on copy complexity for an explicit phase family under collective separable measurements.

Result: (1) For every fixed odd-prime local dimension, maximal-stabilizer eigenbases have copy complexity that diverges with system size under individual-copy separable measurements. (2) Every maximal-stabilizer eigenbasis in odd-prime local dimension can be perfectly decoded by one-round collective LOCC using at most three copies—showing a sharp behavioral change. (3) For every fixed local dimension d≥2, there exist complete bases within an explicit phase family whose copy complexity remains unbounded even under collective separable measurements, with a square-root (in number of subsystems) lower-bound scale.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that sample number (number of copies), spatial measurement power (individual vs. collective separable measurements), and coherent access across repeated local inputs (collective processing at each laboratory) are three distinct resources in distributed quantum measurement. Collective access across repeated local inputs resolves the original question affirmatively for certain bases (making them easy), but does not eliminate multicopy hardness in general, as other basis families retain unbounded copy complexity even with collective processing.

Abstract: More than two decades ago, Bennett \emph{et al.} [Phys. Rev. A \textbf{59}, 1070 (1999)] asked whether perfect local discrimination of orthogonal quantum states can require more than two copies. This question was subsequently answered for adaptive protocols that process the copies separately [Phys. Rev. Lett. \textbf{126}, 210505 (2021)], but remained open when each laboratory is allowed to process its local copies collectively. Here we resolve this stronger setting and identify collective access across repeated local inputs as a distinct resource. For every fixed odd-prime local dimension, there exist complete maximal-stabilizer eigenbases whose copy complexity under individual-copy separable measurements diverges with system size. Under collective processing this behavior changes sharply: every maximal-stabilizer eigenbasis in odd-prime local dimension is perfectly decoded by one-round collective LOCC using at most three copies. Collective processing, however, does not remove multicopy hardness in general. For every fixed local dimension $d\ge2$, we prove the existence, within an explicit phase family, of complete bases whose copy complexity remains unbounded even under collective separable measurements, with a square root of the number of subsystems as lower-bound scale. Thus sample number, spatial measurement power, and coherent access across repeated local inputs are distinct resources in distributed quantum measurement.

</details>


### [81] [Asymmetric quantum error correction efficiently tackles application-specific noise effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07235)
*Abhishek Yadav,Peter K. Schuhmacher,Michael Epping*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies how bit-flip and phase-flip noise asymmetrically affect the quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) applied to the flight-gate assignment problem, and shows that asymmetric quantum error-correcting codes (specifically the quantum parity code) can efficiently mitigate noise even when the physical noise model is symmetric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to understand how different types of noise (bit-flip vs. phase-flip) impact quantum optimization algorithms differently, and to leverage this asymmetry for more efficient error correction.

Method: The authors simulate QAOA on a small flight-gate assignment instance under both layer-level and gate-level noise models, using two circuit decompositions (CNOT-based and native-R_ZZ). They compare performance degradation from bit-flip vs. phase-flip errors, analyze the underlying mechanisms (mixing, measurements, error propagation), and then demonstrate asymmetric error correction using the quantum parity code (QPC), a generalization of the 9-qubit Shor code.

Result: Bit-flip noise causes larger performance degradation than phase-flip noise in QAOA, with the asymmetry most pronounced in layer-level and native-R_ZZ models. A smaller asymmetric QPC achieves nearly the same error correction improvement as a larger symmetric code.

Conclusion: Error-correction resources should be allocated based not only on physical error rates but also on how strongly each error channel affects the application. Asymmetric quantum error correction remains beneficial even when the noise model itself is symmetric.

Abstract: Noise is a major challenge for current quantum computers. It can be broadly categorized into bit-flip and phase-flip errors. These two types do not necessarily affect the executed algorithm, thus also the application, in the same way. We illustrate this general effect for the example of the quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) applied to a small instance of the flight-gate assignment (FGA) problem. We compare bit-flip and phase-flip Pauli noise under both layer-level and gate-level noise models, using two circuit decompositions of the same ideal QAOA unitary: a CNOT-based decomposition and a native-$R_{ZZ}$ decomposition. In the simulations, bit-flip noise produces the larger degradation in the performance of the quantum optimization. The asymmetry is most visible in the layer-level and native-$R_{ZZ}$ simulations. We explain this by how the errors affect mixing, final measurements, and how they propagate inside the circuit. We then exploit these insights to tackle noise particularly efficiently using asymmetric error-correcting codes. As an illustration, we use the quantum parity code (QPC), a generalization of the 9-qubit Shor code, and show that a smaller asymmetric code can achieve nearly the same improvement as a larger symmetric choice. This demonstrates that error-correction resources should be assigned not only according to physical error rates, but also according to how strongly each error channel affects the application. As a result, asymmetric quantum error correction proves useful even in cases where the noise model is symmetric. Finally, we discuss how information about the noise obtained through calibration can be exploited in our approach.

</details>


### [82] [Hudson's theorem fails for the SU(1,1) discrete series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07242)
*Chon-Fai Kam*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that Hudson's theorem—which equates Wigner positivity with Gaussianity for pure bosonic states—has no analogue on curved phase space. For the positive discrete series of SU(1,1) realized on a two-sheeted hyperboloid, the set of Wigner-positive pure states strictly exceeds the Perelomov coherent orbit. Superpositions of the lowest-weight state with the first excited state remain positive up to a 24.93° mixing angle at Bargmann index k=1, and the admissible region has positive volume. The quadratic form governing positivity degenerates in the far field to a single mixing angle bounded by arctan(1/√(2k)), while the threshold itself remains fixed at intermediate hyperbolic distance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to determine whether Hudson's theorem, a foundational result connecting Wigner negativity to non-classicality for Gaussian (flat) phase space, extends to curved phase spaces. This matters because quantum systems defined on curved manifolds (e.g., SU(1,1) coherent states on hyperboloids) underlie many quantum optics and quantum information settings, and the validity of using Wigner negativity as a non-classicality witness hinges on whether the Gaussian/coherent-state correspondence persists in such geometries.

Method: The authors construct Wigner functions on the upper sheet of a two-sheeted hyperboloid for the positive discrete series of SU(1,1). They examine pure-state superpositions (specifically two-state superpositions of the lowest-weight and first-excited states) and analyze the positivity threshold of the Wigner function as a function of mixing angle and Bargmann index k. Analytic analysis of the quadratic form controlling Wigner positivity is performed, including its far-field (large hyperbolic distance) degeneracy, combined with numerical exploration of the intermediate-distance regime.

Result: Wigner-positive pure states on the curved phase space form a strictly larger set than the SU(1,1) Perelomov coherent orbits, contradicting any naive curved-space analogue of Hudson's theorem. Specifically, two-state superpositions remain Wigner-positive up to a mixing angle of 24.93° at k=1, and the admissible set has positive volume. The far-field quadratic form degenerates to a single mixing angle bounded by arctan(1/√(2k)), but the actual threshold is set at intermediate hyperbolic distances rather than in the asymptotic regime.

Conclusion: Hudson's theorem fails for the positive discrete series of SU(1,1) on curved phase space: Wigner positivity is a strictly weaker condition than being a coherent (Perelomov) state. The boundary of the Wigner-positive set is determined at intermediate hyperbolic distances, not in the far-field limit, where the controlling quadratic form degenerates. This has implications for interpreting Wigner negativity as a non-classicality signature in curved-space quantum systems.

Abstract: Hudson's theorem states that a pure state of a bosonic mode has a non-negative Wigner function if and only if it is Gaussian. It underwrites the reading of Wigner negativity as a faithful signature of pure-state non-classicality. We show the statement has no analogue on curved phase space. For the positive discrete series of $SU(1,1)$, realised on the upper sheet of a two-sheeted hyperboloid, the Wigner-positive pure states form a strictly larger set than the Perelomov coherent orbit. Superpositions of the lowest weight state with the first excited state stay positive up to a mixing angle of $24.93^\circ$ at Bargmann index $k=1$, and the admissible set has positive volume, with a maximal width that is not attained in the two-state direction. We show analytically that the quadratic form controlling positivity degenerates in the far field onto a single mixing angle. That degeneracy bounds the window by $\arctan(1/\sqrt{2k})$ and leaves the threshold itself fixed at intermediate hyperbolic distance.

</details>


### [83] [Qubit-Based Benchmarking of InP HEMT LNAs: Readout Fidelity Versus Power Consumption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07250)
*Junjie Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a qubit-in-the-loop benchmarking method that uses the 2D IQ histogram of single-shot superconducting qubit readout as a direct figure of merit to evaluate cryogenic InP HEMT low-noise amplifiers, replacing conventional Y-factor noise temperature measurements that are decoupled from actual quantum measurement performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-fidelity single-shot readout of superconducting qubits is essential for fault-tolerant quantum computation, and in the absence of quantum-limited amplifiers, the cryogenic HEMT LNA at the 4K stage is the dominant noise source. However, conventional Y-factor characterization of HEMT LNAs measures noise temperature independently of the quantum measurement they serve, potentially missing performance-relevant factors that only manifest in actual qubit readout.

Method: The authors use the two-dimensional IQ histogram of single-shot qubit readout as a direct figure of merit to benchmark amplifiers in a qubit-in-the-loop configuration. They apply this method to three state-of-the-art cryogenic InP HEMT LNAs with 53%, 60%, and 70% channel indium content, plus a commercial reference amplifier. They map assignment fidelity against HEMT LNA dc power consumption for each device.

Result: The 60% and 70% devices have similar noise temperatures in Y-factor measurements (both lower than the 53% device), and the 70% device has the highest gain, but qubit readout SNR and assignment fidelity peak at 60%. Fidelity does not simply saturate with increasing power; it declines above device-dependent power levels well below typical operating points. For the 60% device, ~1 mW is needed to maintain fidelity >85%, and across all four devices fidelity >80% is achieved with as little as ~0.3 mW, roughly an order of magnitude below typical operating points.

Conclusion: The results establish a qubit-referenced benchmarking methodology that provides practical guidance for amplifier selection and bias in power-constrained multi-qubit readout systems, showing that conventional metrics like noise temperature and gain do not fully predict qubit readout performance.

Abstract: High-fidelity single-shot readout of superconducting qubits is essential for fault-tolerant quantum computation. Without a quantum-limited amplifier such as a Josephson parametric amplifier, the cryogenic high-electron-mobility-transistor (HEMT) low-noise amplifier (LNA) at the \SI{4}{K} stage is the dominant noise source in the readout chain. HEMT LNAs are conventionally characterized by Y-factor measurements of the noise temperature $\Tn$, independently of the quantum measurement they ultimately serve. Here we present a qubit-in-the-loop benchmarking method. The method uses the two-dimensional IQ histogram of single-shot readout as a direct figure of merit. We apply it to three state-of-the-art cryogenic InP HEMT LNAs with 53\%, 60\%, and 70\% channel indium content, together with a commercial reference amplifier. The 60\% and 70\% devices have similar $\Tn$ in Y-factor measurements, both lower than the 53\% device. The 70\% device has the highest gain, but qubit readout SNR and assignment fidelity instead peak at 60\%. Using this method, we map assignment fidelity against HEMT LNA dc power consumption for each device. Fidelity does not simply saturate with increasing power. For several devices it declines above a device-dependent power well below typical operating points. For the 60\% device, which has the highest overall fidelity, about \SI{1}{mW} is needed to maintain $\fid > 85\%$. Across all four devices, $\fid > 80\%$ is reached with as little as about \SI{0.3}{mW}, roughly an order of magnitude below typical operating points. These results establish a qubit-referenced benchmarking methodology and provide practical guidance for amplifier selection and bias in power-constrained multi-qubit readout systems.

</details>


### [84] [Spectral Criterion for Disorder-Free Localization of Quantum Walks on Hypercube: QBN Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07267)
*Ce Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies disorder-free localization in a spin-coupled quantum walk on the hypercube using the quantum Bernoulli noises framework. A general criterion is derived: disorder-free localization occurs iff some spectral subspace of a reduced unitary evolution contains a vector with non-uniform position marginal probability. For the Grover walk with distance-dependent coupling in dimension d=4, a non-uniform fixed point is explicitly constructed, proving disorder-free localization occurs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand when quantum walks can exhibit localization without any disorder in the system, extending the study to spin-coupled quantum walks on hypercubes within the quantum Bernoulli noises formalism—a question of fundamental interest in quantum transport and localization phenomena.

Method: The authors employ the quantum Bernoulli noises framework to model spin-coupled quantum walks on the hypercube. They derive a spectral criterion linking disorder-free localization to the existence of non-uniform spectral vectors in a reduced unitary evolution. They then analyze the specific case of the Grover walk with distance-dependent coupling φ_σ=|σ|π/(n+1) in dimension d=4, explicitly constructing a non-uniform fixed point in the all +1 spin configuration.

Result: The derived criterion is proven to be both necessary and sufficient for disorder-free localization. Explicit construction of a non-uniform fixed point for the Grover walk (d=4, coupling φ_σ=|σ|π/(n+1), all +1 spins) demonstrates that the criterion is satisfied, thereby establishing disorder-free localization in this model.

Conclusion: Disorder-free localization occurs in the spin-coupled quantum walk on the hypercube under the specified Grover walk parameters. The non-uniform fixed point in the all +1 spin configuration serves as the explicit witness, confirming the phenomenon within the quantum Bernoulli noises framework.

Abstract: We study disorder-free localization in a spin-coupled quantum walk on the hypercube within the quantum Bernoulli noises framework. We derive a general criterion for disorder-free localization: it occurs if and only if some spectral subspace of a reduced unitary evolution contains a vector whose position marginal probability is non-uniform. For the Grover walk with distance-dependent coupling \(φ_σ=|σ|π/(n+1)\) in dimension \(d=4\), we explicitly construct a non-uniform fixed point in the all \(+1\) spin configuration. This implies that disorder-free localization occurs in our model.

</details>


### [85] [Implementing QESEM's High-Accuracy Error Mitigation on a Quantum Computer: a Water Potential Energy Surface Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07284)
*Renato Olarte Hernandez,Emanuele Rossi,Sonia Coriani,Karl Michael Ziems,Erik Kjellgren,Jacob Kongsted,Tali Shnaider,Stephan P. A. Sauer*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper applies QESEM, a characterization-based unbiased quantum error mitigation method, on IBM's Aachen quantum processor to compute the ground-state potential energy surface of the symmetrically-stretched water molecule using an 8-qubit (4,4) active space with STO-3G basis and a perfect-pairing tiled unitary product state ansatz. Raw QPU results overestimate energies by ~500 mHa, while QESEM-mitigated results fall within ~100 mHa, ~30 mHa, or chemical accuracy (~1.5 mHa) depending on precision target. Tightening precision systematically improves accuracy but incurs substantial sampling overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum error mitigation (QEM) is essential for extracting chemically accurate results from near-term quantum hardware, yet many widely used QEM methods rely on uncontrolled heuristics whose bias depends on the specific circuit and noise realization. The authors aim to demonstrate that a characterization-based, unbiased QEM approach (QESEM) can yield quantitatively meaningful potential energy surfaces on current quantum hardware, while also quantifying the associated sampling cost.

Method: The authors employ QESEM, a characterization-based unbiased quasi-probabilistic mitigation method, on IBM's Aachen quantum processor. They map a classically-optimized, single-layer perfect-pairing tiled unitary product state ansatz to an 8-qubit register corresponding to a (4,4) active space in the STO-3G basis set for the water molecule. They benchmark QESEM at loose (0.1 Ha) and tight (0.01 Ha) precision targets, evaluating both individual and merged batches of runs, and quantify the number of shots required at each precision level.

Result: Raw QPU results typically overestimate ground-state energies by ~500 mHa across scanned geometries. QESEM-mitigated results fall within ~100 mHa, ~30 mHa, or chemical accuracy (~1.5 mHa), depending on target precision. Accuracy improves systematically as the precision target is tightened, matching or exceeding literature results. Achieving the highest accuracies demands substantial QPU time at current hardware error levels, highlighting the sampling overhead associated with QEM as a central bottleneck.

Conclusion: The characterization-based, unbiased error mitigation provided by QESEM allows quantitative measurement of potential energy surfaces on current quantum hardware. However, the sampling overhead of quantum error mitigation remains a central bottleneck for scaling to larger chemical problems and higher precision.

Abstract: Quantum error mitigation (QEM) is essential for extracting chemically accurate results from near-term quantum hardware. Many widely used QEM methods rely on uncontrolled heuristics whose bias depends on the specific circuit and noise realization. In this work, we employ QESEM---a characterization-based, unbiased quasi-probabilistic mitigation method---on IBM's Aachen quantum processor to compute the ground-state potential energy surface (PES) of the symmetrically-stretched water molecule. We consider a classically-optimized, single-layer perfect-pairing tiled unitary product state ansatz. We map this ansatz to an 8-qubit register corresponding to a (4,4) active space and the STO-3G basis set. Compared to the statevector reference, we find that raw QPU results typically overestimate the ground-state energy by around 500~mHa across the scanned geometries. On the other hand, QESEM-mitigated results fall approximately within 100~mHa, 30~mHa, or ``chemical accuracy'' ($\sim$1.5~mHa), depending on the target precision. We benchmark QESEM at both loose (0.1~Ha) and tight (0.01~Ha) precision targets, evaluating both individual and merged batches of runs. As the precision target is tightened, the accuracy improves systematically, matching or exceeding results reported in the literature. We further quantify the sampling cost of these results, reporting the number of shots required at each precision level. Our results show how, with the current levels of hardware error, reaching the highest accuracies demands substantial QPU time. The results demonstrate that the characterization-based, unbiased error mitigation provided by QESEM allows to measure quantitatively meaningful potential energy surfaces on current quantum hardware. Concurrently, they highlight the sampling overhead associated with QEM, which remains a central bottleneck en route to larger chemical problems and higher precision.

</details>


### [86] [Momentum-Space-Engineered Spatial Photonic Ising Machine for Long-Range Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07297)
*Haijun Zhou,Hengyang Li,Maolin Wang,Yunru Chen,Yingxiong Qin,Xiahui Tang,Gang Xu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a momentum-space-engineered spatial photonic Ising machine (SPIM) framework that maps long-range coupling kernels onto optical momentum-space masks, enabling parallel Hamiltonian evaluation and systematic study of long-range Ising models (LRIMs) with power-law and RKKY interactions. The framework is validated through numerical annealing dynamics and a proof-of-principle experiment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While the spatial photonic Ising machine (SPIM) leverages intrinsic optical parallelism for Ising computation, its capability to faithfully encode dense long-range couplings and capture the resulting equilibrium thermodynamic signatures has remained underexplored. This gap limits SPIM access to broader classes of many-body systems with nonlocal and competing interactions.

Method: The authors develop a momentum-space-engineered SPIM framework that encodes prescribed long-range coupling kernels directly onto momentum-space masks for parallel Hamiltonian evaluation. A high-fidelity optical field propagation model is employed to systematically simulate annealing dynamics for LRIMs with power-law and RKKY interaction types. For the power-law model, the critical temperature modulation is studied as a function of decay exponent σ and cutoff radius R; for RKKY, complex competing interactions are used to reproduce diverse ordered states.

Result: The framework successfully maps and evaluates dense long-range couplings in parallel. For power-law LRIMs, the estimated critical temperature's dependence on σ and R is characterized. For RKKY models, multiple distinct ordered phases induced by competing long-range interactions are reproduced. A proof-of-principle experiment confirms physical feasibility of the approach.

Conclusion: The momentum-space-engineered SPIM framework broadens the class of many-body systems accessible to the SPIM platform, extending it beyond local interactions to dense long-range and competing interaction models relevant to statistical physics and quantum simulation.

Abstract: Long-range Ising models (LRIMs) with dense nonlocal and competing interactions are central to statistical physics, quantum simulation, and complex networks. Although the spatial photonic Ising machine (SPIM) exploits intrinsic optical parallelism for Ising computation, its ability to faithfully encode dense long-range couplings and capture the resulting thermodynamic signatures remains underexplored. Here, we present a momentum-space-engineered SPIM framework that maps prescribed long-range coupling kernels onto momentum-space masks for parallel Hamiltonian evaluation. Based on a high-fidelity optical field propagation model, the annealing dynamics of LRIMs with power-law and Ruderman-Kittel-Kasuya-Yosida (RKKY) interactions are systematically investigated. For the power-law model, we investigate the modulation of the estimated critical temperature by the decay exponent σ and coupling cutoff radius R. For the RKKY model, we reproduce diverse ordered states induced by complex competing long-range interactions. A proof-of-principle experiment demonstrates the physical feasibility of our approach. This framework broadens the class of many-body systems accessible to the SPIM platform.

</details>


### [87] [Symmetry-resolved tree tensor network analysis of Bell-state discrimination with ancilla-assisted passive linear optics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07319)
*Anand Kumar,Wojciech Roga,Devendra Kumar Mishra,Masahiro Takeoka*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a photon-number-resolved tree tensor network method to evaluate the detector support (i.e., photon-counting patterns) enabling Bell-state discrimination in passive linear-optical analyzers with ancillary photons. The approach exploits photon-number conservation and mode symmetries to separate most Bell outputs before examining the remaining detector support, without enumerating all photon detector patterns individually. The method is validated on recursive, product, and asymmetric ancillary states up to 32 optical modes against known analytical and literature benchmarks, and an exact composition relation is derived for symmetric factorized ancillary resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Passive linear-optical Bell-state discrimination (BSM) can exceed the standard 1/2 success probability limit when ancillary photons are used, but evaluating the discrimination probability requires determining which photon-counting patterns occur uniquely for each Bell input. This becomes computationally intractable for large systems, motivating an efficient method to evaluate these detector supports in recursively structured analyzers without enumerating all relevant photon detection patterns.

Method: The authors develop a photon-number-resolved tree tensor network (TTN) method for evaluating detector supports in recursively structured linear-optical Bell-state analyzers. Photon-number conservation and mode symmetries are used to separate most of the Bell-state outputs first; then a tree tensor network-based construction evaluates the remaining detector support without individually recalling all relevant photon-counting patterns. They also derive an exact composition relation for factorized ancillary states with fixed photon numbers in halves of the analyzer that possess a certain symmetry, and show that for reflected asymmetric pairings the success probability equals the arithmetic mean of corresponding symmetric configurations.

Result: The method is applied to recursive, product, and asymmetric ancillary states with up to 32 optical modes, reproducing known analytical and literature benchmarks. Results are further verified via complete enumeration for smaller systems, permanent-based amplitude calculations, and independent evaluations.

Conclusion: The tree tensor network approach provides an efficient and scalable means of computing Bell-state discrimination probabilities with ancillary photons in recursive linear-optical analyzers, and the derived composition relations offer additional analytical insight into symmetric and asymmetric ancillary configurations.

Abstract: Ancillary photons allow passive linear-optical Bell-state discrimination to exceed the one-half probability of success limit achievable with vacuum auxiliary modes. For a fixed analyzer and ancillary resource, the discrimination probability is determined by the photon-counting patterns that occur uniquely for each Bell input. We develop a photon-number-resolved tree tensor network method for evaluating these detector supports in recursively structured analyzers. Photon-number conservation and mode symmetries separate most of the Bell-state outputs before the remaining detector support is examined. We establish a tree tensor network based construction to evaluate the remaining support without need of recalling all relevant photon detector patterns separately. We apply the method to recursive, product, and asymmetric ancillary states with up to $32$ optical modes and reproduce known analytical and literature benchmarks. For factorized ancillary resources with fixed photon numbers in halves of the analyzer and the symmetry property used in our analysis, we also derive an exact composition relation; for reflected asymmetric pairings the success probability is the arithmetic mean of the corresponding symmetric configurations. The complete enumeration for smaller systems, permanent-based amplitude calculations, and independent evaluations provide additional checks of the numerical results.

</details>


### [88] [Phase estimation in spontaneous nonlinear interferometry for enhanced quantum imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07338)
*Jonas L. Moos,Cristofero Oglialoro,Enno Giese,Markus Gräfe*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates both experimentally and theoretically that a symmetric nonlinear interferometer in the spontaneous (low-gain) regime retains a phase-sensitivity advantage over non-entangled configurations, despite remaining shot-noise-limited. The advantage manifests as a shift of the optimal operating point toward the dark fringe, serving as a low-gain signature of the mechanism behind high-gain Heisenberg-limited supersensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bicolor quantum imaging using squeezed light from high-gain parametric down-conversion can theoretically approach the Heisenberg limit, but established quantum-imaging setups operate in the low-gain spontaneous regime where such supersensitivity scaling is generally considered inaccessible. The authors seek to determine whether any metrological advantage persists in the spontaneous regime.

Method: The authors combine experimental measurements with theoretical analysis of a symmetric nonlinear interferometer operating in the spontaneous (low-gain) regime. They compare its phase sensitivity against configurations that do not exploit entanglement as a quantum-metrological resource, and derive design guidelines for phase-optimized nonlinear interferometers.

Result: The spontaneous-regime nonlinear interferometer retains a phase-sensitivity advantage over non-entanglement-exploiting configurations, although the sensitivity remains shot-noise-limited. The advantage is characterized by an optimal working point shifted toward the dark fringe, and specific design choices are formulated for phase-optimized interferometers that favor entanglement-based configurations.

Conclusion: Even without reaching Heisenberg-limited supersensitivity, spontaneous-regime nonlinear interferometers that exploit entanglement as a metrological resource provide a genuine phase-sensitivity benefit over classical configurations. The optimal operating point shift toward the dark fringe provides practical guidance for experimental implementations of phase-optimized quantum imaging.

Abstract: Bicolor quantum imaging can reach, in principle, phase supersensitivity approaching the Heisenberg limit using squeezed light from high-gain parametric down-conversion, but established quantum-imaging setups typically operate in the low-gain, spontaneous regime, where such scaling is inaccessible. Here, we show experimentally and theoretically that a symmetric nonlinear interferometer, even in the spontaneous regime, retains a phase-sensitivity advantage over configurations where entanglement is not exploited as a quantum-metrological resource, although the achievable phase sensitivity remains shot-noise-limited. This advantage appears as a shift of the optimal working point toward the dark fringe, the low-gain signature of the mechanism underlying high-gain supersensitivity. We derive design choices for phase-optimized nonlinear interferometers accordingly, favoring configurations that exploit entanglement as a metrological resource and specifying their optimal operating point.

</details>


### [89] [Cat-Code-Protected Controlled Quantum Communication via Non-Local CNOT Gates over Star Quantum Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07376)
*Subham Das,Abhishek Sharma,Kailash S*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a protocol for controlled quantum communication that combines cat-state encoding for error correction with optimal non-local CNOT gates, protecting against amplitude damping in optical fibre networks. Simulations show higher fidelity at 50 km than standard protocols, with security against beam-splitter attacks verified via CHSH tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical controlled quantum communication over optical fibre networks suffers from amplitude damping, which exponentially reduces fidelity with distance. Existing protocols lack error correction mechanisms suitable for distributed implementations.

Method: The protocol integrates cat-state encoding (a quantum error correction code resilient to amplitude damping) with optimal non-local CNOT gates for distributed gate operations. The physical position qubit does not traverse the fibre, reducing damping events. Density-matrix simulations are used to characterize fidelity, and CHSH tests are employed to analyze security against beam-splitter attacks on the distributed entanglement resource.

Result: The cat-code-protected protocol with non-local CNOT achieves significantly higher fidelity at 50 km compared to the standard protocol. Security analysis confirms that CHSH tests provide protection against beam-splitter attacks despite the cat code's error correction. The protocol is shown to be feasible with current technology and structurally extensible to multiple controllers.

Conclusion: Cat-code-protected controlled quantum communication is experimentally feasible with present technology and offers a theoretical framework for error-corrected controlled quantum communication over long-distance star quantum networks with multiple controllers.

Abstract: Controlled quantum communication enables secure state transfer between a sender and receiver with the assistance of one or more controllers. However, practical implementation over optical fibre networks is severely hindered by amplitude damping, which reduces fidelity exponentially with distance. We address this challenge by combining two powerful techniques: cat-state encoding for error correction and optimal non-local CNOT gates for distributed gate implementation. The protocol eliminates the need for the physical position qubit itself to travel through the optical fibre, reducing damping events. We show, through density-matrix simulations, that our cat-code-protected protocol with a non-local CNOT operation achieves higher fidelity at 50 km, significantly outperforming the standard protocol. We analyse the protocol's security against beam-splitter attacks and show that CHSH tests provide security against beam-splitter attacks on the distributed entanglement resource despite the cat code's error correction. Our results establish that cat-code-protected controlled quantum communication is feasible with current technology and structurally extensible to multiple controllers, providing a theoretical framework for studying error-corrected controlled quantum communication over long-distance star quantum networks.

</details>


### [90] [Multi-copy and Catalytic Superactivation of Genuine Multipartite Nonlocality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07380)
*Bora Ulu,Mirjam Weilenmann,Nicolas Brunner*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that genuine multipartite Bell nonlocality (GMNL) can be superactivated: combining two copies of a biseparable (non-GMNL) correlation via local wirings can produce a GMNL correlation. This holds for any number of parties and for quantum-realizable correlations, and even at the single-copy level via a catalytic protocol. These findings challenge the operational meaningfulness of the biseparable-based definition of GMNL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether the standard definition of GMNL based on biseparable models constructed from non-signalling correlations is operationally well-founded, by testing whether non-GMNL correlations can be activated into GMNL ones through local operations.

Method: The authors construct explicit local wiring protocols that combine two copies of biseparable (not GMNL) correlations to produce a GMNL correlation. They extend this construction to arbitrary numbers of parties and demonstrate it works for quantum-realizable correlations. They further develop a catalytic protocol enabling superactivation at the single-copy level using only local wirings.

Result: Superactivation of GMNL is shown to be possible: locally wiring two biseparable correlations yields a GMNL correlation for any number of parties and for quantum-realizable correlations. Additionally, a single-copy catalytic protocol achieving superactivation via local wirings is presented.

Conclusion: The existence of superactivation—where non-GMNL correlations can be combined to produce GMNL correlations—raises fundamental questions about the operational relevance and physical meaningfulness of the biseparable-based definition of GMNL, since it implies this property is not preserved under local composition.

Abstract: Genuine multipartite Bell nonlocality (GMNL) aims at capturing correlations between many distant observers that are globally Bell nonlocal. A commonly used definition for GMNL is based on biseparable models constructed from non-signalling correlations. Here we uncover an effect of superactivation of GMNL in this framework. Specifically, by locally wiring together two copies of a biseparable correlation, i.e. not GMNL, one can obtain another correlation that is GMNL. We show that this is possible for any number of parties, and for quantum-realizable correlations. Finally, we show that superactivation of GMNL is also possible at the single-copy level, via a catalytic protocol involving only local wirings. These results question the operational meaning of this definition of GMNL.

</details>


### [91] [Wavelength-Uniform Quantum Algorithms for Quantum Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07384)
*Shi Jin,Chuwen Ma*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A quantum simulation algorithm for the semi-classical regime using Weyl variables to eliminate oscillation, combined with exact Hermite moments and quantum singular value transformation, achieving polynomial complexity in spatial dimension and query bounds free of negative powers of wavelength—even working when the spatial grid cannot resolve the frequency, defying Nyquist-Shannon sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum simulation in the semi-classical regime is prohibitively expensive because solutions become highly oscillatory when the de Broglie wavelength is small relative to characteristic length scales, making classical and standard quantum discretizations costly or intractable.

Method: The approach transforms to Weyl variables, under which the solution is non-oscillatory. Exact Hermite moments are used to treat polynomial components of the potential, and quantum singular value transformation (QSVT) handles Fourier components, yielding a unified quantum algorithm valid across all wavelength regimes.

Result: The algorithm achieves polynomial complexity in spatial dimension, with discretization and query bounds that contain no negative powers of the (possibly small) wavelength. It can capture correct physical observables even when the spatial grid does not resolve the oscillation frequency, thus circumventing the Nyquist-Shannon sampling theorem.

Conclusion: This work provides an efficient quantum algorithm for semi-classical quantum simulation across all wavelength scales, eliminating previous bottlenecks caused by oscillatory behavior and grid-resolution requirements.

Abstract: One of the main challenges in quantum simulation is the prohibitive cost of computing its solutions in the semi-classical regime, in which the de Broglie wavelength is small compared with the characteristic length scale and the solution is highly oscillatory. This difficulty is overcome by using the Weyl variable, under which the solution is not oscillatory. Furthermore, we use the exact Hermite moments and quantum singular value transformation to treat the polynomial and Fourier components of the potential, resulting in a quantum algorithm efficient for {\it all} ranges of wavelengths. Specifically, it has a {\it polynomial} complexity in spatial dimension, and discretization and query bounds {\it without} negative powers of possibly small wavelength, thus enabling it to capture the correct physical observables even if the spatial grid does not resolve the frequency, hence defying the Nyquist-Shannon sampling theorem.

</details>


### [92] [Unitary equivalence of Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures survives in nonlinear quantum mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07391)
*Lajos Diósi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that Schrödinger and Heisenberg picture equivalence is preserved in nonlinear quantum mechanics when Hamiltonians depend on the state through instantaneous expectation values, despite the absence of a state-independent unitary propagator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a common belief that the equivalence between Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures breaks down in nonlinear quantum mechanics because no state-independent unitary propagator exists. The authors aim to investigate whether this equivalence truly fails or can be maintained under certain conditions.

Method: The authors show that for Hamiltonians depending on the state via instantaneous expectation values, the unitary intertwiner between pictures becomes initial-state dependent but remains well-defined. They illustrate the framework using analytically solvable examples: nonlinear spin precession, a mean-field harmonic force, and local field theory with mean-field coupling.

Result: The equivalence of the Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures is preserved for state-dependent Hamiltonians of the mean-field type. The unitary intertwiner, while initial-state dependent, guarantees identical physical predictions across both pictures.

Conclusion: Nonlinear quantum mechanics with instantaneous expectation-value-dependent Hamiltonians does not inherently break Schrödinger-Heisenberg picture equivalence; it only requires relaxing the state-independence of the unitary transformation.

Abstract: The equivalence of Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures is often thought to break down in nonlinear quantum mechanics due to the absence of a state-independent unitary propagator. We show that for Hamiltonians depending on the state through instantaneous expectation values, the equivalence is preserved. While the unitary intertwiner becomes initial-state dependent, it remains well-defined and ensures identical physical predictions in both pictures. We illustrate this with the analytically solvable examples of a nonlinear spin precession and a mean-field harmonic force, as well as in local field theory with mean-field coupling.

</details>


### [93] [Riemannian Optimization for Multi-Player Quantum Games on Product Unitary Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07397)
*Alireza Habibi,Setareh Maghsudi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper extends the EWL quantum Prisoner's Dilemma to multi-player quantum games with mixed strategies (EEWL) and proposes the Unitary Strategy Matrix Exponential Algorithm (USMEA), a geometry-aware sequential algorithm that jointly learns local unitary actions and classical mixing probabilities for each player, with convergence guarantees and numerical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The EWL quantum game framework resolves classical dilemmas in two-player settings but is limited to pure quantum strategies. Extending this to multiplayer scenarios with mixed strategies requires a formal protocol and efficient learning methods for players navigating the quantum strategy space.

Method: The paper formalizes the Extended EWL (EEWL) protocol for multiplayer quantum games with mixed strategies, where each player employs unitary operators as actions with classical mixing probabilities and payoffs are expectation values of Hermitian reward operators. It then introduces USMEA, a geometry-aware sequential optimization algorithm that jointly updates both the set of trainable unitary actions and the classical mixing distribution. Convergence is analyzed under smoothness and step-size conditions, with numerical experiments validating the theory.

Result: USMEA demonstrates convergent behavior under standard assumptions in the EEWL mixed-strategy setting, supported by numerical experiments that validate the theoretical convergence properties and show the effectiveness of the geometry-aware sequential learning approach.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates how classical optimization methodologies can be systematically integrated into engineered quantum strategic interactions, providing a learning-and-control framework for multi-agent quantum decision systems.

Abstract: Quantum game theory is an extension of classical game theory that uses quantum principles in game theory. The Eisert-Wilkens-Lewenstein (EWL) quantum game is an early example of the two-player classical Prisoner's Dilemma transformed into a quantum Prisoner's Dilemma. In the EWL game, the players choose pure quantum strategies represented by unitary matrices. This extension can resolve the classical dilemma by enabling cooperative equilibrium with higher payoff. In this paper, we first discuss the Extended EWL (EEWL) for multiplayer quantum games with mixed strategies. In EEWL, each player controls a set of unitary operators as quantum actions and uses a classical mixed strategy over these actions.
