The payoffs are defined as expectation values of Hermitian reward operators acting on a shared quantum state, which is generated and measured according to the EEWL protocol. We then propose the Unitary Strategy Matrix Exponential Algorithm (USMEA), a geometry-aware sequential algorithm for the EEWL mixed-strategy setting, in which each player jointly learns a trainable set of local unitary actions and the associated classical mixing probabilities. Thereby it acts as a learning-and-control layer for multi-agent quantum decision systems. We analyze the convergence properties of USMEA under standard smoothness and step-size conditions and validate the theory with numerical experiments. These results show how classical optimization methods can be systematically integrated into the design and analysis of engineered quantum strategic interactions.

</details>


### [94] [Photonic Time-Delayed Quantum Extreme Learning Machine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07416)
*Ekaterina Protsenko,Caterina Vigliar,Francesco Da Ros*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes and numerically simulates a photonic quantum extreme learning machine (QELM) using a time-delay non-linear interferometer with photon-number-resolving detection. It demonstrates computational capability via binary classification of the non-linear two moons benchmark, shows that compact feature sets based on photon-number correlations preserve accuracy, and demonstrates resilience to losses through natural weight redistribution across features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To propose an experimentally accessible photonic platform for quantum extreme learning machines that leverages non-linear interactions in a time-delay interferometer, and to assess its computational capabilities, measurement efficiency, and robustness to losses.

Method: Numerical simulation of a photonic QELM built on a time-delay non-linear interferometer with photon-number-resolving detection. The reservoir's computational power is evaluated through binary classification of the non-linear two moons benchmark, with analysis of learned output weights and alternative quantum readout schemes. Performance under losses is tested by observing weight redistribution across expanded feature sets.

Result: The QELM successfully performs binary classification on the non-linear two moons benchmark. Compact feature sets based on photon-number correlations retain classification accuracy while potentially reducing measurement complexity. The system maintains classification performance under losses by automatically redistributing learned weights across a larger set of available features.

Conclusion: The time-delay photonic implementation of the QELM demonstrates resilience and robustness, highlighting its potential as an experimentally accessible quantum extreme learning machine platform.

Abstract: We propose and numerically simulate a photonic quantum extreme learning machine (QELM) based on non-linear interactions in a time-delay non-linear interferometer with photon-number-resolving detection. We theoretically demonstrate the reservoir's computational capability by binary classification of the non-linear two moons benchmark. Analysis of the learned output weights and alternative quantum readouts shows that compact feature sets based on photon-number correlations preserve classification accuracy while potentially reducing measurement complexity. We further show that the QELM maintains its classification performance under the effect of losses by naturally redistributing the learned weights across a larger set of available features. These results demonstrate the resilience of the time-delay photonic implementation and highlight its potential as an experimentally accessible QELM platform.

</details>


### [95] [High-purity fluorescence photon bundle emission from two separate emitters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07454)
*Zhicai Chen,Jun Xu,Deyi Kong,Xiangming Hu,Fei Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a scheme for generating high-purity fluorescence two-photon bundles from two spatially separated two-level emitters, using a strongly driven auxiliary emitter to mediate correlated excitation. The finite excitation space of the emitters naturally suppresses higher-excitation manifolds, eliminating impurity photons, and the authors analytically characterize off-resonant single-excitation state leakage to further improve bundle purity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of bosonic-mode-based approaches for two-photon bundle generation, which suffer from impurity photons arising from undesirable higher-excitation states, by exploiting the intrinsically finite excitation space of two-level emitters.

Method: The scheme uses two spatially separate two-level emitters whose correlated excitation is mediated by a strongly driven auxiliary emitter. Analytical characterization of the residual population of off-resonant single-excitation states is provided, along with strategies to suppress corresponding leakage for improved photon bundle purity.

Result: The finite excitation space of the target emitters intrinsically excludes higher-excitation manifolds, eliminating impurity photons. Suppressing residual off-resonant single-excitation leakage improves the purity of the generated two-photon bundles.

Conclusion: The proposed scheme provides a feasible pathway for constructing high-purity quantum light sources with promising applications in quantum information processing.

Abstract: We propose a scheme for generating high-purity fluorescence two-photon bundles from two spatially separate two-level emitters, whose correlated excitation is mediated by a strongly driven auxiliary emitter. Unlike bosonic-mode-based approaches, the finite excitation space of the two target emitters intrinsically excludes higher-excitation manifolds, and thus eliminates impurity photons associated with undesired higher-excitation states. We further analytically characterize the residual population of off-resonant single-excitation states and show that suppressing the corresponding leakage improves photon bundle purity. Our scheme offers a feasible pathway for constructing high-purity quantum light sources with promising applications in quantum information processing.

</details>


### [96] [Stationary states for a particle in a box with slanted walls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07505)
*Nivaldo A. Lemos*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies a quantum particle in an infinite potential well with slanted walls. The energy eigenvalues are governed by a transcendental equation involving Airy functions. The study reveals that for large quantum numbers energy levels become arbitrarily close (satisfying Bohr's correspondence principle), and analyzes the confinement of stationary states by measuring probability in classically forbidden regions. A notable finding is that in a symmetric well, beyond a critical wall slope, the ground state is no longer the least confined eigenstate—unlike the harmonic oscillator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore a less standard quantum well geometry (slanted walls) that introduces Airy functions, provides richer physics than the textbook particle-in-a-box, and offers pedagogical value by exposing students to Airy functions and their properties.

Method: The energy eigenvalues are derived from a transcendental equation involving the Airy function of the first kind. Analytical treatment of the equation is complemented by graphical and numerical methods. Confinement of stationary states is quantified via the probability of finding the particle in the classically forbidden region.

Result: For large quantum numbers, consecutive energy levels converge—an asymptotic property confirmed both via an intuitive argument and through properties of Airy functions, yielding a stronger fulfillment of Bohr's correspondence principle than standard box or oscillator cases. The confinement analysis shows that for symmetric wells of sufficiently steep slope, the ground state ceases to be the least confined state, contrasting with the harmonic oscillator.

Conclusion: The slanted-wall infinite well exhibits novel and instructive features, including non-monotonic confinement behavior as a function of energy, and serves as an effective pedagogical vehicle for introducing Airy functions to undergraduate physics students.

Abstract: The quantum mechanical problem of a particle in a infinite potential well with slanted walls is studied. The energy eigenvalues are determined by a transcendental equation involving the Airy function of the first kind. Two limiting cases are discussed. Next, the allowed energies are found by graphical and numerical methods. The graphical analysis hints that for large quantum numbers the consecutive energy levels get arbitrarily close together. This asymptotic behavior of the energy spectrum actually holds, as shown by means of an intuitive argument and confirmed by resorting to basic properties of Airy functions. In a sense, Bohr's correspondence principle is more accurately fulfilled than in the cases of the standard particle in a box or the harmonic oscillator. A state is regarded the more confined the less the probability of finding the particle in the classically forbidden region. For some stationary states the confinement degree is calculated and a comparison is made with the harmonic oscillator, for which the ground state is the least confined of all stationary states. In the symmetric case --- right and left walls of equal slope --- something unexpected happens: beyond a certain critical slope the ground state is no longer the least confined eigenstate. Over and above its interesting physical features, this problem provides students with the opportunity to get acquainted with Airy functions, which do not belong to the standard mathematical repertoire of physics undergraduates.

</details>


### [97] [Achieving the Quantum Limits with Twin-Field Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07517)
*Yaohua Li,Fan Yang,Klaus Mølmer*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that for a wide class of quantum sensors, the quantum Fisher information bound can be saturated by jointly measuring signals from the original sensor and a system copy (a "twin-field sensor" or TFS). Simple photon counting or homodyne detection saturates the theoretical quantum limit across diverse implementations, including two-level atoms and hybrid quantum Rabi models, and the approach also achieves quantum limits for joint multi-parameter sensing as quantified by the quantum Fisher information matrix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous measurements are fundamental to quantum experiments, but extracting maximal possible information from continuous probe signals remains challenging. There is a need to understand how to saturate the quantum Fisher information bound in practical sensing scenarios.

Method: The authors introduce a twin-field sensor (TFS) concept, which combines measurement signals from the original sensor with signals from a suitable system copy. They analyze the physical mechanism underpinning TFS and apply it across various implementations, from two-level atoms to hybrid quantum Rabi models, using simple measurement strategies such as photon counting and homodyne detection.

Result: For a wide class of sensors, the TFS saturates the quantum Fisher information bound via combined measurements of the original sensor and a system copy. Simple photon counting or homodyne detection achieves the theoretical quantum limit across all tested implementations. The TFS also attains quantum limits for joint sensing of parameter pairs, as quantified by the quantum Fisher information matrix.

Conclusion: The twin-field sensor provides a general and experimentally accessible strategy to saturate fundamental quantum sensing limits using straightforward measurement techniques, extending from single-parameter to multi-parameter estimation scenarios.

Abstract: Continuous measurements play a fundamental role in quantum experiments, yet understanding and extracting the maximal possible information in a continuous probe signal remains a significant challenge. In this work, we show that for a wide class of sensors, the quantum Fisher information bound can be saturated through a combined measurement of signals from the original sensor and a suitable system copy. We elucidate the physical mechanism of such a twin-field sensor (TFS) and study its performance across diverse implementations, ranging from two-level atoms to a hybrid quantum Rabi model. In all of these cases, we find that simple photon counting or homodyne detection saturates the theoretical quantum limit. We further prove that the TFS achieves the quantum limits for the joint sensing of pairs of parameters as quantified by the quantum Fisher information matrix.

</details>


### [98] [Toward Fault-Tolerant Variational Optimization: QAOA under [[4,2,2]] Error Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07537)
*Matteo Robert Child,Emanuele Dri,Giacomo Vitali,Chiara Vercellino,Alberto Leporati*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a partially fault-tolerant implementation of QAOA for Max-Cut on a square graph using the [[4,2,2]] error-detection code. The key contribution is a novel ancilla-mediated logical R_ZZ gate connecting qubits across different code blocks. Under five noise models and two connectivity regimes, post-selection on stabilizer measurements consistently improves sampling of optimal bitstrings, with five measurements giving the strongest benefit, supporting error detection as a practical near-term strategy for variational quantum algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum error correction is a major barrier for near-term quantum computing. Variational algorithms like QAOA are noise-sensitive, but full fault tolerance is impractical with current hardware. The authors aim to demonstrate that lightweight error detection (via the [[4,2,2]] code) can serve as a practical intermediate strategy to improve algorithmic performance without the overhead of full fault-tolerant error correction.

Method: The authors implement QAOA targeting Max-Cut on a square graph, encoding logical qubits using the [[4,2,2]] error-detection code. They introduce a novel ancilla-mediated logical R_ZZ gate to enable logical entangling interactions between qubits residing in distinct code blocks. They benchmark unencoded vs. encoded circuits under five distinct noise models and under both all-to-all and grid-routed qubit connectivity, using the Cirq and qsimcirq simulators with parallel CPU execution. Post-selection is applied on stabilizer measurement outcomes to suppress errors.

Result: Post-selection on stabilizer measurements consistently improves the probability of sampling optimal Max-Cut bitstrings. Five stabilizer measurements provide the strongest performance benefit across the tested noise models and connectivity settings, demonstrating that the encoded approach with error detection outperforms or at least remains competitive with unencoded implementations under realistic noise.

Conclusion: The results indicate that error detection—rather than full error correction—can be an effective near-term strategy for improving the quality of variational quantum algorithms. The proposed ancilla-mediated logical R_ZZ gate and [[4,2,2]]-encoded QAOA pipeline offer a viable path toward better performance on near-term devices.

Abstract: We present a partially fault-tolerant implementation of QAOA based on the $[[4,2,2]]$ error-detection code, targeting the Max-Cut problem on a square graph. Our main contribution is a novel ancilla-mediated logical $R_{ZZ}$ gate enabling interactions between qubits in different $[[4,2,2]]$ blocks. We evaluate unencoded and encoded circuits under five noise models, with both all-to-all and grid-routed connectivity, using the Cirq and qsimcirq frameworks with parallel CPU execution. Post-selection on stabilizer measurements consistently improves the probability of sampling optimal bitstrings, with five measurements providing the strongest benefit. These results support error-detection as a practical near-term strategy for improving the quality of variational quantum algorithms.

</details>


### [99] [Discrete Dirac equation on quantum graphs: A model of a Dirac particle in a branched lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07539)
*Mashrab Akramov,Carsten Trunk,Davron Matrasulov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies the one-dimensional time-independent discrete Dirac equation on graphs with discrete edges, focusing on star graphs with three edges. Exact eigenstates and spectra are derived and compared with continuum results, showing good agreement, providing a framework for relativistic quantum transport on branched structures relevant to nanostructures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a systematic framework for analyzing relativistic quantum transport on branched structures such as star graphs, relevant to nanostructures and quantum device design, by extending Dirac equation treatments from continuous to discrete graph formulations.

Method: The paper considers both continuous and discrete formulations of graphs with discrete edges, focusing on star graphs with three edges as a representative example. Exact solutions for eigenstates and spectra are derived analytically and compared against the continuum case via tabulated results.

Result: Exact eigenstate and spectral solutions are obtained for the discrete Dirac equation on star graphs, showing good agreement with the corresponding continuum case in tabulated numerical comparisons.

Conclusion: The discrete formulation provides a systematic and reliable approach for analyzing relativistic quantum transport on branched graph structures, validated by agreement with continuum results and applicable to nanostructure and quantum device contexts.

Abstract: We study the one-dimensional time-independent discrete Dirac equation on graphs with discrete edges. Both continuous and discrete formulations of the graphs are considered, with particular focus on star graphs with three edges as a simple example. Exact solutions for eigenstates and spectra are derived and compared with the continuum case, showing good agreement in tabulated results. This approach offers a systematic framework for analyzing relativistic quantum transport on branched structures relevant to nanostructures and quantum device design.

</details>


### [100] [Geometry Dependence of Error Thresholds in Two-Dimensional Toric Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07579)
*D. Lessing,A. Langheld,C. Krämer,J. A. Koziol,K. P. Schmidt*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates how lattice geometry affects the maximum-likelihood error thresholds of 2D toric codes by mapping them to the random-bond Ising model (RBIM) and using replica-exchange Monte Carlo simulations to compute optimal thresholds on the Nishimori line for five lattices (square, honeycomb, triangular, dice, kagome).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous studies of error thresholds across lattice geometries used suboptimal decoders like minimum-weight perfect matching. With the rise of near-optimal decoders, it is important to determine the fundamental maximum-likelihood code capacity limits, which represent the ultimate error thresholds achievable.

Method: The toric code error correction problem is mapped onto the 2D random-bond Ising model (RBIM). The critical behavior of the RBIM is determined via replica-exchange Monte Carlo simulations. Optimal (maximum-likelihood) error thresholds are computed on the Nishimori line for square, honeycomb, triangular, dice, and kagome lattices.

Result: Optimal thresholds of dual-lattice pairs display a characteristic duality-driven splitting around the self-dual square lattice, mirroring qualitative behavior seen with suboptimal decoders. Average coordination number has the strongest influence on the threshold, but the detailed arrangement of vertices and plaquettes also significantly affects the precise threshold value.

Conclusion: The optimal error threshold for toric codes is primarily governed by lattice coordination number, with secondary but significant contributions from the specific lattice structure. Lattice duality imposes symmetry relations between bit- and phase-flip thresholds across dual lattice pairs.

Abstract: We investigate how the lattice geometry influences the error thresholds of two-dimensional toric codes. In the presence of bit- or phase-flip errors, the toric code maps onto the two-dimensional random-bond Ising model (RBIM). We determine the critical behaviour of the RBIM using replica-exchange Monte Carlo simulations. While previous studies have explored thresholds for general lattice geometries under suboptimal decoders such as minimum-weight perfect matching, the rapid development of near-optimal decoders makes resolving the ultimate, maximum-likelihood code capacity relevant. We compute these optimal error thresholds on the Nishimori line for the square, honeycomb, triangular, dice, and kagome lattices. Owing to lattice duality, the phase-flip threshold on a given lattice is equivalent to the bit-flip threshold on its dual. We find that the optimal thresholds of dual-lattice pairs display a characteristic duality-driven splitting around the self-dual square-lattice, mirroring the qualitative behaviour observed for suboptimal decoders. While the average coordination number has the strongest impact on the error threshold, our results demonstrate that the detailed arrangement of vertices and plaquettes also plays a significant role in determining its precise value.

</details>


### [101] [Quantum Approximate Counting with Bernoulli Oracles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07604)
*Chengshen Gao,Yongzhen Xu,Lvzhou Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies quantum counting with Bernoulli oracles, where labels follow unknown probability distributions rather than being exact. The authors prove an upper bound of Õ(√ρ/(Δε) + 1/(Δ√ε)) queries using QSVT-based bias amplification combined with two-stage adaptive amplitude estimation, and establish a near-matching lower bound of Ω(√ρ/(Δε)) via a new composition theorem for the quantum adversary method. Results specialize to characterize the query complexity for bounded-error oracles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical quantum counting assumes exact labeling by a membership oracle, but in practice labels are often probabilistic (e.g., noisy or uncertain classification). The gap between ideal and realistic oracle models has not been studied rigorously. The authors aim to extend quantum counting to handle Bernoulli oracles where each element's label follows a Bernoulli distribution with unknown bias, and to determine the optimal quantum query complexity for this more realistic setting.

Method: The algorithm uses Quantum Singular Value Transformation (QSVT) to coherently amplify the bias gap across distributions without collapsing the superposition, followed by two-stage adaptive amplitude estimation to estimate the fraction ρ of positive distributions. For lower bounds, the authors develop a new composition theorem for the quantum adversary method in the Boolean-over-average-case direction.

Result: The upper bound achieved is Õ(√ρ/(Δε) + 1/(Δ√ε)) queries, a quadratic speedup over classical sampling complexity of Θ(ρ/(Δ²ε²)). The lower bound is Ω(√ρ/(Δε)), nearly matching the upper bound. For the constant-gap case Δ=Θ(1), corresponding to bounded-error oracles, the bounds specialize to Õ(√ρ/ε + 1/√ε) and Ω(√ρ/ε).

Conclusion: The paper fully characterizes the quantum query complexity of quantum counting with Bernoulli oracles up to logarithmic factors, demonstrating that quadratic quantum speedup persists even when oracles have probabilistic labels. The composition theorem for the adversary method is a new technical contribution with potential broader applicability.

Abstract: Quantum counting is a fundamental quantum algorithm that estimates the fraction of marked elements using a membership oracle, achieving a quadratic speedup over classical sampling. The membership oracle, however, assumes exact labeling of each element, but this assumption fails when the labels are inherently probabilistic. We study quantum counting with \emph{Bernoulli oracles}, where given $m$ Bernoulli distributions with unknown biases $p_1,\dots,p_m$ and a gap parameter $Δ$, the goal is to estimate the fraction $ρ$ of \emph{positive} distributions ($p_i\ge1/2+Δ$) to within additive error $ε$. We prove an upper bound of $\tilde{O}\!\big(\frac{\sqrtρ}{Δε}+\frac{1}{Δ\sqrtε}\big)$ queries, achieving a quadratic speedup over the classical sample complexity. % of $Θ(ρ/Δ^2ε^2)$.
  Our algorithm first uses the Quantum Singular Value Transformation (QSVT) to coherently amplify the bias gap without collapsing the superposition over distributions, and then applies two-stage adaptive amplitude estimation. We complement this upper bound with a near-matching lower bound of $Ω(\sqrtρ/(Δε))$ via a new composition theorem for the quantum adversary method in the Boolean-over-average-case direction. For the special case of a constant gap $Δ=Θ(1)$, which corresponds to the bounded-error oracle where each query returns the correct label with constant probability, our bounds specialize to $\tilde{O}\big(\frac{\sqrtρ}ε+\frac{1}{\sqrtε}\big)$ and $Ω(\frac{\sqrtρ}ε)$, thereby characterizing the query complexity of quantum counting with bounded-error oracles.

</details>


### [102] [Experimental realization of quantum-computing-enhanced sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07613)
*Yifang Xu,Hongwei Huang,Yilong Zhou,Yunlai Zhu,Chuanlong Ma,Lida Sun,Lintao Xiao,Ziyue Hua,Weiting Wang,Zijie Chen,Weizhou Cai,Hekang Li,Haohua Wang,Xiaoting Wang,Ming Li,Chang-Ling Zou,Luyan Sun*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates quantum-computing-enhanced sensing using a spin-oscillator architecture where a microwave cavity serves as a high-dimensional quantum register and a superconducting qubit acts as the sensor. The unknown signal directly generates the phase oracle operation, enabling the first Grover search in a bosonic mode and quantum amplitude amplification across a Hilbert space of over 128 photons, extracting more information and resolving more frequency candidates than classical sequential search for the same number of sensing iterations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While quantum algorithms offer computational advantages, it remains challenging to incorporate them into quantum sensing and convert these computational advantages into enhanced information acquisition in physical sensing tasks.

Method: A spin-oscillator architecture is employed where a microwave cavity provides a high-dimensional quantum register and a coupled superconducting qubit acts as the sensor. The unknown signal directly produces the phase oracle operation, creating a natural physical interface between quantum computation and sensing. This enables running oracle-based quantum algorithms such as Grover search directly within the sensing protocol.

Result: The experiment demonstrates the first Grover search in a bosonic mode and observes quantum amplitude amplification in a Hilbert space spanning over 128 photons. For the same number of sensing iterations, the protocol extracts more information and resolves more frequency candidates than classical sequential search.

Conclusion: The results establish signal-driven oracles as a route for harnessing oracle-based quantum algorithms and realizing quantum-computing-enhanced sensing.

Abstract: Quantum algorithms offer computational advantages, yet incorporating them into quantum sensing and converting these advantages into enhanced information acquisition remains challenging. Here, we realize quantum-computing-enhanced sensing in a spin-oscillator architecture, where a microwave cavity provides a high-dimensional quantum register and a coupled superconducting qubit serves as the sensor. The unknown signal directly generates the phase oracle operation, establishing a natural physical interface between quantum computation and sensing. We experimentally demonstrate the first Grover search in a bosonic mode and observe quantum amplitude amplification in a Hilbert space spanning more than 128 photons. For the same number of sensing iterations, our protocol extracts more information and resolves more frequency candidates than classical sequential search. Our results establish signal-driven oracles as a route for harnessing oracle-based quantum algorithms and realizing quantum-computing-enhanced sensing.

</details>


### [103] [Benchmarking dynamical-structure-factor protocols in programmable neutral-atom geometries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07634)
*Matteo Grotti,Sergi Julià-Farré,Elisa Ercolessi,Alexandre Dauphin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper numerically benchmarks a protocol for measuring the dynamical structure factor using neutral-atom quantum simulators, extending beyond integrable 1D models to dimerized chains and 2D transverse-field Ising models, and demonstrates robustness under realistic experimental conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The dynamical structure factor is a key observable for probing excitation spectra in quantum materials, traditionally measured via inelastic neutron scattering. While quantum simulators can extract analogous information, demonstrations have been limited to integrable 1D models. This work aims to establish feasibility in broader, non-integrable Ising-type systems where classical numerical methods struggle at scale.

Method: The authors use numerical emulations of a neutral-atom quantum processing unit to assess the measurement of the dynamical structure factor. They benchmark the protocol across three systems: the standard 1D transverse-field Ising chain, 1D chains with dimerized interactions, and the 2D transverse-field Ising model. They additionally analyze robustness under realistic experimental imperfections including finite simulation times, pulse modulation, positional disorder, and laser noise.

Result: The protocol proves feasible in all benchmarked systems, including the dimerized chain and 2D transverse-field Ising model where spectral properties are classically challenging. The approach remains robust against realistic experimental noise sources including finite-time effects, pulse modulation, positional disorder, and laser noise.

Conclusion: Neutral-atom quantum simulators offer a practical route to probing dynamical response functions in regimes where classical simulations become demanding, supporting near-term experimental implementation for non-integrable quantum many-body systems.

Abstract: The dynamical structure factor is a central observable in condensed-matter physics, providing direct insight into the excitation spectrum and dynamical response of quantum materials. While it is traditionally accessed in real materials through inelastic neutron scattering, recent works have shown that analogous information can be extracted in quantum simulators using suitable dynamical protocols. So far, these approaches have been demonstrated mainly in paradigmatic integrable one-dimensional models. In this work, we use numerical emulations of a neutral-atom quantum processing unit to assess the feasibility of measuring the dynamical structure factor in a broader class of Ising-like spin systems. Beyond the standard one-dimensional transverse-field Ising chain, we benchmark the protocol in chains with dimerized interactions and in the two-dimensional transverse-field Ising model, where spectral properties are difficult to access with classical numerical methods at large scales. We further analyze the robustness of the protocol under realistic experimental conditions, including finite simulation times, pulse modulation, positional disorder, and laser noise. Our results show that neutral-atom quantum simulators can provide a practical route to probing dynamical response functions in regimes where classical simulations become increasingly demanding, paving the way toward experimental implementation.

</details>


### [104] [Analog-to-digital conversion in the quantum regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07643)
*Thomas Pousset,Matteo Schiavon,Guillaume Ricard,Elie Awwad,Romain Alléaume,Nicolas Fabre*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper provides a rigorous treatment of the digitization chain in double homodyne detection, spanning optical, electronic, and sampling stages, establishing a generalized Nyquist-Shannon dimensioning rule that accounts for quantum fluctuations (not just signal bandwidth) in CV quantum optics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While linear signal processing in double homodyne detection has been studied under the assumption that post-measurement processing can be backpropagated optically, there is a lack of rigorous treatment of the full digitization chain—optical, electronic, and sampling stages.

Method: Employ a continuous mode description of double homodyne detection to compute the maximum mode-matching coefficient as a function of electronic noise, signal bandwidth, receiver electronics bandwidth, and sampling rate. Analyze the case of additive white electronic noise to derive optimal dimensioning criteria.

Result: Numerical results show that optimal signal-to-noise ratio is achieved when the electronic bandwidth is large enough to pass the signal yet narrower than the sampling rate, mitigating noise amplification. A generalized Nyquist-Shannon dimensioning rule that accounts for quantum fluctuations is established.

Conclusion: The work yields a concrete design criterion for coherent detection in CV quantum information systems, generalizing classical sampling theory to incorporate quantum noise when dimensioning receiver electronics and sampling chains.

Abstract: Digital signal processing has become essential in quantum optics, particularly for continuous-variable (CV) information encoding, enabling coherent detection schemes and mitigation of linear impairments. While linear signal processing in double homodyne detection has been studied under the assumption that all post-measurement processing can be backpropagated in the optical domain, a rigorous treatment of the digitization chain, spanning optical, electronic, and sampling stages, is still lacking. In this work, we consider the continuous mode description of double homodyne detection to fully capture the maximum mode-matching coefficient achievable as a function of electronic noise, signal bandwidth, receiver electronics bandwidth, and sampling rate, that is the entry point of digital signal processing. We establish a dimensioning rule that generalizes the Nyquist-Shannon criterion to account for quantum fluctuations of the measured signal, not merely its bandwidth. Analyzing the case of additive white electronic noise, we find numerically that optimal signal-to-noise ratio is achieved when the electronic bandwidth is large enough to pass the signal yet narrower than the sampling rate in order to mitigate noise amplification, yielding a concrete design criterion for coherent detection.

</details>


### [105] [Geometric inflation of deviations challenges neural quantum states in dynamics of quantum Ising models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07645)
*Wladislaw Krinitsin,Jonas B. Rigo,Mohammad Abedi,Markus Schmitt*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates why neural quantum state (NQS) simulations of quantum quench dynamics in a 1D Ising model are surprisingly challenging despite ample representational capacity. Using supervised state reconstruction, the authors show the parameter count is not the limiting factor. Instead, they identify geometric inflation of small parameter deviations caused by rotation of the null space of the quantum geometric tensor (QGT) during time evolution, which amplifies previously irrelevant deviations within the infinitesimal time-dependent variational principle (TDVP). This null-space rotation serves as a diagnostic for sensitive NQS dynamics and a target for improving TDVP algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NQS offer a scalable framework for simulating non-equilibrium dynamics in strongly correlated systems, yet they fail unexpectedly in certain physical regimes of limited complexity. The paper aims to determine whether this difficulty stems from representational limits or from the time-evolution algorithm itself.

Method: The authors use supervised state reconstruction on quench dynamics of a 1D quantum Ising model to establish tighter empirical upper bounds on the required number of parameters. They analyze the time-dependent variational principle (TDVP) and investigate the geometry of the QGT kernel, comparing NQS solutions against matrix product state (MPS) solutions of the same TDVP.

Result: The representational capacity of the NQS is sufficient under much tighter parameter bounds than previously estimated, ruling out expressivity as the key obstruction. The true bottleneck is a geometric inflation mechanism: as the QGT kernel rotates dynamically, small parameter deviations that were previously in the null space can suddenly acquire physical significance, leading to numerical instability. MPS solutions of the same TDVP remain stable, indicating the neural network ansatz non-linearity is responsible for the sensitivity.

Conclusion: QGT null-space rotation is identified as a geometric diagnostic for sensitive NQS dynamics and a concrete target for improving TDVP algorithms, shifting the focus from representational capacity to algorithmic geometry in time-evolution methods.

Abstract: Neural quantum states (NQS) have emerged as a powerful framework for simulating non-equilibrium dynamics in strongly correlated quantum systems, offering scalable variational representations of highly entangled states. Yet, accurate NQS simulations have been found to be surprisingly challenging in some physical regimes of limited complexity. Here, we address paradigmatic quench dynamics of a one-dimensional quantum Ising model as a controlled benchmark. Through supervised state reconstruction we establish substantially tighter empirical upper bounds on the required parameter count than previous estimates, ruling out representational limitations as the key obstruction. Instead, we uncover a geometric inflation of small deviations as a hitherto overlooked challenge for accurate solutions of the infinitesimal time-dependent variational principle (TDVP): the dynamical rotation of the kernel of the quantum geometric tensor (QGT) can suddenly lend physical significance to previously irrelevant parameter deviations. The stability of matrix product state solutions of the same TDVP suggests that the non-linearity of the neural network ansatz is the origin of the sensitivity. These results identify QGT-null-space rotation as a geometric diagnostic of sensitive NQS dynamics and as a concrete target for improving TDVP algorithms

</details>


### [106] [Microwave-Free $^{13}$C Hyperpolarization of Diamond Particles Enabled by Magic Angle Spinning and NV Centers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07653)
*Rémi Blinder,Guzel Musabirova,Daehee Kim,Anshuman Nayak,Jason Twamley,Viateschlav N. Agafonov,Raiker Witter,Jörg Matysik,Fedor Jelezko*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates 13C nuclear hyperpolarization in randomly oriented diamond microparticles (0.2–2 μm) using optical illumination of NV centers combined with magic angle spinning (MAS), without microwave irradiation. At magnetic fields of 7.1 T and 9.4 T, light-induced polarization enhancements up to 411-fold are achieved, with steady-state absolute 13C polarization above 0.1%. MAS mitigates the anisotropy of the NV spin Hamiltonian, enabling over 99.9% of NV orientations to participate in polarization transfer via a mechanism modeled on three-spin clusters (NV, P1, and 13C) near level anticrossings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an alternative to conventional microwave-driven dynamic nuclear polarization (DNP) for nuclear hyperpolarization, leveraging optically pumped NV color centers in diamond as a room-temperature platform capable of polarizing nuclei without the need for microwave irradiation.

Method: The protocol combines continuous optical illumination of NV centers in randomly oriented diamond particles with magic angle spinning (MAS) at 7.1 T and 9.4 T. Polarization enhancements were measured as a function of MAS frequency (0–6 kHz) at natural 13C abundance (1.1%) and 20% isotopic enrichment. Analysis is supported by a model of optical pumping of NV centers and spin dynamics near level anticrossings in three-spin clusters (NV–P1–13C).

Result: Maximum light-induced 13C polarization enhancements of 280-fold (enriched sample) and 411-fold (natural abundance) were observed at 7.1 T. Steady-state absolute 13C polarization exceeded 0.1% under continuous illumination. MAS enabled more than 99.9% of NV orientations to participate in the polarization transfer, effectively mitigating the anisotropy of the NV spin Hamiltonian.

Conclusion: These findings represent a first step toward transferring nuclear polarization from diamond particles to external nuclei, with potential applications in sensitive and high-resolution NMR at room temperature.

Abstract: Nuclear hyperpolarization from optically pumped color centers in solids offers an alternative to conventional microwave-driven dynamic nuclear polarization (DNP). Diamond can host the nitrogen vacancy (NV) center, whose ground spin state can be readily polarized by light at room temperature, making diamond a candidate platform for nuclear hyperpolarization. We report $^{13}{\rm C}$ nuclear hyperpolarization in randomly oriented diamond particles with sizes ranging from 0.2 to 2 $μ$m, both at natural $^{13}{\rm C}$ abundance (1.1 %) and at 20 % isotopic enrichment, at magnetic fields of 7.1 T and 9.4 T. The protocol combines optical illumination with magic angle spinning (MAS) and does not require microwave irradiation. By investigating the nuclear polarization as a function of the MAS frequency between 0 and 6 kHz at the magnetic field of 7.1 T, we find maximum light-induced polarization enhancements of $280$-fold for the isotopically enriched sample and $411$-fold for the natural abundance sample. Under continuous illumination, steady-state absolute $^{13}{\rm C}$ polarization levels above 0.1 % are reached. A model involving optical pumping of NV centers and spin dynamics near level anticrossings (LACs) in three-spin clusters formed by NV, a substitutional nitrogen (P1) and $^{13}{\rm C}$ is used to describe these findings. The protocol strongly mitigates the effect of the anisotropy of the NV spin Hamiltonian, allowing more than $99.9\%$ of NV orientations to participate in the polarization transfer process. These results represent a first step toward transferring nuclear polarization from diamond particles to external nuclei, with potential applications in sensitive and high-resolution NMR at room temperature.

</details>


### [107] [Dynamical Crossover of the Quantum Fisher Information in the Spin-Boson Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07674)
*D. Parlato,G. Di Bello,F. Pavan,G. De Filippis,C. A. Perroni*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the dynamical quantum Fisher information (QFI) for estimating the qubit gap of a two-level system coupled to a bosonic environment. Using analytical methods and matrix-product-state simulations, it reveals a coupling-dependent dynamical crossover in the Ohmic pure-dephasing regime at zero temperature: QFI grows unbounded at weak coupling, saturates at the crossover coupling, and vanishes at strong coupling. This crossover is absent in non-Ohmic baths at zero temperature, but reappears temperature-dependently in super-Ohmic quadratic baths at finite temperature. Adding amplitude-damping relaxation replaces unbounded growth with finite asymptotic QFI tied to the ground state, while early-time dynamics retain signatures of the pure-dephasing crossover.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the low-frequency structure of a bosonic bath and additional relaxation channels influence the ultimate precision limits (quantum Fisher information) for estimating the qubit energy gap in open quantum systems, thereby establishing connections between environmental spectral properties and metrological performance.

Method: Combination of analytical calculations for exactly solvable regimes with numerically controlled matrix-product-state (MPS) simulations of the two-level system coupled to a bosonic environment. The study systematically varies bath spectral density (Ohmic, sub-Ohmic, super-Ohmic), temperature, and introduces an additional amplitude-damping coupling channel.

Result: The long-time QFI exhibits a dynamical crossover in the zero-temperature Ohmic pure-dephasing case: algebraic unbounded growth at weak coupling, finite asymptote at the crossover coupling, and decay to zero at strong coupling. Thermal fluctuations suppress growth and produce a finite-time maximum retaining crossover signatures. Non-Ohmic baths at zero temperature show no crossover (QFI vanishes in sub-Ohmic, diverges in super-Ohmic). At finite temperature, super-Ohmic quadratic baths exhibit a temperature-dependent crossover. Adding amplitude damping replaces unbounded growth with finite asymptotic QFI from the reduced ground state, while early-time dynamics preserve pure-dephasing crossover features.

Conclusion: The low-frequency structure of the bath directly determines the asymptotic metrological performance of dynamical gap sensing. Thermal fluctuations and energy relaxation serve as regularization mechanisms that tame the ideal pure-dephasing dynamical crossover of the quantum Fisher information, clarifying when and how environmental noise limits quantum metrology precision.

Abstract: We investigate the dynamical quantum Fisher information of a two-level system coupled to a bosonic environment, focusing on the estimation of the qubit gap. We combine analytical calculations with numerically controlled matrix-product-state simulations. In the exactly solvable pure-dephasing Ohmic regime at zero temperature, the long-time quantum Fisher information displays a coupling-dependent algebraic behavior, leading to a dynamical crossover: it grows without bound at weak coupling, approaches a finite asymptotic value at the crossover coupling, and vanishes at strong coupling. At finite temperature, thermal fluctuations suppress the long-time growth and generate a finite-time maximum, whose dependence on the dephasing coupling retains a clear signa- ture of the zero-temperature crossover. We show that this crossover is absent at zero temperature in non-Ohmic baths: the long-time quantum Fisher information vanishes in the sub-Ohmic and diverges in the super-Ohmic regimes. At non-zero temperature instead, the crossover appears for a super-Ohmic quadratic bath and the crossover coupling becomes temperature-dependent. Moreover, in the zero-temperature Ohmic regime, we introduce an additional amplitude-damping system-bath coupling that induces energy relaxation. This relaxation channel replaces the unbounded long-time growth with a finite asymptotic quantum Fisher information associated with the reduced interacting ground state, while a signature of the pure-dephasing crossover persists in the early-time dynamics. These results establish a direct connection between the low-frequency structure of the bath and the asymptotic metrological behavior of dynamical gap sensing, and show how thermal fluctuations and energy relaxation regularize the ideal pure-dephasing dynamical crossover of the quantum Fisher information.

</details>


### [108] [Subsystem self-correction of the GKP qubit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07702)
*Brian Chung Hang Cheung,Lasse Bjørn Kristensen,Frederik Nathan,Michael Kastoryano*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper provides a self-contained derivation of the self-correcting behavior of the ideal GKP Hamiltonian from a quantum-information perspective using subsystem code decomposition. The logical lifetime exhibits Arrhenius-type scaling because detailed-balance jump operators decompose into a dominant gauge-only part and an exponentially suppressed logical boundary term, achieving an effective string-tension mechanism in modular phase space akin to topological spin memories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Realizing passive quantum error correction (self-correction) is a key goal in quantum information. Recent theoretical work suggests the GKP code may enable self-correcting quantum memories, but a clear quantum-information-based understanding of the mechanism was needed.

Method: The authors use subsystem code decomposition to analyze the ideal GKP Hamiltonian. They show that detailed-balance jump operators, when coupled via physical quadratures q and p, split into a dominant gauge-subsystem part and a boundary term acting on the logical qubit, which is exponentially suppressed by Gibbs weights near modular-cell edges.

Result: The logical lifetime of the GKP memory follows Arrhenius-type scaling. Unlike spin-based systems like the 2D surface code, the GKP Hamiltonian realizes an effective string tension in modular phase space, where the energy cost increases as errors approach the boundary of logical sectors.

Conclusion: The GKP Hamiltonian can indeed exhibit self-correcting behavior, with the suppression mechanism arising from exponentially suppressed logical boundary terms in the jump operators, providing an alternative paradigm to spin-based self-correcting memories.

Abstract: Passive quantum error correction, also known as self-correction, is a holy grail in quantum information science. Recent theoretical advances suggest that the Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) code can exhibit self-correction properties, positioning it as a candidate for the realization of self-correcting quantum memories. In this article, we provide a self-contained derivation of the self-correcting behavior of the ideal GKP Hamiltonian, manifested in the Arrhenius type scaling of the logical lifetime, from a quantum-information perspective based on the subsystem code decomposition. When coupling through the physical quadrature $q$ and $p$, the detailed-balance jump operators decompose into a dominant part acting only on the gauge subsystem and a boundary term that acts non-trivially on the logical qubit. The boundary term is then exponentially suppressed by the Gibbs weights near the edge of the modular cells. In contrast to spin-based quantum memories such as the two-dimensional surface code, the GKP Hamiltonian realizes an effective string tension in modular phase space, whereby the energy cost increases as an error approaches the boundary of a logical sector.

</details>


### [109] [Single- and Two-Qubit Gates Driven by an Integrated Photonic Laser](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07708)
*Chris Caron,Zhenyu Wei,Andrei Isichenko,David Heim,Meiting Song,Nick Montifiore,Kaikai Liu,Josiah Dill,Daniel J. Blumenthal,Robert J. Niffenegger*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates the first coherent single- and two-qubit gates on trapped ⁸⁸Sr⁺ ions driven by an integrated visible-wavelength Brillouin laser stabilized to an integrated coil resonator, achieving 99.61% single-qubit gate fidelity and 92.35% Bell state fidelity, showing that integrated photonic lasers can meet the phase-noise requirements for coherent trapped ion quantum logic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trapped ion quantum computers rely on bulky tabletop laser systems that hinder scaling and robustness. While integrated silicon nitride lasers have shown frequency-selective state preparation, measurement, and clock transition interrogation, coherent qubit gates have not been demonstrated because they impose much stricter phase-noise and performance requirements on the laser source.

Method: The authors use a visible-wavelength integrated Brillouin laser stabilized to an integrated coil resonator to drive coherent quantum logic on ⁸⁸Sr⁺ optical qubits. They perform randomized benchmarking to measure single-qubit gate fidelity per Clifford gate and use a two-qubit Mølmer-Sørensen interaction to generate Bell states. Ramsey coherence measurements (with and without spin echo) characterize the qubit coherence.

Result: Average single-qubit fidelity of 99.61% ± 0.03% per Clifford gate was measured via randomized benchmarking. A two-qubit Mølmer-Sørensen interaction produced an entangled Bell state with 92.35% ± 1.50% fidelity. The qubit showed a bare Ramsey coherence time of 660 ± 9 μs, extended to 1.750 ± 0.033 ms via spin echo.

Conclusion: Integrated visible-wavelength narrow-linewidth photonic lasers can satisfy the phase-noise requirements for coherent trapped ion quantum logic, enabling a path toward scalable optical systems monolithically integrated within trapped ion quantum processors.

Abstract: Trapped ions are a leading technology for quantum computing, but their reliance on bespoke tabletop laser and optical systems remains a major obstacle to scaling and robustness. Integrated silicon nitride lasers, compatible with future monolithic integration with surface electrode ion traps, have recently demonstrated frequency-selective qubit state preparation and measurement as well as interrogation of an optical clock transition. However, coherent qubit gates driven by integrated laser sources have not yet been demonstrated because coherent quantum logic imposes substantially more stringent performance requirements on the laser. Here, we use a visible-wavelength integrated Brillouin laser stabilized to an integrated coil resonator to drive coherent single- and two-qubit gates with $^{88}$Sr$^+$ optical qubits. We measure an average single-qubit fidelity of 99.61% $\pm$ 0.03% per Clifford gate with randomized benchmarking and use a two-qubit Mølmer-Sørensen interaction to generate an entangled Bell state with a fidelity of 92.35% $\pm$ 1.50%. The qubit exhibits a bare Ramsey coherence time of 660 $\pm$ 9 $μ$s, extended to 1.750 $\pm$ 0.033 ms by spin echo. These results demonstrate that integrated visible-wavelength narrow-linewidth photonic lasers can meet the phase-noise requirements for coherent trapped ion quantum logic, providing a path for scalable optical systems integrated within trapped ion quantum processors.

</details>


### [110] [Propaq: A Python package for Heisenberg Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07730)
*Hrishikesh Belagali,Ryan LaRose*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Propaq is a performant and flexible Python package for Heisenberg propagation-based classical simulation of quantum circuits, compatible with Qiskit and Cirq, featuring unique capabilities such as basis-agnostic propagation, hybrid Schrödinger-Heisenberg simulation, custom truncation, noisy simulation methods, and detailed logging for accuracy/runtime/memory analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for a flexible and performant classical simulation tool for quantum circuits using Heisenberg propagation techniques that goes beyond existing state-of-the-art packages with additional research-oriented features.

Method: The package implements Heisenberg propagation techniques and offers distinctive features including propagation in arbitrary bases, hybrid Schrödinger-Heisenberg simulation, custom truncation strategies, noisy simulation support, and comprehensive logging for analyzing simulation accuracy, runtime, and memory usage. It integrates with major quantum software frameworks such as Qiskit and Cirq.

Result: Propaq matches or outperforms similar state-of-the-art packages for Heisenberg propagation while providing additional distinguishing simulation and research capabilities beyond standard functionality.

Conclusion: Propaq is presented as a performant, flexible tool with unique features for quantum circuit simulation and research, including detailed documentation of its usage and backend implementation.

Abstract: We introduce Propaq, a performant and flexible Python package implementing Heisenberg propagation techniques for classically simulating quantum circuits. Propaq is compatible with major quantum software packages like Qiskit and Cirq, and matches or outperforms similar state-of-the-art packages for Heisenberg propagation. Beyond this, Propaq has multiple distinguishing features for simulation and research. These include Heisenberg propagation in any basis, hybrid Schrödinger-Heisenberg simulation, custom truncation and noisy simulation methods, and detailed logging information for analyzing simulation accuracy, runtime, and memory usage. We describe these features, how to use Propaq for research, and its backend implementation.

</details>


### [111] [QROB: Quantifying Realization Overhead in Quantum Compilation via Reverse Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07776)
*Jintao Li,Kaiqi Li,Rui Wang,Yilun Zhao,Kaixuan Huang,Ying Wang,Jialin Zhang,Zheng-An Wang,Xiaoming Sun,Heng Fan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: QROB is a scalable reverse-construction benchmarking methodology that generates quantum compilation instances backward from directly realizable configurations, providing compiler-independent reference paths for evaluating NISQ SWAP routing, fault-tolerant lattice-surgery scheduling, and quantum memory-access scheduling across 9 to 156 qubit systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum compilation evaluations at scale lack calibrated references for realization overhead, making it difficult to assess how well existing compilers reconcile idealized program interaction topologies with hardware locality constraints.

Method: QROB generates compilation instances backward from directly realizable configurations, retaining the inverse paths as feasible and compiler-independent reference solutions. This provides a common evaluation substrate across NISQ SWAP routing, fault-tolerant lattice-surgery scheduling, and capacity-constrained quantum memory-access scheduling.

Result: Across 9 to 156 qubit systems: NISQ compilers incur up to 24.1x reference SWAP cost and fault-tolerant compilers up to 7.0x reference makespan; a router trained on QROB references outperforms Qiskit SABRE on 84.8% of real-world circuits; QROB reference realizations achieve median mirror-circuit survival rate 1.65x that of full Qiskit O3 on three 156-qubit IBM Heron-r2 processors.

Conclusion: QROB serves as both a diagnostic benchmark exposing substantial realization gaps in existing compilers and a supervision source for data-driven compilation, demonstrating that closing algorithmic compilation gaps translates directly into physical fidelity gains.

Abstract: Quantum compilation reconciles a program's idealized interaction topology with hardware locality constraints, yet evaluations at scale lack calibrated references for realization overhead. We present QROB, a scalable reverse-construction methodology that generates compilation instances backward from directly realizable configurations, retaining the inverse paths as feasible, compiler-independent references. QROB provides a common evaluation substrate for NISQ SWAP routing and fault-tolerant lattice-surgery scheduling, while extending its reference-preserving principle to capacity-constrained quantum memory-access scheduling.
  Across systems ranging from 9 to 156 qubits, evaluations highlight QROB's utility as both a diagnostic benchmark and a data source. First, for compiler characterization, QROB reveals substantial realization gaps in existing tools, with NISQ compilers incurring up to 24.1x the reference SWAP cost and fault-tolerant compilers requiring up to 7.0x the reference makespan. Second, as a supervision source for data-driven compilation, a router trained on QROB references outperforms Qiskit SABRE on 84.8% of real-world application circuits. Finally, on real hardware, QROB reference realizations achieve a median mirror-circuit survival rate 1.65x that of full Qiskit O3 compilations across three 156-qubit IBM Heron-r2 processors, demonstrating that closing algorithmic compilation gaps translates directly into physical fidelity gains.

</details>


### [112] [Realization of decoherence-induced averaged symmetry-protected topological phases on quantum processors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07831)
*Ruizhe Shen,Xue-Jia Yu,Ching Hua Lee*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper experimentally realizes decoherence-induced averaged symmetry-protected topological (ASPT) order on programmable quantum processors. Using sublattice-selective Pauli-Z dephasing via ancilla-assisted circuits on a 1D cluster state, the authors observe symmetry conversion, nontrivial Renyi-2 string correlators via SWAP readout, and spectral weight redistribution among reduced-stabilizer sectors while preserving the twofold pairing of the half-chain entanglement spectrum. This establishes a gate-based route for engineering mixed-state topological quantum matter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SPT phases are traditionally defined for pure states with exact symmetries, but open quantum systems produce mixed states where average symmetry only emerges after averaging over microscopic trajectories. The authors aim to demonstrate that structured decoherence can serve as a programmable resource to realize such averaged SPT (ASPT) order on hardware.

Method: The authors start from a one-dimensional cluster SPT state and engineer sublattice-selective Pauli-Z dephasing using ancilla-assisted quantum circuits. They characterize the resulting ASPT order by measuring symmetry charge decay, a two-replica Renyi-2 string correlator through destructive SWAP readout, spectral weight redistribution among reduced-stabilizer sectors, and the half-chain entanglement spectrum.

Result: Symmetry conversion is observed through decay of the symmetry charge. The Renyi-2 string correlator remains nontrivial, revealing ASPT structure at the density-matrix level. Engineered dephasing redistributes spectral weight among reduced-stabilizer sectors while preserving the characteristic twofold pairing of the half-chain entanglement spectrum.

Conclusion: The work establishes a gate-based route to engineering and probing ASPT order, demonstrating that structured decoherence can be used as a programmable resource for realizing mixed-state topological quantum matter on quantum processors.

Abstract: Symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases are conventionally formulated for pure states protected by exact symmetries. In open quantum systems, however, decoherence generates mixed-state ensembles in which average symmetry can instead emerge only after averaging over microscopic trajectories. In this work, we realize decoherence-induced averaged SPT (ASPT) order on programmable quantum processors. Starting from a one-dimensional cluster SPT, we engineer sublattice-selective Pauli-$Z$ dephasing through ancilla-assisted quantum circuits. We observe the resulting symmetry conversion through the decay of the symmetry charge. Moreover, a two-replica Renyi-2 string correlator measured through destructive SWAP readout remains nontrivial, revealing the ASPT structure encoded at the density-matrix level. We further show that the engineered dephasing redistributes spectral weight among reduced-stabilizer sectors while preserving the characteristic twofold pairing of the half-chain entanglement spectrum. These results establish a gate-based route to engineering and probing ASPT order and demonstrate structured decoherence as a programmable resource for realizing mixed-state topological quantum matter.

</details>


### [113] [Snakes on a Hyper-Plane: Dynamical and Ontological Locality in Everettian Quantum Mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07860)
*Travis Norsen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper challenges the emerging consensus that Spacetime State Realism (SSR) Everettian Quantum Mechanics is dynamically local despite being ontologically non-separable, arguing that the theory's non-local beables (RDMs for non-localized regions) complicate dynamical locality formulations and may undermine its claimed compatibility with relativistic causality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To critically examine whether SSR EQM, as developed by Wallace and Timpson, can truly be considered compatible with relativistic causality despite its acknowledged ontological non-locality, since this compatibility claim rests on a specific formulation of dynamical locality that may not adequately handle the theory's non-local beables.

Method: The paper analyzes the ontological structure of SSR EQM—in which local beables are localized Reduced Density Matrices and non-local beables are RDMs for non-localized regions—and assesses whether existing formulations of dynamical locality can accommodate the presence of such non-local beables without conflict with relativistic causal constraints.

Result: The analysis shows that the non-local beables posited by SSR EQM introduce complications for extant formulations of dynamical locality, suggesting that the theory may not be as straightforwardly compatible with relativistic causality as the emerging consensus maintains.

Conclusion: Contrary to the emerging consensus, SSR Everettian Quantum Mechanics should perhaps not be regarded as fully compatible with relativistic causality, given the complications that its non-local beables introduce for dynamical locality.

Abstract: An apparently emerging consensus holds that Everettian Quantum Mechanics (EQM), in the Spacetime State Realism (SSR) version of Wallace and Timpson, involves ontological non-locality (i.e., non-separability) but is fully compatible with the dynamical notion of locality usually taken to be required by relativity. The claim of dynamical locality is based on the fact that the local beables postulated by the theory (namely, Reduced Density Matrices, RDMs, associated with localized regions) are not affected by interventions outside their past light cones. However, the theory's non-separability means that it posits non-local beables (namely RDMs for non-localized regions) in addition to the local ones. We argue here that the involvement of non-local beables introduces complications for extant formulations of dynamical locality and at least suggests, contrary to the emerging consensus, that SSR EQM should perhaps not be considered compatible with relativistic causality after all.

</details>


### [114] [Quantifying Margenau--Hill Nonclassicality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07869)
*Sudip Chakrabarty*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces logarithmic Margenau-Hill quasiprobability (MHQ) negativity as a quantifier of nonclassicality, derives rigorous lower bounds from low-order moments (with the fourth-moment bound being strongest among even-moment bounds), provides tight upper bounds for qubits, identifies extremal states, and shows moments admit exact multicopy representations estimable via interferometry or classical shadows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify nonclassicality via the Margenau-Hill quasiprobability distribution using only low-order moments, avoiding the need to reconstruct the full quasiprobability distribution.

Method: Introduces a logarithmic MHQ negativity measure, derives a hierarchy of lower bounds based on low-order moments, analyzes qubit-specific tight bounds and positivity thresholds, and demonstrates that relevant moments can be estimated via multicopy representations using interferometry or classical shadow techniques.

Result: Established a moment-based framework for quantifying MHQ negativity: the fourth-moment bound is strongest among even-moment bounds; tight upper bound and extremal states are identified for qubits; an optimal fourth-moment positivity threshold is derived for arbitrary qubit observable pairs; moments admit exact multicopy representations.

Conclusion: Nonclassicality quantified by MHQ negativity can be effectively extracted from a finite set of low-order observables through moment-based bounds, providing a practical and experimentally accessible framework without full distribution reconstruction.

Abstract: Quasiprobability distributions offer a useful way of describing nonclassical features of quantum systems through their departure from classical probability theory. In this work, we investigate the quantification of nonclassicality associated with the Margenau--Hill quasiprobability (MHQ) distribution using its moments, without requiring reconstruction of the full distribution. First we introduce the logarithmic MHQ negativity as a quantifier of nonclassicality, and then derive a hierarchy of rigorous lower bounds in terms of low-order moments. Within the resulting hierarchy, the fourth-moment bound is the strongest among the bounds based on even moments. For qubits, we further derive a tight upper bound on the exact MHQ negativity and identify the corresponding extremal state. We also obtain an optimal positivity threshold based on the fourth moment for arbitrary pairs of qubit observables. Finally, we show that the relevant moments admit exact multicopy representations, providing a route to their estimation using interferometry or classical shadow techniques. Our results establish a moment-based framework for extracting quantitative information about MHQ negativity from a finite set of low-order observables.

</details>


### [115] [Measurement-induced remote activation of nonclassicality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07875)
*Sudip Chakrabarty*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that nonclassicality—defined via nonpositive quasiprobability distributions like Wigner and Kirkwood-Dirac distributions—can be remotely activated through measurement-induced steering. A correlated partner's local measurement alone can drive a party with a classically reduced state into a genuinely nonclassical conditional state. The authors derive closed-form expressions for two-qubit systems, prove optimality of two-outcome projective measurements under unbiased conditions, analyze noise effects, and extend the results to hybrid qubit-oscillator systems showing Wigner negativity generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish measurement-induced steering as an operational resource for remotely activating nonclassicality, showing that quantum correlations can be leveraged so that one party's local measurement can create nonclassical conditional states in another party whose reduced state is classical.

Method: The authors: (1) define nonclassicality through nonpositivity of quasiprobability distributions (Wigner and Kirkwood-Dirac); (2) derive closed-form expressions for maximal single-branch effect and probability-weighted average under best two-outcome projective measurements in two-qubit systems; (3) prove optimality over all measurements when the shared state has no local bias; (4) analyze local noise effects, distinguishing non-unital from unital channels; (5) connect the activated nonclassicality to anomalous weak values; (6) use an exactly solvable hybrid qubit-oscillator model to demonstrate Wigner negativity generation.

Result: Nonclassicality can be remotely activated via steering: classical reduced states become nonclassical conditional states under partner measurements. Closed forms are obtained for two-qubit systems with optimal projective measurements proven under unbiased conditions. Non-unital local noise misaligned with the party's axis can enhance the effect, while unital channels never help. The nonclassicality unlocks anomalous weak values unavailable to unsteered states, and the same mechanism generates Wigner negativity in hybrid qubit-oscillator systems.

Conclusion: Remote activation of nonclassicality is a genuine operational phenomenon enabled by measurement-induced steering, offering a new resource framework for generating and detecting quantum advantages beyond local state properties.

Abstract: Nonclassicality, defined through nonpositivity of quasiprobability distributions such as the Wigner and Kirkwood-Dirac distributions, underlies key quantum advantages. We show that it can be remotely activated: local measurements by a correlated partner alone can drive a party whose reduced state is classical into a genuinely nonclassical conditional state. For two-qubit systems we obtain closed forms for the maximal single-branch effect and for its probability-weighted average under the best two-outcome projective measurement, the latter proven optimal over all measurements when the shared state carries no local bias. Local noise on the steered party, evaluated relative to its own noise-updated reference basis, can enhance the effect when non-unital and misaligned with that party's axis, whereas no unital channel ever helps. This nonclassicality is shown to unlock, for the same reference observable, anomalous weak values unavailable to the unsteered state under arbitrary postselection. An exactly solvable hybrid qubit-oscillator model shows the same mechanism generates Wigner negativity. These results identify measurement-induced steering as an operational resource for remotely activating nonclassicality.

</details>


### [116] [Riemannian optimization for linear optical problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07890)
*Pablo V. Parellada*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The authors derive a closed analytical formula for the Riemannian gradient of differentiable functions over multiport interferometer unitaries, enabling efficient Riemannian optimization (gradient descent, BFGS) to find optimal interferometer configurations for quantum applications like heralded state preparation. Their method improves success probabilities for NOON and photon catalysis state preparations and is orders of magnitude faster than existing approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many linear optics quantum applications require finding optimal configurations of multiport interferometers, such as maximizing fidelity and success probability in heralded state preparations. Existing methods are computationally slow, limiting the scale of problems (number of modes and photons) that can be addressed.

Method: The paper derives a closed analytical formula for the Riemannian gradient of any differentiable function defined over the manifold of interferometer unitaries. This gradient is then employed in Riemannian optimization algorithms, including gradient descent and BFGS (Broyden–Fletcher–Goldfarb–Shanno), to search for optimal interferometer setups.

Result: The optimizer improves some success probabilities previously reported in the literature for NOON state and photon catalysis preparations. Furthermore, the proposed method is shown to be orders of magnitude faster than other optimization methods in the literature, enabling the exploration of linear optical problems involving more modes and photons.

Conclusion: The derived Riemannian gradient formula combined with Riemannian optimization provides a fast and effective framework for finding optimal multiport interferometer configurations, advancing the scalability of linear optical state preparation and related quantum applications.

Abstract: Many applications of linear optics in quantum science involve searching for some optimal configuration of a multiport interferometer. For example, to find heralded state preparations, one can optimize the interferometer to maximize the fidelity and success probability of the state preparation. In this paper, we derive a closed analytical formula for the Riemannian gradient of any differentiable function defined over the interferometer unitaries. We use this gradient in Riemannian optimization algorithms, such as gradient descent or BFGS, to find the optimal setup of the interferometer. As an interesting use case, we search for linear optical state preparations, improving some of the success probabilities reported in the literature for NOON or photon catalysis preparations. Finally, we show that our optimizer is orders of magnitude faster than other methods in the literature, opening the door for solving linear optical problems involving more modes and photons.

</details>


### [117] [Few-Body Decay Dynamics in Colloidal CsPbI3 Quantum-Dot Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07892)
*Emma Daggett,Christian M. Lange,Nicholas Favate,Ankit Kundu,Ishita Agarwal,Arya D. Keni,Adhyyan S. Mansukhani,Christina W. Li,Libai Huang,Jonathan D. Hood*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper characterizes CsPbI3 perovskite quantum dots from isolated emitters to self-assembled clusters of up to ten dots. Using photon autocorrelation, blinking statistics, and brightness measurements, the authors estimate emitter counts in clusters. Clusters with two or more emitters show biexponential decay with a faster short lifetime that decreases with emitter number. Despite single-dot emission being far from the lifetime-limited regime (as shown by photon-correlation Fourier spectroscopy), emitter-number-dependent decay dynamics still emerge, establishing these clusters as a platform for studying collective optical behavior between single-emitter and ensemble limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how collective optical behavior emerges as the number of coupled emitters increases, transitioning from isolated single-emitter physics to the ensemble limit in colloidal perovskite quantum dots.

Method: Photon autocorrelation (g(2) measurements), blinking statistics, and emission brightness are combined to estimate the number of emitters in each self-assembled cluster (up to ten dots). Time-resolved photoluminescence and photon-correlation Fourier spectroscopy are employed to characterize decay dynamics and coherence from isolated dots to clusters.

Result: Clusters containing two or more emitters exhibit biexponential decay with a short lifetime component that decreases as the number of emitters increases, plus an additional long-lived component. Although single-dot emission is far from the lifetime-limited (Fourier-limited) regime, emitter-number-dependent decay dynamics are still observed in this low-coherence regime.

Conclusion: Self-assembled CsPbI3 perovskite quantum-dot clusters serve as a tunable platform for investigating how collective optical phenomena emerge between the single-emitter and ensemble limits, even when individual emitters are not in the lifetime-limited coherence regime.

Abstract: Colloidal perovskite quantum dots combine bright emission with the ability to self- assemble into closely spaced structures, enabling radiative dynamics to be studied from isolated emitters to few-dot clusters. Here, we characterize CsPbI3 perovskite quan- tum dots from isolated emitters to self-assembled clusters containing up to ten dots. We estimate the number of emitters in each cluster using a combination of photon autocorrelation, blinking statistics, and emission brightness. Compared with isolated dots, clusters containing two or more emitters exhibit biexponential decay, with a short lifetime that decreases with emitter number and an additional long-lived component.
