Photon-correlation Fourier spectroscopy shows that the single-dot emission remains far from the lifetime-limited regime. Nevertheless, we observe emitter-number-dependent decay dynamics even in this low-coherence regime. Together, these measurements es- tablish self-assembled perovskite quantum-dot clusters as a platform for studying how collective optical behavior emerges between the single-emitter and ensemble limits

</details>


### [118] [A Novel Steganography Scheme Using Quantum Hilbert Transform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07894)
*Nitin Jha,Abhishek Parakh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a finite-dimensional Quantum Hilbert Transform (QHT) as a unitary phase operator built on the Quantum Fourier Transform, and uses it to develop a quantum steganography scheme that embeds classical bits as weak signed phase perturbations of quantum cover states. Bob recovers the hidden message via binary state discrimination, block aggregation, and classical error-correcting decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phase-domain information hiding has not been fully explored for quantum systems, despite the main goal of steganography being to transmit hidden messages in legitimate-looking communication. The authors aim to address this gap by exploring phase-domain steganographic embedding in the quantum setting.

Method: The authors construct a finite-dimensional Quantum Hilbert Transform (QHT) as a unitary phase operator derived from the Quantum Fourier Transform. They then build a steganography scheme that embeds classical bits as weak signed phase perturbations of quantum cover states. The receiver (Bob) employs binary state discrimination followed by block aggregation and classical error-correcting decoding to extract the hidden message.

Result: The paper presents a novel QHT-based quantum steganography framework that enables embedding and recovery of classical hidden messages through phase-domain perturbations of quantum states, with a structured recovery pipeline involving discrimination, aggregation, and error correction.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates a viable approach to phase-domain quantum steganography using the Quantum Hilbert Transform, expanding the tools available for covert quantum communication.

Abstract: The main goal of steganography is to transmit hidden messages in legitimate-looking communication messages. Phase-domain information hiding, however, has not been fully explored for quantum systems. This work introduces a finite-dimensional Quantum Hilbert Transform (QHT) as a unitary phase operator based on the Quantum Fourier Transform. Using this construction, we develop a QHT-based quantum steganography scheme that embeds classical bits as weak signed phase perturbations of quantum cover states. Bob recovers the hidden message through binary state discrimination, block aggregation, and classical error-correcting decoding.

</details>


### [119] [Quantum Wavefunction Augmentation via Variational Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07972)
*Sonaldeep Halder,Chayan Patra,Rahul Maitra*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Q-WAVE is a hybrid quantum-classical method that combines hardware-sampled determinants (from SqDRIFT Krylov circuits) and CISD determinants with a β-annealed variational autoencoder to iteratively expand the determinant basis toward the variational ground state, achieving sub-millihartree accuracy on strongly correlated molecular systems including H2O and N2 dissociation, and demonstrating scalability to 52-qubit ethylene and 60-qubit Cr2 systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD) degrades in accuracy for strongly correlated regimes because the relevant determinant space exceeds what finite-shot quantum sampling can capture, limiting its practical utility for quantum-centric supercomputing.

Method: Q-WAVE merges determinants sampled via SqDRIFT Krylov circuits and CISD determinants, feeding them into a custom β-annealed variational autoencoder that learns the wavefunction's primary support structure in a continuous latent space. The VAE generates new dominant determinants beyond any fixed excitation hierarchy, and this process is iteratively repeated to expand the basis toward the variational ground state.

Result: The method achieves sub-millihartree accuracy versus full configuration interaction for H2O and N2 dissociation. It scales to a 52-qubit ethylene system achieving sub-millihartree accuracy relative to CCSD(T), and a 60-qubit Cr2 stress test attains chemical accuracy after a final perturbative correction.

Conclusion: The compact wavefunctions produced by Q-WAVE exceed what can be extracted from raw hardware samples alone, establishing generative machine learning as an effective tool to expand quantum-sampled determinant spaces in strongly correlated systems.

Abstract: Sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD) has emerged as a promising route for quantum-centric supercomputing, relying on classical diagonalization of the molecular Hamiltonian within a hardware-sampled determinant subspace. However, its accuracy degrades in strongly correlated regimes where the relevant determinant space exceeds what finite-shot sampling can capture. In this work, we introduce Quantum Wavefunction Augmentation via Variational Autoencoders (Q-WAVE), a hybrid method that combines determinants sampled via SqDRIFT Krylov circuits and configuration interaction singles and doubles (CISD) determinants with generative machine learning. Using a custom $β$-annealed variational autoencoder (VAE) model, Q-WAVE iteratively expands this basis toward the variational ground state. The VAE learns the wavefunction's primary support structure from the combined hardware and CISD seed in a continuous latent space, generating new dominant determinants beyond any fixed excitation hierarchy. The resulting compact wavefunction exceeds what can be extracted from raw hardware samples alone. We demonstrate sub-millihartree accuracy compared to full configuration interaction for $\text{H}_2\text{O}$ and $\text{N}_2$ dissociation. Finally, we establish Q-WAVE's scalability on a 52-qubit ethylene system (achieving sub-millihartree accuracy versus CCSD(T)) and a highly correlated 60-qubit $\text{Cr}_2$ stress test that attains chemical accuracy upon a final perturbative correction.

</details>


### [120] [Learned Diffractive Optics for Quantum-Optimal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08014)
*Matthew J. Filipovich,Alexander Duplinskii,A. I. Lvovsky*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper applies learned diffractive optics (diffractive optical neural networks) to quantum-limited visual sensing tasks—state discrimination and parameter estimation—for coherent and diffraction-limited incoherent light fields under constrained photon budgets, showing that these physically realizable networks outperform standard measurements and approach quantum limits without prior knowledge of optimal measurement strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While quantum mechanics establishes ultimate bounds on photon-limited sensing, practical measurement schemes that attain these bounds are only known in special cases—particularly lacking for visual sensing problems where the goal is to infer features of distant objects from the spatial structure of emitted or reflected light fields, due to the potentially complex structure of such objects and fields making optimal measurement construction challenging.

Method: The authors apply learned diffractive optics (diffractive optical neural networks) to state discrimination and parameter estimation of coherent and diffraction-limited incoherent light fields under a restricted photon budget. The diffractive networks are optimized directly on each task's figure of merit, without prior knowledge of the optimal measurement, and are physically realizable.

Result: The optimized diffractive optical neural networks substantially outperform standard measurements and approach the quantum limits for a given number of photons as well as in the asymptotic limit.

Conclusion: Learned diffractive optics provide a practical, physically realizable approach to approaching quantum-limited visual sensing without requiring prior knowledge of optimal measurement strategies, bridging the gap between fundamental quantum limits and achievable performance in photon-limited imaging tasks.

Abstract: Quantum mechanics sets the ultimate bounds on photon-limited sensing, yet practical measurements attaining these bounds are known only in special cases. This is particularly the case for visual sensing problems, where the goal is to infer features of a distant object based on the spatial structure of the light field it emits or reflects. Because of the potentially complex structure of such objects and fields, constructing optimal measurements on them is a challenging task. Here, we apply learned diffractive optics to state discrimination and parameter estimation of coherent and diffraction-limited incoherent light fields under a restricted photon budget. Optimized directly on each task's figure of merit, without prior knowledge of the optimal measurement, the physically realizable diffractive optical neural networks substantially outperform standard measurements and approach the quantum limits for a given number of photons as well as in the asymptotic limit.

</details>


### [121] [Sklar's Theorem and Quantum State Reconstruction from All-Context Dependence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08035)
*Yi-Yu Lin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper applies Sklar's theorem (separating marginals from dependence structure via copulas) to Born statistics from local quantum measurements on two-qubit states. The dependence structure (nuclei) over all local binary projective measurement contexts determines non-product two-qubit states up to a discrete double-spin-flip ambiguity, and combining the resulting scalar optimization with quantum total correlation reproduces global quantum discord.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a copula-based framework for quantum correlations, connecting classical dependence theory to quantum measurement statistics and quantum discord.

Method: Application of Sklar's theorem to Born statistics of local binary projective measurements on two-qubit states; analyzing dependence nuclei (copulas) over measurement contexts to determine state uniqueness.

Result: Non-product two-qubit states are determined up to at most a discrete double-spin-flip ambiguity by the collection of dependence nuclei across all local binary projective measurement contexts; the scalar optimization over dependence plus quantum total correlation yields global quantum discord.

Conclusion: The copula/dependence-nucleus viewpoint provides an equivalent characterization of quantum discord for two-qubit systems, bridging Sklar's theorem with quantum information theory.

Abstract: Sklar's theorem separates the marginals of a joint distribution from its dependence structure. We apply this viewpoint to Born statistics generated by local quantum measurements. For non-product two-qubit states, the dependence nuclei over all local binary projective measurement contexts determine the state up to at most a discrete double-spin-flip ambiguity. The corresponding scalar optimization, combined with the quantum total correlation, reproduces global quantum discord.

</details>


### [122] [Quantum magnonics with engineered dissipative and coherent interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08050)
*Debsuvra Mukhopadhyay,Jayakrishnan M. P. Nair,Girish S. Agarwal*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This Perspective reviews the evolution of cavity magnonics beyond conventional coherent spin-photon hybridization, examining how dissipative coupling, anti-PT symmetry, Floquet engineering, parametric driving, and active gain enable new regimes of collective dynamics, quantum state engineering, and hybrid quantum technologies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cavity magnonics is expanding beyond conventional coherent spin-photon hybridization toward controlled engineering of dissipative/coherent interactions, Floquet dynamics, and quantum fluctuations. The aim is to articulate how these new ingredients reshape collective dynamics and open avenues for quantum state engineering, transport, and sensing.

Method: The paper adopts a Perspective format, analyzing: (1) dissipative coupling and anti-PT symmetry for long-lived collective modes and spectral response, (2) Floquet engineering for nonreciprocal transport and phase-controlled interactions, (3) quantum magnonics including squeezed/entangled magnon states and Schrödinger-cat state preparation, and (4) parametric driving and active gain effects on hybrid susceptibilities.

Result: The review identifies that dissipative coupling and anti-PT symmetry enable long-lived collective modes, Floquet engineering enables nonreciprocal transport and phase-controlled interactions, quantum magnonics extends to squeezed thermal magnons and cat-state schemes, and parametric driving/active gain amplify magnon-photon response.

Conclusion: Cavity magnonics exceeds conventional polariton physics and establishes itself as a versatile platform for sensing, transduction, spin transport, and hybrid quantum technologies.

Abstract: Cavity magnonics is moving beyond the conventional paradigm of coherent spin--photon hybridization toward the controlled engineering of dissipative and coherent interactions, Floquet dynamics, and quantum fluctuations. This Perspective examines how these ingredients reshape collective dynamics and open new avenues for quantum state engineering, transport, and sensing. We discuss the role of dissipative coupling and anti-$\mathcal{PT}$ symmetry in enabling long-lived collective modes and enhanced spectral response, and highlight Floquet engineering as a means of engineering nonreciprocal transport and phase-controlled interactions. We then review recent progress in quantum magnonics, ranging from squeezed and entangled magnon states, including the squeezing of thermal magnons, to prospective schemes for preparing magnonic Schrödinger-cat states. We also discuss how parametric driving and active gain strongly modify hybrid susceptibilities and amplify magnon--photon response. These advances expand the scope of cavity magnonics beyond conventional polariton physics, establishing it as a versatile platform for sensing, transduction, spin transport, and hybrid quantum technologies.

</details>


### [123] [From Bits to Qubits: The Theory and Practice of Quantum Data Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08058)
*Xiao-Ming Zhang,Arthur G. Rattew,Bujiao Wu,Georgios Styliaris,Xiaoming Sun,Bálint Koczor,Xiao Yuan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper is a comprehensive review of quantum data encoding, covering the concepts, algorithms, and practical considerations for encoding classical data into quantum systems—from access models like quantum state preparation, unitary synthesis, QRAM, and block encoding—to circuit resources, fault-tolerant implementation, and structured data representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Encoding classical data into quantum systems is a foundational yet critical bottleneck for nearly all quantum algorithms; realizing practical quantum advantage requires a thorough understanding of the costs, tradeoffs, and best practices for different encoding approaches.

Method: The review systematically surveys quantum data encoding from early foundations to recent advances, analyzing common access models (quantum state preparation, unitary synthesis, QRAM, block encoding), evaluating circuit size/depth, space-time tradeoffs, and non-Clifford resources for fault tolerance, while paying special attention to structured data such as sparse data, Boolean functions, and tensor networks.

Result: The paper provides a comprehensive catalog of encoding methods with their resource requirements and tradeoffs, discusses the roles of different access models in quantum algorithms, and highlights how structured data representations can reduce encoding overhead.

Conclusion: Quantum data encoding plays a pivotal role in quantum computing, and continued advances in encoding techniques—particularly for structured data—will be essential for enabling practical quantum advantage.

Abstract: Encoding classical data into quantum systems is a foundational step in the execution of nearly all quantum algorithms, and a critical bottleneck in realizing practical quantum advantage. This review provides a comprehensive account of the concepts, algorithms, and practical considerations associated with quantum data encoding. We trace the development from its early conceptual foundations to recent advances, considering commonly used access models, such as quantum state preparation, unitary synthesis, QRAM and block encoding. We survey the circuit size, depth, space-time tradeoffs, as well as non-Clifford resources required for fault-tolerant implementation. We also discuss the roles of different access models in quantum algorithms. Special attention is given to structured data, such as sparse data, Boolean functions and data represented by tensor networks. This review bridges theory and applications, serving both as a pedagogical guide for newcomers and as a reference for active researchers. We also highlight the pivotal role of quantum data encoding in quantum computing and provide insights into future directions that will enable quantum advantage.

</details>


### [124] [Bottom-Up Design of Quantum Optical Experiments Using Discrete Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08073)
*Isaac L. Huidobro-Meezs,Simón Paiva-Ortega,Rodrigo A. Vargas-Hernández*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Grinch, a bottom-up reward-driven generative framework for designing quantum optical experiments by learning to sample optical graphs directly from fidelity-based rewards, without needing pre-existing training data. It demonstrates the framework on multipartite entangled states, graph/cluster states, and nonlocal photonic-gate targets, finding multiple high-fidelity optical graphs including asymptotic solutions for non-exactly-generatable states and nonlocal Toffoli gates with two ancillas. Hardware-constraint objectives produce alternative CNOT and Toffoli gate constructions under restricted ancilla connectivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum optical experiment design requires searching over discrete circuit topologies and continuous parameters, often with multiple realizations of the same target state. Existing graph-based methods typically optimize a dense graph and prune toward a single circuit, lacking flexibility and constraint-aware exploration.

Method: Grinch is a bottom-up reward-driven generative framework that learns to sample optical graphs directly from fidelity-based rewards, without relying on a pre-existing training dataset. The approach integrates hardware-connectivity constraints directly into the graph search process.

Result: The framework produces multiple high-fidelity optical graphs for multipartite entangled states, graph/cluster states, and nonlocal photonic-gate targets. It identifies asymptotic solutions for states not exactly generatable by graphs, constructs nonlocal Toffoli gates requiring two ancillas, and yields alternative CNOT/Toffoli gate designs under restricted ancilla-node connectivity.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates a reward-driven approach to quantum optical inverse design that explores alternative circuit topologies while directly incorporating connectivity and hardware constraints into the search, offering a flexible alternative to pruning-based methods.

Abstract: Designing quantum optical experiments requires searching over discrete circuit topologies and continuous parameters, often with multiple realizations of the same target state. Graph-based methods commonly address this problem by optimizing a dense graph and pruning it toward a single circuit. We introduce \texttt{Grinch}, a bottom-up reward-driven generative framework that learns to sample optical graphs directly from fidelity-based rewards without relying on a pre-existing training dataset. We demonstrate the framework on multipartite entangled states, graph and cluster states, and nonlocal photonic-gate targets, obtaining multiple high-fidelity optical graphs. We identify asymptotic solutions for states that cannot be generated exactly by graphs, as well as nonlocal Toffoli gate constructions requiring two ancillas. Our work presents hardware-constraint objectives, which lead to alternative constructions of CNOT and Toffoli gates when connections between ancilla nodes are restricted. These findings illustrate a reward-driven approach to quantum optical inverse design, exploring alternative circuit topologies while directly incorporating connectivity constraints into the search process.

</details>


### [125] [Spectral Sign Implication for Quantum Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08099)
*Kenji Tokuo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies a spectral sign implication operation for quantum logic, defined via nonnegative spectral projections of the difference between two projection operators. It satisfies key implicative conditions, is uniquely characterized among continuous Borel constructions by entailment/contraposition/falsity axioms, and is linked to optimal Helstrom discrimination between subspace states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard polynomial quantum implications lack certain desirable logical properties or require commuting projections. The authors aim to construct a quantum implication for arbitrary pairs of projections that satisfies classical implicative axioms, drawing on the spectral structure of their difference rather than polynomial algebra.

Method: The authors define the spectral sign implication using the nonnegative spectral projection of (consequent antecedent). They verify classical implication agreement on commuting projections and check satisfaction of Hardegree's implicative conditions, contraposition, and falsity. They prove a uniqueness theorem within a class of Borel constructions under axioms (entailment, contraposition, falsity), with a measurable-branch correspondence. In finite dimensions, they relate the operation to optimal projective tests in Helstrom discrimination.

Result: The spectral sign implication satisfies four minimal implicative conditions, contraposition, and falsity, agrees with classical material implication on commuting projections, and is uniquely determined among continuous Borel constructions by entailment/contraposition/falsity. The value of the implication need not lie in the ortholattice generated by its arguments. In finite dimensions it is the largest acceptance projection among optimal projective tests for Helstrom discrimination with rank-proportional priors.

Conclusion: The spectral sign implication bridges quantum logic with the operator theory of two projections and binary quantum state discrimination, offering a canonical logical connective that is axiomatically characterized and operationally meaningful in quantum decision theory.

Abstract: We study the spectral sign implication for quantum logic, defined by the nonnegative spectral projection of the operator obtained by subtracting the antecedent projection from the consequent projection. The construction agrees with classical material implication on commuting projections and compares arbitrary pairs of projections through the spectral structure of their difference. It satisfies Hardegree's four minimal implicative conditions, his law of contraposition, and a falsity condition. It differs from the standard polynomial implications in that its value need not belong to the ortholattice generated by its arguments. Within a uniform class of Borel constructions for pairs of projections, the operations satisfying entailment, contraposition, and the falsity condition correspond exactly to measurable choices of spectral branch. In the continuous subclass, these three conditions determine the spectral sign implication uniquely. In finite dimensions, the operation is also the largest among the acceptance projections of optimal projective tests for Helstrom discrimination between subspace states with priors proportional to rank. These results connect quantum implication with the operator theory of two projections and binary quantum discrimination.

</details>


### [126] [Quantum algorithm for PageRank computation through multistep quantum resonant transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08140)
*Chuqing Wang,Hefeng Wang,Hua Xiang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a quantum algorithm that encodes the PageRank vector of the Google matrix into a quantum state using multistep quantum resonant transition (mQRT), achieved by decomposing the web graph into nested subgraphs and constructing a Hamiltonian evolution path from a simple initial Hamiltonian to the problem Hamiltonian, requiring only one ancillary qubit with runtime proportional to the number of steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PageRank is a critical algorithm for ranking web pages, but computing it for large-scale networks is computationally expensive. Quantum computing offers potential speedups for obtaining the PageRank vector, and there is a need for efficient quantum algorithms that can encode the PageRank vector as a quantum state for large-scale networks.

Method: The algorithm encodes the PageRank vector in the ground state of a problem Hamiltonian associated with the Google matrix. The web graph is divided into a series of nested subgraphs, and a sequence of intermediate Hamiltonians is constructed based on these subgraphs, forming a Hamiltonian evolution path. The ground state is reached by successively passing through ground states of intermediate Hamiltonians via quantum resonant transitions (QRT) step by step. The algorithm requires only one ancillary qubit, and its runtime is proportional to the number of steps in the evolution path.

Result: The algorithm successfully obtains a quantum state encoding the PageRank vector using mQRT with only one ancillary qubit. The runtime scales linearly with the number of steps in the Hamiltonian evolution path, offering an efficient approach for large-scale network PageRank computation.

Conclusion: The proposed mQRT-based quantum algorithm provides a new and efficient method for preparing the quantum state of the PageRank vector, potentially enabling quantum speedup for ranking in large-scale web graphs and complex networks.

Abstract: We present a quantum algorithm for obtaining a quantum state that encodes the PageRank vector of the Google matrix through multistep quantum resonant transition (mQRT). In the algorithm, the PageRank vector is encoded in the ground state of a problem Hamiltonian associated with the Google matrix. By dividing the web graph corresponding to the Google matrix into a series of subgraphs with nested structure, we construct a sequence of Hamiltonians based on the subgraphs to form a Hamiltonian evolution path from a simple initial Hamiltonian to the problem Hamiltonian. The ground state of the problem Hamiltonian is obtained by going through ground states of the intermediate Hamiltonians via QRT step by step. This algorithm requires only one ancillary qubit, and the runtime of the algorithm is proportional to the number of steps. It provides a new way for efficiently obtaining the quantum state of the PageRank vector of large-scale networks.

</details>


### [127] [Quantum-state texture measure and texture transformation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08163)
*Yufan Lin,Yu Guo,Fei He,Shuanping Du*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper extends the resource theory of quantum-state texture by solving deterministic state transformations under texture-free operations in the qubit case, proposing two new classes of texture measures (convex-function-based measures and texture cost), and proving a converse to the convex-roof extension framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum-state texture serves as a resource for gate characterization in universal circuits, but the existing theory lacks a complete resource-theoretic formulation—including state convertibility conditions and a broader class of texture measures.

Method: The authors examine pure-state to arbitrary-state convertibility under texture-free operations and fully solve the deterministic transformation problem between any two qubit states. They propose convex-function-based texture measures and define texture cost via the minimal cost of texture in pure states. They also prove that every texture monotone induces a non-negative function satisfying the same properties required for convex-roof extension, establishing a converse theorem.

Result: A complete solution to deterministic qubit texture transformations, two novel classes of texture measures, and a converse result showing that texture monotones generate valid convex-roof extension functions.

Conclusion: The work provides a relatively comprehensive resource-theoretic formulation of quantum-state texture, unifying convertibility, monotones, and convex-roof extensions within a single framework.

Abstract: Quantum-state texture quantifies structural irregularities of a state in a selected basis and has emerged as a valuable resource for gate characterization in universal circuits. Consequently, a class of contractive distance based texture measures and a framework for constructing convex-roof extended texture measure have been proposed. Here, we extend this theory in several directions. We examine the convertibility of a pure state to any state under texture-free operations, and fully solve the deterministic transformation problem between any two states in the qubit case. We then propose two classes of texture measures: convex-function-based measures and texture cost defined via minimal cost of texture contained in pure states. Moreover, we show that every texture monotone gives rise to a non-negative function which admits the same properties as the function used in the convex-roof extension---thus providing a converse to that construction. Our work thereby offers a relatively comprehensive resource-theoretic formulation of quantum-state texture.

</details>


### [128] [Saving resources through repeat-until-success positive-operator-valued-measure measurements in quantum computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08168)
*Hefeng Wang,Sixia Yu,Hua Xiang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a quantum computation approach guided by POVM measurements using one ancillary qubit coupled to a register of working qubits, where each computation step is a repeat-until-success procedure achieving deterministic desired states. By introducing intermediate measurements on the ancillary qubit, both qubit count and unitary operations can be polynomially reduced compared to deferring measurements to the end of the circuit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether intermediate measurements in quantum circuits, contrary to or complementing the principle of deferred measurement, can improve computational efficiency in terms of resource requirements (qubits and unitary operations).

Method: The approach couples one ancillary qubit to a register of working qubits, implements POVM measurements on working qubits via a unitary operation on the whole system followed by a projective measurement on the ancillary qubit. Each computation step is a repeat-until-success procedure, achieving desired states deterministically on the working qubits through POVM measurements with intermediate measurements on the ancillary qubit.

Result: The authors demonstrate that introducing intermediate measurements on the ancillary qubit in the computation process polynomially reduces both the number of qubits and the number of unitary operations required, compared to the case where intermediate measurements are deferred to the end of the computation (as per the principle of deferred measurement). A method for implementing the approach is also provided.

Conclusion: Intermediate measurements—rather than being merely deferable without consequence—can provide polynomial resource savings in quantum computation when POVM-guided protocols are employed, and the proposed implementation method makes this approach practically viable.

Abstract: We present a quantum computation approach in which computation is guided by positive-operator-valuedmeasure (POVM) measurements following a given computation path in multisteps. In this approach, one ancillary qubit is coupled to a register of working qubits, and a POVM measurement is implemented effectively on the working qubits by applying a unitary operation on the whole system followed by a projective measurement performed on the ancillary qubit. Each step of the computation is a repeat-until-success procedure such that the desired state of the step is obtained deterministically on the working qubits via POVM measurements. The principle of deferred measurement states that measurements can always be moved from an intermediate stage of a quantum circuit to the end of the circuit without affecting the efficiency of the computation.We demonstrate that in our approach, by introducing intermediate measurements on the ancillary qubit in the computation process, both the number of qubits and unitary operations can be reduced polynomially, compared to the case where the intermediate measurements are deferred to the end of the computation. We also provide a method for implementation of the approach.

</details>


### [129] [Classical Active-Space Hybrid Quantum Subspace Expansion (CASH-QSE): Quantum Corrections without Remeasuring the Classically Calculable Energy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08170)
*Artur F. Izmaylov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: CASH-QSE is a hybrid quantum-classical method that combines a classically computed CASSCF reference with orthogonal quantum states to evaluate molecular energies without overlap measurements, achieving chemical accuracy on H2O and N2 bond-stretching while dramatically reducing sampling costs and circuit depths compared to VQE/ADAPT-VQE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VQE-prepared wave functions often contain a large classically tractable component whose energy is unnecessarily sampled on a quantum device. While splitting the wave function into a classical reference and quantum states can avoid this sampling, near-linear dependence among components amplifies measurement errors and destabilizes energy estimates. A method is needed that retains the classical reference but constructs robustly orthogonal quantum states.

Method: CASH-QSE retains a CASSCF reference computed classically and uses occupation structure to construct quantum states that are exactly orthogonal to the reference and to each other. Because of this exact orthogonality, the energy is obtained from an ordinary Hermitian eigenvalue problem without overlap measurements, and the occupation constraints simplify the measured operators. Component selection is guided by full configuration interaction data.

Result: Tested along H2O and N2 bond-stretching coordinates (STO-3G for both, restricted cc-pVDZ for H2O), CASH-QSE reaches chemical accuracy while limiting the largest complete measurement circuits to a few hundred all-to-all logical CNOT gates. In favorable cases it reduces idealized final-energy sampling cost by several orders of magnitude relative to ADAPT-VQE.

Conclusion: CASH-QSE effectively leverages classically tractable components of strongly correlated wave functions to reduce quantum resource requirements—both in circuit depth and sampling cost—while preserving chemical accuracy, by sidestepping overlap measurements through exact orthogonality construction.

Abstract: The wave function prepared by a variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) can contain a substantial classically tractable component, yet its energy contribution is sampled on the quantum device. Representing the wave function as a combination of a classical reference and separately prepared quantum states allows the reference energy to be evaluated without quantum sampling. However, near-linear dependence among these components can amplify measurement errors and destabilize the resulting energy estimate. We introduce Classical Active-Space Hybrid Quantum Subspace Expansion (CASH-QSE), which retains a complete-active-space self-consistent field (CASSCF) reference classically and uses occupation structure to construct quantum states exactly orthogonal to the reference and to one another. The energy follows from an ordinary Hermitian eigenvalue problem without overlap measurements, while the same occupation constraints simplify the measured operators. Using full configuration interaction to guide component selection, we test CASH-QSE along H$_2$O and N$_2$ bond-stretching coordinates spanning weakly to strongly correlated regimes. The benchmarks use STO--3G for both molecules and a restricted cc-pVDZ orbital space for H$_2$O. CASH-QSE reaches chemical accuracy while limiting the largest complete measurement circuits to a few hundred all-to-all logical controlled-NOT gates. In favorable cases, it also reduces the idealized final-energy sampling cost by several orders of magnitude relative to VQE with an adaptive derivative-assembled pseudo-Trotter ansatz (ADAPT-VQE).

</details>


### [130] [Transversal Fanout for Fault Tolerant Distributed Quantum Computing: Analysis and Application](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08233)
*Seng W. Loke*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a resource-efficient method for implementing logical fanout operations in fault-tolerant distributed quantum computing by leveraging transversal operations on quantum error-correcting code (QECC) blocks, specifically using Bivariate Bicycle (BB) codes. The approach reduces non-local communication requirements while preserving the logical action of fanout, and is extended to distributed implementation of the global GCZ gate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Logical fanout operations across distributed quantum processors require substantial non-local communication when implemented directly between encoded blocks. This poses a bottleneck for distributed fault-tolerant quantum computation, motivating the need for more resource-efficient approaches that minimize non-local operations while preserving logical correctness.

Method: The authors exploit the structure of encoded quantum information blocks and the availability of transversal logical operations to construct distributed fanout circuits. The construction is developed for encoded quantum information and illustrated using Bivariate Bicycle (BB)-code blocks. Circuit synthesis and scheduling algorithms reduce non-local operations by leveraging transversal gate concurrency.

Result: The paper analyzes physical gate counts, entanglement requirements, circuit depth, and ancilla overhead for the proposed distributed fanout implementation. It demonstrates systematic reductions in required non-local operations and applies the method to realize a distributed GCZ global gate by exploiting concurrency in transversal distributed fanouts across BB-code-encoded logical qubits.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a systematic, resource-efficient method for implementing large logical fanout operations across distributed error-corrected quantum processors, with implications for scalable fault-tolerant distributed quantum computing architectures.

Abstract: We study a resource-efficient approach for implementing logical fanout operations in fault-tolerant distributed quantum computing using transversal operations on quantum error-correcting code blocks. Logical fanout, comprising multiple controlled-NOT operations from a common control qubit to target qubits located at remote nodes, is an important primitive for distributed quantum computation but can require substantial non-local communication when implemented directly between encoded blocks. We exploit the structure of encoded blocks and the availability of transversal logical operations to construct distributed fanout circuits that reduce the required non-local operations while preserving the logical action of the fanout operation. The construction is developed for encoded quantum information and illustrated using Bivariate Bicycle (BB)-code blocks. We analyze the resulting physical gate, entanglement, circuit-depth, and ancilla requirements. The approach provides a systematic method for implementing large logical fanout operations across distributed error-corrected quantum processors. Also, we study a distributed implementation of the global gate GCZ involving logical qubits (encoded using BB-code blocks), exploiting the concurrency in transversal distributed fanouts.

</details>


### [131] [Instantaneous-Frame Theory of Strongly Driven Parametric Gates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08348)
*Kentaro Kubo,Shinichi Inoue,Jushin Tei,Yinghao Ho,Yasunobu Nakamura,Hayato Goto*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper establishes an instantaneous-frame theory for parametric two-qubit gates in tunable-coupler superconducting processors, showing that these gates are better described as coherent rotations between instantaneous eigenstates (governed by non-Abelian Berry connections) rather than idle eigenstates. This theory achieves quantitative agreement with exact simulations across multiple coupler architectures and remains accurate where idle-frame descriptions fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional theories of parametric two-qubit gates formulated in the idle eigenbasis break down in the large-amplitude regime required for fast gate operations on superconducting quantum processors, necessitating a more accurate theoretical framework.

Method: The authors formulate parametric gates as coherent rotations between instantaneous eigenstates—a moving frame where the non-Abelian Berry connection governs transitions and compactly describes exchange and effective ZZ couplings. The theory is validated against exact numerical simulations across architectures including double-transmon, capacitively shunted double-transmon, and single-transmon couplers.

Result: The instantaneous-frame theory achieves quantitative agreement with exact numerical simulations across all representative tunable-coupler architectures, remains accurate in regimes where idle-frame descriptions fail, and substantially reduces the reliance on computationally demanding exact simulations.

Conclusion: The work establishes an instantaneous-frame theory of parametric gates that extends beyond conventional idle-frame models, offering a new perspective on driven quantum dynamics in superconducting processors.

Abstract: Parametric two-qubit gates using tunable couplers are a promising approach to scalable superconducting quantum processors. However, conventional theories formulated in the idle eigenbasis break down in the large-amplitude regime required for fast gate operations. Here, we show that parametric gates are more naturally and accurately described as coherent rotations between instantaneous eigenstates rather than idle eigenstates. This approach may appear counterintuitive, since instantaneous eigenbases are usually associated with adiabatic dynamics. However, they also form a natural moving frame for driven dynamics, where the non-Abelian Berry connection governs transitions and provides a compact description of the exchange and effective $ZZ$ couplings. We show that this theory achieves quantitative agreement with exact numerical simulations across representative tunable-coupler architectures, including double-transmon, capacitively shunted double-transmon, and single-transmon couplers. The description remains accurate even in regimes where idle-frame descriptions fail, while substantially reducing the reliance on computationally demanding exact simulations. These results establish an instantaneous-frame theory of parametric gates beyond conventional idle-frame models and provide a new perspective on driven quantum dynamics.

</details>


### [132] [Scaling-Enhanced Rapid Readout of a Qubit Ensemble Assisted by High-Frequency Detector Modes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08374)
*Hiroki Nakabayashi,Yuichiro Matsuzaki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates how readout time for collective quantum measurements of large qubit ensembles scales with the number of qubits N. It establishes an N^{-1/2} benchmark for detectors without ultraviolet high-frequency modes, then shows that detectors with unbounded high-frequency modes can surpass this benchmark, achieving scaling of N^{-1/(2-ν)} for 0<ν<2, where ν characterizes the detector's spectral structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the importance of rapid collective readout of large qubit ensembles, it remains unclear what physical factors determine how the readout time scales with the number of qubits N. Understanding this scaling is critical for quantum information processing and measurement applications.

Method: The authors establish a benchmark scaling of N^{-1/2} for a broad class of detectors lacking ultraviolet high-frequency modes. They then analyze detectors possessing unbounded high-frequency modes and demonstrate these can surpass the benchmark, deriving a general scaling law N^{-1/(2-ν)} parameterized by ν, which characterizes the spectral structure of the detector.

Result: Detectors with unbounded high-frequency modes can achieve a characteristic readout time scaling of N^{-1/(2-ν)} for 0<ν<2, exceeding the N^{-1/2} benchmark. This yields a readout time that decreases faster with N, providing a scaling advantage in collective quantum readout.

Conclusion: High-frequency detector modes constitute a resource for achieving scaling advantages in collective quantum readout, with the detector's spectral structure directly controlling the scaling exponent of the readout time.

Abstract: Quantum measurements of large qubit ensembles are often performed indirectly by coupling the ensemble to a detector and subsequently measuring the detector. Despite the importance of rapid collective readout, it remains unclear what determines how the readout time scales with the number of qubits $N$. Here, we first establish a benchmark $N^{-1/2}$ for a broad class of detectors without ultraviolet high-frequency modes. We then show that the detector with unbounded high-frequency modes can surpass this benchmark and yield a characteristic time scaling $N^{-1/(2-ν)}$ for $0 < ν< 2$, where $ν$ characterizes the spectral structure of the detector. Our results establish high-frequency detector modes as a resource for achieving a scaling advantage in collective quantum readout, with the detector spectrum directly controlling the scaling exponent of the readout time.

</details>


### [133] [Non-Markovian dynamics of a qubit on the zigzag edge of photonic graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08382)
*Enrico Di Benedetto*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Analytical study of a qubit coupled to the zigzag edge of a photonic honeycomb lattice, showing that the zero-energy flat band suppresses branch-cut-induced decay tails and instead produces damped Rabi oscillations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the dynamical properties of a qubit coupled to the edge of a photonic honeycomb lattice, which is expected to differ fundamentally from the bulk-coupling case due to the presence of edge-localized flat-band states.

Method: Uses a resolvent operator approach combined with exact lattice Green's functions to map the qubit-photon bound-state spectrum and complex poles, from which long-time scaling laws of the qubit population are derived.

Result: The zero-energy flat band connecting the Dirac points suppresses long-time branch-cut-induced decay tails that would otherwise arise, and instead leads to damped Rabi oscillations in the qubit population dynamics.

Conclusion: Qubits coupled to the zigzag edge exhibit qualitatively different long-time dynamics compared to bulk coupling, with potential experimental platforms identified for realization.

Abstract: We present an analytical study of the dynamics of a qubit coupled to the zigzag edge of a photonic honeycomb lattice. Leveraging a resolvent operator approach and exact lattice Green's functions, we map the spectrum of qubit-photon bound states and complex poles to uncover the long-time scaling laws of the qubit population. Unlike a qubit coupled to the bulk of graphene, the presence of a zero-energy flat band connecting the Dirac points suppresses long-time branch-cut-induced tails, instead driving damped Rabi oscillations. Potential experimental platforms are briefly discussed.

</details>


### [134] [Dynamics of entanglement in non-Hermitian system of nonreciprocal coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08388)
*Jiguo Wu,Shaojie Yang,Xiong Liu,Zhibo Jiang,Zhiyou Zhang,Rongchun Ge*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work theoretically investigates interactions between different orders of exceptional points (EPs) in a non-reciprocally coupled cavity system. The system exhibits both second and third-order EPs, and reveals a purely quantum continuous entanglement phase transition demonstrating how multi-EP interactions alter entanglement dynamics while maintaining accelerated generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The ability to engineer orders of exceptional points is fundamental for applications in quantum control (e.g., accelerating entanglement generation), yet a systematic study of interactions between different orders of EPs was still missing in the literature.

Method: The authors employ a non-reciprocally coupled cavity system that is experimentally realizable using a two-dimensional reconfigurable non-Hermitian gauged laser array. The system is deliberately designed to simultaneously exhibit both second and third-order EPs, enabling study of multi-EP interactions.

Result: A novel purely quantum continuous entanglement phase transition is observed. The results reveal how multi-EP interactions alter entanglement dynamics across different regimes while preserving accelerated entanglement generation, demonstrating genuinely quantum non-Hermitian physics of higher-order EPs.

Conclusion: The findings open a new avenue for investigating higher-order degeneracy applications and dynamic phase transitions in quantum systems.

Abstract: The exploration of the effect of non-Hermitian (NH) in quantum systems is gaining renewed momentum as a result of the recent progress in experiments. It has been indicated that the ability to engineer the orders of exceptional points (EPs) can be fundamental to its applications in quantum control, such as accelerating entanglement generation; however, an initial survey of the interactions between different orders of EPs is still missing. In this work, we try to partially address this issue by employing a non-reciprocally coupled cavity system which can be experimentally realizable with a two-dimensional reconfigurable NH gauged laser array. With deliberate design, our systems display both second and third orders of EPs. The sign of a novel, purely quantum continuous entanglement phase transition is observed, which reveals how multi-EP interactions alter entanglement dynamics across different regimes while maintaining accelerated generation. Our findings reveil the genuinely quantum NH physics of higher-order EPs, which has opened up a new avenue for future investigations of the higher-order degeneracy application and the dynamic phase transitions of quantum systems.

</details>


### [135] [A theoretical study of subcarrier-wave quantum key distribution system integration with an optical transport network utilizing dense wavelength division multiplexing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08389)
*Fedor Kiselev,Natalia Veselkova,Roman Goncharov,Vladimir Egorov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the performance of subcarrier-wave quantum key distribution (SCW QKD) in DWDM optical transport networks, considering Raman scattering, four-wave mixing, and channel isolation noise. It shows that allocating the quantum channel at 1310 nm outperforms C-band allocation due to lower nonlinear noise, despite higher fiber attenuation at that wavelength.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate how SCW QKD performs when coexisting with classical DWDM channels, and to identify the optimal quantum channel wavelength allocation that minimizes impairments from Raman scattering and four-wave mixing.

Method: The authors model the impact of spontaneous Raman scattering, four-wave mixing, and channel isolation on SCW QKD. They compute quantum bit error rate (QBER) and secure key generation rate for different DWDM parameters and quantum channel allocations.

Result: Allocating the quantum channel at 1310 nm is preferable to C-band allocation because it yields lower nonlinear noise, even though fiber attenuation is larger at this wavelength.

Conclusion: For practical coexistence of SCW QKD with classical DWDM traffic, the 1310 nm wavelength region offers better QKD performance than the C-band due to reduced nonlinear impairments, guiding system design choices for hybrid quantum-classical networks.

Abstract: In this paper we study the performance of subcarrier-wave quantum key distribution (SCW QKD) in the presence of classical channels of optical transport network utilizing dense wavelength division multiplexing (DWDM). We consider the impact of spontaneous Raman scattering noise as well as the four-wave mixing and channel isolation efficiency. We calculate quantum bit error rate as well as the secure key generation rate of SCW-QKD protocol for different parameters of DWDM system and quantum channel allocations. Our calculations show, that quantum channel allocation at the wavelength of 1310 nm is preferable to allocation at C-band due to lower nonlinear noise, despite fiber's larger attenuation at this wavelength.

</details>


### [136] [Performance of the subcarrier-wave quantum key distribution in the presence of spontaneous Raman scattering noise generated by classical DWDM channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08406)
*Fedor Kiselev,Roman Goncharov,Natalia Veselkova,Eduard Samsonov,Alexei D. Kiselev,Vladimir Egorov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the performance of subcarrier-wave quantum key distribution (SCW QKD) systems affected by spontaneous Raman scattering (SpRS) noise from co-propagating classical DWDM channels in a single-mode fiber. A mathematical model is developed to compute QBER and secure key generation rate for both continuous-wave and pulsed regimes of the SCW QKD system. Results show the pulsed regime delivers superior secure key rates and longer maximum transmission distances than the continuous-wave regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate how SpRS noise generated by classical DWDM channels degrades SCW QKD system performance and to determine which operational regime (continuous wave vs. pulsed) is more robust, thereby guiding practical co-existence of QKD and classical optical networks.

Method: A mathematical model is formulated to compute QBER and secure key generation rate incorporating SpRS noise, applied to both continuous-wave and pulsed regimes of the SCW QKD system, with performance analyzed as a function of classical DWDM receiver sensitivity.

Result: The pulsed regime outperforms the continuous-wave regime in both secure key generation rate and maximum achievable transmission distance under SpRS noise.

Conclusion: Pulsed SCW QKD operation is the preferred regime for mitigating SpRS-induced degradation when coexisting with classical DWDM channels, offering improved secure key rates and extended reach.

Abstract: In this paper we study performance of the subcarrier-wave quantum key distribution system (SCW QKD) in the presence of spontaneous Raman scattering (SpRS) noise generated by classical channels of dense wavelength division multiplexing (DWDM) network within a single-mode optical fiber. We present the mathematical model for evaluation of the quantum bit error rate (QBER) and the secure key generation rate with the SpRS noise taken into account. We consider two regimes of the SCW QKD system: the continuous wave regime that uses continuous wave laser and the pulsed regime. For these regimes, performance of the system is analyzed depending on receiver sensitivity of classical DWDM. It is found that the pulsed regime outperforms the continuous wave regime in both the secure key generation rate and the maximum achievable distance.

</details>


### [137] [Efficient quantum state preparation on Quantinuum hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08414)
*Archie Butterworth,Josh Green,Yusen Wu,Jie Pan,Jingbo Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The authors demonstrate an end-to-end framework combining resource-efficient quantum state preparation with rapid verification on near-term quantum hardware. They prepare a structured quantum state encoding an acoustic signal on the Quantinuum H2-1 trapped-ion platform, achieving hardware fidelity of 0.929 without fault-tolerant overhead. They introduce a pre-measurement basis-change technique that reduces the verification parameter τ by over 10 orders of magnitude for structured states, compressing the shadow overlap verification to just 1,000 measurement shots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preparing and verifying specific quantum states is essential for quantum advantage, but current validation methods like shadow overlap have prohibitively high sample complexity for structured states used in practical algorithms. Additionally, existing approaches often rely on idealized assumptions or deep fault-tolerant overhead that are unrealistic for NISQ-era devices.

Method: The framework combines resource-minimal circuits optimized for NISQ-era and early fault-tolerant devices with a pre-measurement basis-change technique applied to the shadow overlap verification method. This basis rotation reduces the verification parameter τ by over 10 orders of magnitude for structured targets, integrating tensor-network state preparation and shadow validation into a unified workflow.

Result: Experimental preparation and validation of a digitized acoustic signal state on Quantinuum H2-1 achieved hardware fidelity F_hw = 0.929. The basis-change technique reduced τ by over 10 orders of magnitude, and the robust verification procedure was compressed to just 1,000 measurement shots.

Conclusion: The framework shifts the paradigm of mapping structured classical data to quantum hardware under realistic noise and measurement budgets, offering an immediate, scalable benchmarking standard for NISQ-era and early fault-tolerant quantum devices.

Abstract: Preparation and verification of specific quantum states is an important capability for quantum devices to realise advantages over classical computations and algorithms. In this work, we have demonstrated an end-to-end framework that combines resource-efficient quantum state preparation with rapid, robust fidelity verification on near-term quantum hardware. By experimentally preparing and validating a structured complex quantum state encoding a digitized acoustic signal on the Quantinuum H2-1 trapped-ion platform, we achieved a high hardware fidelity of $F_{\mathrm{hw}} = 0.929$. Crucially, this milestone was realized without relying on idealized assumptions or deep fault-tolerant overhead, but rather through resource-minimal circuits optimized for NISQ-era and early fault-tolerant devices.
  Furthermore, we addressed a key limitation in current quantum state certification. While validation methods like shadow overlap work well for random states, their sample complexity can become prohibitively high for the structured states used in practical algorithms. We mitigate this by introducing a pre-measurement basis-change technique that reduces the verification parameter, $τ$, by over 10 orders of magnitude for structured targets. This approach tightens the theoretical certification guarantees of the shadow overlap method and integrates tensor-network preparation and shadow validation into a unified workflow. These results shift the paradigm of how structured classical data can be mapped to and verified on quantum hardware under realistic noise and measurement budgets. By compressing a robust verification procedure to just 1,000 measurement shots, this framework offers an immediate, scalable benchmarking standard.

</details>


### [138] [Time-Dependent Quantum Monte Carlo for fermions: from Bayesian conditioning to spinor dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08415)
*Ivan P. Christov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper derives, rather than postulates, the conditional interaction used in time-dependent quantum Monte Carlo (TDQMC), showing that the Nadaraya-Watson form emerges naturally from Bayes theorem when walkers are treated as localizations of electrons at finite resolution. The formulation is extended to active-exchange (fermionic) regimes and generalized to spinors, where Pauli suppression is graded by local spin alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TDQMC has been used heuristically with a conditional interaction built from walker positions, but the form of this interaction was postulated rather than rigorously derived. Prior applications were largely limited to opposite-spin electrons where exchange effects are dormant; a principled formulation for the regime where exchange is active (fermions) is needed. Additionally, the method's nonlocality length lacked a clear physical meaning.

Method: Treating each walker as a localization of its electron to finite resolution, the authors apply Bayes theorem combined with a single empirical substitution to derive the Nadaraya-Watson conditional interaction. The nonlocality length is reinterpreted as the width of the conditioning rather than a fitted coupling. For fermions, exchange is confined to the wave-function sector (keeping walker dynamics sign-problem-free), while orthonormality is enforced via a term derived from constraints since conditioning removes the gauge freedom that normally eliminates these multipliers in Hartree-Fock. The formulation is generalized to spinors with graded Pauli suppression based on local spin alignment, and shown to reduce through collinear and mean-field limits to known two-particle spin equations.

Result: The Nadaraya-Watson form used heuristically in TDQMC is shown to follow from Bayes theorem and a single empirical substitution. The pair and mean-field limits emerge from one unified construction. The derived framework handles active exchange for fermions without reintroducing the sign problem, since exchange remains in the wave sector. The spinor generalization yields graded Pauli suppression and correctly reduces to the known collinear and mean-field two-particle spin equations.

Conclusion: The paper places TDQMC's conditional interaction on firm theoretical footing by deriving rather than postulating it, clarifies the meaning of the nonlocality length, and extends the method's validity to fermionic regimes with active exchange and to spinor wavefunctions while preserving polynomial cost and avoiding the sign problem.

Abstract: The time-dependent quantum Monte Carlo method represents a many-electron state by an ensemble of replicas, in which each electron is described by a cloud of walkers which samples its density in physical space, one walker and one guide wave per replica, and it replaces the Hartree potential by a conditional interaction built from the walker positions. The sampling is in physical space rather than in configuration space, which is what keeps the cost polynomial. Here that conditional interaction is derived rather than postulated. The method has so far been applied mostly to opposite-spin electrons, where the exchange is dormant rather than absent, and the present formulation addresses the regime in which it is active. Treating the walker as a localization of its electron to a finite resolution, Bayes theorem and a single empirical substitution yield the Nadaraya-Watson form which the method has used heuristically. The nonlocality length thereby acquires a meaning as the width of the conditioning rather than as a fitted coupling, and the pair and mean-field limits follow from one construction. For fermions, exchange cannot be carried by the walkers and stays in the wave sector, which is what leaves the positive walker sampling free of the sign problem. Because the conditioning removes the gauge freedom which eliminates the orthonormality multipliers in Hartree-Fock, orthonormality is enforced here by a term derived from the constraint. The formulation is generalized to spinors, where the Pauli suppression at coincidence becomes graded by the local spin alignment, and it reduces through the collinear and mean-field limits to the known two-particle spin equations.

</details>


### [139] [Trapdoor Functions with Secure Key Leasing and Copy Protection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08423)
*Fuyuki Kitagawa,Ryo Nishimaki,Yuki Shirakawa,Takashi Yamakawa*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces the first study of trapdoor functions (TDFs) in quantum cryptographic settings, defining both TDFs with secure key leasing (TDF-SKL) and TDFs with copy protection (TDF-CP). The authors provide constructions for TDF-SKL based on LWE and on PKE with hinting PRGs, and a TDF-CP construction using indistinguishability obfuscation and LWE. They further use these to build robust PKE-SKL and single-decryptor encryption schemes whose quantum decryption keys remain reusable even after decrypting malicious ciphertexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While unclonable quantum cryptographic primitives like secure key leasing and copy protection have been developed following the no-cloning theorem, trapdoor functions—fundamental to public-key cryptography—had not been studied in these unclonability settings. Additionally, existing PKE-SKL and single-decryptor encryption schemes have a critical vulnerability where quantum decryption keys can be destroyed when decrypting maliciously chosen ciphertexts, motivating the need for robust schemes.

Method: The authors formalize TDF-SKL both with and without domain samplers. They construct TDF-SKL without domain sampler from the LWE assumption, and TDF-SKL with domain sampler from standard PKE combined with hinting pseudorandom generators. They then define TDF-CP, where the inversion functionality is copy-protected via a quantum trapdoor, and construct it using indistinguishability obfuscation (iO) and LWE, following the modular copy protection framework of Ananth and Behera. Finally, they derive robust PKE-SKL and single-decryptor encryption schemes from TDF-SKL and TDF-CP respectively, ensuring decryption keys remain reusable after arbitrary ciphertext operations.

Result: The paper delivers: (1) formal definitions of TDF-SKL with and without domain sampler; (2) two TDF-SKL constructions based on LWE and on PKE + hinting PRGs respectively; (3) a definition of TDF-CP with a construction from iO and LWE; (4) robust PKE-SKL and SDE schemes from TDF-SKL and TDF-CP that survive malicious ciphertext decryption without key destruction.

Conclusion: This work fills a gap by bringing trapdoor functions into the domain of unclonable quantum cryptography, offering both secure key leasing and copy protection variants. The resulting robust PKE-SKL and single-decryptor encryption constructions resolve a critical vulnerability in prior schemes, and the approach opens further avenues for quantum-resistant public-key cryptography with unclonable properties.

Abstract: Inspired by the no-cloning theorem in quantum theory, a variety of quantum cryptographic primitives with unclonable functionalities, such as secure key leasing and copy protection, have been proposed and attracted significant attention. However, trapdoor functions (TDFs), fundamental primitives in public-key cryptography, have not been extensively studied in these areas. In this work, we initiate a study of TDFs in both secure key leasing and copy protection settings. We first introduce the definition of TDFs with secure key leasing (TDF-SKL), which enables leasing and deleting of quantum trapdoors. We formalize TDF-SKL both with and without domain sampler, and give a construction of TDF-SKL without domain sampler based on the LWE assumption and a construction of TDF-SKL with domain sampler based on any standard PKE schemes combined with hinting pseudorandom generators [Koppula and Waters, CRYPTO 2019]. Next, we define TDFs with copy protection (TDF-CP), where the inversion functionality is copy protected by a quantum trapdoor. We establish a construction of TDF-CP assuming indistinguishability obfuscation and the LWE assumption, following a modular framework of copy protection proposed by Ananth and Behera [CRYPTO 2024]. We also present applications of TDF-SKL and TDF-CP. Existing constructions of public-key encryption with secure key leasing (PKE-SKL) and single-decryptor encryption (SDE) suffer from a critical vulnerability: quantum decryption keys may be destroyed after decrypting maliciously chosen ciphertexts. We construct PKE-SKL schemes and SDE schemes with robust quantum decryption keys that remain reusable after decrypting arbitrary ciphertexts from TDF-SKL and TDF-CP, respectively.

</details>


### [140] [Explicit block encodings of rate matrices for simulating polymerization kinetics on quantum computers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08432)
*Yuhei Ikeda,Hokuto Iwakiri,Soichiro Nishio,Kentaro Matsumoto*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper constructs explicit quantum block-encoding circuits for simulating two living polymerization kinetic models on fault-tolerant quantum hardware, achieving exponential state-space compression with only O(log N) qubits and demonstrating via numerical simulation that the encodings correctly reproduce classical time evolution for copolymerization reactivity ratios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical simulation of polymer molecular weight distribution and monomer sequence evolution faces a fundamental trade-off between molecular resolution and computational cost, since for copolymers the number of distinguishable species grows exponentially with chain length. Quantum computing could overcome this via exponential state-space compression, but requires embedding non-unitary kinetic rate matrices into unitary quantum circuits—a task known as block encoding that had not been previously constructed for polymerization kinetics.

Method: The authors construct explicit block-encoding circuits for two kinetic models: Model A (single-monomer polymerization) uses a lower-bidiagonal rate matrix encoded via a sparse-oracle construction and a two-term linear combination of unitaries (LCU) decomposition. Model B (two-monomer copolymerization) uses a bijective labeling of polymer species by an integer index (the m-index) to yield a structured sparse matrix, encoded via either a five-term LCU or a sparse-oracle construction. Numerical simulations verify the encodings against classical time evolution, and resource estimation characterizes qubit and gate requirements.

Result: Sparse-oracle encodings reproduce classical time evolution for reactivity ratios spanning near-random (r1r2 ≈ 1) and blocky (r1r2 > 1) microstructures from reported olefin copolymerization systems. LCU encodings are verified by explicit reconstruction of the encoded matrix block. Both implementations require only O(log N) qubits in the matrix dimension N—an exponential memory saving over classical state space—with gate counts reaching 10^4 to 10^5 gates at 10^3 system qubits.

Conclusion: This work establishes a concrete quantum circuit foundation for simulating polymerization kinetics on fault-tolerant quantum hardware, serving as a first step toward exploiting exponential state-space compression for high-dimensional polymer reaction networks.

Abstract: Predicting how molecular weight distribution and monomer sequence evolve during polymerization is central to polymer science, yet classical approaches face a trade-off between molecular resolution and computational cost: for copolymers, the number of distinguishable species grows exponentially with chain length. Quantum computing offers a potential alternative, provided the non-unitary rate matrices governing the kinetics can be embedded into unitary quantum circuits, a task known as block encoding. Here we construct explicit block-encoding circuits for two kinetic models of living polymerization: Model A, single-monomer polymerization, whose lower-bidiagonal rate matrix is encoded via a sparse-oracle construction and a two-term linear combination of unitaries (LCU) decomposition; and Model B, two-monomer copolymerization, where a bijective labeling of polymer species by an integer index (the m-index) yields a structured sparse matrix encoded via either a five-term LCU or a sparse-oracle construction. Numerical simulations with the sparse-oracle encodings reproduce the classical time evolution for reactivity ratios drawn from reported olefin copolymerization systems spanning near-random ($r_1 r_2 \simeq 1$) and blocky ($r_1 r_2 > 1$) microstructures, and the LCU encodings are verified by explicit reconstruction of the encoded matrix block. Resource estimation shows that both implementations require only $O(\log N)$ qubits in the matrix dimension $N$ (an exponential memory saving over the classical state space), with gate counts growing gradually, reaching $10^4$ to $10^5$ gates at $10^3$ system qubits. These results establish a concrete quantum circuit foundation for simulating polymerization kinetics on fault-tolerant quantum hardware, and a first step toward exploiting exponential state-space compression for high-dimensional polymer reaction networks.

</details>


### [141] [The Forgotten History of Wave Function Variance Optimization and its Relevance for Neural-Network VMC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08453)
*Dario Bressanini*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper revisits the century-old question of which local energy functional best optimizes trial wave functions in VMC and NN-VMC. It shows that standard variance minimization (L^2) fails due to heavy-tailed E^-4 local energy distributions in Coulombic systems, and benchmarks robust alternatives (MAD/L^1, Cauchy loss, L_-4) on H_2^+ across the full potential energy curve, finding no single functional universally optimal—MAD wins in bonding regions while L_-4 excels in dissociation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Variance minimization in Variational Monte Carlo dates back to the 1930s but assumes Gaussian local energy distributions, which is unjustified. Modern neural-network wave functions have heavy-tailed local energy distributions with high statistical noise, making this assumption particularly problematic and renewing interest in understanding which cost functional best optimizes trial wave functions.

Method: The authors retrace the historical development of variance optimization and its connection to Neural Quantum States. They identify that Coulombic systems produce local energy distributions with E^-4 power-law tails, which breaks the Central Limit Theorem for the variance estimator. They propose and benchmark robust alternatives: Mean Absolute Deviation (L^1), Cauchy loss, and the L_-4 functional (whose tail is analytically matched to the E^-4 exponent). The functionals are tested on the exactly solvable H_2^+ system using the Guillemin-Zener wave function across all internuclear distances.

Result: Energy minimization by construction yields the lowest energy. Variance minimization is systematically outperformed at every internuclear distance R by the alternative functionals. MAD (L^1) is superior in the bonding region, while L_-4 performs best in the dissociation regime—no single cost functional dominates universally.

Conclusion: The choice of local energy functional matters significantly for wave function optimization quality. The long-held preference for variance minimization is unjustified for Coulombic systems with heavy-tailed local energy distributions. Robust functionals tailored to the specific tail behavior of the system (like L_-4 matching E^-4 tails) provide better optimization, suggesting practical guidance for VMC and NN-VMC practitioners.

Abstract: We revisit the almost century-old question of which functional of the local energy best optimizes a trial wave function, a problem of central importance in Variational Monte Carlo (VMC) and, more recently, in Neural-Network VMC (NN-VMC). While variance optimization dates back to the 1930s, the high statistical noise and heavy-tailed local energy distributions inherent to modern neural-network wave functions have renewed interest in this approach. We retrace its long and largely forgotten history here, showing its direct relevance to modern Neural Quantum States (NQS) frameworks. Minimizing the variance (an $L^2$ norm) implicitly assumes a Gaussian local energy distribution: an unjustified assumption. For Coulombic systems, the local energy distribution exhibits $E^{-4}$ power-law tails, causing the Central Limit Theorem to fail for the variance estimator. This instability can be mitigated by robust cost functions: the Mean Absolute Deviation (MAD, an $L^1$ norm), the Cauchy loss, or the $L_{-4}$ functional, which features a tail analytically designed to match the $E^{-4}$ exponent. We benchmark these functionals on $H_2^+$, an exactly solvable system at every internuclear distance, using the Guillemin-Zener wave function across the full potential energy curve. While energy minimization by construction yields the lowest energy, variance minimization is surpassed at every $R$ by alternative functionals: MAD proves superior in the bonding region, while $L_{-4}$ performs best in the dissociation regime.

</details>


### [142] [Deriving the Kijowski Arrival-Time POVM from the Schrödinger Current: Minimal Positivity and Uniqueness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08458)
*Avi Marchewka*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives the minimal modification of the free Schrödinger current that guarantees nonnegative positivity for all states with positive momentum support, replacing the momentum kernel K_Sch(p,p')=(p+p')/2m with K_min(p,p')=sqrt(pp')/m. Extending this no-backflow requirement to states containing both momentum signs forces cross-sector kernels to vanish, yielding exactly the Kijowski time-of-arrival POVM. This provides a current-based physical motivation for the directional kernels of the Kijowski POVM and their separation, with uniqueness within the diagonal-preserving pairwise-minimal construction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum backflow—the appearance of negative Schrödinger current for states with entirely positive momentum support—is a paradox that undermines the interpretation of current as a physical flow.
