The authors seek the smallest controlled modification of the current that removes this pathology while preserving per-component momentum contributions, in order to arrive at a physically meaningful, positive arrival-time probability measure.

Method: The authors formulate the problem as finding the minimal modification of the free-particle momentum kernel K_Sch(p,p') that guarantees nonnegative current for every state with positive momentum support, while preserving individual momentum-component contributions. They impose a diagonal-preserving pairwise-minimal construction and solve for the resulting kernel, requiring K_min(p,p')=sqrt(pp')/m. They then extend the no-backflow constraint to states containing both momentum signs, which forces the cross-sector kernel elements to vanish.

Result: The minimal modification yields a positive, normalized current kernel K_min(p,p')=sqrt(pp')/m, which defines an arrival-time POVM. Extending the directional no-backflow requirement to mixed-momentum states forces K_min^{+-}=K_min^{-+}=0, making the full current a sum of two independent positive directional contributions. This resulting POVM is exactly the Kijowski time-of-arrival POVM, and the construction is unique within the diagonal-preserving pairwise-minimal framework.

Conclusion: The authors provide a physical, current-based derivation of the Kijowski time-of-arrival POVM, motivated purely by eliminating quantum backflow with the minimal allowed modification. The result rationalizes both the specific directional kernels and their separation into independent positive and negative momentum sectors. The construction does not impose a first-arrival condition, leaving that aspect outside the scope of the present derivation.

Abstract: Quantum backflow refers here to the appearance of a negative Schrödinger current for a state whose momentum support is entirely positive. We ask for the smallest modification of the free Schrödinger current that makes it nonnegative for every such state, while preserving the current of each individual momentum component. We show that the required minimal modification changes the free-particle momentum kernel according to \[ K_{\rm Sch}(p,p')=\frac{p+p'}{2m} \;\longrightarrow\; K_{\min}(p,p')=\frac{\sqrt{pp'}}{m}. \] The resulting current is positive and normalized and therefore defines an arrival-time POVM. Extending the directional no-backflow requirement to states containing both momentum signs forces the cross-sector kernel to vanish, \[ K_{\min}^{+-}=K_{\min}^{-+}=0, \] so that the full current is the sum of two independent directional contributions. The resulting POVM is exactly the Kijowski time-of-arrival POVM, providing a current-based physical motivation for both its directional kernels and their separation. Within the diagonal-preserving pairwise-minimal construction considered here, the result is unique. The construction itself does not impose a first-arrival condition.

</details>


### [143] [Kinematic Control and Dephasing Dynamics of Quantum Resources in $e^{+}e^{-}\toτ^{+}τ^{-}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08555)
*Elhabib Jaloum,Omar Bachain,Mohamed Amazioug,Rachid Ahl Laamara*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper characterizes quantum resources (Bell nonlocality, steerability, entanglement of formation, coherence) in the spin state of τ⁺τ⁻ pairs from e⁺e⁻ annihilation at Belle-II energy (√s = 10.579 GeV). The spin state is expressed as a velocity- and angle-dependent mixture of separable and maximally entangled components, connecting the threshold state at √s = 2m_τ to the Bell-state limit at ultrarelativistic energies. The two-spin state is then propagated through a phenomenological correlated-dephasing channel, revealing that environmental memory and inter-channel classical correlations cause collapses and revivals absent in Markovian evolution, separating kinematic correlation creation from noise-controlled survival.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize the quantum resources available in the spin correlations of τ⁺τ⁻ pairs produced in electron-positron annihilation and to understand how environmental noise (memory effects and classical correlations) affects the survival of these resources.

Method: The authors express the two-spin state of the τ⁺τ⁻ pair as a velocity- and angle-dependent mixture of a separable contribution and a maximally entangled Bell-state component. They analyze quantum resource measures (Bell nonlocality, steerability, entanglement of formation, coherence) at Belle-II energy √s = 10.579 GeV, then propagate the state through a phenomenological correlated-dephasing channel incorporating environmental memory and inter-channel classical correlations (non-Markovian dynamics).

Result: The quantum resources are governed by the production angle and are maximal for transverse emission (ϑ = π/2). The mixture representation connects threshold (separable) and ultrarelativistic (Bell-state) limits. Environmental memory effects induce collapses and revivals in resource dynamics that do not occur under monotonic Markovian evolution.

Conclusion: The kinematic mechanism creating spin correlations in the τ⁺τ⁻ pair is cleanly separated from the noise properties controlling resource survival. Non-Markovian (memory-carrying) environmental effects produce qualitatively different resource dynamics (collapses and revivals) compared to Markovian decoherence.

Abstract: We characterize several quantum resources carried by the spins of the $τ^{+}τ^{-}$ pair produced in $e^{+}e^{-}$ annihilation. At the Belle-II energy, $\sqrt{s}=10.579\,\mathrm{GeV}$, Bell nonlocality, steerability, entanglement of formation, and coherence are governed by the production angle and are largest for transverse emission, $\vartheta=π/2$. Their common kinematic origin is exposed by expressing the spin state as a velocity and angle-dependent mixture of a separable contribution and a maximally entangled component. Within the physical production domain, this representation connects the weakly correlated threshold state at $\sqrt{s}=2m_τ$ to the Bell-state limit approached at ultrarelativistic energies. We then propagate the two-spin state through a phenomenological correlated-dephasing channel to determine how environmental memory and inter-channel classical correlations affect the available resources. Memory effects generate collapses and revivals that are absent from the monotonic Markovian evolution. The analysis therefore separates the kinematic mechanism that creates the spin correlations from the noise properties that control their subsequent survival.

</details>


### [144] [Multimode squeezed light generation and characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08591)
*Alexander Chudakov,Vladislav Severin,Anastasia Poshevkina,Danil Malyshev,Kirill Kuznetsov,Olga Tikhonova,Dmitry Kalashnikov,Polina Sharapova*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper theoretically and experimentally investigates multimode squeezed light generated in type-0 and type-II optical parametric amplifiers (OPAs), examining how the degree of squeezing depends on the spectral profile of the local oscillator using pulse shaping techniques, with theoretical analysis based on Schmidt-mode theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimode squeezed states of light enable high-dimensional continuous-variable quantum systems, but homodyne detection requires the local oscillator profile to perfectly match the measured mode—a condition rarely met in practice, necessitating a proper theoretical treatment of multimode squeezing.

Method: Combined theoretical and experimental study: type-0 and type-II OPAs pumped by ultrafast lasers generate multimode squeezed light; pulse shaping controls the LO spectral profile; Schmidt-mode decomposition provides the theoretical framework for analyzing mode structure and squeezing dependence on the LO profile.

Result: The sources were characterized and the degree of squeezing was measured as a function of the LO spectral profile for both type-0 and type-II OPA configurations, with results supported by Schmidt-mode theory.

Conclusion: The work provides a rigorous characterization framework for multimode squeezed light sources relevant to continuous-variable quantum information processing, with potential significance for realizing multimode quantum protocols.

Abstract: Nowadays, the realization of quantum computations and communications based on continuous variables has attracted a significant attention due to a substantial expansion of the system dimensionality. The main progress in this area is attributed to the implementation of multimode systems based on squeezed states of light. One of the simplest ways to generate such states relies upon their producing in a single-pass optical parametric amplifier (OPA) using ultrafast pumping. However, for homodyne detection of such multimode states, the profile of the local oscillator (LO) must perfectly match the profile of the measured mode. Usually, this is not the case; therefore a proper treatment of multimode squeezing is required. In this work, we study both theoretically and experimentally the multimode squeezed light generated in type-0 and type-II OPA. We characterize such sources and investigate the degree of squeezing in dependence on the LO spectral profile, employing a pulse shaping technique. The theoretical analysis is performed using the Schmidt-mode theory. This work might have a significant impact on the realization of multimode quantum protocols

</details>


### [145] [Recovering Readout-Limited Fisher Information in Superconducting-Qubit Magnetometry with Squeezed Microwaves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08598)
*M. -R. Yun,Y. -J. Qu,Zheng Shan,L. -L. Yan,Yu Jia,S. -L. Su*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes how squeezed-microwave-assisted dispersive readout can recover magnetic-field information lost during qubit-state assignment in superconducting-qubit magnetometers. The authors develop an effective detected-mode framework linking projected quadrature noise, state-assignment error, and classical Fisher information from binary readout outcomes. They find an optimal squeezing strength arises from the competition between squeezed and anti-squeezed fluctuations due to quadrature mismatch, and show squeezed readout reduces the readout-limited magnetic-field sensitivity bound by 27.3%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The performance of superconducting-qubit magnetometers depends not only on magnetic-field encoding during Ramsey interrogation, but also on the efficiency of information recovery during readout. The authors aim to quantify how measurement-stage information loss can be mitigated using squeezed-microwave-assisted dispersive readout.

Method: The authors develop an effective detected-mode framework that connects projected quadrature noise, state-assignment error, and the classical Fisher information accessible from binary readout outcomes. They analyze the trade-off between squeezed and anti-squeezed fluctuations when there is a finite mismatch between the squeezed quadrature and the discrimination axis, which yields an optimal squeezing strength.

Result: For representative parameters, squeezed readout reduces the readout-limited magnetic-field sensitivity bound by 27.3%. The improvement is achieved by recovering information lost during the readout stage rather than by increasing information encoded during Ramsey interrogation.

Conclusion: Squeezed-microwave-assisted dispersive readout offers a practical route for mitigating measurement-stage information loss in superconducting quantum sensing applications.

Abstract: The performance of superconducting-qubit magnetometers depends not only on magnetic-field encoding during Ramsey interrogation, but also on how efficiently the encoded information is recovered during readout. Here we quantify how squeezed-microwave-assisted dispersive readout can recover magnetic-field information lost during qubit-state assignment. We develop an effective detected-mode framework linking projected quadrature noise, state-assignment error, and the classical Fisher information accessible from binary readout outcomes. A finite mismatch between the squeezed quadrature and the discrimination axis produces an optimal squeezing strength through the competition between squeezed and anti-squeezed fluctuations. For representative parameters, squeezed readout reduces the readout-limited magnetic-field sensitivity bound by $27.3\%$. This improvement arises from recovering information lost in the readout stage rather than from increasing the information encoded during Ramsey interrogation. These results may provide a practical route for mitigating measurement-stage information loss in superconducting quantum sensing.

</details>


### [146] [Finite-size Entanglement Certification via Third-Order Local Randomized Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08635)
*Giovanni Scala,Corrado Cosimo Mattiacci,Dorota Pietryka,Gniewomir Sarbicki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives a finite-size entanglement certificate using third-order local randomized measurements, extending beyond second-order correlations to detect entanglement that is invisible at lower order, with a dimension-independent concentration bound for rigorous finite-sample error control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full state tomography for certifying entanglement in high-dimensional systems is prohibitively expensive as dimension grows. Local randomized measurements are scalable but existing second-order correlation-based tests only extract limited information about the quantum state, potentially missing detectable entanglement.

Method: The authors extend local randomized measurement schemes from second-order to third-order correlations, deriving a finite-size entanglement certificate based on this additional third-order information, supplemented by a dimension-independent concentration bound that rigorously controls finite-sample statistical errors.

Result: The third-order extension reveals entanglement that remains undetected at second order, while the dimension-independent concentration bound ensures rigorous control of finite-sample errors without scaling with system dimension.

Conclusion: The approach provides a practical route to extracting richer entanglement information from experimental platforms without incurring the dimension-dependent overhead of full state tomography.

Abstract: Certifying entanglement in high-dimensional systems usually requires full state tomography, whose cost grows rapidly with the system dimension. Local randomized measurements offer a scalable alternative, but existing tests based on second-order correlations access only limited information about the state. Here, we derive a finite-size entanglement certificate that extends local randomized measurements to third order. The additional third-order information reveals entanglement that remains undetected at second order, while a dimension-independent concentration bound provides rigorous control of finite-sample errors. Our result opens a practical route to extracting stronger entanglement information from experimental platforms without the dimension-dependent overhead of state tomography.

</details>


### [147] [Causal-Class Hierarchies in Coherence-Constrained Channel Transformation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08639)
*Lin Zhu,Benchi Zhao,Xuanqiang Zhao,Ranyiliu Chen,Xin Wang,Shenggen Zheng*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether causal architectures (parallel, sequential, or general higher-order) improve quantum channel transformations under resource-theoretic constraints, specifically in the dynamical resource theory of coherence. Using a unified SDP framework, the authors prove a strict causal hierarchy for qubit amplitude-damping channels under incoherent superchannels, but show that mixed-Pauli channels admit teleportation simulations that make all causal strategies equivalent, identifying free program-state parallelization as the structural reason preventing causal enhancement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While higher-order quantum transformations offer causal freedom beyond parallel and sequential arrangements, whether this freedom yields advantages when superprocesses are constrained by a resource theory is largely unexplored. The paper aims to close this gap by studying causal enhancement in the dynamical resource theory of coherence.

Method: The authors employ a unified semidefinite-programming (SDP) framework to compare parallel, fixed-order sequential, and general higher-order strategies for channel transformations. They analyze two qubit channel types: amplitude-damping channels under maximally incoherent superchannels (MISC) and dephasing-covariant incoherent superchannels (DISC), and mixed-Pauli channels leveraging teleportation simulation into Bell-diagonal program states.

Result: For qubit amplitude-damping channels to the identity target, a strict causal hierarchy is proven at every nontrivial damping strength under both MISC and DISC. In contrast, mixed-Pauli channels admit a common teleportation simulation that transfers channel dependence to Bell-diagonal program states prepared in parallel, allowing the remaining processing to be absorbed into a single quantum operation—thus parallel, sequential, and general higher-order strategies achieve identical optimal error for any target.

Conclusion: The findings identify free program-state parallelisation as a structural obstruction to causal enhancement: when channel dependence can be parallelized into program states, no causal advantage emerges; causal hierarchies only arise for channels lacking such free simulation, such as amplitude-damping channels.

Abstract: Higher-order quantum transformations allow multiple channel uses to be combined through different causal architectures, from parallel and fixed-order sequential networks to general higher-order processes. Whether this causal freedom improves channel transformation when the higher-order operation is also constrained by a resource theory remains largely unexplored. We study this question in the dynamical resource theory of coherence using a unified semidefinite-programming framework. For two qubit amplitude-damping channels and the identity target, we prove a strict causal hierarchy at every nontrivial damping strength under both maximally incoherent superchannels (MISC) and dephasing-covariant incoherent superchannels (DISC). In contrast, mixed-Pauli channels admit a common teleportation simulation that transfers the channel dependence to Bell-diagonal program states prepared in parallel. The remaining processing can then be absorbed into a single quantum operational, so parallel, fixed-order sequential, and general higher-order strategies achieve the same optimal error for any target. These results identify free program-state parallelisation as a structural obstruction to causal enhancement.

</details>


### [148] [Closing the Loop: Non-Causal Computation, Partial Traces, & Postselected Entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08716)
*Mark Carney*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper provides a categorical interpretation of Baumeler & Wolf's logically consistent non-causal circuits, connecting them to postselected quantum teleportation, and proves that classical logical consistency is equivalent to a specific Bell-postselection probability condition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between classical non-causal circuit frameworks and quantum postselection, providing a rigorous categorical foundation for understanding logical consistency in non-causal computation.

Method: Uses categorical trace in the category of non-negative matrices to represent looped feedback, proving stochasticity conditions via trace conditions and fixed-point uniqueness. Represents classical non-causal circuits as measure-and-prepare quantum channels with Bell-state postselection, and derives the equivalence between logical consistency and the 1/d^2 postselection probability condition.

Result: Classical logical consistency is shown to be equivalent to the postselected Bell outcome having probability exactly 1/d^2 for each classical input distribution, and the normalized conditional output agrees exactly with the classical categorical trace.

Conclusion: The work identifies a class of quantum Bell-postselection constructions whose conditional evolution maintains linear dependence on classical input distributions, unifying classical and quantum perspectives on non-causal circuits.

Abstract: This paper gives a categorical interpretation of Baumeler \& Wolf's logically consistent non-causal circuits, connecting them to postselected quantum teleportation. Looped feedback is represented by a trace in the category of non-negative matrices, and it is shown that the traced process is stochastic precisely when the induced loop transition matrix has trace $1$ for every external input, a condition shown to be equivalent to a unique fixed point for the loop for each input to a deterministic circuit. A classical non-causal circuit is represented by a measure-and-prepare quantum channel with an internal register utilising a maximally entangled Bell-state with postselection. The main result is that classical logical consistency is equivalent to the postselected Bell outcome having, for loop dimension $d$, probability exactly $1/d^2$ for each classical input distribution. The subsequent normalised conditional output then agrees exactly with the classical categorical trace. This work identifies a class of quantum Bell-postselection constructions whose conditional evolution maintains linear dependence on classical input distributions.

</details>


### [149] [Adaptive AI for Pulse-Level Quantum Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08727)
*Sanjeev Shapkota,Yayu Mo,Sanjaya Lohani*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a reinforcement-learning approach (deep Q-network) to optimize control pulses for the Control Variational Quantum Eigensolver (ctrl-VQE), enabling faster, lower-error quantum-state preparation on superconducting qubit platforms. Demonstrated on Hydrogen molecule ground-state preparation in simulation, the agent discovers high-fidelity pulse sequences that outperform random-search baselines, requiring no ansatz and operating at nanosecond time resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The ctrl-VQE method directly optimizes microwave pulses for faster and lower-error quantum-state preparation, but its continuous control landscape demands efficient search strategies that current approaches do not adequately provide.

Method: A reinforcement-learning agent based on a deep Q-learning network autonomously discovers high-performance pulse sequences using only system parameters and a reward function. The approach is fully general for superconducting qubit platforms, requires no ansatz, and operates at nanosecond resolution compatible with hardware constraints.

Result: As a proof of concept on a simulated superconducting device for Hydrogen molecule ground-state preparation, the agent consistently identifies optimized control sequences that achieve high fidelity and outperform random-search baselines.

Conclusion: Adaptive reinforcement learning is a promising hardware-ready framework for pulse-level quantum control, offering a scalable alternative to traditional optimization approaches in quantum-state preparation.

Abstract: The Control Variational Quantum Eigensolver (ctrl-VQE) directly optimizes microwave pulses to enable faster and lower-error quantum-state preparation, but its continuous control landscape re- quires efficient search strategies. We demonstrate that a reinforcement-learning agent based on a deep Q learning network can autonomously discover high-performance pulse sequences using only system parameters and a reward function. The approach is fully general for superconducting qubit platforms, requires no ansatz, and operates at nanosecond resolution compatible with hardware con- straints. As a proof of concept, we apply the method to ground-state preparation of the Hydrogen molecule on a simulated superconducting device. The agent consistently identifies optimized control sequences that achieve high fidelity and outperform random-search baselines. These results highlight adaptive learning as a promising hardware-ready framework for pulse-level quantum control.

</details>


### [150] [On the Power of Adaptivity in Testing Quantum States in Fidelity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08733)
*Jan Seyfried,Sayantan Sen,Marco Tomamichel*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies quantum state certification, equivalence testing, and independence testing with decision gaps measured in fidelity rather than trace distance. The authors prove that certification does not benefit from adaptivity and requires Θ̃(r^{3/2}/ε) samples, provide adaptive algorithms for equivalence and independence testing with improved sample complexities, and show a separation between equivalence testing and certification by proving that adaptivity matters for equivalence testing in fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While sample complexities for quantum state certification, equivalence testing, and independence testing are well-understood for decision gaps in trace distance, the fidelity-based decision gap regime is unexplored. Understanding whether these problems separate from each other and whether adaptivity helps in the fidelity setting is an open question that this work addresses.

Method: The authors develop a framework that uses partial learning combined with a reduction to testing in ℓ₂-distance, adapted from classical distribution testing literature. This framework is applied to design adaptive algorithms for equivalence and independence testing in fidelity, while lower bounds are proven for certification and non-adaptive equivalence testing.

Result: Certification with respect to fidelity for a rank-r state σ requires Θ̃(r^{3/2}/ε) samples and does not benefit from adaptivity. Adaptive algorithms are provided for equivalence testing using Θ̃(min{d^{3/2}/ε², d^{9/4}/ε}) samples and for independence testing using Θ̃(min{(d_Ad_C)^{3/2}/ε², d_A^{9/4}d_C^{3/4}/ε}) samples. A lower bound of Ω̃(1/ε²) samples is proven for non-adaptive equivalence testing even for qubits (d=2), establishing that adaptivity matters and separating equivalence testing from certification.

Conclusion: In the fidelity-based decision gap regime, certification, equivalence testing, and independence testing exhibit distinct sample complexities and adaptivity behaviors. Certification does not benefit from adaptivity, while equivalence testing (and likely independence testing) does, demonstrating a fundamental separation between these quantum property testing tasks when the distance measure is fidelity.

Abstract: We study the problems of quantum state certification, equivalence testing and independence testing. In certification, given samples of an unknown quantum state $ρ$ and the description of a state $σ$, the goal is to test whether $ρ=σ$, or whether $ρ$ and $σ$ are far in a given distance measure. In equivalence testing, $σ$ is also unknown and only accessible via samples. Independence testing decides whether $ρ_{AC}=ρ_A\otimesρ_C$, or is far from being a product. The sample complexities of these problems are now well-understood for a decision gap $\varepsilon$ in trace distance: in the single-copy measurement setting with $d$-dimensional states, all three tasks can be solved using the same non-adaptive approach, which uses $Θ(d^{3/2}/\varepsilon^2)$ samples and is optimal in general, even without adaptivity.
  In this work, we consider decision gaps expressed in fidelity and study possible separations between these problems and how adaptivity can help. We prove that certification with respect to fidelity for a state $σ$ of rank $r$ does not benefit from adaptivity and requires $\widetildeΘ(r^{3/2}/\varepsilon)$ samples. For equivalence testing and independence testing, we provide adaptive algorithms using $\widetilde{O}(\min\{d^{3/2}/\varepsilon^2,d^{9/4}/\varepsilon\})$ and $\widetilde{O}(\min\{(d_Ad_C)^{3/2}/\varepsilon^2,d_A^{9/4}d_C^{3/4}/\varepsilon\})$ samples, for $d_A\geq d_C$, respectively. Our main technique is a framework that uses partial learning and a reduction to testing in $\ell_2$-distance, adapted from the distribution testing literature. We show that adaptivity matters for equivalence testing in fidelity by proving that $\widetildeΩ(1/\varepsilon^2)$ samples are necessary in the non-adaptive case even for qubits, showing a separation from certification.

</details>


### [151] [Uniqueness and Cramér-Rao Efficiency of Quantum U-Statistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08745)
*Ayanava Dasgupta,Naqueeb Ahmad Warsi,Premanshu Chatterjee*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes rigorous theoretical foundations for unbiased estimation of polynomial functionals of quantum states. It proves an equivalence between first-order marginals of permutation-invariant observables and functional gradients, shows quantum U-statistics are the unique unbiased permutation-invariant estimators that extend to arbitrary copy numbers, and derives a universal variance expansion where the leading 1/n term coincides with the multiparameter quantum Cramér-Rao bound—establishing asymptotic efficiency. The paper also characterizes higher-order scaling degeneracies and applies these results to the Bures χ²-divergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimation of quantum state functionals from independent copies is a fundamental problem in quantum statistics, yet a complete theoretical understanding of unbiased estimation, variance scaling, and optimality across all functional classes has been lacking. This work aims to fill that gap by providing structural characterizations of optimal unbiased estimators and their asymptotic efficiency.

Method: The authors study scalar polynomial functionals of quantum states using permutation-invariant finite-copy observables. They establish an equivalence between first-order marginal structure and functional gradients, prove uniqueness of quantum U-statistics as the canonical unbiased estimator extension, and derive a universal variance expansion. They analyze the leading term relative to the multiparameter quantum Cramér-Rao bound and characterize higher-order scaling where gradient variance vanishes. Application to Bures χ²-divergence testing bounds on reference-state spectra is included.

Result: Key results include: (1) equivalence between first-order marginals and functional gradients; (2) quantum U-statistics are the unique unbiased permutation-invariant estimators for arbitrary copy numbers; (3) a universal variance expansion with 1/n leading term equal to the multiparameter quantum Cramér-Rao variance (asymptotic efficiency); (4) characterization of higher-order O(1/n²) scaling at degenerate points; (5) for Bures χ²-divergence, a spectral lower bound on the reference state is sufficient but not necessary for bounded-variance estimation.

Conclusion: The paper provides a complete structural theory for unbiased estimation of polynomial quantum state functionals, establishing quantum U-statistics as provably optimal (asymptotically efficient) estimators. The results generalize classical U-statistic theory to the quantum setting and offer practical guidance, including a nuanced picture for the Bures χ²-divergence where bounded-variance estimation is achievable under more general conditions than simple spectral bounds.

Abstract: We study unbiased estimation of scalar-valued polynomial functionals of quantum states from independent copies. We establish an equivalence between the first-order marginal of a permutation-invariant finite-copy observable and the functional gradient. We then prove that, among unbiased permutation-invariant estimators, the quantum U-statistic is the unique extension to an arbitrary number of copies. We further derive a universal variance expansion in which the leading $1/n$ term is determined by the variance of the functional gradient, while higher-order contributions are of order $O(1/n^2)$. This leading variance coincides with the multiparameter quantum Cramér-Rao limit, establishing asymptotic efficiency of quantum U-statistics. We also characterize the higher-order scaling at points where the variance of the first-order gradient vanishes. As an application, we analyze the Bures $χ^2$-divergence and show that a spectral lower bound on the reference state is sufficient but not necessary for bounded-variance estimation.

</details>


### [152] [Quantum speed limits based on quantifiers of quantum-state texture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08752)
*Yuhang Xie,Yanjun Chu,Chenyang Cui,Shao-Ming Fei*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates the relationship between quantum speed limits and quantum-state texture using canonical quantifiers (trace distance, state rugosity, and Jensen-Shannon divergence), deriving minimum evolution time bounds for systems under dephasing, dissipative, and amplitude damping dynamics, and exploring the tightness of these bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum speed limits establish fundamental lower bounds on evolution time scales of quantum systems, while quantum-state texture is an emerging concept in quantum resource theory. Investigating their interplay could reveal deeper fundamental constraints on quantum evolutionary speed with implications for quantum technologies.

Method: The authors derive quantum speed limit bounds based on quantum-state texture quantifiers including trace distance, state rugosity, and Jensen-Shannon divergence. They analyze minimum evolution times for physical systems under dephasing and dissipative dynamics, and further study nonunitary dynamics via completely positive and trace-preserving maps using the amplitude damping channel as a canonical example. The tightness of the derived bounds is examined across these dynamical models.

Result: The results demonstrate that quantum speed limits formulated from quantum-state texture accurately capture fundamental constraints on quantum evolutionary speed. The bounds are analyzed for their tightness across the considered dephasing, dissipative, and amplitude damping dynamical scenarios.

Conclusion: Quantum speed limits derived from quantum-state texture provide meaningful fundamental constraints on quantum evolution speed, offering promising applications in quantum computing, quantum control, and quantum metrology.

Abstract: Quantum speed limits impose intrinsic lower bounds on the shortest time scale for quantum system evolution. As an emerging paradigm in quantum resource theory, quantum-state texture has attracted research interest amid the rapid advancement of quantum theory. Herein, we investigate the interplay between quantum speed limits and quantum-state texture via several canonical quantifiers, including trace distance, state rugosity and Jensen-Shannon divergence. To demonstrate our findings, we analyze the minimum evolution time of physical systems subject to dephasing and dissipative dynamics. For the Jensen-Shannon divergence, we further explore nonunitary dynamics described by completely positive and trace-preserving maps, taking the amplitude damping channel as a typical example. In addition, we explore the tightness of these bounds in the considered dynamical models. Our results reveal that quantum speed limits derived from quantum-state texture capture the fundamental constraints on quantum evolutionary speed, with promising applications in quantum computing, quantum control and quantum metrology.

</details>


### [153] [Dual-Trigger of Series Nanowire Detector for Event-Based Photon Number Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08811)
*Stefanie Grotowski,Daniel Landes,Fabian Wietschorke,Paul Pucknus,Rasmus Flaschmann,Torsten Langer,Torsten Krause,Kai Müller,Jonathan J. Finley*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work introduces a dual-trigger readout scheme for three-pixel series-connected SNSPDs that triggers on both rising and falling edges of the detection pulse, preserving precise arrival times while mapping photon number onto the inter-edge time interval. This achieves 99% posterior confidence classification for n=1, 2, or 3 photons, with timing jitter decreasing below 41 ps for n≥2, and reveals that CFD outperforms edge-triggering for single-photon arrival-time extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional single-channel edge-triggered readout of PNR detectors fails to resolve photon number in real time and cannot characterize how timing jitter depends on photon number n, limiting both photon-number resolution and timing precision in photonic quantum technologies.

Method: A dual-trigger method is applied to a three-pixel series-connected SNSPD, triggering on both the rising and falling edges of the detection pulse. The photon number is mapped onto the time interval between the rising and falling edges, preserving the precise arrival time while enabling clear separation of detection event classes. Edge-triggering is compared with constant-fraction discrimination (CFD) for arrival-time extraction.

Result: Detection events are assigned to n=1, 2, or 3 photons with 99% posterior confidence across all three classes. Timing jitter decreases as n increases, falling below 41 ps for n≥2. CFD provides lower jitter for single-photon events and a nearly constant mean arrival time compared to edge-triggering.

Conclusion: Dual-triggering establishes itself as a robust, low-latency readout scheme for PNR detectors, while the revealed photon-number dependence of timing jitter has direct implications for the timing precision achievable in heralded photonic quantum applications.

Abstract: Photon-number-resolving (PNR) detectors are essential components of photonic quantum technologies. However, conventional single-channel edge-triggered readout struggles to resolve the photon number $n$ in real time or to characterize how timing jitter depends on $n$. In this work, we use a dual-trigger method on a three-pixel series-connected superconducting nanowire single-photon detector (SNSPD) that triggers on both the rising and falling edges of the detection pulse. By doing so, we preserve the precise arrival time of the detection event while mapping the photon number onto the time interval between the rising and falling edges, allowing clear separation of the events. Using this technique, we assign each detection event to $n = 1, 2,$ or 3 photons with $99\,\%$ posterior confidence across all three classes. The timing jitter decreases as $n$ increases, reaching values below $41\, \text{ps}$ for $n \geq 2$. Comparing edge-triggering with constant-fraction discrimination (CFD) for arrival-time extraction, we find that CFD yields lower jitter for single-photon events and a nearly constant mean arrival time. Altogether, our results establish dual-triggering as a robust, low-latency readout scheme for PNR detectors, while revealing a photon-number dependence of the timing jitter relevant to timing precision achievable in heralded photonic quantum applications.

</details>


### [154] [Distributed Quantum Property Testing with Quantum Carrier Pigeons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08864)
*Kenny Chen,Mina Doosti,Ryan Sweke,Chirag Wadhwa*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a framework for distributed quantum inference under communication constraints, where m nodes each receive one copy of an unknown d-dimensional quantum state and communicate via constrained one-way channels to a central node. For the problem of quantum state certification with limited communication (nc bits and nq qubits per channel, with nc + nq ≤ log d), they establish that the copy complexity is Θ(d²/(2^{nq} 2^{nc/2} ε²)) when nodes share randomness, prove an Ω(d³/(4^{nq} 2^{nc} ε²)) lower bound in the private-coin setting showing shared randomness is necessary, and provide a near-optimal private-coin algorithm matching this bound up to a √(log d) factor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To generalize the classical distributed inference framework of Acharya, Canonne, and Tyagi to the quantum setting by allowing quantum resources (quantum communication and shared entanglement), and to characterize the fundamental copy complexity of distributed quantum state certification under communication constraints.

Method: The paper establishes a distributed quantum state certification framework with one-way constrained communication channels (nc bits and nq qubits per node, with nc + nq ≤ log d). They analyze two settings: public-coin (shared randomness) where they achieve Θ(d²/(2^{nq} 2^{nc/2} ε²)) copy complexity, and private-coin where they prove a lower bound of Ω(d³/(4^{nq} 2^{nc} ε²)) and construct an algorithm achieving this bound up to a √(log d) factor.

Result: The paper derives tight copy complexity bounds for distributed quantum state certification in both public-coin and private-coin settings, demonstrating that shared randomness substantially improves the achievable complexity and characterizing the effect of quantum and classical communication resources on performance.

Conclusion: This work establishes a general framework for distributed quantum inference with communication constraints and provides a near-complete characterization of the copy complexity of distributed state certification, showing the role of shared randomness and quantum communication in this distributed setting.

Abstract: We introduce a framework for distributed quantum inference under communication constraints. In our model, $m$ distributed nodes each receive one copy of an unknown $d$-dimensional quantum state $ρ$, before communicating via a constrained one-way communication channel with a central node, which aims to infer some property of $ρ$. This framework generalizes the classical distributed inference framework introduced by Acharya, Canonne, and Tyagi [COLT 2019], by allowing quantum resources such as quantum communication and shared entanglement.
  Within this setting, we focus on the fundamental problem of quantum state certification: Given a complete description of some state $σ$, decide whether $ρ=σ$ or $\|ρ-σ\|_1\geq ε$. Additionally, we focus on the case of limited communication between distributed nodes and the central node: we assume each communication channel is limited to only $n_c$ bits and $n_q$ qubits with $n_c + n_q \leq \log d$. When all nodes can make use of a shared source of randomness, we show that the copy complexity of distributed state certification is $Θ(\frac{d^2}{2^{n_q} 2^{n_c/2}ε^2})$. We further demonstrate that shared randomness is necessary to achieve the above complexity, by proving an $Ω(\frac{d^3}{4^{n_q} 2^{n_c} ε^2})$ lower bound in the $\textit{private-coin}$ setting. Moreover, we develop a private-coin algorithm that matches this bound up to a $\sqrt{\log d}$ factor, showing this complexity is near-optimal. Together, our work establishes a general framework for distributed quantum inference with communication constraints and characterizes the complexity of distributed state certification with limited communication.

</details>


### [155] [A Platform-aware Compilation Framework for Fault-tolerant Quantum Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08908)
*Srushti Patil,Susan X. Chen,Andreas Juul Bay-Smidt,Stefan Alaric Schäffer,Peter Krogstrup,Stefano Paesani,Gemma C. Solomon*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a platform-aware quantum compilation framework that re-compiles quantum circuits into hardware-compatible instruction sets and fault-tolerant operations, providing end-to-end resource estimates (physical-qubit count, time-to-solution, classical processing time) across different hardware modalities. It introduces a transversal active volume (t-AV) compilation architecture for long-range logical connectivity platforms and benchmarks it on Hamiltonian simulation of the 2D Fermi Hubbard model and eigenenergy estimation of trimethylenemethane, showing end-to-end quantum simulations can be achieved with ~10^4 physical qubits and runtimes from 10^2 ms (photonics, superconducting) to 10^5 ms (neutral atoms).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current quantum compilation frameworks are typically committed to a single architecture-hardware configuration, making it difficult to assess and compare resource estimates across different physical hardware platforms and error correction models. A platform-aware approach is needed to enable fair cross-platform resource estimation.

Method: The authors develop a platform-aware compilation framework that re-compiles quantum circuits into hardware-compatible instruction sets and fault-tolerant operations, then provides end-to-end resource estimates in terms of physical-qubit count, time-to-solution, and classical processing time. They benchmark the framework using different compilers tailored to specific hardware modalities (connectivity, clock speed, noise model) and introduce a transversal active volume (t-AV) compilation architecture for platforms supporting long-range logical connectivity. Benchmarks include Hamiltonian simulation of the 2D Fermi Hubbard model and eigenenergy estimation of trimethylenemethane (a small molecule candidate for early fault-tolerant quantum chemistry).

Result: The framework provides end-to-end resource estimates across platforms. For trimethylenemethane eigenenergy estimation, results show quantum simulations achievable with ~10^4 physical qubits, with runtimes ranging from 10^2 ms for photonics and superconducting platforms to 10^5 ms for neutral atoms.

Conclusion: A platform-aware compilation framework enables meaningful cross-platform resource estimation for fault-tolerant quantum computing, demonstrating that early fault-tolerant quantum chemistry simulations are feasible at the ~10^4 physical qubit scale, with significant runtime variation depending on hardware modality.

Abstract: The compilation of an algorithm can vary significantly with the choice of physical hardware platform and error correction model. Yet, current compilation frameworks typically commit to a single architecture-hardware configuration, making it difficult to assess resource estimates across platforms. We present a platform-aware compilation framework that re-compiles a quantum circuit into a hardware-compatible instruction set as well as fault-tolerant operations and provides end-to-end resource estimates in terms of physical-qubit count, time-to-solution, and classical processing time. We benchmark the framework by obtaining end-to-end resource estimates for different compilers, each tailored to the functionalities of specific hardware modalities: connectivity, clock speed, and noise model. As part of this framework, we introduce a transversal active volume (t-AV) compilation architecture designed for the efficient execution of fault-tolerant operations in platforms supporting long-range logical connectivity. We benchmark the framework for Hamiltonian simulation of the 2D Fermi Hubbard model as well as for eigenenergy estimation of a small molecule (trimethylenemethane) as a candidate for early fault-tolerant demonstration of quantum chemistry. For the latter, we show that end-to-end quantum simulations can be achieved with $\sim10^4$ physical qubits and runtimes ranging from $10^2$ ms (photonics, superconducting) to $10^5$ ms (neutral atoms).

</details>


### [156] [Resilience Beyond the Light Cone: Error-Detected Primitives for Practical Dynamic Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08925)
*Kevin C. Smith,Bibek Pokharel,Satvik Maurya,Maika Takita*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a general error-detection framework for dynamic quantum circuits that use mid-circuit measurements and classical feedforward, allowing long-range entanglement primitives to trade infidelity for postselection overhead without additional ancillas. The framework unifies and improves fan-out gates, multi-qubit Pauli rotations, W/Dicke state preparation, and non-normal matrix product states, while also proposing a reduced-depth error-detected Hadamard test. Experiments on a superconducting processor demonstrate a 100-qubit long-range entangled Bell pair with fidelity 0.59 (exceeding the 0.5 certification threshold that the baseline fails at 0.39), and 20-qubit W-state preparation via 40-qubit GHZ states with ~0.2 absolute fidelity improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic circuits can generate long-range entanglement in constant depth beyond the light-cone limits of unitary circuits, but they require system-size-scaling mid-circuit measurements and feedforward that introduce errors degrading the very entanglement they enable. The authors seek to alleviate this tension by designing error-detection schemes that reduce infidelity at the cost of postselection, without extra ancillas, making these low-depth primitives practical on present-day hardware.

Method: The authors formulate a unified framework for a broad class of dynamic-circuit primitives and show that each admits an error-detection scheme that exchanges infidelity for postselection overhead with no additional ancilla qubits. They develop error-detected implementations for fan-out gates, multi-qubit Pauli rotations, W and higher-weight Dicke state preparation, and certain non-normal matrix product states, and additionally introduce a reduced-depth error-detected Hadamard test. The schemes are validated experimentally on a superconducting quantum processor using error-detected preparation of long-range entangled Bell pairs across a 100-qubit chain and constant-depth W-state preparation via GHZ-state consumption.

Result: On a superconducting processor, the error-detected preparation of a 100-qubit long-range entangled Bell pair achieved fidelity F=0.59±0.02, surpassing the entanglement-certification threshold F>0.5, whereas the baseline dynamic-circuit implementation failed at 0.39±0.01. Constant-depth W-state preparation of up to 20 qubits (consuming GHZ states of up to 40 qubits) showed absolute fidelity improvements of ΔF≈0.2 at the largest sizes studied.

Conclusion: The proposed error-detection framework makes low-depth dynamic-circuit primitives practically viable on current hardware by enabling infidelity-to-postselection trade-offs without ancilla overhead. The experimental demonstrations of 100-qubit long-range entanglement above certification thresholds and markedly improved large W-state fidelities indicate these primitives are within reach for near-term quantum processors.

Abstract: Dynamic circuits, which augment unitary operations with mid-circuit measurements and classical feedforward, can generate long-range entanglement in constant depth, enabling low-depth primitives ranging from nontrivial state preparation to many-qubit entangling gates. Escaping the light-cone constraints of unitary circuits, however, comes at a cost: these primitives typically require a number of mid-circuit measurements that scales with system size and that, together with feedforward latency, can introduce errors that degrade the long-range entanglement they rely on. Here, we alleviate this tension by showing that many such primitives, when cast into a common framework, admit an error-detection scheme that trades infidelity for postselection overhead with no additional ancillas. Our framework thus unifies and upgrades a broad class of primitives including fan-out gates, multi-qubit Pauli rotations, the preparation of W and higher-weight Dicke states, and certain non-normal matrix product states. We also introduce a reduced-depth, error-detected implementation of the Hadamard test, extending the use cases of dynamic circuits to a key algorithmic primitive. Finally, we establish the practical utility of our scheme through experiments on a superconducting quantum processor. We demonstrate the error-detected preparation of a long-range entangled Bell pair spanning a 100-qubit chain with fidelity $F=0.59\pm0.02$, surpassing the entanglement-certification threshold $F>0.5$ that the baseline dynamic-circuit implementation fails to reach ($0.39\pm0.01$). Separately, we demonstrate constant-depth preparation of W states of up to 20 qubits by consuming GHZ states of up to 40 qubits, finding absolute fidelity improvements of $ΔF\approx 0.2$ across the largest sizes studied. Altogether, these results bring low-depth dynamic-circuit primitives within practical reach on present-day hardware.

</details>


### [157] [First fault-tolerant quantum memory demonstration for a generalized superfast encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08957)
*James Brown,Kenny Heitritter*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces an even-distance constant stabilizer-weight fermion-to-qubit mapping called Generalized Superfast Encoding (GSE), where each of N modes is assigned a d-qubit block on a ring. The stabilizer generators have constant weight 4 or 6 for any even distance d, and the full stabilizer set partitions into four qubit-wise commuting groups enabling compact syndrome extraction. Simulating quantum memory under circuit-level depolarizing noise for [[48,8,6]] and [[64,8,8]] codes shows a threshold of ~4×10^-3, marking the first fault-tolerant quantum-memory characterization of a fermion mapping with threshold-like scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While GSE fermion-to-qubit mappings have inherent error-correcting/detecting properties, all prior demonstrations were limited to error detection only, with no observed fault-tolerance under circuit-level noise. This work aims to construct a GSE code that achieves genuine fault tolerance with constant-weight stabilizers and compact syndrome-extraction scheduling.

Method: The authors construct an even-distance d GSE code where each of N modes is assigned a d-qubit block arranged on a ring. Stabilizer generators have constant weight 4 or 6 for any even distance d, and the stabilizer set is partitioned into four qubit-wise commuting groups, enabling efficient syndrome-extraction scheduling. They simulate quantum memory experiments under circuit-level depolarizing noise for two code instances, [[48,8,6]] and [[64,8,8]].

Result: The quantum memory simulations for the [[48,8,6]] and [[64,8,8]] GSE codes reveal a threshold of approximately 4×10^-3 under circuit-level depolarizing noise. This is the first demonstration of fault-tolerant quantum-memory behavior for a fermion-to-qubit mapping showing threshold-like scaling.

Conclusion: The proposed constant stabilizer-weight GSE construction achieves genuine fault tolerance under circuit-level noise, extending fermion-to-qubit mappings beyond error detection into full error correction with observed threshold behavior. The constant-weight stabilizers and four-group commuting partition provide a practical path for fault-tolerant quantum simulation of fermionic systems.

Abstract: The Generalized Superfast Encoding (GSE) is a fermion-to-qubit mapping that has error-correcting/detecting properties. To this point, all demonstrations have been relegated to error-detection only, as no fault-tolerance under circuit-level noise has been observed. Here, we introduce an even-distance $d$ constant stabilizer-weight GSE where each of $N$ modes is assigned a $d$-qubit block arranged on a ring. The resulting stabilizer generators have constant weight 4 or 6 for any even distance $d$. Furthermore, the full stabilizer set of this construction can always be partitioned into four qubit-wise commuting groups, which enables compact syndrome-extraction scheduling. We simulate quantum memory experiments under circuit-level depolarizing noise for two instances of this code, $[[48,8,6]]$ and $[[64,8,8]]$ and the threshold is observed to be $\approx 4\times10^{-3}$. This is, to our knowledge, the first fault-tolerant quantum-memory characterization of a fermion-mapping with threshold-like scaling.

</details>


### [158] [Restart and first detection in a lackadaisical quantum walk with flat-band localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08973)
*Debraj Das*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes stochastic and sharp restart protocols in a one-dimensional lackadaisical discrete-time quantum walk with self-loop weight ℓ. The walk features a flat band (causing intrinsic localization) and two dispersive bands. The authors compare flat-band-active (finite overlap) and flat-band-dark (zero overlap) initial states under geometric restart (mean square displacement scales as q⁻²), power-law restart (stationary distributions exist only under specific conditions on the exponent s), and monitored sharp-restart first-detection protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how restart protocols interact with the multi-band structure of lackadaisical quantum walks—specifically how intrinsic localization from the flat band competes with or is modified by restart-induced dynamics—and to reveal qualitatively different behaviors depending on initial states' flat-band overlap.

Method: The authors study stochastic restart (geometric and power-law waiting-time distributions) and sharp (deterministic) restart in a 1D lackadaisical DTQW. They derive stationary mean-squared displacement scaling, restart-site occupation probabilities, and conditions for existence of normalized stationary distributions. For sharp restart, they analyze monitored first-detection statistics as a function of self-loop weight ℓ and restart period r, distinguishing flat-band-active versus flat-band-dark initial states.

Result: For geometric restart, MSD scales as q⁻² as q→0. Restart-site occupation tends to the intrinsic localized value for flat-band-active states but vanishes as q ln(1/q) for flat-band-dark states. For power-law restart, normalized stationary distributions require s>2, and p-th spatial moments require s>p+2; for 1<s≤2, flat-band-active occupations converge to the intrinsic flat-band profile while flat-band-dark occupations vanish. For sharp-restart monitoring, flat-band-active mean first-detected-passage time exhibits a minimum at intermediate self-loop weight, while flat-band-dark approaches a ballistic detection limit as ℓ→∞.

Conclusion: The presence of a flat band fundamentally alters restart statistics: flat-band-active quantum walks retain intrinsic localization signatures under weak restart, whereas flat-band-dark states are more sensitive to restart and lose localization. The divergent restart behaviors across geometric, power-law, and sharp protocols reveal rich, band-structure-dependent control of quantum transport and detection.

Abstract: We study stochastic and sharp restart in a one-dimensional lackadaisical discrete-time quantum walk with self-loop weight $\ell$. In the absence of restart, the dynamics has a flat band responsible for intrinsic localization and two dispersive bands supporting ballistic propagation. We compare two initially localized benchmark states: a flat-band-active state with finite flat-band overlap and a flat-band-dark state with zero flat-band overlap. For geometric stochastic restart with per-step restart probability $q$, the stationary mean-squared displacement scales as $q^{-2}$ as $q\to0$. In the same limit, the restart-site occupation probability approaches the restart-free intrinsic localized value for the flat-band-active state, whereas for the flat-band-dark state it vanishes as $q\ln(1/q)$. For power-law restart, where $p_m\propto m^{-s}$ is the probability that the waiting time to the next restart is $m$ steps, a normalized stationary site-occupation distribution exists only for $s>2$, while the stationary absolute spatial moment of order $p$ is finite only for $s>p+2$. In the regime $1<s\leq2$, at every fixed lattice site, the flat-band-active occupation converges to the intrinsic flat-band profile, while the flat-band-dark occupation tends to zero. We also consider monitored first detection with sharp restart, in which the walk is reinitialized after a fixed number $r$ of consecutive unsuccessful measurements. For fixed $r$, the mean first-detected-passage time of the flat-band-active state exhibits a minimum at an intermediate self-loop weight, whereas the flat-band-dark state approaches a ballistic detection limit as $\ell\to\infty$.

</details>


### [159] [No information transmission through quantum channels above capacity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08998)
*Hao-Chung Cheng,Marco Tomamichel*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves exponential strong converse theorems for unassisted quantum and classical communication over arbitrary finite-dimensional memoryless quantum channels, showing that transmission rates above channel capacity fail catastrophically with exponentially decaying fidelity and success probability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish that quantum channel capacity demarcates a sharp phase transition, ruling out the possibility of reliable transmission above capacity even when arbitrarily large errors are tolerated.

Method: The authors employ the classical Arimoto strategy for strong converse proofs, augmented by a crucial new ingredient: integral representations of Rényi information measures that yield asymptotic continuity bounds for Rényi capacities.

Result: Exponential strong converse theorems are proven for both unassisted quantum communication (entanglement-generation fidelity decays exponentially) and classical communication (success probability decays exponentially) at rates exceeding the respective channel capacities.

Conclusion: The quantum channel capacity represents a fundamental phase transition: reliable communication is always achievable below capacity, while any attempt exceeding capacity fails catastrophically with exponentially decaying success metrics, establishing a strong converse for arbitrary finite-dimensional memoryless quantum channels.

Abstract: We show that the capacity of a quantum channel demarcates a phase transition: while reliable transmission below capacity is always possible, any attempt to transmit information above it fails catastrophically. Specifically, we prove exponential strong converse theorems for unassisted quantum and classical communication over arbitrary finite-dimensional memoryless quantum channels. At rates beyond the respective capacity, the entanglement-generation fidelity and the success probability for classical communication decay exponentially with the number of channel uses. This rules out transmission above capacity even when one tolerates arbitrarily large errors. Our proof follows the classical Arimoto strategy, augmented by a crucial new ingredient: integral representations of Rényi information measures that lead to asymptotic continuity bounds for Rényi capacities.

</details>


### [160] [Preparation of a Bell state in an analogue device hosting the transverse-field Ising model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09013)
*Ana Palacios*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A protocol is proposed for preparing the singlet Bell state in a transverse-field Ising model analogue device by directly controlling Hamiltonian terms. It combines adiabatic ground-state preparation with resonant population transfer between the two lowest energy levels, achieving gate-comparable fidelities (~100 ns) and infidelities down to ~10⁻⁴.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard gate-based Bell state preparation requires decomposing into elementary gates, whereas analogue quantum simulators implementing the transverse-field Ising model may not readily support such decompositions. There is a need for a protocol that prepares maximally entangled states using only the native Hamiltonian controls available in analogue devices.

Method: The protocol exploits that the singlet Bell state is the first excited state of a symmetric Hamiltonian family within the transverse-field Ising model with ZZ interactions and local X/Z fields. It proceeds in two steps: (1) an adiabatic interpolation that prepares the ground state of the symmetric Hamiltonian, followed by (2) resonant population transfer between the ground and first excited states via direct Hamiltonian control.

Result: For realistic superconducting platform parameters, the protocol achieves fidelities comparable to standard gate-based preparation on similar time scales (~100 ns), and can reach infidelities on the order of 10⁻⁴ with moderately extended duration. High fidelities are maintained under systematic control errors and implementation imperfections.

Conclusion: The proposed Hamiltonian-control-only scheme provides a viable alternative to gate-based approaches for preparing singlet Bell states in analogue Ising-type devices, offering competitive fidelity and speed while being robust to realistic experimental imperfections.

Abstract: We propose a protocol for preparing the singlet Bell state in an analogue device implementing the transverse-field Ising model with ZZ interactions and local X and Z fields, which can be engineered within the superconducting platform, among others. This protocol is performed by direct control of the terms of the Hamiltonian alone. The method exploits the fact that the singlet state is the first excited state of a symmetric Hamiltonian family, and comprises two steps: an adiabatic interpolation preparing the ground state of said symmetric Hamiltonian, followed by the resonant population transfer between its two lowest energy levels. For realistic parameters, the protocol achieves fidelities comparable to standard gate-based preparation within similar time scales ($\sim$100 ns), and can reach infidelities on the order of $10^{-4}$ with a moderately increased duration. We further analyse robustness against systematic control errors and show that high fidelities are maintained under implementation imperfections.

</details>


### [161] [Modular commutator as a robust topological invariant and approximate Markovianity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09019)
*Tai Hsuan Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper shows that the modular commutator, a probe of the chiral central charge in gapped topological states, remains asymptotically invariant under deformations even when the local quantum Markov condition holds only approximately. It proves trace-norm continuity of the modular commutator, combines these results to establish asymptotic invariance within gapped quantum phases connected by quasi-local unitary paths, derives a quantitative exponential lower bound on the conditional mutual information required for a nonzero modular commutator given an entanglement area law, and extends all results to the Hall conductance estimator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previously, invariance of the modular commutator under deformations was established only under an exact local quantum Markov condition (zero conditional mutual information across strip tripartitions of a disk). However, this exact condition forces the probe to vanish, so it was unclear whether the modular commutator remains robust in physically relevant states where the Markov property holds only approximately. The paper aims to resolve this tension for finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces.

Method: The paper provides mathematical proofs for finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces: (1) proves that approximate local quantum Markov property implies asymptotically vanishing change of the modular commutator under topology-preserving deformations; (2) proves trace-norm continuity of the modular commutator; (3) combines deformation invariance with continuity to establish asymptotic invariance within gapped quantum phases connected by quasi-local unitary paths preserving approximate local quantum Markov condition; (4) analyzes finite-time dynamics generated by modular Hamiltonians to derive a quantitative lower bound on conditional mutual information; (5) demonstrates the results apply to the Hall conductance estimator.

Result: The results establish that the modular commutator remains asymptotically invariant within gapped quantum phases even when the local quantum Markov condition is only approximately satisfied. The quantitative lower bound shows that the conditional mutual information I(A:C|B) across strip tripartitions cannot decay faster than exponentially with the width of B, given an entanglement area law and a nonzero modular commutator—extending the exact no-go theorem to a quantitative finite bound. All conclusions extend to the Hall conductance estimator.

Conclusion: The tension between Markov property robustness and nontrivial modular commutator values is resolved: the probe remains asymptotically robust when the Markov property holds approximately rather than exactly, provided an entanglement area law holds. The exponential lower bound on conditional mutual information represents a quantitative finite-size refinement of previous exact results, with direct implications for both the chiral central charge probe and the Hall conductance estimator.

Abstract: The modular commutator provides a bulk, local, single-wave-function probe of the chiral central charge $c_-$ for gapped ground states. Its invariance under deformations was previously established under a local quantum Markov condition---namely, the conditional mutual information $I(A:C|B)$ is zero for all tripartitions of a disk into three consecutive strips A, B, and C \cite{Modular-commutator-Gapped}. However, the local quantum Markov condition also forces the probe to vanish, leaving open whether modular commutator remains robust in physically relevant states where the Markov property holds only approximately. In this paper, we resolve this tension for finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces: the approximate local quantum Markov property implies the change of the modular commutator under topology-preserving deformations vanishes asymptotically. We next prove trace-norm continuity of the modular commutator. Combining deformation invariance with trace-norm continuity, we establish that the modular commutator remains asymptotically invariant within gapped quantum phases connected by quasi-local unitary paths that preserve the approximate local quantum Markov condition. Conversely, by analyzing finite-time dynamics generated by modular Hamiltonians, we derive a quantitative lower bound on the conditional mutual information required for a non-zero modular commutator: $I(A:C|B)$ across such strip tripartitions cannot decay faster than exponentially with the width of B given that the state satisfies entanglement area law, extending the exact no-go theorem of \cite{strict-J-2024} to a quantitative finite bound . Finally, we demonstrate that those conclusions apply equally to the Hall conductance estimator \cite{FanSahayVishwanath2023}.

</details>


### [162] [Semidefinite extension complexity of the separable set, with applications to approximate disentanglers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09033)
*Sevag Gharibian,Carsten Hecht,Dorian Rudolph*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves quantitative lower bounds on semidefinite extension complexity for approximating the set of separable quantum states, showing that any SDP approximating the maximum acceptance probability over separable states (the QMA(2) problem) requires superpolynomial size in d for uniform additive error a = o(1), improving prior quasipolynomial bounds. The proof combines quantitative pseudo-density theorems with explicit block-positive operators and Chebyshev amplification, with results verified in Lean.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish quantitative limitations of semidefinite programming approaches for approximating separable quantum states, providing lower bounds on the extended-formulation size needed to approximate the QMA(2) optimization problem, and to give a quantitative counterpart to Fawzi's no-go theorem for exact semidefinite representations.

Method: The proof combines the quantitative pseudo-density theorem of Lee, Raghavendra, and Steurer with explicit block-positive operators and Chebyshev amplification techniques. The analysis is carried out in the extended-formulation model of Harrow, Natarajan, and Wu (HNW), where measurements share a common feasible region with objective-independent embeddings of product states. Main results are formally verified using the Lean proof assistant.

Result: For every 0<θ<2/7, there exist constants such that any SDP with uniform additive error a has size at least d^{c_θ·min{a^{-1/3}, d^θ}}. The bound is superpolynomial in d when a=o(1), becomes d^{Ω(d^θ)} when a≤d^{-3θ}, improving HNW's quasipolynomial bound at inverse-square error. The same bound holds for any SDP-representable convex set within trace distance a of the separable set.

Conclusion: Semidefinite programs approximating the separable quantum states set require superpolynomial size even at small constant error, establishing strong quantitative lower bounds for the SDP extended-formulation model in QMA(2). Results are machine-verified in Lean.

Abstract: We prove quantitative lower bounds on the semidefinite extension complexity of the set of separable quantum states on $\mathbb{C}^d\otimes\mathbb{C}^d$. We consider semidefinite programs (SDPs) that approximate the maximum acceptance probability of a measurement over separable states, the optimization problem underlying QMA(2). In the extended-formulation model of Harrow, Natarajan, and Wu (HNW), all measurements share a common feasible region and an objective-independent embedding of product states that exactly reproduces their acceptance probabilities. For every $0<θ<2/7$, there are constants $c_θ,a_θ>0$ such that, for sufficiently large $d$, any such SDP with uniform additive error $0<a\le a_θ$ has size at least $d^{c_θ\min\{a^{-1/3},d^θ\}}$. The bound applies at sufficiently small constant error, is superpolynomial in $d$ whenever $a=o(1)$, and becomes $d^{Ω(d^θ)}$ when $a\le d^{-3θ}$, improving HNW's quasipolynomial bound at inverse- square error. The same bound holds for any SDP-representable convex set of states that contains all separable states and lies within trace distance $a$ of them, giving a quantitative counterpart to Fawzi's theorem that the separable set has no exact semidefinite representation. Our proof combines the quantitative pseudo-density theorem of Lee, Raghavendra, and Steurer with explicit block-positive operators and Chebyshev amplification. Our main results are supported by Lean proofs.

</details>


### [163] [Near-Optimal Quantum Lower Bounds for Convex Optimization via Fourier Rank](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09035)
*Brandon Augustino,Shouvanik Chakrabarti,Enrico Fontana,Dylan Herman,Junhyung Lyle Kim,Guneykan Ozgul,Nadezhda Voronova*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves a near-linear quantum query lower bound Ω(n/(log n · log log n)) for high-accuracy convex optimization over explicit n-dimensional ellipsoids with membership queries, resolving an open question up to logarithmic factors. The proof relies on a novel Fourier-rank-based polynomial method yielding lower bounds for determinant computation and minimum eigenvalue estimation in the real-valued setting, extending prior work from finite fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to resolve an open question posed by Chakrabarti et al. (2020) and van Apeldoorn et al. (2020) regarding the quantum query complexity of high-accuracy convex optimization. While upper bounds were previously known, matching lower bounds were lacking, preventing a tight characterization of the query complexity for this fundamental optimization problem.

Method: The proof is built around a lower bound for determinant computation derived via a novel polynomial method based on Fourier-rank. In the continuous matrix phase-query model, the authors show computing the determinant of a real n×n matrix requires at least n/2 matrix-vector product queries. The construction leverages lower bounds in the phase-query model for determinant and minimum eigenvalue estimation, extended from finite fields to the real-valued setting.

Result: The authors establish that any algorithm returning feasible points with additive objective error Θ(n⁻²) requires Ω(n/(log n · log log n)) membership queries, both for exact and approximate feasibility (within Θ(n⁻²) distance). They also prove an Ω(n) phase-query lower bound for estimating minimum eigenvalues to additive accuracy Θ(n⁻²), and a near-optimal gradient-query lower bound for constant-accuracy optimization of smooth and strongly convex functions.

Conclusion: Combined with existing upper bounds, this work tightly characterizes the quantum query complexity of high-accuracy convex optimization up to logarithmic factors, settling the open question. The Fourier-rank-based polynomial method introduced here provides a new technique for deriving quantum lower bounds in continuous/real-valued settings, with implications for determinant computation and eigenvalue estimation problems.

Abstract: We establish a near-linear quantum query lower bound for high-accuracy convex optimization over an explicit family of $n$-dimensional ellipsoids. We focus on linear optimization with an explicitly given objective, where the feasible set is accessed through a membership oracle. We show that any algorithm that, for every unit linear objective, returns an exactly feasible point with additive objective error $Θ(n^{-2})$ requires $Ω\!\left(\frac{n}{\log n\,\log\log n}\right)$ membership queries. The same lower bound can be shown to hold if the returned point is only required to be approximately feasible, within $Θ(n^{-2})$ distance from the feasible set. This resolves, up to logarithmic factors, an open question posed by Chakrabarti, Childs, Li, and Wu~(\textit{Quantum}, 2020) and by van Apeldoorn, Gilyén, Gribling, and de Wolf~(\textit{Quantum}, 2020). Coupled with the upper bounds in these papers, the query complexity of high-accuracy convex optimization is characterized tightly up to logarithmic factors. The proof is built around a lower bound for determinant computation that is derived via a novel polynomial method based on Fourier-rank. In the continuous matrix phase-query model, computing the determinant of a real $n\times n$ matrix requires at least $n/2$ matrix-vector product queries. The construction also yields an $Ω(n)$ phase-query lower bound for estimating the minimum eigenvalue of a real symmetric $n\times n$ matrix to additive accuracy $Θ(n^{-2})$. These results extend the determinant and minimum-eigenvalue lower bounds of Childs, Hung, and Li~(ICALP 2021) from finite fields to the real-valued setting. Based on the same constructions, we also prove a near-optimal gradient-query lower bound for constant-accuracy optimization of smooth and strongly convex functions.

</details>


### [164] [Impossibility of One-Way One-Round Quantum 4-Coloring via Matrix-Space Stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09091)
*Tom Gur,Longcheng Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves that one-way one-round quantum LOCAL algorithms cannot 4-color directed cycles with high probability, even given unbounded local computation and quantum message length. This is the first high-probability quantum LOCAL lower bound beyond non-signaling and bounded-dependence models.
