The proof connects distributed quantum computing to noncommutative extremal combinatorics via a dimension-independent weighted stability theorem for a directed noncommutative analogue of Mantel's theorem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish fundamental limitations of quantum distributed algorithms in the LOCAL model, specifically showing that quantum advantages do not extend to high-probability graph coloring tasks. The work aims to bridge distributed quantum computing with extremal combinatorics to obtain lower bounds that go beyond previously known models.

Method: The proof identifies local collision probabilities in distributed quantum algorithms with the weighted multiplicative energy of matrix-space decompositions. This leads to a lower bound derived from proving a dimension-independent weighted stability theorem for a directed noncommutative analogue of Mantel's theorem, connecting the problem to noncommutative extremal combinatorics.

Result: A lower bound showing one-way one-round quantum LOCAL algorithms cannot achieve high-probability 4-coloring of directed cycles, even with unlimited local computation and quantum message sizes. This is the first such bound exceeding the non-signaling and bounded-dependence model limitations.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that exploiting the structure of distributed quantum algorithms yields stronger lower bounds than previous approaches, and establish a novel connection between distributed quantum computing and noncommutative extremal combinatorics through Mantel-type theorems.

Abstract: We show that one-way one-round quantum LOCAL algorithms cannot $4$-color directed cycles with high probability, even with unbounded local computation and quantum message length. This is the first lower bound in the high-probability quantum LOCAL setting that goes beyond the non-signaling and bounded-dependence models, exploiting the structure of distributed quantum algorithms.
  Our proof connects distributed quantum computing with noncommutative extremal combinatorics by identifying local collision probabilities with the weighted multiplicative energy of matrix-space decompositions. We obtain our lower bound by proving a dimension-independent weighted stability theorem for a directed noncommutative analogue of Mantel's theorem.

</details>


### [165] [Near-optimal synthesis of non-Gaussian phase gates via qubit-oscillator Rabi control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09132)
*Zhen Yang,Shan Jin,Zi-Wen Liu,Xiaoting Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents an efficient qubit-oscillator Rabi synthesis scheme for approximating non-Gaussian polynomial phase gates in continuous-variable quantum computation, achieving near-optimal total interaction time that scales polylogarithmically with inverse error, without requiring numerical optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-Gaussian gates are essential for universal continuous-variable quantum computation but are difficult to implement due to the engineering challenges of required nonlinearities. Existing approaches often rely on heavy numerical optimization, limiting scalability to large multimode systems.

Method: The authors develop an analytically constructed Rabi sequence for qubit-oscillator systems to approximate degree-R polynomial phase gates. The construction is closed-form (no numerical optimization), with total time scaling as O(log^((R-1)/2+o(1))(1/ε)). They also derive a matching lower bound of Ω(log^((R-1)/2)(1/ε)) to establish near-optimality.

Result: The scheme achieves polylogarithmic scaling in inverse error for phase-gate synthesis, is proven near-optimal via matching lower bounds, and extends naturally to arbitrarily large multimode systems. Applications include simulating representative CV quantum dynamics and implementing a CV quantum algorithm for solving linear PDEs.

Conclusion: Qubit-oscillator Rabi control is established as an efficient, analytically compilable, and near-optimal primitive for CV quantum information processing, addressing the non-Gaussian gate bottleneck in a scalable manner.

Abstract: Non-Gaussian gates remain a key bottleneck for universal continuous-variable (CV) quantum computation because the nonlinearities they require are difficult to engineer. To address this challenge, we develop an efficient qubit-oscillator Rabi synthesis scheme for polynomial phase gates, with a total interaction time that scales polylogarithmically with the inverse target error \(\varepsilon\). Specifically, for a class of readily preparable initial states, we show that a degree-\(R\) phase gate can be approximated by an analytically constructed Rabi sequence with total time \(O(\log^{(R-1)/2+o(1)}(1/\varepsilon))\). This construction requires no numerical optimization and therefore extends naturally to arbitrarily large multimode systems. We further establish a total-time lower bound of \(Ω(\log^{(R-1)/2}(1/\varepsilon))\), showing that the synthesis is near optimal. As applications, we use this scheme to simulate representative CV quantum dynamics and implement a CV quantum algorithm for solving linear partial differential equations. These results establish qubit-oscillator Rabi control as an efficient, analytically compilable, and near-optimal primitive for CV quantum information processing.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.dis-nn'></div>

# cond-mat.dis-nn [[Back]](#toc)

### [166] [A Heterogeneous General Model for Neuromorphic-Inspired Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05482)
*Matteo Mirigliano*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper introduces a general computational model based on an input-dependent stochastic weight network ("substrate") where weights evolve via input-triggered stochastic updates with correlations described by a matrix-valued covariance kernel. Implemented with quadratic polynomial weight functions, numerical simulations show that stochastic correlations significantly affect system response, suggesting a mechanism for neuromorphic-inspired computation without conventional weight training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is growing interest in computational architectures inspired by biological neural systems to reduce training costs, but a major gap exists in general models and design guidelines for emerging computational systems and hardware.

Method: A general substrate model is introduced with input-triggered stochastic weight updates, where weight correlations are characterized by a matrix-valued covariance kernel. The framework is realized using quadratic polynomial weight functions, with input amplitude controlling stochastic perturbation magnitude and substrate-dependent distance governing the correlation structure.

Result: Numerical simulations demonstrate that correlations in the stochastic weight evolution significantly influence the system response, indicating potential for neuromorphic-inspired computation without conventional weight training.

Conclusion: The work aims to provide a general formulation of the model and to identify its main properties and characteristics, contributing toward a generalized theory for neuromorphic and unconventional computing.

Abstract: In recent years, both academia and industry have focused on the development of computational architectures inspired by the distributed, adaptive, and event-driven characteristics of biological neural systems, with the aim of reducing the computational cost associated with conventional training approaches [1]. However, a major challenge is the lack of general models and design guidelines for emerging computational systems and hardware. This work introduces a general model based on an input-dependent stochastic weight network, referred to as a substrate. The substrate weights evolve through input-triggered stochastic updates, with correlations between weight coefficients described by a matrix-valued covariance kernel. The proposed framework is implemented using quadratic polynomial weight functions, where the input amplitude controls the magnitude of the stochastic perturbation and a substrate-dependent distance determines the correlation structure. Numerical simulations show that correlations in the stochastic weight evolution significantly affect the system response, suggesting a potential mechanism for neuromorphic-inspired computation without conventional weight training. The aim of this work is to provide a general formulation of the model and identify its main properties and characteristics. 1 H. Jaeger, Towards a generalized theory comprising digital, neuromorphic and unconventional computing, Neuromorphic Comput. Eng., vol. 1, no. 1, p. 012002, Sep. 2021

</details>


### [167] [Autonomous Chaotic Time Series Prediction using Physical Neuromorphic Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06395)
*Akshaya Rajesh,Yinhao Xu,Wave Ngampruetikorn,Zdenka Kuncic*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This study demonstrates fully autonomous closed-loop prediction of Mackey-Glass chaotic time series using simulated neuromorphic nanowire networks as physical reservoirs. Both virtual node (VN) and non-VN methods achieve high prediction accuracy (89.7-90.4% for VN; 76.2-81.5% for non-VN) over short horizons, with both approaches reproducing qualitative attractor structure over long horizons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical reservoir computing with neuromorphic networks is a promising brain-inspired approach for information processing, but autonomous (closed-loop) prediction of complex chaotic systems using nanowire network reservoirs, particularly at higher complexity regimes like τ=21, has not been previously evaluated.

Method: Simulated neuromorphic nanowire networks are used as physical reservoirs to predict the Mackey-Glass chaotic time series in a fully autonomous closed-loop configuration. Two strategies are compared: the virtual node (VN) method using temporal multiplexing to expand feature space, and a non-VN approach using all physical node readouts directly without temporal multiplexing. Performance is evaluated at τ=18 and τ=21 across short (T=100) and long (T=500) prediction horizons.

Result: VN achieves 90.4% and 89.7% accuracy at τ=18 and τ=21 respectively over T=100, while non-VN achieves 81.5% and 76.2%. Over T=500, both approaches reproduce the qualitative attractor structure and dominant spectral content, with trajectories remaining bounded. Non-VN performance suggests intrinsic nanowire network dynamics suffice without virtual node augmentation.

Conclusion: Neuromorphic nanowire networks possess intrinsic dynamics sufficient for meaningful autonomous chaotic time series prediction without virtual node augmentation. Performance may improve as physical networks scale to millions of nodes in hardware, though experimental validation on physically fabricated arrays remains necessary.

Abstract: Physical reservoir computing (PRC) with neuromorphic networks offers a promising approach to brain-inspired information processing, exploiting emergent nonlinear dynamics of physical neural networks as a computational resource. This study demonstrates fully autonomous closed-loop prediction of the Mackey--Glass (MG) chaotic time series using a simulated neuromorphic nanowire network as the physical reservoir. Two strategies are evaluated: the virtual node (VN) method, which expands the feature space by temporal multiplexing of reservoir states, and a non-VN approach that uses all physical node readouts directly without temporal multiplexing. Results are reported for two values of the MG time delay parameter, $τ= 18$ and $τ= 21$, the latter representing a more complex chaotic regime not previously evaluated for this class of physical reservoir. Over a short prediction horizon of $T = 100$ timesteps, the VN approach achieves autonomous prediction accuracies of $90.4$% and $89.7$% at $τ= 18$ and $τ= 21$, respectively, while the non-VN approach achieves $81.5$% and $76.2$%. Long-horizon analysis over $T = 500$ timesteps shows that both approaches reproduce the qualitative attractor structure and dominant spectral content of the true MG signal, with trajectories remaining bounded throughout. These results suggest that the intrinsic dynamics of neuromorphic nanowire networks are sufficient to support meaningful autonomous chaotic time series prediction without virtual node augmentation, and that performance may improve further as physical network sizes scale to the millions of nodes achievable in hardware. As this study uses simulated networks, extrapolation to physically fabricated large-scale arrays remains to be validated experimentally.

</details>


### [168] [Finite-rank multiplicative perturbations of rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07220)
*Pierre Bousseyroux,Marc Potters*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper analyzes finite-rank multiplicative perturbations of rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrices of the form A(I+T), characterizing outlier eigenvalues, their fluctuations, and eigenvector overlaps, providing a multiplicative non-Hermitian extension of the Baik–Ben Arous–Péché framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the classical additive BBP (Baik–Ben Arous–Péché) phase transition theory for spiked random matrices to the multiplicative, non-Hermitian setting, where current results are limited.

Method: The authors study models of the form A(I+T), where A is a large rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrix and T is a finite-rank normal perturbation, analyzing the spectral properties via outlier eigenvalue characterization, fluctuation behavior, and eigenvector overlap computations.

Result: The paper establishes a complete characterization of outlier eigenvalues, their fluctuation distributions, and the associated eigenvector overlaps for finite-rank multiplicative perturbations of rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrices, yielding a multiplicative non-Hermitian analogue of the classical BBP framework.

Conclusion: The results generalize the BBP phase transition phenomenon to multiplicative non-Hermitian random matrix ensembles, offering a unified theoretical framework for eigenvalue outliers and eigenvector structure under such perturbations.

Abstract: We study finite-rank multiplicative deformations of rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrices. More precisely, we consider models of the form $\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{T})$, where $\mathbf{A}$ is a large rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrix, $\mathbf{T}$ is a finite-rank normal perturbation, and $\mathbf{I}$ denotes the identity matrix. We characterize the emergence of outlier eigenvalues, their fluctuations, and the associated eigenvector overlaps. Our results provide a multiplicative non-Hermitian counterpart to the classical Baik--Ben Arous--Péché framework.

</details>


### [169] [Storing Infinite Dynamical Attractors in Nonreciprocal Associative Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07341)
*Miguel Aguilera,Daniele De Martino*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper develops a dynamical mean-field theory for nonreciprocal associative neural networks capable of storing extensive numbers of dynamical attractors (from limit cycles to strange attractors). Using path integral methods under quenched disorder, the authors derive self-consistent equations and show that memory storage capacity is governed by eigenvalue phase coherence of coupling matrices. Coherently aligned eigenphases yield retrieval far below classical limits for limit cycles, while uniformly distributed eigenphases enable substantial capacity enhancement—validated by microscopic simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and quantify how nonreciprocal (non-symmetric) interactions in associative networks affect the storage capacity of dynamical memories, extending beyond the classical equilibrium capacity bounds which apply only to fixed-point attractors.

Method: Dynamical mean-field theory derived via path integral formalism under quenched disorder. Self-consistent mean-field equations are obtained for pattern overlaps, autocorrelation functions, and linear response functions. The analysis focuses on the spectral structure (eigenvalue phases) of the coupling matrices encoding stored patterns.

Result: When eigenvalues are coherently aligned, retarded self-interactions and quenched noise interfere destructively: at zero eigenphase the classical equilibrium capacity is recovered, but for limit cycle attractors retrieval collapses far below the classical bound. When eigenphases are uniformly distributed, retarded self-interactions and most quenched noise cancel, reducing the system to an effective single-spin process and substantially amplifying storage capacity. Theoretical predictions are validated against microscopic simulations for both limit-cycle and chaotic attractors.

Conclusion: Eigenvalue decoherence in the coupling matrices—specifically, uniformly distributed eigenphases—is identified as the key mechanism enabling enhanced storage of dynamical memories in nonreciprocal associative networks. The resulting effective single-spin dynamics bypass noise and feedback penalties, achieving higher capacity than coherent-phase regimes for non-fixed-point attractors.

Abstract: We develop a dynamical mean-field theory for nonreciprocal associative networks that store an extensive number of dynamical attractors, from limit cycles to strange attractors. Using a path integral calculation under quenched disorder, we derive self-consistent dynamical mean-field equations for pattern overlaps, autocorrelations and response functions. Memory retrieval capacity is governed by the spectral structure of the coupling matrices encoding stored patterns. When their eigenvalues are coherently aligned, retarded self-interactions and quenched noise feed back destructively: at zero eigenphase (fixed point attractors) the classical equilibrium capacity bound is recovered, while for limit cycles retrieval collapses far below it. In contrast, for uniformly distributed eigenphases, retarded self-interactions and much of the quenched noise cancels, reducing the dynamics to an effective single-spin process and amplifying capacity substantially. We validate the theory against microscopic simulations for limit-cycle and chaotic attractors, identifying eigenvalue decoherence as the mechanism enabling enhanced storage of dynamical memories.

</details>


### [170] [Graph neural networks and the energetic cavity method for combinatorial optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07456)
*Joe Bacchus George,George T. Cantwell*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper investigates using graph neural networks (GNNs) to approximate ground states of Ising models. While vanilla GNNs underperform compared to established mean-field-based heuristics (eigenvector method and min-sum/energetic cavity algorithm), incorporating insights from these heuristics into GNN architecture modifications significantly improves performance. However, the enhanced GNN remains only competitive with other deep-learning approaches and is not superior to simulated annealing at comparable computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finding ground states of Ising models is significant because many combinatorial optimization problems can be formulated as Ising models. Exact solving is hard, but there are good heuristic methods. The paper seeks to explore whether GNNs can serve as competitive heuristics for this problem class.

Method: The authors compare standard GNNs against established mean-field heuristics (leading eigenvector method and min-sum/energetic cavity method), then introduce small architectural modifications to GNNs that incorporate ideas derived from these heuristics, and evaluate the modified approach against simulated annealing and other deep-learning baselines.

Result: Unmodified GNNs perform worse than both mean-field heuristics. Incorporating heuristic-derived modifications into GNNs considerably improves performance, making them competitive with other deep-learning approaches. However, simulated annealing remains reliably at least as good as deep learning methods at the same computational cost.

Conclusion: While modifying GNNs with domain-specific heuristic knowledge boosts their performance on Ising ground-state search, deep learning methods still do not surpass classical approaches like simulated annealing given equal computational budgets.

Abstract: We study the use of graph neural networks (GNNs) for finding approximate ground states of Ising models. Efficiently finding these ground states is of broad significance because many combinatorial optimization problems can be formulated as an Ising model with the appropriate choice of couplings and fields. Exactly solving these problems is hard but there are many good heuristic methods. A lineage of these heuristics build from mean-field approximations: one approach uses the leading eigenvector of an appropriately defined matrix, another is the min-sum algorithm, also known as the energetic cavity method. Without modification, GNNs perform worse than both of these methods. We consider small modifications to the GNN to incorporate these heuristics and find that this considerably improves performance. While the modified approach is competitive against other deep-learning approaches, we still find that simulated annealing is reliably at least as good as deep learning methods for the same computational cost.

</details>


### [171] [A Bifurcation Theory for the Equilibria of Modern Hopfield Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07757)
*Vincenzo Maria Schimmenti,Matteo Ciarchi*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper develops a general stability and bifurcation theory for Modern Hopfield networks (MHNs) with arbitrary stored-pattern statistics, showing how memory correlations give rise to hierarchical attractors via successive bifurcations, validated on MNIST and hematopoietic cell data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern Hopfield networks unify associative memory, transformer attention, diffusion models, and biological attractor dynamics, but no general theory of their fixed-point bifurcations has existed beyond specific architectures and idealized pattern ensembles.

Method: The authors derive stability and bifurcation criteria for fixed points of general convex-dual Modern Hopfield networks under general pattern statistics, then apply this framework to random, block-correlated, and infinitely hierarchical pattern ensembles to show how memory correlations organize hierarchical attractor emergence through successive bifurcations.

Result: The theoretical predictions quantitatively capture retrieval bifurcations in MHNs storing MNIST patterns and recapitulate the hierarchical organization of hematopoietic cell identities.

Conclusion: The results establish a general bifurcation theory for Modern Hopfield networks and identify fixed-point landscape organization as a unifying principle underlying their computational and biological behavior.

Abstract: Modern Hopfield networks provide a unifying framework for associative memory, transformer attention, diffusion-based generative models, and biological attractor dynamics, linking these systems through a common energy-based dynamics in which states are updated toward weighted combinations of stored patterns. Across these settings, network dynamics is determined by the organization of the energy landscape and the bifurcations of its fixed points. Despite their central role, a general theory of these bifurcations has remained unavailable beyond specific architectures and idealized pattern ensembles. Here we derive stability and bifurcation criteria for the fixed points of general convex-dual Modern Hopfield networks for a general statistics of the stored patterns. Applying this framework to random, block-correlated, and infinitely hierarchical pattern ensembles, we show how memory correlations systematically organize the emergence of hierarchical attractors through successive bifurcations. We further demonstrate that these predictions quantitatively describe retrieval bifurcations in MHNs storing patterns sampled from MNIST, and recapitulate the hierarchical organization of hematopoietic cell identities. Our results establish a general bifurcation theory for Modern Hopfield networks and identify the organization of fixed points as a unifying principle underlying their computational and biological behavior.

</details>
